Q: Bill the miner discovers a vein of fool's gold. He successfully sells 8 ounces to a merchant, but when he tries to sell to the sheriff he's arrested, fined $50 and run out of town on a rail. If Bill earned $9 for every ounce of fool's gold he sold, how much money is he left with?
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Bill sold 8 ounces of fool's gold.  
- He earned $9 for every ounce sold.  
- He was fined $50 after being arrested.  
- We assume Bill had no other income or expenses during this transaction.  
- The goal is to calculate how much money Bill is left with after paying the fine.  

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
**Method 1:** Calculate the total earnings from selling fool's gold, then subtract the fine directly.  
**Method 2:** Break the problem into smaller steps: calculate earnings per ounce, multiply by the number of ounces sold, and then subtract the fine.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Total earnings = 8 ounces × $9 per ounce.  
  Calculation: \( 8 \times 9 = 72 \).  
- Subtract the fine: \( 72 - 50 = 22 \).  
- Double-check: \( 72 - 50 = 22 \).  

**Method 2:**  
- Step 1: Calculate earnings per ounce: $9 per ounce.  
- Step 2: Multiply by the number of ounces sold: \( 9 \times 8 = 72 \).  
- Step 3: Subtract the fine: \( 72 - 50 = 22 \).  
- Double-check: \( 72 - 50 = 22 \).  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- Both methods are straightforward and yield the same result.  
- Method 1 is slightly more direct, as it combines the multiplication and subtraction steps into a single process.  
- Method 2 is more detailed, breaking the problem into smaller steps, which may be helpful for clarity in more complex problems.  

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Method 1 is better for this problem due to its simplicity.  
- Solution: \( 8 \times 9 = 72 \), then \( 72 - 50 = 22 \).  

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Recalculate: \( 8 \times 9 = 72 \), \( 72 - 50 = 22 \).  
- The calculations are correct, and the result is reasonable given the context.  

The answer is **22**.

Q: Anna sold 36 glasses of plain lemonade for $0.75 each. If she made $16 total from selling strawberry lemonade, how much more did she make from plain lemonade than strawberry?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Anna sold **36 glasses of plain lemonade** at **$0.75 each**.
- She made **$16 total** from selling strawberry lemonade.
- We are tasked with finding how much **more** Anna made from plain lemonade than strawberry lemonade.
- Assumptions:
  - All glasses of lemonade were sold at the stated prices.
  - There are no additional costs or discounts involved.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total revenue from plain lemonade by multiplying the number of glasses sold by the price per glass. Subtract the revenue from strawberry lemonade ($16) from the revenue from plain lemonade to find the difference.

**Method 2:**  
Estimate the revenue from plain lemonade by rounding the price per glass ($0.75) to $1 and adjusting for the difference later. Subtract the strawberry lemonade revenue ($16) from the adjusted plain lemonade revenue to find the difference.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Calculation**
1. Calculate the revenue from plain lemonade:
   \[
   \text{Revenue from plain lemonade} = 36 \times 0.75
   \]
   Perform the multiplication:
   \[
   36 \times 0.75 = 27
   \]
   So, Anna made **$27** from plain lemonade.

2. Subtract the revenue from strawberry lemonade:
   \[
   \text{Difference} = 27 - 16
   \]
   Perform the subtraction:
   \[
   27 - 16 = 11
   \]
   So, Anna made **$11 more** from plain lemonade than strawberry lemonade.

#### **Method 2: Estimation and Adjustment**
1. Estimate the revenue from plain lemonade by rounding $0.75 to $1:
   \[
   \text{Estimated revenue} = 36 \times 1 = 36
   \]
   Adjust for the rounding error:
   - Each glass of plain lemonade is $0.25 less than $1.
   - Total adjustment:
     \[
     \text{Adjustment} = 36 \times 0.25 = 9
     \]
   Subtract the adjustment from the estimated revenue:
   \[
   \text{Actual revenue from plain lemonade} = 36 - 9 = 27
   \]

2. Subtract the revenue from strawberry lemonade:
   \[
   \text{Difference} = 27 - 16
   \]
   Perform the subtraction:
   \[
   27 - 16 = 11
   \]
   So, Anna made **$11 more** from plain lemonade than strawberry lemonade.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more direct and precise, as it involves straightforward multiplication and subtraction without estimation. It minimizes the risk of errors and is computationally efficient.
- **Method 2** introduces estimation and adjustment, which can be useful for mental math but adds extra steps and potential for mistakes. It is less reliable for exact calculations.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

**Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and accuracy. Using Method 1:
1. Calculate revenue from plain lemonade:
   \[
   36 \times 0.75 = 27
   \]
2. Subtract strawberry lemonade revenue:
   \[
   27 - 16 = 11
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recalculate revenue from plain lemonade:
  \[
  36 \times 0.75 = 27
  \]
- Verify subtraction:
  \[
  27 - 16 = 11
  \]
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **11**.

Q: The moon has a surface area that is 1/5 that of Earth. The surface area of the Earth is 200 square acres. The land on the moon is worth 6 times that of the land on the Earth. If the total value of all the land on the earth is 80 billion dollars, what is the total value in billions of all the land on the moon?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

- The surface area of the moon is **1/5** that of Earth.  
- The surface area of Earth is **200 square acres**.  
- The land on the moon is worth **6 times** the value of land on Earth.  
- The total value of all the land on Earth is **80 billion dollars**.  
- We assume the value of land is proportional to the surface area.  
- We are tasked with finding the total value of all the land on the moon in billions of dollars.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.

**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the surface area of the moon using the ratio (1/5 of Earth's surface area).  
2. Determine the value of land per square acre on Earth by dividing Earth's total land value by its surface area.  
3. Multiply the moon's surface area by the value per acre on Earth, then multiply by 6 (since moon land is worth 6 times more).  

**Method 2:**  
1. Use the ratio of surface areas (1/5) and the multiplier for moon land value (6 times Earth’s value) to directly calculate the moon’s total land value as a fraction of Earth’s total land value.  
2. Multiply Earth’s total land value by the combined factor (1/5 × 6).  

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Step-by-step calculations**  
1. **Calculate the surface area of the moon:**  
   - Moon's surface area = (1/5) × Earth's surface area = (1/5) × 200 = **40 square acres**.  

2. **Determine the value of land per square acre on Earth:**  
   - Value per acre on Earth = Earth's total land value ÷ Earth's surface area = 80 billion ÷ 200 = **0.4 billion dollars per acre**.  

3. **Calculate the total value of land on the moon:**  
   - Moon's total land value = Moon's surface area × Value per acre on Earth × 6.  
   - Moon's total land value = 40 × 0.4 × 6 = **96 billion dollars**.  

---

#### **Method 2: Step-by-step calculations**  
1. **Combine the ratios for surface area and land value multiplier:**  
   - Moon's total land value = Earth's total land value × (1/5 × 6).  
   - Moon's total land value = 80 × (1/5 × 6) = 80 × (6/5) = **96 billion dollars**.  

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** involves breaking the problem into smaller steps (surface area, value per acre, total value), which is helpful for clarity and understanding. However, it requires more intermediate calculations.  
- **Method 2** is more efficient, as it directly uses the ratios to calculate the moon's total land value in one step. It avoids intermediate calculations, reducing the chance of error.  

**Conclusion:** Method 2 is better for efficiency and simplicity, but Method 1 provides more detailed insight into the problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

Using **Method 2**:  
- Moon's total land value = Earth's total land value × (1/5 × 6).  
- Moon's total land value = 80 × (6/5) = **96 billion dollars**.  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.

- Using **Method 1**, we calculated the moon's surface area (40 acres), the value per acre on Earth (0.4 billion dollars), and the total value of moon land (40 × 0.4 × 6 = 96 billion dollars).  
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the accuracy of the solution.  

---

The answer is **96 billion dollars**.

Q: Elsa and her sister watch an opera show every year at Central City Opera. Last year, opera seating cost $85 per ticket. This year, it costs $102. What is the percent increase in the cost of each ticket?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Last year, the cost of an opera ticket was $85.
- This year, the cost of an opera ticket is $102.
- We are tasked with finding the **percent increase** in the ticket price.
- Assumptions:
  - The increase is calculated as a percentage of last year's price ($85).
  - No additional fees or discounts are involved.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct Percent Change Formula**  
- Use the formula for percent change:  
  \[
  \text{Percent Increase} = \frac{\text{New Price} - \text{Old Price}}{\text{Old Price}} \times 100
  \]
- Subtract the old price from the new price to find the absolute increase, divide by the old price, and multiply by 100 to express the result as a percentage.

**Method 2: Ratio and Scaling Approach**  
- Find the ratio of the new price to the old price:  
  \[
  \text{Ratio} = \frac{\text{New Price}}{\text{Old Price}}
  \]
- Subtract 1 from the ratio to find the fractional increase, then multiply by 100 to convert to a percentage.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Percent Change Formula**
1. Subtract the old price from the new price to find the absolute increase:  
   \[
   \text{Absolute Increase} = 102 - 85 = 17
   \]
2. Divide the absolute increase by the old price:  
   \[
   \frac{17}{85} = 0.2
   \]
3. Multiply by 100 to convert to a percentage:  
   \[
   0.2 \times 100 = 20\%
   \]
4. Double-check: The absolute increase is $17, which is 20% of $85 (since $85 \times 0.2 = 17).

#### **Method 2: Ratio and Scaling Approach**
1. Find the ratio of the new price to the old price:  
   \[
   \text{Ratio} = \frac{102}{85} = 1.2
   \]
2. Subtract 1 from the ratio to find the fractional increase:  
   \[
   1.2 - 1 = 0.2
   \]
3. Multiply by 100 to convert to a percentage:  
   \[
   0.2 \times 100 = 20\%
   \]
4. Double-check: A ratio of 1.2 means the new price is 120% of the old price, so the increase is 20%.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more intuitive and directly applies the percent change formula, making it easier to follow for most people.
- **Method 2** is slightly more abstract but useful for understanding proportional relationships. It is less direct but equally reliable.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer and more efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Absolute increase:  
   \[
   102 - 85 = 17
   \]
2. Divide by the old price:  
   \[
   \frac{17}{85} = 0.2
   \]
3. Convert to a percentage:  
   \[
   0.2 \times 100 = 20\%
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- The absolute increase is $17, which is 20% of $85 (since $85 \times 0.2 = 17).  
- The result is reasonable because the new price ($102) is clearly 20% higher than the old price ($85).

---

The answer is **20%**.

Q: Jesse is playing with a pile of building blocks. He first builds a building with 80 building blocks. Then he builds a farmhouse with 123 building blocks. He adds a fenced-in area next to the farm made of 57 building blocks. If Jesse has 84 building blocks left, how many building blocks did he start with?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jesse builds three structures:
  1. A building using **80 building blocks**.
  2. A farmhouse using **123 building blocks**.
  3. A fenced-in area using **57 building blocks**.
- After building these structures, Jesse has **84 building blocks left**.
- We are tasked with finding how many building blocks Jesse started with.
- Assumptions:
  - No blocks are lost, broken, or added during the process.
  - All blocks used in the structures and the remaining blocks are accounted for.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Add up all the blocks used and the remaining blocks.**  
- The total number of blocks Jesse started with is the sum of:
  1. The blocks used for the building, farmhouse, and fenced-in area.
  2. The blocks he has left over.

**Method 2: Work backward from the remaining blocks.**  
- Start with the remaining blocks (84) and add back the blocks used for each structure:
  1. Add the blocks used for the fenced-in area.
  2. Add the blocks used for the farmhouse.
  3. Add the blocks used for the building.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Add up all the blocks used and the remaining blocks.**
1. Calculate the total number of blocks used:
   - Building: **80 blocks**.
   - Farmhouse: **123 blocks**.
   - Fenced-in area: **57 blocks**.
   - Total blocks used = \( 80 + 123 + 57 = 260 \).
2. Add the remaining blocks to the total blocks used:
   - Remaining blocks = **84**.
   - Total blocks Jesse started with = \( 260 + 84 = 344 \).

**Check for pitfalls:**
- Ensure all blocks used and remaining are accounted for.
- Double-check the addition of the blocks used and the final total.

#### **Method 2: Work backward from the remaining blocks.**
1. Start with the remaining blocks:
   - Remaining blocks = **84**.
2. Add the blocks used for the fenced-in area:
   - \( 84 + 57 = 141 \).
3. Add the blocks used for the farmhouse:
   - \( 141 + 123 = 264 \).
4. Add the blocks used for the building:
   - \( 264 + 80 = 344 \).

**Check for pitfalls:**
- Ensure the order of adding blocks is consistent and logical.
- Double-check each step of addition to avoid errors.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is straightforward and involves fewer steps, as it directly calculates the total blocks used and adds the remaining blocks. It is less prone to errors because it consolidates the calculations into two main steps.
- **Method 2** is more intuitive for someone who prefers working backward, but it involves more intermediate steps, increasing the chance of arithmetic errors.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Calculate the total blocks used:
   - \( 80 + 123 + 57 = 260 \).
2. Add the remaining blocks:
   - \( 260 + 84 = 344 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

1. Verify the total blocks used:
   - \( 80 + 123 = 203 \), \( 203 + 57 = 260 \). Correct.
2. Verify the total blocks Jesse started with:
   - \( 260 + 84 = 344 \). Correct.
3. Verify the reasonableness of the result:
   - Jesse used 260 blocks and has 84 left, so it makes sense that he started with 344 blocks.

---

The answer is **344**.

Q: Jason has six fish in his aquarium. He realizes that every day the number of fish doubles. On the third day he takes out one-third of the fish. On the fifth day he takes out one-fourth of the fish. On the seventh day he adds 15 more fish. How many fish in total does he have?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

- Jason starts with **6 fish** in his aquarium.  
- **Every day, the number of fish doubles.**  
- On the **third day**, Jason removes **one-third** of the fish.  
- On the **fifth day**, Jason removes **one-fourth** of the fish.  
- On the **seventh day**, Jason **adds 15 more fish**.  
- We assume no fish die or escape, and the doubling happens before any fish are removed.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.

#### Method 1: Sequential calculation of fish count day by day.  
- This method involves calculating the number of fish at the end of each day, applying the doubling and removal operations step by step.  

#### Method 2: Use a formula-based approach.  
- This method involves deriving a general formula for the number of fish at any given day, applying the doubling and removal operations algebraically, and solving for the final count.  

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation day by day.**

1. **Day 1:** Jason starts with **6 fish**.  
   Fish count = 6.  

2. **Day 2:** The number of fish doubles.  
   Fish count = \( 6 \times 2 = 12 \).  

3. **Day 3:** The number of fish doubles again, then Jason removes one-third of the fish.  
   - Fish count after doubling: \( 12 \times 2 = 24 \).  
   - Fish removed: \( \frac{1}{3} \times 24 = 8 \).  
   - Fish remaining: \( 24 - 8 = 16 \).  

4. **Day 4:** The number of fish doubles.  
   Fish count = \( 16 \times 2 = 32 \).  

5. **Day 5:** The number of fish doubles again, then Jason removes one-fourth of the fish.  
   - Fish count after doubling: \( 32 \times 2 = 64 \).  
   - Fish removed: \( \frac{1}{4} \times 64 = 16 \).  
   - Fish remaining: \( 64 - 16 = 48 \).  

6. **Day 6:** The number of fish doubles.  
   Fish count = \( 48 \times 2 = 96 \).  

7. **Day 7:** The number of fish doubles again, then Jason adds 15 more fish.  
   - Fish count after doubling: \( 96 \times 2 = 192 \).  
   - Fish added: \( 192 + 15 = 207 \).  

**Final fish count:** 207.

---

#### **Method 2: Formula-based approach.**

1. **Day-by-day doubling:**  
   - Day 1: \( 6 \).  
   - Day 2: \( 6 \times 2 = 12 \).  
   - Day 3: \( 12 \times 2 = 24 \), then remove one-third: \( 24 - \frac{1}{3} \times 24 = 24 - 8 = 16 \).  
   - Day 4: \( 16 \times 2 = 32 \).  
   - Day 5: \( 32 \times 2 = 64 \), then remove one-fourth: \( 64 - \frac{1}{4} \times 64 = 64 - 16 = 48 \).  
   - Day 6: \( 48 \times 2 = 96 \).  
   - Day 7: \( 96 \times 2 = 192 \), then add 15: \( 192 + 15 = 207 \).  

2. **Final fish count:** 207.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more intuitive and straightforward, as it calculates the fish count step by step, making it easier to follow and verify.  
- **Method 2** is formulaic and efficient but requires careful tracking of operations, which can lead to errors if not done systematically.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

- **Method 1** is better for clarity and reliability.  
- Using Method 1, the final fish count is **207**.  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recalculate each step:  
  - Day 1: 6.  
  - Day 2: \( 6 \times 2 = 12 \).  
  - Day 3: \( 12 \times 2 = 24 \), remove \( \frac{1}{3} \times 24 = 8 \), remaining: \( 24 - 8 = 16 \).  
  - Day 4: \( 16 \times 2 = 32 \).  
  - Day 5: \( 32 \times 2 = 64 \), remove \( \frac{1}{4} \times 64 = 16 \), remaining: \( 64 - 16 = 48 \).  
  - Day 6: \( 48 \times 2 = 96 \).  
  - Day 7: \( 96 \times 2 = 192 \), add 15: \( 192 + 15 = 207 \).  

- The calculations check out.  

### Final Answer:  
The answer is **207**.

Q: The total age of the people in Kaydence's family is 200. If Kaydence's father is 60 years old, Kaydence's mother 2 years younger than Kaydence's father, Kaydence's brother 1/2 the age of Kaydence's father, and Kaydence's sister 40 years old, how old is Kaydence?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The total age of Kaydence's family is **200 years**.
- Kaydence's father is **60 years old**.
- Kaydence's mother is **2 years younger than Kaydence's father**, so her age is \( 60 - 2 = 58 \).
- Kaydence's brother is **half the age of Kaydence's father**, so his age is \( 60 \div 2 = 30 \).
- Kaydence's sister is **40 years old**.
- Kaydence's age is unknown, and we need to calculate it.
- Assume there are no other family members contributing to the total age.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Subtract the known ages from the total age.**  
- Add up the ages of Kaydence's father, mother, brother, and sister.
- Subtract this sum from the total family age (200) to find Kaydence's age.

**Method 2: Use an equation to represent the total age.**  
- Let Kaydence's age be \( x \).
- Write an equation for the total family age:  
  \( 60 + 58 + 30 + 40 + x = 200 \).  
- Solve for \( x \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtract the known ages from the total age**
1. Calculate the sum of the known ages:
   - Father's age: \( 60 \)
   - Mother's age: \( 58 \)
   - Brother's age: \( 30 \)
   - Sister's age: \( 40 \)
   - Total of known ages:  
     \( 60 + 58 + 30 + 40 = 188 \).
2. Subtract the sum of the known ages from the total family age:
   - \( 200 - 188 = 12 \).
3. Kaydence's age is \( 12 \).

#### **Method 2: Use an equation to represent the total age**
1. Let Kaydence's age be \( x \).
2. Write the equation for the total family age:  
   \( 60 + 58 + 30 + 40 + x = 200 \).
3. Simplify the equation:
   - \( 188 + x = 200 \).
4. Solve for \( x \):
   - \( x = 200 - 188 \).
   - \( x = 12 \).
5. Kaydence's age is \( 12 \).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and involves fewer steps, making it faster and less prone to errors. It directly subtracts the known ages from the total.
- **Method 2** is more formal and uses algebra, which is useful for more complex problems but unnecessary here since the subtraction approach is simpler.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient for this specific problem.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice.
1. Add the known ages:  
   \( 60 + 58 + 30 + 40 = 188 \).
2. Subtract the sum from the total family age:  
   \( 200 - 188 = 12 \).
3. Kaydence's age is \( 12 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
1. Verify the total family age:
   - Father's age: \( 60 \)
   - Mother's age: \( 58 \)
   - Brother's age: \( 30 \)
   - Sister's age: \( 40 \)
   - Kaydence's age: \( 12 \)
   - Total: \( 60 + 58 + 30 + 40 + 12 = 200 \).
2. The total checks out, confirming the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **12**.

Q: Maria has 19 cookies. She decided to give her friend 5 of them, and half of the rest to her family. From the rest, Maria decided to eat 2 cookies. How many cookies will she have left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Maria starts with **19 cookies**.
- She gives **5 cookies** to her friend.
- She gives **half of the remaining cookies** to her family.
- From the cookies left after giving to her family, Maria eats **2 cookies**.
- We assume no cookies are lost, and all operations (giving, dividing, eating) are performed sequentially.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Sequential subtraction and division.  
- Subtract 5 cookies given to her friend from the initial 19 cookies.  
- Divide the remaining cookies by 2 to find how many are given to her family.  
- Subtract 2 cookies that Maria eats from the remaining cookies.  

**Method 2:** Use algebra to represent the sequence of operations.  
- Let the number of cookies Maria has after each step be represented by variables.  
- Write equations for each step and solve them sequentially to find the final number of cookies Maria has left.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential subtraction and division**
1. Start with 19 cookies.  
   - Maria gives 5 cookies to her friend:  
     \( 19 - 5 = 14 \).  
   - Remaining cookies: **14**.

2. Maria gives half of the remaining cookies to her family:  
   \( \frac{14}{2} = 7 \).  
   - Remaining cookies: **7**.

3. Maria eats 2 cookies:  
   \( 7 - 2 = 5 \).  
   - Remaining cookies: **5**.

#### **Method 2: Algebraic representation**
1. Let \( x = 19 \) (initial number of cookies).  
   - After giving 5 cookies to her friend:  
     \( x - 5 = 14 \).  

2. Half of the remaining cookies are given to her family:  
   \( \frac{14}{2} = 7 \).  

3. After eating 2 cookies:  
   \( 7 - 2 = 5 \).  

Both methods yield the same result: **5 cookies**.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and involves simple arithmetic operations performed step by step. It is easy to follow and minimizes the risk of errors.  
- **Method 2** uses algebra, which is more abstract but equally reliable. However, for a problem of this complexity, algebra may be unnecessarily complicated.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this problem due to its simplicity and clarity.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Start with 19 cookies.  
   \( 19 - 5 = 14 \).  
   Remaining cookies: **14**.

2. Give half to her family:  
   \( \frac{14}{2} = 7 \).  
   Remaining cookies: **7**.

3. Eat 2 cookies:  
   \( 7 - 2 = 5 \).  
   Remaining cookies: **5**.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Initial cookies: 19.  
- After giving 5 to her friend: \( 19 - 5 = 14 \).  
- After giving half to her family: \( \frac{14}{2} = 7 \).  
- After eating 2 cookies: \( 7 - 2 = 5 \).  

All calculations are correct and consistent with the problem's logic.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **5**.

Q: Sarah, Mary, and Tuan decided to go to the restaurant for a meal. They decided to split the cost of the meal evenly. If the total price of the meal comes to $67 and they have a coupon for $4, how much does each person need to contribute to the bill?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The total price of the meal is $67.
- They have a coupon worth $4, which reduces the total cost of the meal.
- Sarah, Mary, and Tuan will split the final cost evenly.
- We assume no additional taxes, tips, or fees are applied to the bill.
- We also assume the coupon is applied to the total cost before splitting.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
1. Subtract the coupon value ($4) from the total cost of the meal ($67) to find the discounted total.  
2. Divide the discounted total by 3 to determine how much each person contributes.  

**Method 2:**  
1. First, calculate the cost per person without the coupon by dividing the total cost ($67) by 3.  
2. Then, calculate the coupon's value per person by dividing $4 by 3.  
3. Subtract the coupon's value per person from the initial cost per person to find the final contribution for each individual.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Subtract the coupon value from the total cost:  
   \[
   67 - 4 = 63
   \]  
   The discounted total is $63.  

2. Divide the discounted total by 3 to find each person's share:  
   \[
   63 \div 3 = 21
   \]  
   Each person contributes $21.  

3. Double-check: Multiply each person's contribution by 3 to ensure it matches the discounted total:  
   \[
   21 \times 3 = 63
   \]  
   The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate the cost per person without the coupon:  
   \[
   67 \div 3 = 22.33\ (\text{rounded to two decimal places})
   \]  
   Each person would pay $22.33 without the coupon.  

2. Calculate the coupon's value per person:  
   \[
   4 \div 3 = 1.33\ (\text{rounded to two decimal places})
   \]  
   Each person gets a $1.33 discount from the coupon.  

3. Subtract the coupon's value per person from the initial cost per person:  
   \[
   22.33 - 1.33 = 21
   \]  
   Each person contributes $21.  

4. Double-check: Multiply each person's contribution by 3 to ensure it matches the discounted total:  
   \[
   21 \times 3 = 63
   \]  
   The calculation checks out.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and involves fewer steps, as it directly applies the coupon to the total cost before dividing.  
- **Method 2** is more complex, as it calculates the cost per person and the coupon's value per person separately, which introduces more intermediate steps and potential for rounding errors.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and less prone to error.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method.  
1. Subtract the coupon value from the total cost:  
   \[
   67 - 4 = 63
   \]  
2. Divide the discounted total by 3:  
   \[
   63 \div 3 = 21
   \]  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the total contribution:  
  \[
  21 \times 3 = 63
  \]  
  The total matches the discounted cost, confirming the solution is correct.  

The answer is **21**.

Q: The new pad of paper has 120 pages. Sammy uses 25% of the pages for his science project, and another 10 pages for his math homework. How many pages remain in the pad?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The pad of paper has **120 pages** initially.
- Sammy uses **25% of the pages** for his science project.
- Sammy uses an additional **10 pages** for his math homework.
- We assume no other pages are used, lost, or damaged.
- The goal is to determine how many pages remain in the pad after these uses.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Sequential subtraction approach**
1. Calculate the number of pages used for the science project by finding 25% of 120.
2. Subtract the pages used for the science project from the total.
3. Subtract the additional 10 pages used for math homework from the remaining pages.

**Method 2: Total pages used first, then subtract**
1. Calculate the number of pages used for the science project (25% of 120).
2. Add the 10 pages used for math homework to the pages used for the science project.
3. Subtract the total pages used from the initial total of 120 pages.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential subtraction approach**
1. **Calculate 25% of 120:**
   - \( 25\% = \frac{25}{100} = 0.25 \)
   - \( 0.25 \times 120 = 30 \)
   - Sammy uses **30 pages** for his science project.

2. **Subtract the 30 pages used for the science project:**
   - \( 120 - 30 = 90 \)
   - **90 pages remain** after the science project.

3. **Subtract the 10 pages used for math homework:**
   - \( 90 - 10 = 80 \)
   - **80 pages remain** after both uses.

4. **Check for errors:**
   - Total pages used: \( 30 + 10 = 40 \).
   - Remaining pages: \( 120 - 40 = 80 \), which matches the result.

#### **Method 2: Total pages used first, then subtract**
1. **Calculate 25% of 120:**
   - \( 0.25 \times 120 = 30 \)
   - Sammy uses **30 pages** for his science project.

2. **Add the 10 pages used for math homework:**
   - \( 30 + 10 = 40 \)
   - Sammy uses a total of **40 pages**.

3. **Subtract the total pages used from the initial total:**
   - \( 120 - 40 = 80 \)
   - **80 pages remain** after both uses.

4. **Check for errors:**
   - Total pages used: \( 30 + 10 = 40 \).
   - Remaining pages: \( 120 - 40 = 80 \), which matches the result.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more intuitive because it mirrors the sequence of events (science project first, then math homework). It is easier to follow step by step.
- **Method 2** is slightly more efficient because it combines the total pages used before subtracting, reducing the number of steps. However, it requires keeping track of the total pages used, which might introduce errors if not done carefully.
- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer for most people due to its alignment with the problem's narrative.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and alignment with the problem's sequence.

1. Calculate 25% of 120:
   - \( 0.25 \times 120 = 30 \)
   - Sammy uses **30 pages** for his science project.

2. Subtract the 30 pages used for the science project:
   - \( 120 - 30 = 90 \)
   - **90 pages remain** after the science project.

3. Subtract the 10 pages used for math homework:
   - \( 90 - 10 = 80 \)
   - **80 pages remain** after both uses.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Total pages used: \( 30 + 10 = 40 \).
- Remaining pages: \( 120 - 40 = 80 \), which matches the result.
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **80**.

Q: Lily types 15 words a minute and takes a 2-minute break every 10 minutes. How long does it take for Lily to type 255 words?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Lily types **15 words per minute**.
- She takes a **2-minute break every 10 minutes** of typing.
- The goal is to determine how long it takes Lily to type **255 words**.
- Assumptions:
  - Lily's typing speed is constant at 15 words per minute.
  - Breaks are strictly 2 minutes and occur after every 10 minutes of typing.
  - Breaks do not contribute to the word count.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Calculate in cycles of 10 minutes of typing + 2 minutes of break.**
- In 10 minutes, Lily types \( 15 \times 10 = 150 \) words.
- After every 10 minutes of typing, she takes a 2-minute break, making each cycle 12 minutes long.
- Determine how many full cycles are needed to type 255 words, then account for any remaining words.

**Method 2: Calculate total time without breaks first, then add break time.**
- First, calculate how many minutes Lily would need to type 255 words without considering breaks: \( \text{Total words} \div \text{Typing speed} \).
- Then, calculate how many breaks she would take based on the total typing time and add the corresponding break time.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Calculate in cycles of 10 minutes of typing + 2 minutes of break.**

1. **Determine how many words Lily types per cycle:**
   - In 10 minutes, Lily types \( 15 \times 10 = 150 \) words.
   - Each cycle (10 minutes of typing + 2 minutes of break) takes 12 minutes.

2. **Determine how many full cycles are needed:**
   - Lily can type 150 words per cycle.
   - To type 255 words, calculate how many full cycles are needed:
     \[
     \text{Number of full cycles} = \left\lfloor \frac{255}{150} \right\rfloor = 1 \text{ full cycle (150 words)}.
     \]
   - After 1 full cycle, Lily has typed 150 words. She still needs:
     \[
     255 - 150 = 105 \text{ words}.
     \]

3. **Calculate the time for the remaining words:**
   - To type 105 words, Lily needs:
     \[
     \frac{105}{15} = 7 \text{ minutes}.
     \]

4. **Add up the total time:**
   - The first full cycle takes 12 minutes (10 minutes of typing + 2 minutes of break).
   - The remaining 105 words take 7 minutes, with no additional break since it’s less than 10 minutes.
   - Total time:
     \[
     12 + 7 = 19 \text{ minutes}.
     \]

---

#### **Method 2: Calculate total time without breaks first, then add break time.**

1. **Calculate the total typing time without breaks:**
   - To type 255 words at 15 words per minute:
     \[
     \frac{255}{15} = 17 \text{ minutes}.
     \]

2. **Determine how many breaks Lily takes:**
   - Lily takes a 2-minute break after every 10 minutes of typing.
   - In 17 minutes of typing, she completes 1 full 10-minute block and starts a second block but does not reach 20 minutes.
   - Therefore, she takes **1 break**.

3. **Add the break time:**
   - Total time = Typing time + Break time:
     \[
     17 + 2 = 19 \text{ minutes}.
     \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more structured and explicitly accounts for the cycles of typing and breaks. It is easier to follow for problems involving repetitive patterns like this one.
- **Method 2** is faster and more direct but requires careful attention to ensure the correct number of breaks is added.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more reliable for problems with more complex cycles or additional constraints.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and reliability.
- Using Method 1, the total time for Lily to type 255 words is:
  \[
  12 \text{ minutes (1 full cycle)} + 7 \text{ minutes (remaining words)} = 19 \text{ minutes}.
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recalculate using Method 2:
  - Typing time = \( \frac{255}{15} = 17 \) minutes.
  - Breaks = 1 (after the first 10 minutes).
  - Total time = \( 17 + 2 = 19 \) minutes.
- Both methods confirm the result.

---

The answer is **19 minutes**.

Q: Elias uses a bar of soap every month. If each bar of soap costs $4, how much does he spend on bars of soap in two years?
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Elias uses one bar of soap every month.  
- Each bar of soap costs $4.  
- Two years consist of 24 months (12 months per year × 2 years).  
- We assume the cost of soap remains constant over the two years.  

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
Method 1: Multiply the number of months (24) by the cost per bar of soap ($4). This method directly calculates the total cost.  
Method 2: Break the problem into smaller steps: calculate the cost for one year (12 months × $4), then double the result to account for two years.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Total months = 24.  
- Cost per bar = $4.  
- Multiply: 24 × 4 = 96.  
- Double-check: Break 24 into smaller parts (20 + 4), then calculate:  
  - 20 × 4 = 80.  
  - 4 × 4 = 16.  
  - Add: 80 + 16 = 96.  
- Result: $96.  

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the cost for one year:  
  - Months in one year = 12.  
  - Cost per bar = $4.  
  - Multiply: 12 × 4 = 48.  
- Double the result for two years:  
  - 48 × 2 = 96.  
- Double-check: Break 48 into smaller parts (40 + 8), then calculate:  
  - 40 × 2 = 80.  
  - 8 × 2 = 16.  
  - Add: 80 + 16 = 96.  
- Result: $96.  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- Method 1 is more direct and efficient, as it calculates the total cost in one step.  
- Method 2 involves breaking the problem into smaller parts, which may be helpful for mental math but introduces more intermediate steps.  
- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but Method 1 is simpler and faster for this problem.  

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.  
- Solution: 24 × 4 = 96.  

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Recalculate using Method 2:  
  - Cost for one year = 12 × 4 = 48.  
  - Cost for two years = 48 × 2 = 96.  
- Both methods confirm the result.  

The answer is **96**.

Q: Jacque arrived in France and his suitcase weighed 5 pounds.  While in France, he picked up 5 bottles of perfume that weighed 1.2 ounces each, 4 pounds of chocolate, 2 bars of soap that weighed 5 ounces each and 2 jars of jam that weighed 8 ounces each.  Considering 16 ounces is the same as 1 pound, how much did Jacque's suitcase weigh on the return flight home?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jacque's suitcase initially weighed **5 pounds**.
- He added the following items while in France:
  - **5 bottles of perfume**, each weighing **1.2 ounces**.
  - **4 pounds of chocolate**.
  - **2 bars of soap**, each weighing **5 ounces**.
  - **2 jars of jam**, each weighing **8 ounces**.
- **16 ounces = 1 pound** (conversion factor).
- We assume no items were removed from the suitcase, and the weight of the suitcase itself remains constant.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Convert all weights to pounds first, then sum them.**
- Convert the weights of the perfume, soap, and jam from ounces to pounds.
- Add these converted weights to the initial suitcase weight and the weight of the chocolate.

**Method 2: Work in ounces first, then convert the total to pounds.**
- Convert the initial suitcase weight and the chocolate weight from pounds to ounces.
- Add the weights of the perfume, soap, and jam (already in ounces).
- Convert the total weight back to pounds.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Convert all weights to pounds first, then sum them.**
1. **Convert the weights of the items to pounds:**
   - Perfume: \( 5 \times 1.2 \, \text{oz} = 6 \, \text{oz} \). Convert to pounds:  
     \( 6 \, \text{oz} \div 16 \, \text{oz/lb} = 0.375 \, \text{lb} \).
   - Soap: \( 2 \times 5 \, \text{oz} = 10 \, \text{oz} \). Convert to pounds:  
     \( 10 \, \text{oz} \div 16 \, \text{oz/lb} = 0.625 \, \text{lb} \).
   - Jam: \( 2 \times 8 \, \text{oz} = 16 \, \text{oz} \). Convert to pounds:  
     \( 16 \, \text{oz} \div 16 \, \text{oz/lb} = 1 \, \text{lb} \).

2. **Add all weights in pounds:**
   - Initial suitcase weight: \( 5 \, \text{lb} \).
   - Chocolate: \( 4 \, \text{lb} \).
   - Perfume: \( 0.375 \, \text{lb} \).
   - Soap: \( 0.625 \, \text{lb} \).
   - Jam: \( 1 \, \text{lb} \).

   Total weight:  
   \[
   5 + 4 + 0.375 + 0.625 + 1 = 11 \, \text{lb}.
   \]

3. **Check for errors:**
   - All conversions and additions are consistent. No errors detected.

#### **Method 2: Work in ounces first, then convert the total to pounds.**
1. **Convert the initial suitcase weight and chocolate weight to ounces:**
   - Initial suitcase weight: \( 5 \, \text{lb} \times 16 \, \text{oz/lb} = 80 \, \text{oz} \).
   - Chocolate: \( 4 \, \text{lb} \times 16 \, \text{oz/lb} = 64 \, \text{oz} \).

2. **Add the weights of the other items (already in ounces):**
   - Perfume: \( 5 \times 1.2 \, \text{oz} = 6 \, \text{oz} \).
   - Soap: \( 2 \times 5 \, \text{oz} = 10 \, \text{oz} \).
   - Jam: \( 2 \times 8 \, \text{oz} = 16 \, \text{oz} \).

   Total weight in ounces:  
   \[
   80 + 64 + 6 + 10 + 16 = 176 \, \text{oz}.
   \]

3. **Convert the total weight back to pounds:**
   \[
   176 \, \text{oz} \div 16 \, \text{oz/lb} = 11 \, \text{lb}.
   \]

4. **Check for errors:**
   - All conversions and additions are consistent. No errors detected.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward because it avoids working with large numbers in ounces and focuses on pounds directly. It is less prone to arithmetic errors.
- **Method 2** involves more conversions and larger numbers, which increases the chance of mistakes. However, it is useful if the problem requires working primarily in ounces.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- **Method 1** is better due to its clarity and efficiency.
- Using Method 1, the total weight of Jacque's suitcase is:
  \[
  5 + 4 + 0.375 + 0.625 + 1 = 11 \, \text{lb}.
  \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:  
  Total weight in ounces: \( 80 + 64 + 6 + 10 + 16 = 176 \, \text{oz} \).  
  Convert to pounds: \( 176 \, \text{oz} \div 16 \, \text{oz/lb} = 11 \, \text{lb} \).  
  Both methods agree, confirming the result.

The answer is **11 pounds**.

Q: Javier is selling donuts to raise money for a new game. He wants to raise $96. He buys each dozen donuts for $2.40 and then sells each donut for $1. How many dozen donuts does he need to buy and sell to reach his goal?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Javier wants to raise **$96** by selling donuts.
- He buys **1 dozen donuts (12 donuts)** for **$2.40**.
- He sells **each donut** for **$1**, meaning he earns **$12 per dozen** in revenue.
- His **profit per dozen** is the revenue minus the cost:  
  \[
  \text{Profit per dozen} = 12 - 2.40 = 9.60 \, \text{dollars}.
  \]
- To reach his goal of $96, Javier needs to sell enough dozens of donuts to generate a total profit of $96.
- Assumptions:  
  1. Javier sells all the donuts he buys.  
  2. There are no additional costs or losses.  

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### **Method 1: Use the profit per dozen to calculate the number of dozens needed.**
- Divide the total profit goal ($96) by the profit per dozen ($9.60) to find the number of dozens Javier needs to sell.

#### **Method 2: Use a step-by-step approach to calculate total revenue and costs.**
- Start with an estimate of the number of dozens, calculate the total revenue and cost, and adjust until the profit equals $96.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Using profit per dozen.**
1. Calculate the profit per dozen:  
   \[
   \text{Profit per dozen} = 12 - 2.40 = 9.60 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
2. Divide the total profit goal by the profit per dozen to find the number of dozens:  
   \[
   \text{Number of dozens} = \frac{\text{Total profit goal}}{\text{Profit per dozen}} = \frac{96}{9.60}.
   \]
3. Perform the division:  
   \[
   \frac{96}{9.60} = 10.
   \]
4. Javier needs to buy and sell **10 dozens** of donuts.

#### **Method 2: Step-by-step calculation of revenue and costs.**
1. Start with an estimate of the number of dozens, say **10 dozens**.
2. Calculate the total revenue from selling 10 dozens:  
   \[
   \text{Revenue} = 10 \times 12 = 120 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
3. Calculate the total cost of buying 10 dozens:  
   \[
   \text{Cost} = 10 \times 2.40 = 24 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
4. Calculate the profit:  
   \[
   \text{Profit} = \text{Revenue} - \text{Cost} = 120 - 24 = 96 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
5. The profit matches the goal, so Javier needs to buy and sell **10 dozens** of donuts.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it uses a single division to calculate the number of dozens needed. It avoids unnecessary intermediate steps.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and involves verifying the revenue, cost, and profit step by step. While it is more thorough, it is less efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and clarity.
- Using Method 1:  
  \[
  \text{Number of dozens} = \frac{96}{9.60} = 10.
  \]
- Javier needs to buy and sell **10 dozens** of donuts.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.
1. Verify the profit per dozen:  
   \[
   \text{Profit per dozen} = 12 - 2.40 = 9.60 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
2. Verify the total profit for 10 dozens:  
   \[
   \text{Total profit} = 10 \times 9.60 = 96 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
3. The calculations are correct, and the result is reasonable.

---

The answer is **10 dozens**.
Extracted:
10.0
A:
10.0

Q: Jerry bought 48 firecrackers for the 4th of July. A police officer discovered and confiscated 12 of them. 1/6 of the remaining ones were defective. If Jerry set off half the good firecrackers, how many firecrackers did he set off?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jerry initially bought **48 firecrackers**.
- A police officer confiscated **12 firecrackers**, leaving Jerry with **48 - 12 = 36 firecrackers**.
- **1/6 of the remaining firecrackers** were defective.
- Jerry set off **half of the good firecrackers**.
- Assumptions:
  - "Defective" firecrackers cannot be set off.
  - All calculations are exact, and no firecrackers are lost or destroyed except as described.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Sequential calculation approach**  
- Subtract the confiscated firecrackers to find the remaining ones.
- Calculate the number of defective firecrackers as **1/6 of the remaining firecrackers**.
- Subtract the defective firecrackers to find the number of good firecrackers.
- Divide the good firecrackers by 2 to find how many Jerry set off.

**Method 2: Fraction-based approach**  
- Start with the total number of firecrackers and subtract the confiscated ones.
- Use fractions to calculate the defective firecrackers and the good firecrackers directly.
- Divide the good firecrackers by 2 to find how many Jerry set off.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation approach**

1. **Subtract confiscated firecrackers**:  
   Jerry starts with 48 firecrackers. After the police confiscated 12, he has:  
   \[
   48 - 12 = 36 \text{ firecrackers remaining.}
   \]

2. **Calculate defective firecrackers**:  
   **1/6 of the remaining firecrackers** are defective:  
   \[
   \frac{1}{6} \times 36 = 6 \text{ defective firecrackers.}
   \]

3. **Find the number of good firecrackers**:  
   Subtract the defective firecrackers from the remaining firecrackers:  
   \[
   36 - 6 = 30 \text{ good firecrackers.}
   \]

4. **Calculate how many Jerry set off**:  
   Jerry set off **half of the good firecrackers**:  
   \[
   \frac{1}{2} \times 30 = 15 \text{ firecrackers set off.}
   \]

---

#### **Method 2: Fraction-based approach**

1. **Subtract confiscated firecrackers**:  
   Jerry starts with 48 firecrackers. After the police confiscated 12, he has:  
   \[
   48 - 12 = 36 \text{ firecrackers remaining.}
   \]

2. **Calculate defective firecrackers directly**:  
   **1/6 of the remaining firecrackers** are defective:  
   \[
   \frac{1}{6} \times 36 = 6 \text{ defective firecrackers.}
   \]

3. **Calculate good firecrackers directly**:  
   The number of good firecrackers is the remaining firecrackers minus the defective ones:  
   \[
   36 - 6 = 30 \text{ good firecrackers.}
   \]

4. **Calculate how many Jerry set off**:  
   Jerry set off **half of the good firecrackers**:  
   \[
   \frac{1}{2} \times 30 = 15 \text{ firecrackers set off.}
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is a step-by-step approach that breaks the problem into smaller, manageable calculations. It is clear and easy to follow, especially for sequential reasoning.  
- **Method 2** uses fractions directly, which is slightly more efficient but requires comfort with fraction-based calculations. It is less intuitive for some people.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better for clarity and reliability in this context.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

Using **Method 1**:

1. Subtract confiscated firecrackers:  
   \[
   48 - 12 = 36
   \]

2. Calculate defective firecrackers:  
   \[
   \frac{1}{6} \times 36 = 6
   \]

3. Find the number of good firecrackers:  
   \[
   36 - 6 = 30
   \]

4. Calculate how many Jerry set off:  
   \[
   \frac{1}{2} \times 30 = 15
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Starting with 48 firecrackers, subtracting 12 leaves 36.  
- **1/6 of 36** is 6 defective firecrackers, leaving 30 good ones.  
- Setting off **half of 30** results in 15 firecrackers.  
- All calculations check out, and the result is reasonable.

---

The answer is **15**.

Q: Hannah's AdBlock blocks all but 20% of ads, and 20% of the ads it doesn't block are actually interesting. What percentage of ads aren't interested and don't get blocked?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Hannah's AdBlock blocks **80%** of ads, leaving **20%** unblocked.
- Of the **20%** unblocked ads, **20%** are interesting.
- We are tasked with finding the percentage of ads that are **not interesting** and **do not get blocked**.
- Assumptions:
  - Ads are either blocked or unblocked, and unblocked ads are either interesting or not interesting.
  - Percentages are calculated relative to the total number of ads.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### **Method 1: Sequential breakdown of percentages**
1. Start with the total ads (100%).
2. Calculate the percentage of ads that are unblocked (20%).
3. Determine the percentage of unblocked ads that are not interesting (80% of the unblocked ads).
4. Multiply the percentage of unblocked ads by the percentage that are not interesting to find the final result.

#### **Method 2: Complementary approach**
1. Calculate the percentage of ads that are blocked (80%).
2. Subtract the percentage of ads that are interesting (20% of the unblocked ads) from the remaining unblocked ads (20%).
3. Combine the results to find the percentage of ads that are not interesting and unblocked.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Sequential breakdown of percentages**
1. Start with the total ads: **100%**.
2. Calculate the percentage of ads that are unblocked:  
   \[
   100\% - 80\% = 20\%
   \]
3. Determine the percentage of unblocked ads that are **not interesting**:  
   \[
   100\% - 20\% = 80\%
   \]
4. Multiply the percentage of unblocked ads by the percentage that are not interesting:  
   \[
   20\% \times 80\% = 16\%
   \]
Thus, **16%** of the ads are not interesting and unblocked.

---

#### **Method 2: Complementary approach**
1. Calculate the percentage of ads that are blocked:  
   \[
   80\%
   \]
2. Determine the percentage of ads that are interesting among the unblocked ads:  
   \[
   20\% \times 20\% = 4\%
   \]
3. Subtract the percentage of interesting ads from the unblocked ads:  
   \[
   20\% - 4\% = 16\%
   \]
Thus, **16%** of the ads are not interesting and unblocked.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is straightforward and follows a logical sequence of breaking down percentages step by step. It is easy to follow and minimizes the risk of errors.
- **Method 2** uses a complementary approach, which is slightly less intuitive but still valid. It involves subtracting percentages, which may introduce confusion for some readers.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better due to its clarity and simplicity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Start with the total ads: **100%**.
2. Calculate the percentage of ads that are unblocked:  
   \[
   100\% - 80\% = 20\%
   \]
3. Determine the percentage of unblocked ads that are **not interesting**:  
   \[
   100\% - 20\% = 80\%
   \]
4. Multiply the percentage of unblocked ads by the percentage that are not interesting:  
   \[
   20\% \times 80\% = 16\%
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Verify calculations:
  - Unblocked ads: **20%**.
  - Not interesting among unblocked ads: **80%**.
  - Final result: **20% × 80% = 16%**.
- The result is reasonable because it aligns with the problem's logic.

---

The answer is **16%**.

Q: Cheryl needs 4 cups of basil to make 1 cup of pesto.  She can harvest 16 cups of basil from her farm every week for 8 weeks.  How many cups of pesto will she be able to make?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Cheryl needs **4 cups of basil** to make **1 cup of pesto**.
- Cheryl can harvest **16 cups of basil** per week.
- Cheryl will harvest basil for **8 weeks**.
- We assume all harvested basil is used for pesto and none is wasted.
- We assume the conversion ratio of basil to pesto remains constant throughout the 8 weeks.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total amount of basil harvested over 8 weeks, then divide by the number of cups of basil required to make 1 cup of pesto (4 cups). This method uses direct multiplication and division.

**Method 2:**  
Calculate how many cups of pesto Cheryl can make per week (16 cups of basil ÷ 4 cups of basil per cup of pesto), then multiply this weekly pesto production by the number of weeks (8 weeks). This method breaks the problem into smaller steps.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Total basil harvested divided by conversion ratio**
1. Calculate the total amount of basil harvested over 8 weeks:
   \[
   16 \, \text{cups/week} \times 8 \, \text{weeks} = 128 \, \text{cups of basil}.
   \]
2. Divide the total basil by the number of cups needed to make 1 cup of pesto:
   \[
   128 \, \text{cups of basil} \div 4 \, \text{cups of basil/cup of pesto} = 32 \, \text{cups of pesto}.
   \]
3. Double-check:
   - If Cheryl harvests 16 cups of basil per week, she can make:
     \[
     16 \div 4 = 4 \, \text{cups of pesto per week}.
     \]
   - Over 8 weeks, this results in:
     \[
     4 \, \text{cups/week} \times 8 \, \text{weeks} = 32 \, \text{cups of pesto}.
     \]
   The calculations match.

#### **Method 2: Weekly pesto production multiplied by number of weeks**
1. Calculate how many cups of pesto Cheryl can make per week:
   \[
   16 \, \text{cups of basil/week} \div 4 \, \text{cups of basil/cup of pesto} = 4 \, \text{cups of pesto/week}.
   \]
2. Multiply the weekly pesto production by the number of weeks:
   \[
   4 \, \text{cups of pesto/week} \times 8 \, \text{weeks} = 32 \, \text{cups of pesto}.
   \]
3. Double-check:
   - Total basil harvested over 8 weeks:
     \[
     16 \, \text{cups/week} \times 8 \, \text{weeks} = 128 \, \text{cups of basil}.
     \]
   - Divide total basil by conversion ratio:
     \[
     128 \div 4 = 32 \, \text{cups of pesto}.
     \]
   The calculations match.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it calculates the total basil harvested first and then divides by the conversion ratio. It avoids intermediate steps and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2** breaks the problem into smaller steps, which may be helpful for understanding but introduces more intermediate calculations, increasing the chance of errors.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
**Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency. Using Method 1:
1. Total basil harvested:
   \[
   16 \, \text{cups/week} \times 8 \, \text{weeks} = 128 \, \text{cups of basil}.
   \]
2. Divide by conversion ratio:
   \[
   128 \div 4 = 32 \, \text{cups of pesto}.
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Weekly pesto production:
  \[
  16 \div 4 = 4 \, \text{cups of pesto/week}.
  \]
- Total pesto over 8 weeks:
  \[
  4 \times 8 = 32 \, \text{cups of pesto}.
  \]
The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **32 cups of pesto**.

Q: There are three times as many hogs as cats in King Henry's kingdom. If there are 75 hogs, what's 5 less than 60% of the number of cats in King Henry's kingdom?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are three times as many hogs as cats in King Henry's kingdom.
- The number of hogs is given as 75.
- We need to calculate **5 less than 60% of the number of cats**.
- Assume all numbers are whole numbers, and the relationships between hogs and cats are exact.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Direct calculation using the relationship between hogs and cats.
1. Use the fact that there are three times as many hogs as cats to find the number of cats: \( \text{Number of cats} = \frac{\text{Number of hogs}}{3} \).
2. Calculate 60% of the number of cats: \( 0.6 \times \text{Number of cats} \).
3. Subtract 5 from the result.

#### Method 2: Reverse-engineer the problem using proportions.
1. Start by expressing the relationship between hogs and cats as a ratio: \( \text{Hogs} : \text{Cats} = 3 : 1 \).
2. Use the ratio to find the number of cats: \( \text{Number of cats} = \frac{\text{Number of hogs}}{3} \).
3. Calculate 60% of the number of cats and subtract 5.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### Method 1: Direct calculation.
1. **Find the number of cats**:  
   \[
   \text{Number of cats} = \frac{\text{Number of hogs}}{3} = \frac{75}{3} = 25.
   \]
   - Check: \( 3 \times 25 = 75 \), confirming the relationship is correct.

2. **Calculate 60% of the number of cats**:  
   \[
   0.6 \times \text{Number of cats} = 0.6 \times 25 = 15.
   \]

3. **Subtract 5**:  
   \[
   15 - 5 = 10.
   \]

#### Method 2: Reverse-engineer using proportions.
1. **Express the ratio of hogs to cats**:  
   \[
   \text{Hogs} : \text{Cats} = 3 : 1.
   \]

2. **Find the number of cats**:  
   \[
   \text{Number of cats} = \frac{\text{Number of hogs}}{3} = \frac{75}{3} = 25.
   \]

3. **Calculate 60% of the number of cats**:  
   \[
   0.6 \times \text{Number of cats} = 0.6 \times 25 = 15.
   \]

4. **Subtract 5**:  
   \[
   15 - 5 = 10.
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- Both methods rely on the same mathematical operations and yield identical results. 
- Method 1 is slightly more direct because it avoids explicitly stating the ratio, making it faster for straightforward calculations.
- Method 2 is useful for problems involving more complex ratios or proportional reasoning.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- Method 1 is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.

1. **Find the number of cats**:  
   \[
   \text{Number of cats} = \frac{75}{3} = 25.
   \]

2. **Calculate 60% of the number of cats**:  
   \[
   0.6 \times 25 = 15.
   \]

3. **Subtract 5**:  
   \[
   15 - 5 = 10.
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the number of cats: \( \frac{75}{3} = 25 \), correct.
- Verify 60% of 25: \( 0.6 \times 25 = 15 \), correct.
- Verify subtraction: \( 15 - 5 = 10 \), correct.

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Olga has fish in 5 different colors.
- There are **12 yellow fish**.
- There are **half as many blue fish as yellow fish**, meaning the number of blue fish is \( \frac{12}{2} = 6 \).
- There are **twice as many green fish as yellow fish**, meaning the number of green fish is \( 12 \times 2 = 24 \).
- The number of fish in the other two colors is not specified, so we assume they are not relevant to the calculation.
- We assume all fish are accounted for and no fish are missing or hidden.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Direct calculation of the total number of fish.
- Add the number of yellow, blue, and green fish together: \( 12 + 6 + 24 \).
- This method is straightforward and avoids unnecessary complexity.

#### Method 2: Break the problem into smaller steps and verify each calculation.
- First, calculate the number of blue fish (\( \frac{12}{2} \)).
- Then calculate the number of green fish (\( 12 \times 2 \)).
- Finally, sum the results step by step: \( 12 + 6 + 24 \).

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Direct calculation.
1. Start with the number of yellow fish: \( 12 \).
2. Add the number of blue fish: \( 12 + 6 = 18 \).
3. Add the number of green fish: \( 18 + 24 = 42 \).
4. Total number of fish: \( 42 \).

#### Method 2: Step-by-step breakdown.
1. Calculate the number of blue fish: \( \frac{12}{2} = 6 \).
2. Calculate the number of green fish: \( 12 \times 2 = 24 \).
3. Add the yellow and blue fish: \( 12 + 6 = 18 \).
4. Add the green fish to the total: \( 18 + 24 = 42 \).
5. Total number of fish: \( 42 \).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it calculates the total number of fish in one step without breaking the problem into smaller parts.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and systematic, which can be helpful for verifying intermediate steps or avoiding errors in complex problems.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is preferable for its simplicity and clarity.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its efficiency and simplicity.
- Solution: \( 12 + 6 + 24 = 42 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the calculations:
  - Blue fish: \( \frac{12}{2} = 6 \).
  - Green fish: \( 12 \times 2 = 24 \).
  - Total: \( 12 + 6 + 24 = 42 \).
- The result is reasonable and consistent with the problem's information.

Q: Mike and Leo have to print as many pamphlets as possible for a social gathering. Mike manages to print as fast as 600 pamphlets per hour for 9 consecutive hours. After a break, he resumes the task for another 2 hours achieving a third of the speed he was doing before. Leo, on the other hand, only works a third as many hours as Mike did before his break, but was twice as fast as Mike before he took his break. How many pamphlets do both manage to print at the end?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

- **Mike's printing speed before the break:** 600 pamphlets per hour.  
- **Mike's printing duration before the break:** 9 hours.  
- **Mike's printing speed after the break:** One-third of his original speed, i.e., \( \frac{600}{3} = 200 \) pamphlets per hour.  
- **Mike's printing duration after the break:** 2 hours.  
- **Leo's printing speed:** Twice as fast as Mike's original speed, i.e., \( 2 \times 600 = 1200 \) pamphlets per hour.  
- **Leo's printing duration:** One-third of Mike's printing time before the break, i.e., \( \frac{9}{3} = 3 \) hours.  
- **Assumptions:** Both printers work continuously during their respective hours, and there are no interruptions or inefficiencies.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.

#### Method 1: Calculate the total pamphlets printed by Mike and Leo separately, then sum their totals.  
- For Mike: Compute the pamphlets printed before and after the break, then add them.  
- For Leo: Compute the pamphlets printed during his 3-hour session.  

#### Method 2: Use a combined approach to calculate the total pamphlets printed by both, breaking the problem into smaller parts.  
- Break the problem into segments: Mike's pre-break printing, Mike's post-break printing, and Leo's printing.  
- Sum the contributions from each segment.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### Method 1: Calculate totals for Mike and Leo separately.  

**Step 1:** Calculate Mike's pamphlets before the break.  
Mike prints 600 pamphlets per hour for 9 hours:  
\[
600 \times 9 = 5400 \text{ pamphlets}.
\]

**Step 2:** Calculate Mike's pamphlets after the break.  
Mike prints 200 pamphlets per hour for 2 hours:  
\[
200 \times 2 = 400 \text{ pamphlets}.
\]

**Step 3:** Add Mike's totals.  
\[
5400 + 400 = 5800 \text{ pamphlets}.
\]

**Step 4:** Calculate Leo's pamphlets.  
Leo prints 1200 pamphlets per hour for 3 hours:  
\[
1200 \times 3 = 3600 \text{ pamphlets}.
\]

**Step 5:** Add Mike's and Leo's totals.  
\[
5800 + 3600 = 9400 \text{ pamphlets}.
\]

#### Method 2: Break the problem into smaller parts and sum contributions.  

**Step 1:** Break the problem into three segments:  
- Mike's pre-break printing: \( 600 \times 9 = 5400 \).  
- Mike's post-break printing: \( 200 \times 2 = 400 \).  
- Leo's printing: \( 1200 \times 3 = 3600 \).  

**Step 2:** Sum the contributions from all segments.  
\[
5400 + 400 + 3600 = 9400 \text{ pamphlets}.
\]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1:** Separates the calculations for Mike and Leo, making it easier to track their individual contributions. This method is clear and straightforward.  
- **Method 2:** Breaks the problem into smaller parts but combines the calculations into a single summation. While efficient, it may be less intuitive for tracking individual contributions.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for clarity and reliability, as it explicitly separates the calculations for Mike and Leo.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

Using **Method 1**, we calculate:  
- Mike's total pamphlets: \( 5400 + 400 = 5800 \).  
- Leo's total pamphlets: \( 3600 \).  
- Combined total: \( 5800 + 3600 = 9400 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.

Recalculate each step:  
- Mike's pre-break printing: \( 600 \times 9 = 5400 \).  
- Mike's post-break printing: \( 200 \times 2 = 400 \).  
- Leo's printing: \( 1200 \times 3 = 3600 \).  
- Combined total: \( 5400 + 400 + 3600 = 9400 \).  

The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

### Final Answer:  
The total number of pamphlets printed by Mike and Leo is **9400**.  
The answer is **9400**.

Q: Jeff committed to run for an hour a day during weekdays. On Thursday, he cut short his run by 20 minutes but was able to jog 10 minutes more on Friday. How many minutes was he able to run for that week?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jeff runs for 1 hour (60 minutes) each weekday (Monday through Friday).
- On Thursday, he cut his run short by 20 minutes, meaning he ran for \( 60 - 20 = 40 \) minutes.
- On Friday, he jogged 10 minutes more than his usual 60 minutes, meaning he ran for \( 60 + 10 = 70 \) minutes.
- We assume Jeff ran the full 60 minutes on Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday.
- We are tasked with finding the total number of minutes Jeff ran during the week.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Add the total minutes Jeff ran for each day individually:
- Calculate the minutes for Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, and Friday separately, then sum them.

**Method 2:** Start with the total minutes Jeff would have run if he had completed 60 minutes every weekday, then adjust for the changes on Thursday and Friday:
- Total minutes for 5 days at 60 minutes/day = \( 5 \times 60 = 300 \).
- Subtract the 20 minutes Jeff missed on Thursday and add the 10 extra minutes he ran on Friday.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Add daily totals**
1. Monday: Jeff ran 60 minutes.
2. Tuesday: Jeff ran 60 minutes.
3. Wednesday: Jeff ran 60 minutes.
4. Thursday: Jeff ran \( 60 - 20 = 40 \) minutes.
5. Friday: Jeff ran \( 60 + 10 = 70 \) minutes.
6. Total minutes: \( 60 + 60 + 60 + 40 + 70 = 290 \).

**Checks:**
- Each day's calculation is straightforward and matches the given information.
- Summing the daily totals yields \( 290 \), which is reasonable given the adjustments on Thursday and Friday.

#### **Method 2: Adjust from the baseline**
1. Baseline total for 5 days: \( 5 \times 60 = 300 \) minutes.
2. Adjust for Thursday: Subtract 20 minutes, \( 300 - 20 = 280 \).
3. Adjust for Friday: Add 10 minutes, \( 280 + 10 = 290 \).

**Checks:**
- The baseline calculation assumes Jeff runs 60 minutes every weekday, which matches the problem's setup.
- Adjustments for Thursday and Friday are consistent with the given information.
- Final total is \( 290 \), which matches the result from Method 1.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more intuitive because it breaks the problem into daily totals, making it easy to follow and verify each step. However, it involves slightly more calculations.
- **Method 2** is more efficient because it starts with a baseline and adjusts for deviations, reducing the number of steps. However, it requires careful attention to ensure the adjustments are applied correctly.

Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better for clarity and avoids potential errors in applying adjustments.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Monday: 60 minutes.
2. Tuesday: 60 minutes.
3. Wednesday: 60 minutes.
4. Thursday: \( 60 - 20 = 40 \) minutes.
5. Friday: \( 60 + 10 = 70 \) minutes.
6. Total: \( 60 + 60 + 60 + 40 + 70 = 290 \).

Q: Matt and Tom both walk up the stairs of the library. Matt goes up the stairs 20 steps per minute. Tom goes up the stairs 5 steps per minute more than Matt. If Matt has reached 220 steps above the entrance, how many steps will Tom have gone up by that time?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Matt walks up the stairs at a rate of **20 steps per minute**.
- Tom walks up the stairs **5 steps per minute faster than Matt**, so Tom's rate is **25 steps per minute**.
- Matt has reached **220 steps above the entrance**.
- We assume both Matt and Tom start walking at the same time and maintain their respective rates consistently.
- The goal is to determine how many steps Tom has climbed by the time Matt reaches 220 steps.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Use time to calculate Tom's progress.**
- First, calculate how long it takes Matt to reach 220 steps using his rate of 20 steps per minute.
- Then, use the same time to calculate how many steps Tom climbs at his rate of 25 steps per minute.

**Method 2: Use proportional reasoning.**
- Since Tom climbs 25 steps for every 20 steps Matt climbs, calculate the ratio of their rates (Tom's rate to Matt's rate).
- Use this ratio to directly scale Matt's total steps (220) to find Tom's total steps.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Use time to calculate Tom's progress.**
1. **Calculate the time it takes Matt to climb 220 steps.**
   - Matt climbs at a rate of 20 steps per minute.
   - Time = Total steps ÷ Rate = \( 220 \div 20 = 11 \) minutes.

2. **Calculate how many steps Tom climbs in the same time.**
   - Tom climbs at a rate of 25 steps per minute.
   - Steps = Rate × Time = \( 25 \times 11 = 275 \) steps.

3. **Double-check calculations.**
   - Matt's time: \( 220 \div 20 = 11 \) minutes.
   - Tom's steps: \( 25 \times 11 = 275 \) steps.
   - Both calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

#### **Method 2: Use proportional reasoning.**
1. **Determine the ratio of Tom's rate to Matt's rate.**
   - Tom's rate = 25 steps per minute.
   - Matt's rate = 20 steps per minute.
   - Ratio = \( \frac{25}{20} = 1.25 \).

2. **Scale Matt's total steps (220) by the ratio.**
   - Tom climbs 1.25 times as many steps as Matt for the same amount of time.
   - Tom's steps = Matt's steps × Ratio = \( 220 \times 1.25 = 275 \) steps.

3. **Double-check calculations.**
   - Ratio: \( \frac{25}{20} = 1.25 \).
   - Tom's steps: \( 220 \times 1.25 = 275 \).
   - Both calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is straightforward and uses time as the intermediary variable, making it easy to follow step by step. It is less abstract and avoids potential confusion with ratios.
- **Method 2** is efficient and avoids calculating time explicitly, but it requires understanding proportional reasoning and could be less intuitive for some.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more reliable for general problem-solving due to its clarity and step-by-step approach.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

**Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and reliability.

1. Calculate Matt's time to climb 220 steps: \( 220 \div 20 = 11 \) minutes.
2. Calculate Tom's steps in the same time: \( 25 \times 11 = 275 \) steps.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Matt's time: \( 220 \div 20 = 11 \) minutes.
- Tom's steps: \( 25 \times 11 = 275 \).
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable. Tom climbs faster than Matt, so it makes sense that he climbs more steps in the same time.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **275**.
