Q: Emily just purchased 2 pairs of curtains for $30.00 each and 9 wall prints at $15.00 each.  The store also offers an installation service.  For $50.00 they will come to your house and professionally hang your curtains and prints.  If Emily agrees to this service, how much will her entire order cost?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Emily purchased:
  - 2 pairs of curtains at $30.00 each.
  - 9 wall prints at $15.00 each.
- The store offers an installation service for $50.00.
- If Emily agrees to the installation service, the total cost will include the cost of the curtains, wall prints, and the installation fee.
- Assumptions:
  - There are no additional taxes, fees, or discounts.
  - The prices provided are accurate and final.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the total cost of the curtains by multiplying the price of one pair by the number of pairs.  
- Calculate the total cost of the wall prints by multiplying the price of one print by the number of prints.  
- Add the costs of the curtains, wall prints, and installation service to find the total.

**Method 2:**  
- Combine the costs of the curtains and wall prints into a single subtotal.  
- Add the installation fee to the subtotal to find the total cost.  
- This method emphasizes grouping and simplifies the addition process.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. **Calculate the cost of the curtains:**  
   - Each pair of curtains costs $30.00.  
   - Emily bought 2 pairs:  
     \[
     30.00 \times 2 = 60.00
     \]  
   - Total cost of curtains = $60.00.

2. **Calculate the cost of the wall prints:**  
   - Each wall print costs $15.00.  
   - Emily bought 9 prints:  
     \[
     15.00 \times 9 = 135.00
     \]  
   - Total cost of wall prints = $135.00.

3. **Add the installation fee:**  
   - Installation fee = $50.00.  
   - Total cost = Cost of curtains + Cost of wall prints + Installation fee:  
     \[
     60.00 + 135.00 + 50.00 = 245.00
     \]

4. **Double-check calculations:**  
   - Recalculate each step:  
     - Curtains: \( 30.00 \times 2 = 60.00 \).  
     - Wall prints: \( 15.00 \times 9 = 135.00 \).  
     - Total: \( 60.00 + 135.00 + 50.00 = 245.00 \).  
   - No errors found.

#### **Method 2:**
1. **Combine the costs of the curtains and wall prints into a subtotal:**  
   - Curtains: \( 30.00 \times 2 = 60.00 \).  
   - Wall prints: \( 15.00 \times 9 = 135.00 \).  
   - Subtotal = Cost of curtains + Cost of wall prints:  
     \[
     60.00 + 135.00 = 195.00
     \]

2. **Add the installation fee to the subtotal:**  
   - Installation fee = $50.00.  
   - Total cost = Subtotal + Installation fee:  
     \[
     195.00 + 50.00 = 245.00
     \]

3. **Double-check calculations:**  
   - Subtotal: \( 60.00 + 135.00 = 195.00 \).  
   - Total: \( 195.00 + 50.00 = 245.00 \).  
   - No errors found.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more detailed, breaking the problem into smaller, distinct steps. This makes it easier to follow and verify each component of the calculation.  
- **Method 2** is more streamlined, combining related costs into a subtotal before adding the installation fee. This method is efficient but may be less intuitive for some.  
- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is better for clarity and reducing potential errors, especially for beginners.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Cost of curtains: \( 30.00 \times 2 = 60.00 \).  
2. Cost of wall prints: \( 15.00 \times 9 = 135.00 \).  
3. Total cost: \( 60.00 + 135.00 + 50.00 = 245.00 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate each step:  
  - Curtains: \( 30.00 \times 2 = 60.00 \).  
  - Wall prints: \( 15.00 \times 9 = 135.00 \).  
  - Total: \( 60.00 + 135.00 + 50.00 = 245.00 \).

Q: A roadwork company is paving a newly constructed 16-mile road. They use a mixture of pitch and gravel to make the asphalt to pave the road. Each truckloads of asphalt uses two bags of gravel and five times as many bags of gravel as it does barrels of pitch to make. It takes three truckloads of asphalt to pave each mile of road. The company paved 4 miles of road on one day, then one mile less than double that on the second day. How many barrels of pitch will the company need to finish the remaining road on the third day?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

1. The road is 16 miles long.
2. Each mile of road requires **3 truckloads of asphalt**.
3. Each truckload of asphalt uses:
   - **2 bags of gravel**.
   - **5 times as many bags of gravel as barrels of pitch** (i.e., the ratio of gravel to pitch is 5:1).
4. The company paved:
   - **4 miles** on the first day.
   - **One mile less than double that** on the second day (i.e., \( 2 \times 4 - 1 = 7 \) miles).
5. The remaining road will be paved on the third day.
6. We need to calculate how many **barrels of pitch** are required to pave the remaining road.

Assumptions:
- The roadwork company uses the same ratio of gravel to pitch for all truckloads.
- No materials are wasted or lost during the paving process.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation using the total number of truckloads and the gravel-to-pitch ratio.**
1. Calculate the total number of miles paved on the first and second days.
2. Subtract this from the total road length to find the remaining miles.
3. Determine the number of truckloads needed for the remaining miles.
4. Use the gravel-to-pitch ratio to calculate the number of barrels of pitch required.

#### **Method 2: Break the problem into smaller steps by calculating materials for each day and summing them.**
1. Calculate the number of truckloads used on the first and second days.
2. Subtract these truckloads from the total truckloads required for the entire road.
3. Use the gravel-to-pitch ratio to calculate the barrels of pitch needed for the remaining truckloads.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation.**

1. **Calculate the total miles paved on the first and second days:**
   - First day: \( 4 \) miles.
   - Second day: \( 2 \times 4 - 1 = 7 \) miles.
   - Total miles paved: \( 4 + 7 = 11 \) miles.

2. **Find the remaining miles:**
   - Total road length: \( 16 \) miles.
   - Remaining miles: \( 16 - 11 = 5 \) miles.

3. **Determine the number of truckloads needed for the remaining miles:**
   - Each mile requires \( 3 \) truckloads.
   - Remaining truckloads: \( 5 \times 3 = 15 \) truckloads.

4. **Calculate the number of barrels of pitch per truckload:**
   - Each truckload uses \( 2 \) bags of gravel.
   - Gravel-to-pitch ratio is \( 5:1 \), so each truckload uses \( \frac{2}{5} = 0.4 \) barrels of pitch.

5. **Calculate the total barrels of pitch needed for the remaining truckloads:**
   - Total barrels of pitch: \( 15 \times 0.4 = 6 \) barrels.

---

#### **Method 2: Break the problem into smaller steps.**

1. **Calculate the total truckloads used on the first and second days:**
   - First day: \( 4 \times 3 = 12 \) truckloads.
   - Second day: \( 7 \times 3 = 21 \) truckloads.
   - Total truckloads used: \( 12 + 21 = 33 \) truckloads.

2. **Find the remaining truckloads:**
   - Total truckloads for the entire road: \( 16 \times 3 = 48 \) truckloads.
   - Remaining truckloads: \( 48 - 33 = 15 \) truckloads.

3. **Calculate the number of barrels of pitch per truckload:**
   - Each truckload uses \( 2 \) bags of gravel.
   - Gravel-to-pitch ratio is \( 5:1 \), so each truckload uses \( \frac{2}{5} = 0.4 \) barrels of pitch.

4. **Calculate the total barrels of pitch needed for the remaining truckloads:**
   - Total barrels of pitch: \( 15 \times 0.4 = 6 \) barrels.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it calculates the remaining miles and truckloads in fewer steps.
- **Method 2** involves more intermediate calculations, which could introduce errors if not carefully managed.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer and faster for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Remaining miles: \( 16 - 11 = 5 \).
2. Remaining truckloads: \( 5 \times 3 = 15 \).
3. Barrels of pitch per truckload: \( \frac{2}{5} = 0.4 \).
4. Total barrels of pitch: \( 15 \times 0.4 = 6 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.

1. Verify the total miles paved: \( 4 + 7 = 11 \), remaining \( 16 - 11 = 5 \).
2. Verify truckloads for remaining miles: \( 5 \times 3 = 15 \).
3. Verify barrels of pitch per truckload: \( \frac{2}{5} = 0.4 \).
4. Verify total barrels of pitch: \( 15 \times 0.4 = 6 \).

All calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

The answer is **6 barrels of pitch**.

Q: James spends 10 minutes downloading a game, half as long installing it, and triple that combined amount of time going through the tutorial. How long does it take before he can play the main game?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- James spends **10 minutes** downloading the game.
- He spends **half as long installing it** as he does downloading it. This means the installation time is \( \frac{10}{2} = 5 \) minutes.
- He spends **triple the combined time of downloading and installing** going through the tutorial. The combined time for downloading and installing is \( 10 + 5 = 15 \) minutes, so the tutorial time is \( 3 \times 15 = 45 \) minutes.
- We assume there are no delays or interruptions between these activities.
- The total time before James can play the main game is the sum of the time spent downloading, installing, and completing the tutorial.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Sequential addition of times.**
- Calculate the time for each activity (downloading, installing, tutorial) step by step.
- Add these times together to find the total time.

**Method 2: Express the total time as a single equation.**
- Represent the total time as \( T = 10 + \frac{10}{2} + 3 \times (10 + \frac{10}{2}) \).
- Simplify the equation step by step to find the total time.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential addition of times**
1. **Downloading time**: James spends 10 minutes downloading the game.
   - \( \text{Downloading time} = 10 \) minutes.
2. **Installing time**: He spends half as long installing as downloading.
   - \( \text{Installing time} = \frac{10}{2} = 5 \) minutes.
3. **Tutorial time**: He spends triple the combined time of downloading and installing on the tutorial.
   - Combined time for downloading and installing: \( 10 + 5 = 15 \) minutes.
   - Tutorial time: \( 3 \times 15 = 45 \) minutes.
4. **Total time**: Add the times for downloading, installing, and the tutorial.
   - \( \text{Total time} = 10 + 5 + 45 = 60 \) minutes.

#### **Method 2: Express the total time as a single equation**
1. Represent the total time as:
   \[
   T = 10 + \frac{10}{2} + 3 \times (10 + \frac{10}{2})
   \]
2. Simplify step by step:
   - \( \frac{10}{2} = 5 \), so:
     \[
     T = 10 + 5 + 3 \times (10 + 5)
     \]
   - Combine \( 10 + 5 = 15 \), so:
     \[
     T = 10 + 5 + 3 \times 15
     \]
   - Multiply \( 3 \times 15 = 45 \), so:
     \[
     T = 10 + 5 + 45
     \]
   - Add \( 10 + 5 + 45 = 60 \), so:
     \[
     T = 60
     \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and intuitive, breaking the problem into smaller, manageable steps. It is easy to follow and minimizes the risk of errors.
- **Method 2** is more compact and efficient, especially for those comfortable with algebra. However, it requires careful handling of the equation to avoid mistakes during simplification.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more accessible and easier to verify step by step.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and reliability.
1. Downloading time: \( 10 \) minutes.
2. Installing time: \( \frac{10}{2} = 5 \) minutes.
3. Tutorial time: \( 3 \times (10 + 5) = 3 \times 15 = 45 \) minutes.
4. Total time: \( 10 + 5 + 45 = 60 \) minutes.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate each step:
  - Installing time: \( \frac{10}{2} = 5 \) minutes (correct).
  - Combined time for downloading and installing: \( 10 + 5 = 15 \) minutes (correct).
  - Tutorial time: \( 3 \times 15 = 45 \) minutes (correct).
  - Total time: \( 10 + 5 + 45 = 60 \) minutes (correct).
- The result is reasonable and consistent with the problem's conditions.

---

The answer is **60 minutes**.

Q: Julie started her own landscaping business. She charges $4 an hour for mowing lawns and $8 for pulling weeds. In September she mowed lawns for 25 hours and pulled weeds for 3 hours. If she worked the same number of hours in October, how much money did she earn in September and October?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Julie charges $4 per hour for mowing lawns and $8 per hour for pulling weeds.
- In September, she worked 25 hours mowing lawns and 3 hours pulling weeds.
- She worked the same number of hours in October as in September.
- We assume her rates remain constant across both months.
- The goal is to calculate her total earnings for September and October combined.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Calculate earnings for September, then double the result for both months.**
- First, calculate her earnings for mowing lawns and pulling weeds in September.
- Add these earnings to get her total for September.
- Multiply the September total by 2 to account for both September and October.

**Method 2: Calculate total hours worked for both months, then compute total earnings.**
- Add the hours worked in September and October for each task (mowing lawns and pulling weeds).
- Multiply the total hours for each task by the respective hourly rate.
- Add the earnings from both tasks to get the total for both months.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Calculate September earnings, then double.**
1. **Calculate September earnings for mowing lawns:**
   - Hours worked mowing lawns in September = 25.
   - Hourly rate for mowing lawns = $4.
   - Earnings from mowing lawns = \( 25 \times 4 = 100 \) dollars.

2. **Calculate September earnings for pulling weeds:**
   - Hours worked pulling weeds in September = 3.
   - Hourly rate for pulling weeds = $8.
   - Earnings from pulling weeds = \( 3 \times 8 = 24 \) dollars.

3. **Add September earnings:**
   - Total earnings in September = \( 100 + 24 = 124 \) dollars.

4. **Double the September total for both months:**
   - Total earnings for September and October = \( 124 \times 2 = 248 \) dollars.

---

#### **Method 2: Calculate total hours for both months, then compute earnings.**
1. **Calculate total hours worked for both months:**
   - Hours mowing lawns in September = 25.
   - Hours mowing lawns in October = 25 (same as September).
   - Total hours mowing lawns = \( 25 + 25 = 50 \).

   - Hours pulling weeds in September = 3.
   - Hours pulling weeds in October = 3 (same as September).
   - Total hours pulling weeds = \( 3 + 3 = 6 \).

2. **Calculate total earnings for mowing lawns:**
   - Hourly rate for mowing lawns = $4.
   - Total earnings from mowing lawns = \( 50 \times 4 = 200 \) dollars.

3. **Calculate total earnings for pulling weeds:**
   - Hourly rate for pulling weeds = $8.
   - Total earnings from pulling weeds = \( 6 \times 8 = 48 \) dollars.

4. **Add total earnings from both tasks:**
   - Total earnings for both months = \( 200 + 48 = 248 \) dollars.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is simpler because it calculates earnings for one month and then doubles the result. It involves fewer steps and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2** is more detailed, as it calculates total hours for both months before computing earnings. While this method is more comprehensive, it introduces additional steps that may not be necessary for this problem.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and easier to follow.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice.
1. Calculate September earnings:
   - Mowing lawns: \( 25 \times 4 = 100 \) dollars.
   - Pulling weeds: \( 3 \times 8 = 24 \) dollars.
   - Total for September: \( 100 + 24 = 124 \) dollars.
2. Double the September total for both months:
   - \( 124 \times 2 = 248 \) dollars.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Using **Method 2**, we calculated total hours and earnings for both months:
  - Total hours mowing lawns = 50, total earnings = \( 50 \times 4 = 200 \).
  - Total hours pulling weeds = 6, total earnings = \( 6 \times 8 = 48 \).
  - Total earnings = \( 200 + 48 = 248 \) dollars.
- Both methods agree, confirming the result is correct.

---

The answer is **248**.

Q: The Giants baseball team is trying to make their league playoff. They have played 20 games and won 12 of them. To make the playoffs, they need to win 2/3 of their games over the season. If there are 10 games left, how many do they have to win to make the playoffs?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The Giants baseball team has played 20 games and won 12 of them.
- There are 10 games left in the season, making the total number of games in the season 30.
- To make the playoffs, the team needs to win **2/3 of their total games** over the season.
- We assume all games are played and no ties occur.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total number of wins required to meet the 2/3 threshold for the season, then subtract the number of games already won to determine how many more wins are needed.

**Method 2:**  
Determine the fraction of games remaining that the team needs to win by calculating the difference between the required win percentage and their current win percentage, then apply this to the remaining games.

### Step 3: Work through all intermediate steps in detail for each method.

#### **Method 1: Total wins required approach**
1. **Calculate the total number of games in the season:**  
   Total games = 20 games already played + 10 games remaining = **30 games**.

2. **Calculate the total number of wins required to meet the 2/3 threshold:**  
   Wins required = (2/3) × Total games = (2/3) × 30 = **20 wins**.

3. **Determine how many wins the team already has:**  
   Wins already = **12 wins**.

4. **Calculate how many more wins are needed:**  
   Wins needed = Total wins required - Wins already = 20 - 12 = **8 wins**.

#### **Method 2: Fraction of remaining games approach**
1. **Calculate the current win percentage:**  
   Current win percentage = Wins already / Games played = 12 / 20 = **60%**.

2. **Calculate the required win percentage for the season:**  
   Required win percentage = **2/3 = 66.67%**.

3. **Determine the additional win percentage needed:**  
   Additional win percentage = Required win percentage - Current win percentage = 66.67% - 60% = **6.67%**.

4. **Apply the additional win percentage to the remaining games:**  
   To achieve the required win percentage, the team must win enough of the remaining games to bring their total win percentage to 66.67%.  
   Let x = number of wins needed in the remaining 10 games.  
   Total wins after x games = 12 + x.  
   Total win percentage = (12 + x) / 30.  
   Set this equal to 2/3:  
   (12 + x) / 30 = 2/3.  

5. **Solve for x:**  
   Multiply through by 30:  
   12 + x = 20.  
   Subtract 12:  
   x = **8 wins**.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and intuitive, as it directly calculates the total wins required and subtracts the wins already achieved. It avoids unnecessary intermediate steps and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2** involves more steps and introduces fractions and percentages, which can be more complex and prone to calculation errors. While it ultimately arrives at the same result, it is less efficient.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and clarity.  
- Using Method 1:  
  Total wins required = 20.  
  Wins already = 12.  
  Wins needed = 20 - 12 = **8 wins**.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the calculation:  
  If the team wins 8 more games, their total wins will be 12 + 8 = 20.  
  Total games = 30.  
  Win percentage = 20 / 30 = 2/3 = 66.67%, which meets the playoff requirement.  
- The result is reasonable and checks out.

### Final Answer:
The Giants need to win **8 games** to make the playoffs.  
The answer is **8**.

8.0
A:
8.0

- Jayson's dad is four times his age, so his dad is \( 4 \times 10 = 40 \) years old.
- Jayson's mom is 2 years younger than his dad, so his mom is \( 40 - 2 = 38 \) years old.
- We are tasked with finding how old Jayson's mom was when Jayson was born.
- Assumption: Jayson's mom's age difference relative to Jayson and his dad remains constant over time.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

- Since Jayson is 10 years old, his mom's age when he was born would be her current age minus 10.

- Start with Jayson's mom's current age (38) and work backward to when Jayson was born by subtracting Jayson's age (10). This method explicitly visualizes the timeline.

#### **Method 1: Subtract Jayson's current age from his mom's current age.**
1. Jayson's mom is currently 38 years old.
2. Jayson is currently 10 years old.
3. To find how old Jayson's mom was when he was born, subtract Jayson's age from his mom's current age:
   \[
   38 - 10 = 28
   \]

#### **Method 2: Use a timeline approach.**
1. Start with Jayson's mom's current age, which is 38.
2. Work backward to when Jayson was born by subtracting Jayson's age (10):

   38 - 10 = 28
   \]
3. Double-check: If Jayson's mom was 28 when Jayson was born, adding 10 years (Jayson's current age) to 28 should give her current age of 38. This checks out.

  - Pedro gave **4 gifts**.

- The total number of gifts Rosalina received is the sum of the gifts from Emilio, Jorge, and Pedro.

- Add Emilio's gifts, Jorge's gifts, and Pedro's gifts together: \( 11 + 6 + 4 \).

Q: Amy's grandfather gave her $100 for her birthday. Amy bought 3 dolls, each of which cost $1. How much money does Amy have left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Amy's grandfather gave her $100 for her birthday.
- Amy bought 3 dolls, each costing $1.
- We assume there are no additional costs (e.g., taxes or fees) and that Amy spent exactly $1 per doll.
- We are tasked with finding how much money Amy has left after her purchase.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Subtract the total cost of the dolls from the initial amount of money. First, calculate the total cost of the dolls by multiplying the cost of one doll ($1) by the number of dolls (3). Then subtract this total from $100.

**Method 2:**  
Use a step-by-step subtraction process. Start with $100 and subtract $1 for each doll, repeating this process three times. This method simulates the sequential spending of money.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Calculate the total cost of the dolls:  
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = 3 \times 1 = 3
   \]
2. Subtract the total cost from the initial amount:  
   \[
   \text{Remaining money} = 100 - 3 = 97
   \]
3. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Multiplication: \(3 \times 1 = 3\) is correct.  
   - Subtraction: \(100 - 3 = 97\) is correct.

**Potential pitfalls:**  
This method is straightforward, but errors could occur if the multiplication or subtraction is done incorrectly. However, the simplicity of the numbers minimizes this risk.

---

#### **Method 2: Step-by-step subtraction**
1. Start with $100.  
2. Subtract $1 for the first doll:  
   \[
   100 - 1 = 99
   \]
3. Subtract $1 for the second doll:  
   \[
   99 - 1 = 98
   \]
4. Subtract $1 for the third doll:  
   \[
   98 - 1 = 97
   \]
5. The remaining money is $97.

**Potential pitfalls:**  
This method is more tedious and prone to errors due to the repeated subtraction steps. However, it provides a clear, step-by-step breakdown of the spending process.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more efficient and reliable because it involves fewer steps and uses direct arithmetic operations (multiplication and subtraction). It is less prone to errors and is faster to execute.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and simulates the spending process, which may be helpful for understanding the problem conceptually. However, it is less efficient and more error-prone due to the repeated steps.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is the better choice for solving this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Calculate the total cost of the dolls:  
   \[
   3 \times 1 = 3
   \]
2. Subtract the total cost from the initial amount:  
   \[
   100 - 3 = 97
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the total cost: \(3 \times 1 = 3\) is correct.  
- Verify the subtraction: \(100 - 3 = 97\) is correct.  
- The result is reasonable because Amy spent only $3 out of $100, leaving her with $97.

---

The answer is **97**.

Q: After a trip, Mrs. Nalani took the students to the restaurant and told them they could order either a burger or a hot dog. If the number of students who ordered burgers was 30, twice the number of students who called for a hot dog, calculate the number of students at the restaurant.
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The number of students who ordered burgers is 30.  
- The number of students who ordered burgers is **twice** the number of students who ordered hot dogs.  
- We are tasked with finding the total number of students at the restaurant.  
- Assume every student ordered exactly one item (either a burger or a hot dog).  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Use algebra to represent the relationship between the number of students who ordered burgers and hot dogs. Let the number of students who ordered hot dogs be \( x \). Then, the number of students who ordered burgers is \( 2x \). Add these together to find the total number of students.  

**Method 2:** Use logical reasoning and trial-and-error. Start with the fact that the number of students who ordered burgers is 30, and work backward to find the number of students who ordered hot dogs. Then, sum the two groups to find the total.  

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Algebraic Approach**
1. Let the number of students who ordered hot dogs be \( x \).  
2. The number of students who ordered burgers is \( 2x \).  
3. We know \( 2x = 30 \) (since the number of students who ordered burgers is twice the number of students who ordered hot dogs).  
4. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   x = \frac{30}{2} = 15
   \]
5. The number of students who ordered hot dogs is \( x = 15 \).  
6. The total number of students is the sum of those who ordered burgers and hot dogs:  
   \[
   \text{Total students} = 2x + x = 30 + 15 = 45
   \]

#### **Method 2: Logical Reasoning and Trial-and-Error**
1. Start with the fact that the number of students who ordered burgers is 30.  
2. Since the number of students who ordered burgers is twice the number of students who ordered hot dogs, divide 30 by 2 to find the number of students who ordered hot dogs:  
   \[
   \text{Hot dogs} = \frac{30}{2} = 15
   \]
3. Add the number of students who ordered burgers and hot dogs to find the total:  
   \[
   \text{Total students} = 30 + 15 = 45
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Algebraic Approach):** This method is systematic and generalizable to more complex problems. It clearly defines variables and relationships, making it easy to follow and verify.  
- **Method 2 (Logical Reasoning):** This method is faster for simple problems but relies on intuition and may not scale well to more complex scenarios.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better because it is more structured and reliable, especially for problems involving more variables or relationships.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Let the number of students who ordered hot dogs be \( x \).  
2. The number of students who ordered burgers is \( 2x \).  
3. Solve \( 2x = 30 \):  
   \[
   x = \frac{30}{2} = 15
   \]
4. The total number of students is:  
   \[
   \text{Total students} = 2x + x = 30 + 15 = 45
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the relationship: The number of students who ordered burgers (30) is indeed twice the number of students who ordered hot dogs (15).  
- Verify the total: \( 30 + 15 = 45 \).  
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.  

### Final Answer:
The answer is **45**.

Q: Emmy has a collection of 14 iPods. She loses 6 out of the 14 she had but she still has twice as many as Rosa. How many iPods does Emmy and Rosa have together?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Emmy starts with 14 iPods.
- Emmy loses 6 iPods, leaving her with \( 14 - 6 = 8 \) iPods.
- Emmy has twice as many iPods as Rosa after losing 6.
- We assume no other iPods are gained or lost by either Emmy or Rosa.
- The goal is to find the total number of iPods Emmy and Rosa have together.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Use algebra to solve for Rosa's iPods.**
- Let \( R \) represent the number of iPods Rosa has.
- Emmy has twice as many iPods as Rosa, so \( 8 = 2R \).
- Solve for \( R \), then add Emmy's and Rosa's iPods to find the total.

**Method 2: Use logical reasoning and trial-and-error.**
- Emmy has 8 iPods, which is twice as many as Rosa.
- Divide Emmy's iPods by 2 to find Rosa's iPods.
- Add Emmy's and Rosa's iPods to find the total.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Algebraic approach**
1. Emmy has 8 iPods after losing 6.
2. Emmy has twice as many iPods as Rosa, so \( 8 = 2R \).
3. Solve for \( R \):
   \[
   R = \frac{8}{2} = 4
   \]
4. Rosa has 4 iPods.
5. Add Emmy's and Rosa's iPods to find the total:
   \[
   8 + 4 = 12
   \]
6. Double-check:

#### **Method 2: Logical reasoning and trial-and-error**
1. Emmy has 8 iPods after losing 6.
2. Emmy has twice as many iPods as Rosa.
3. Divide Emmy's iPods by 2 to find Rosa's iPods:
   \[
   \frac{8}{2} = 4
   \]

5. Add Emmy's and Rosa's iPods to find the total:

   8 + 4 = 12

6. Double-check:
   - Emmy has 8 iPods, which is twice as many as Rosa's 4.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Algebraic approach):** This method is systematic and works well for more complex problems. It is reliable and ensures clarity in reasoning.
- **Method 2 (Logical reasoning):** This method is faster and more intuitive for simple problems like this one. However, it may not generalize well to more complex scenarios.

**Conclusion:** Both methods are valid, but Method 1 is more robust and ensures accuracy through algebraic reasoning.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Emmy has 8 iPods after losing 6.
2. Emmy has twice as many iPods as Rosa, so \( 8 = 2R \).
3. Solve for \( R \):
   \[
   R = \frac{8}{2} = 4
   \]
4. Rosa has 4 iPods.
5. Add Emmy's and Rosa's iPods to find the total:
   \[
   8 + 4 = 12
   \]

Q: On Tuesday, Mike had 45 books and Corey had twice as many books as Mike. On Wednesday, Mike gave 10 books to Lily, and Corey gave Lily 15 more than Mike gave. How many books did Lily get?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- On Tuesday:
  - Mike had 45 books.
  - Corey had twice as many books as Mike, so Corey had \( 2 \times 45 = 90 \) books.
- On Wednesday:
  - Mike gave 10 books to Lily.
  - Corey gave Lily 15 more books than Mike gave, so Corey gave \( 10 + 15 = 25 \) books to Lily.
- We assume no books were lost or exchanged elsewhere, and all books given by Mike and Corey went to Lily.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Add the number of books Mike and Corey gave to Lily directly:
- Calculate the books Mike gave (10).
- Calculate the books Corey gave (25).
- Add these two amounts to find the total books Lily received.

**Method 2:** Use a step-by-step breakdown:
- Start with Mike's contribution (10 books).
- Add Corey's contribution (15 more than Mike gave, i.e., 25 books).
- Sum the contributions to find the total.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### Method 1:
1. Mike gave Lily 10 books.
2. Corey gave Lily \( 10 + 15 = 25 \) books.
3. Total books Lily received: \( 10 + 25 = 35 \).

#### Method 2:
1. Start with Mike's contribution: 10 books.
2. Add Corey's contribution: \( 10 + 15 = 25 \) books.
3. Total books Lily received: \( 10 + 25 = 35 \).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves straightforward addition of the two contributions.
- **Method 2** is slightly more detailed but essentially follows the same logic. It may be helpful for breaking down the problem into smaller steps for clarity.
- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is simpler and faster.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.
- Solution:
  - Mike gave Lily 10 books.
  - Corey gave Lily \( 10 + 15 = 25 \) books.
  - Total books Lily received: \( 10 + 25 = 35 \).

- Verify calculations:
  - Mike gave 10 books.

Q: Justice has 3 ferns, 5 palms, and 7 succulent plants in her house. If she wants a total of 24 plants in her home, how many more plants does she need?
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Justice currently has 3 ferns, 5 palms, and 7 succulent plants in her house.  
- The total number of plants she currently has is the sum of these three categories.  
- Justice wants a total of 24 plants in her home.  
- We assume no plants are removed or lost, and she only needs to add more plants to reach her goal.  

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
Method 1: Calculate the total number of plants Justice currently has by summing the three categories (3 + 5 + 7). Subtract this total from her desired total (24).  
Method 2: Use subtraction directly: Start with the desired total (24) and subtract each category of plants one by one (24 - 3 - 5 - 7).  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
1. Add the number of plants Justice currently has:  
   - 3 ferns + 5 palms = 8 plants.  
   - 8 plants + 7 succulents = 15 plants.  
2. Subtract the current total from the desired total:  
   - 24 - 15 = 9.  
3. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Re-add Justice's current plants: 3 + 5 + 7 = 15.  
   - Subtract again: 24 - 15 = 9.  

**Method 2:**  
1. Start with the desired total (24) and subtract each category of plants one by one:  
   - 24 - 3 = 21.  
   - 21 - 5 = 16.  
   - 16 - 7 = 9.  
2. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Subtract step-by-step again: 24 - 3 = 21, 21 - 5 = 16, 16 - 7 = 9.  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- **Method 1** is more straightforward because it involves summing all current plants first and then performing a single subtraction. This reduces the chance of error and is easier to follow.  
- **Method 2** requires multiple subtractions, which introduces more steps and potential for mistakes, especially if the numbers are larger or more complex.  

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and clarity.  
- Solution:  
   - Total current plants: 3 + 5 + 7 = 15.  
   - Plants needed: 24 - 15 = 9.  

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Recalculate:  
   - Current plants: 3 + 5 + 7 = 15.  
   - Plants needed: 24 - 15 = 9.  
- The result is consistent and reasonable.  

The answer is **9**.

Q: Damien jogs 5 miles per day on weekdays only.  How many miles does he run over three weeks?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Damien jogs **5 miles per day**.
- He jogs only on **weekdays** (Monday through Friday).
- A week has **5 weekdays**, so Damien jogs 5 days per week.
- The time period is **three weeks**.
- We assume Damien jogs consistently every weekday without skipping any days.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the total number of weekdays in three weeks: \( 5 \text{ weekdays/week} \times 3 \text{ weeks} \).
- Multiply the total number of weekdays by the distance Damien jogs per day (5 miles).

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the total distance Damien jogs in one week: \( 5 \text{ miles/day} \times 5 \text{ days/week} \).
- Multiply the weekly total by the number of weeks (3 weeks).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the total number of weekdays in three weeks:  
   \[
   5 \text{ weekdays/week} \times 3 \text{ weeks} = 15 \text{ weekdays}.
   \]
2. Multiply the total number of weekdays by the distance Damien jogs per day:  
   \[
   15 \text{ weekdays} \times 5 \text{ miles/day} = 75 \text{ miles}.
   \]
3. Double-check:  
   - Each week has 5 weekdays, so 3 weeks have \( 5 + 5 + 5 = 15 \) weekdays.  
   - Damien jogs 5 miles per day, so \( 15 \times 5 = 75 \) miles.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate the total distance Damien jogs in one week:  
   \[
   5 \text{ miles/day} \times 5 \text{ days/week} = 25 \text{ miles/week}.
   \]
2. Multiply the weekly total by the number of weeks:  
   \[
   25 \text{ miles/week} \times 3 \text{ weeks} = 75 \text{ miles}.
   \]
3. Double-check:  
   - In one week, Damien jogs \( 5 \times 5 = 25 \) miles.  
   - Over three weeks, \( 25 + 25 + 25 = 75 \) miles.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** directly calculates the total number of weekdays and multiplies by the daily distance. It is straightforward and avoids intermediate steps, making it efficient and reliable.
- **Method 2** breaks the problem into weekly totals and multiplies by the number of weeks. While slightly longer, it is intuitive for those who think in weekly terms.
- Both methods are clear and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is slightly more efficient due to fewer steps.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.
1. Calculate the total number of weekdays in three weeks:  
   \[
   5 \text{ weekdays/week} \times 3 \text{ weeks} = 15 \text{ weekdays}.
   \]
2. Multiply the total number of weekdays by the distance Damien jogs per day:  
   \[
   15 \text{ weekdays} \times 5 \text{ miles/day} = 75 \text{ miles}.
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:  
  - Weekly total: \( 5 \times 5 = 25 \) miles.  
  - Over three weeks: \( 25 \times 3 = 75 \) miles.  
- Both methods confirm the result is correct.
- The result is reasonable: Damien jogs 5 miles per day, 5 days per week, for 3 weeks, so \( 5 \times 5 \times 3 = 75 \) miles.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **75 miles**.

Q: Barry has $10.00 worth of dimes.  His little brother, Dan has half that amount but finds 2 more dimes on his way home from school.  How many dimes does Dan have?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Barry has $10.00 worth of dimes.
- Dan has half the amount of money Barry has in dimes, which is $10.00 ÷ 2 = $5.00.
- Dan finds 2 additional dimes on his way home.
- Each dime is worth $0.10.
- We assume all calculations are accurate, and no dimes are lost or miscounted.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate how many dimes Barry has by dividing $10.00 by $0.10.
- Calculate how many dimes Dan has initially by dividing $5.00 by $0.10.
- Add the 2 extra dimes Dan finds to his initial count.

**Method 2:**  
- Use the relationship between Barry's and Dan's amounts to directly calculate Dan's total number of dimes, including the 2 extra dimes.
- First, determine the total number of dimes Barry has, then halve that number to find Dan's initial count.
- Add the 2 extra dimes Dan finds.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate how many dimes Barry has:  
   \[
   \text{Barry's dimes} = \frac{\text{Barry's money}}{\text{value of one dime}} = \frac{10.00}{0.10} = 100 \text{ dimes}.
   \]
2. Calculate how many dimes Dan has initially:  
   \[
   \text{Dan's initial dimes} = \frac{\text{Dan's money}}{\text{value of one dime}} = \frac{5.00}{0.10} = 50 \text{ dimes}.
   \]
3. Add the 2 extra dimes Dan finds:  
   \[
   \text{Dan's total dimes} = \text{Dan's initial dimes} + 2 = 50 + 2 = 52 \text{ dimes}.
   \]

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate how many dimes Barry has:  
   \[
   \text{Barry's dimes} = \frac{\text{Barry's money}}{\text{value of one dime}} = \frac{10.00}{0.10} = 100 \text{ dimes}.
   \]
2. Calculate how many dimes Dan has initially by halving Barry's dimes:  
   \[
   \text{Dan's initial dimes} = \frac{\text{Barry's dimes}}{2} = \frac{100}{2} = 50 \text{ dimes}.
   \]
3. Add the 2 extra dimes Dan finds:  
   \[
   \text{Dan's total dimes} = \text{Dan's initial dimes} + 2 = 50 + 2 = 52 \text{ dimes}.
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** involves calculating the number of dimes for both Barry and Dan using their monetary amounts, then adding the extra dimes. It is straightforward and relies on direct division.
- **Method 2** uses the relationship between Barry's and Dan's dimes to halve Barry's total, then adds the extra dimes. It is slightly more abstract but equally reliable.
- Both methods are clear and efficient, but **Method 1** is slightly more intuitive because it directly uses the monetary amounts.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
We choose **Method 1** for its clarity and directness:
1. Calculate Barry's dimes:  
   \[
   \text{Barry's dimes} = \frac{10.00}{0.10} = 100 \text{ dimes}.
   \]
2. Calculate Dan's initial dimes:  
   \[
   \text{Dan's initial dimes} = \frac{5.00}{0.10} = 50 \text{ dimes}.
   \]
3. Add the 2 extra dimes Dan finds:  
   \[
   \text{Dan's total dimes} = 50 + 2 = 52 \text{ dimes}.
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify Barry's dimes: $10.00 ÷ $0.10 = 100 dimes. Correct.
- Verify Dan's initial dimes: $5.00 ÷ $0.10 = 50 dimes. Correct.
- Verify Dan's total dimes: 50 + 2 = 52 dimes. Correct.

The answer is **52**.

Q: James is a first-year student at a University in Chicago. He has a budget of $1000 per semester. He spends 30% of his money on food, 15% on accommodation, 25% on entertainment, and the rest on coursework materials. How much money does he spend on coursework materials?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- James has a budget of $1000 per semester.
- He spends:
  - 30% of his budget on food.
  - 15% of his budget on accommodation.
  - 25% of his budget on entertainment.
  - The remaining percentage of his budget is spent on coursework materials.
- Assumptions:
  - The percentages are based on the total budget of $1000.
  - There are no additional expenses or savings beyond the categories listed.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Calculate the total percentage spent on food, accommodation, and entertainment, then subtract this from 100% to find the percentage spent on coursework materials. Multiply this percentage by $1000 to find the amount.**

**Method 2: Directly calculate the amounts spent on food, accommodation, and entertainment in dollars, sum them up, and subtract this total from $1000 to find the amount left for coursework materials.**

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Percentage-based approach**
1. Add the percentages spent on food, accommodation, and entertainment:
   - \( 30\% + 15\% + 25\% = 70\% \).
2. Subtract this total from 100% to find the percentage spent on coursework materials:
   - \( 100\% - 70\% = 30\% \).
3. Convert 30% to a decimal and multiply by the total budget to find the dollar amount:
   - \( 30\% = 0.30 \).
   - \( 0.30 \times 1000 = 300 \).
4. Double-check:
   - \( 30\% \) of $1000 is indeed $300.

**Potential pitfalls:** Miscalculating the total percentage or incorrectly converting percentages to decimals.

---

#### **Method 2: Dollar-based approach**
1. Calculate the dollar amounts spent on food, accommodation, and entertainment:
   - Food: \( 30\% \times 1000 = 0.30 \times 1000 = 300 \).
   - Accommodation: \( 15\% \times 1000 = 0.15 \times 1000 = 150 \).
   - Entertainment: \( 25\% \times 1000 = 0.25 \times 1000 = 250 \).
2. Add these amounts to find the total spent on food, accommodation, and entertainment:
   - \( 300 + 150 + 250 = 700 \).
3. Subtract this total from the budget to find the amount left for coursework materials:
   - \( 1000 - 700 = 300 \).
4. Double-check:
   - Adding $300 (food), $150 (accommodation), $250 (entertainment), and $300 (coursework materials) equals $1000, confirming the calculation.

**Potential pitfalls:** Arithmetic errors when summing or subtracting dollar amounts.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Clarity:** Method 1 is conceptually simpler because it focuses on percentages and avoids intermediate dollar calculations.
- **Reliability:** Both methods are reliable, but Method 2 involves more steps, increasing the chance of arithmetic errors.
- **Efficiency:** Method 1 is more efficient because it skips the step of calculating individual dollar amounts for food, accommodation, and entertainment.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.
1. Total percentage spent on food, accommodation, and entertainment:
   - \( 30\% + 15\% + 25\% = 70\% \).
2. Percentage spent on coursework materials:
   - \( 100\% - 70\% = 30\% \).
3. Dollar amount spent on coursework materials:
   - \( 0.30 \times 1000 = 300 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify by summing all expenses:
  - Food: $300, Accommodation: $150, Entertainment: $250, Coursework Materials: $300.
  - Total: \( 300 + 150 + 250 + 300 = 1000 \), which matches the budget.
- The calculations are correct, and the result is reasonable.

---

The answer is **300**.
Extracted:
300.0
A:
300.0

Q: There are 30 major league baseball stadiums. Zach has a goal to take his son to see at least one game at each one. He has calculated the average cost for them to travel and see a game will be $900 per stadium. Zach can save $1,500 per year for his baseball stadium trips. How many years will it take Zach to accomplish his goal?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are 30 major league baseball stadiums Zach wants to visit.
- The average cost for Zach and his son to visit one stadium is $900.
- Zach can save $1,500 per year for these trips.
- We assume the costs remain constant over time (no inflation or changes in travel costs).
- We assume Zach saves consistently every year and uses all his savings for the trips.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Total cost divided by annual savings.**
- Calculate the total cost of visiting all 30 stadiums by multiplying the cost per stadium by the number of stadiums.
- Divide the total cost by Zach's annual savings to determine how many years it will take.

**Method 2: Incremental approach.**
- Calculate how many stadiums Zach can afford to visit each year based on his annual savings.
- Determine how many years it will take for him to visit all 30 stadiums by summing up the number of stadiums he can afford each year until the total reaches 30.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Total cost divided by annual savings**
1. **Calculate the total cost of visiting all 30 stadiums:**
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = \text{Number of stadiums} \times \text{Cost per stadium}
   \]
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = 30 \times 900 = 27,000
   \]

2. **Divide the total cost by Zach's annual savings:**
   \[
   \text{Years required} = \frac{\text{Total cost}}{\text{Annual savings}}
   \]
   \[
   \text{Years required} = \frac{27,000}{1,500} = 18
   \]

3. **Check for potential pitfalls:**
   - Ensure the division is exact (no partial years needed). Since 27,000 is divisible by 1,500, the result is a whole number, so no adjustments are necessary.

#### **Method 2: Incremental approach**
1. **Determine how many stadiums Zach can afford to visit each year:**
   \[
   \text{Stadiums per year} = \frac{\text{Annual savings}}{\text{Cost per stadium}}
   \]
   \[
   \text{Stadiums per year} = \frac{1,500}{900} = 1.6667
   \]
   Zach can afford to visit 1 full stadium per year, with some savings left over.

2. **Calculate how many years it will take to visit all 30 stadiums:**
   - Each year, Zach visits 1 stadium and saves the leftover amount:
     \[
     \text{Leftover savings per year} = 1,500 - (1 \times 900) = 600
     \]
   - After 15 years, Zach will have visited 15 stadiums and accumulated:
     \[
     \text{Total leftover savings after 15 years} = 15 \times 600 = 9,000
     \]
   - In year 16, Zach can use the $9,000 leftover savings to visit the remaining 15 stadiums:
     \[
     \text{Remaining stadiums cost} = 15 \times 900 = 13,500
     \]
   - However, this approach is unnecessarily complex compared to Method 1.

3. **Check for potential pitfalls:**
   - This method introduces unnecessary complexity and requires tracking leftover savings, which is less efficient than directly dividing the total cost by annual savings.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and efficient. It directly calculates the total cost and divides it by the annual savings, avoiding unnecessary intermediate steps.
- **Method 2** is more complex and involves tracking incremental savings and leftover amounts, which is less practical and introduces more room for error.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and reliability.
- Using Method 1:
  \[
  \text{Total cost} = 30 \times 900 = 27,000
  \]
  \[
  \text{Years required} = \frac{27,000}{1,500} = 18
  \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate the total cost:
  \[
  30 \times 900 = 27,000
  \]
- Recalculate the years required:
  \[
  \frac{27,000}{1,500} = 18
  \]
- The calculations are consistent, and the result is reasonable given the problem's context.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **18 years**.
Extracted:
18.0
A:
18.0

Q: Nine adults went to a play with seven children. Adult tickets are $11 each and children's tickets are $7 each. How many dollars more did the adults' tickets cost in total than the children's tickets in total?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- **Number of adults:** 9  
- **Number of children:** 7  
- **Cost of an adult ticket:** $11  
- **Cost of a child ticket:** $7  
- We are tasked with finding how many dollars more the total cost of the adults' tickets is compared to the total cost of the children's tickets.  
- We assume all tickets were purchased at the stated prices, and there are no discounts, taxes, or additional fees.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total cost of the adults' tickets by multiplying the number of adults by the cost of an adult ticket.  
2. Calculate the total cost of the children's tickets by multiplying the number of children by the cost of a child ticket.  
3. Subtract the total cost of the children's tickets from the total cost of the adults' tickets to find the difference.

**Method 2:**  
1. Find the difference in cost between one adult ticket and one child ticket: $11 - $7 = $4.  
2. Multiply this difference by the number of adults (since the adults' tickets are more expensive) to find the total additional cost of the adults' tickets compared to the children's tickets.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of total costs**
1. **Calculate the total cost of the adults' tickets:**  
   \[
   9 \times 11 = 99
   \]  
   The total cost of the adults' tickets is $99.

2. **Calculate the total cost of the children's tickets:**  
   \[
   7 \times 7 = 49
   \]  
   The total cost of the children's tickets is $49.

3. **Find the difference between the two totals:**  
   \[
   99 - 49 = 50
   \]  
   The adults' tickets cost $50 more than the children's tickets.

4. **Check for errors:**  
   - Recalculate: $9 \times 11 = 99$ and $7 \times 7 = 49$. Subtracting $99 - 49$ again gives $50.  
   - No errors found.

---

#### **Method 2: Using the per-ticket price difference**
1. **Find the price difference between an adult ticket and a child ticket:**  
   \[
   11 - 7 = 4
   \]  
   Each adult ticket costs $4 more than each child ticket.

2. **Multiply the price difference by the number of adults:**  
   \[
   9 \times 4 = 36
   \]  
   The total additional cost of the adults' tickets compared to the children's tickets is $36.

3. **Check for errors:**  
   - Recalculate: $11 - 7 = 4$ and $9 \times 4 = 36$. The calculations are consistent.  
   - However, this method does not account for the total cost of the children's tickets, so it does not directly answer the question of how much more the adults' tickets cost in total.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more comprehensive because it calculates the total cost for both groups and directly finds the difference. It is straightforward and avoids any assumptions about proportionality.  
- **Method 2** is faster but assumes that the difference in ticket prices can be scaled directly by the number of adults. While this works in this case, it is less reliable for more complex problems.  
- **Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for clarity, reliability, and accuracy.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:  
1. Total cost of adults' tickets:  
   \[
   9 \times 11 = 99
   \]  
2. Total cost of children's tickets:  
   \[
   7 \times 7 = 49
   \]  
3. Difference in total costs:  
   \[
   99 - 49 = 50
   \]  

The adults' tickets cost $50 more than the children's tickets.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recalculate the totals:  
  Adults: $9 \times 11 = 99$  
  Children: $7 \times 7 = 49$  
  Difference: $99 - 49 = 50$.  
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable. The result makes sense given the problem.

Q: Simone ate 1/2 of an apple each day for 16 days. Lauri ate 1/3 of an apple each day for 15 days. How many apples did the two girls eat altogether?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Simone ate **1/2 of an apple per day** for **16 days**.  
- Lauri ate **1/3 of an apple per day** for **15 days**.  
- We are tasked with finding the **total number of apples** eaten by both girls combined.  
- Assume the apples are divisible into fractions without waste or loss.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total apples eaten by each girl separately using multiplication, then add their totals together.  

**Method 2:**  
Express the fractions as decimals (1/2 = 0.5, 1/3 ≈ 0.333), calculate the total apples eaten by each girl using multiplication, and then add their totals together.  

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Using fractions directly**
1. Calculate the total apples eaten by Simone:  
   - Simone eats **1/2 of an apple per day** for **16 days**.  
   - Total apples eaten by Simone = \( \frac{1}{2} \times 16 = \frac{16}{2} = 8 \).  

2. Calculate the total apples eaten by Lauri:  
   - Lauri eats **1/3 of an apple per day** for **15 days**.  
   - Total apples eaten by Lauri = \( \frac{1}{3} \times 15 = \frac{15}{3} = 5 \).  

3. Add the totals for Simone and Lauri:  
   - Total apples eaten = \( 8 + 5 = 13 \).  

#### **Method 2: Using decimals**
1. Convert fractions to decimals:  
   - \( \frac{1}{2} = 0.5 \), \( \frac{1}{3} \approx 0.333 \).  

2. Calculate the total apples eaten by Simone:  
   - Simone eats **0.5 apples per day** for **16 days**.  
   - Total apples eaten by Simone = \( 0.5 \times 16 = 8 \).  

3. Calculate the total apples eaten by Lauri:  
   - Lauri eats **0.333 apples per day** for **15 days**.  
   - Total apples eaten by Lauri = \( 0.333 \times 15 = 4.995 \).  

4. Add the totals for Simone and Lauri:  
   - Total apples eaten = \( 8 + 4.995 = 12.995 \).  

5. Round the result to account for approximation errors:  
   - Rounded total = \( 13 \).  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more precise because it uses fractions directly, avoiding rounding errors. It is also simpler and avoids unnecessary conversions.  
- **Method 2** introduces rounding errors due to the approximation of \( \frac{1}{3} \) as \( 0.333 \), which can lead to slight inaccuracies. While it may be useful for mental math, it is less reliable for exact calculations.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its precision and simplicity.  
- Using Method 1:  
  - Simone ate \( 8 \) apples.  
  - Lauri ate \( 5 \) apples.  
  - Total apples eaten = \( 8 + 5 = 13 \).  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using fractions:  
  - Simone: \( \frac{1}{2} \times 16 = 8 \).  
  - Lauri: \( \frac{1}{3} \times 15 = 5 \).  
  - Total: \( 8 + 5 = 13 \).  
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.  

### Final Answer:
The answer is **13**.

Q: Hawkeye is driving to his aunt. He has to charge his battery for $3.5 per charge. If he charged his battery four times, and his battery charging budget was $20, how much money was he left with when he reached his aunt's place?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Hawkeye charges his battery at a cost of $3.5 per charge.
- He charged his battery four times during his trip.
- His total budget for battery charging was $20.
- We assume there are no additional costs or fees, and the budget is entirely dedicated to battery charging.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total cost of charging the battery by multiplying the cost per charge ($3.5) by the number of charges (4). Subtract this total cost from the budget ($20) to find the remaining money.

**Method 2:**  
Subtract the cost of each charge ($3.5) from the budget ($20) iteratively for four charges. This method simulates the process of spending the budget step by step.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct multiplication and subtraction**
1. Calculate the total cost of charging:
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = \text{Cost per charge} \times \text{Number of charges}
   \]
   Substituting values:
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = 3.5 \times 4 = 14
   \]
2. Subtract the total cost from the budget:
   \[
   \text{Remaining money} = \text{Budget} - \text{Total cost}
   \]
   Substituting values:
   \[
   \text{Remaining money} = 20 - 14 = 6
   \]
3. Double-check the calculations:
   - Multiplication: \(3.5 \times 4 = 14\) (correct).
   - Subtraction: \(20 - 14 = 6\) (correct).

#### **Method 2: Iterative subtraction**
1. Start with the budget of $20.
2. Subtract the cost of the first charge:
   \[
   20 - 3.5 = 16.5
   \]
3. Subtract the cost of the second charge:
   \[
   16.5 - 3.5 = 13
   \]
4. Subtract the cost of the third charge:
   \[
   13 - 3.5 = 9.5
   \]
5. Subtract the cost of the fourth charge:
   \[
   9.5 - 3.5 = 6
   \]
6. Double-check the calculations:
   - Each subtraction step is correct: \(20 - 3.5 = 16.5\), \(16.5 - 3.5 = 13\), \(13 - 3.5 = 9.5\), \(9.5 - 3.5 = 6\).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more efficient and straightforward, as it uses direct multiplication and subtraction to arrive at the answer in fewer steps. It minimizes the risk of errors by reducing the number of intermediate calculations.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and simulates the spending process step by step, but it involves more calculations, which increases the chance of errors and is less efficient.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and reliability.
1. Calculate the total cost of charging:
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = 3.5 \times 4 = 14
   \]
2. Subtract the total cost from the budget:
   \[
   \text{Remaining money} = 20 - 14 = 6
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Multiplication: \(3.5 \times 4 = 14\) (correct).
- Subtraction: \(20 - 14 = 6\) (correct).
- The result is reasonable: Hawkeye spent $14 on charging, leaving $6 from his $20 budget.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **6**.

Q: Leon ordered 3 sets of toy organizers for $78 per set and 2 gaming chairs for $83 each. If there is a delivery fee that is 5% of the total sales, how much did Leon pay?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Leon ordered:
  - 3 sets of toy organizers at $78 per set.
  - 2 gaming chairs at $83 each.
- There is a delivery fee equal to **5% of the total sales** (before the delivery fee is added).
- We assume no discounts, taxes, or additional fees beyond the delivery fee.
- The goal is to calculate the total amount Leon paid, including the delivery fee.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct Calculation**
1. Calculate the cost of the toy organizers: \( 3 \times 78 \).
2. Calculate the cost of the gaming chairs: \( 2 \times 83 \).
3. Add these two amounts to get the total sales.
4. Calculate the delivery fee as 5% of the total sales.
5. Add the delivery fee to the total sales to get the final amount Leon paid.

**Method 2: Factorization Approach**
1. Combine the quantities and prices into a single expression: \( (3 \times 78) + (2 \times 83) \).
2. Factor out the delivery fee by multiplying the total sales by \( 1.05 \) (since the delivery fee is 5% of the total sales).
3. Perform the calculations step by step to find the final amount Leon paid.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Calculation**
1. **Calculate the cost of the toy organizers:**
   \[
   3 \times 78 = 234
   \]
2. **Calculate the cost of the gaming chairs:**
   \[
   2 \times 83 = 166
   \]
3. **Add these amounts to get the total sales:**
   \[
   234 + 166 = 400
   \]
4. **Calculate the delivery fee (5% of total sales):**
   \[
   5\% \text{ of } 400 = 0.05 \times 400 = 20
   \]
5. **Add the delivery fee to the total sales:**
   \[
   400 + 20 = 420
   \]

#### **Method 2: Factorization Approach**
1. **Combine the quantities and prices into a single expression:**
   \[
   (3 \times 78) + (2 \times 83) = 234 + 166 = 400
   \]
2. **Factor in the delivery fee by multiplying the total sales by \( 1.05 \):**
   \[
   400 \times 1.05 = 400 + (0.05 \times 400) = 400 + 20 = 420
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and breaks the problem into smaller, clear steps. It is easier to follow and verify each calculation.
- **Method 2** is more efficient because it combines the delivery fee calculation into a single step, but it requires a deeper understanding of percentages and factorization.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more reliable for someone who prefers step-by-step clarity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and reliability.

1. **Calculate the cost of the toy organizers:**
   \[
   3 \times 78 = 234
   \]
2. **Calculate the cost of the gaming chairs:**
   \[
   2 \times 83 = 166
   \]
3. **Add these amounts to get the total sales:**
   \[
   234 + 166 = 400
   \]
4. **Calculate the delivery fee (5% of total sales):**
   \[
   0.05 \times 400 = 20
   \]
5. **Add the delivery fee to the total sales:**
   \[
   400 + 20 = 420
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate the total sales:
  \[
  3 \times 78 = 234, \quad 2 \times 83 = 166, \quad 234 + 166 = 400
  \]
- Recalculate the delivery fee:
  \[
  0.05 \times 400 = 20
  \]
- Recalculate the final amount:
  \[
  400 + 20 = 420
  \]
- All calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

The answer is **420**.

Q: Lois has 40 books. She gives a fourth of her books to her nephew. From her remaining books, she donates a third of her books to the library. Then she purchases 3 new books from the book store. How many books does Lois have now?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Lois starts with **40 books**.
- She gives **a fourth of her books** to her nephew.
- From the remaining books, she donates **a third of her books** to the library.
- After donating, she purchases **3 new books**.
- We assume no books are lost, damaged, or otherwise unaccounted for during the process.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the number of books Lois gives to her nephew by finding one-fourth of 40. Subtract this from the total to find the remaining books.  
- From the remaining books, calculate one-third to determine how many are donated to the library. Subtract this from the remaining books.  
- Add the 3 new books to the final count.  

**Method 2:**  
- Use fractions to represent the process algebraically. Start with 40 books, subtract one-fourth, then subtract one-third of the remaining books, and finally add 3.  
- Solve step by step using fractions and arithmetic.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Step-by-step calculations**
1. **Calculate books given to nephew:**  
   Lois gives one-fourth of her books to her nephew:  
   \[
   \frac{1}{4} \times 40 = 10
   \]  
   Subtract this from the total:  
   \[
   40 - 10 = 30
   \]  
   Lois has **30 books** remaining.

2. **Calculate books donated to the library:**  
   Lois donates one-third of her remaining books:  
   \[
   \frac{1}{3} \times 30 = 10
   \]  
   Subtract this from the remaining books:  
   \[
   30 - 10 = 20
   \]  
   Lois has **20 books** remaining after the donation.

3. **Add the 3 new books:**  
   Lois purchases 3 new books:  
   \[
   20 + 3 = 23
   \]  
   Lois now has **23 books**.

#### **Method 2: Step-by-step calculations**
1. **Represent the process algebraically:**  
   Start with 40 books. Subtract one-fourth of 40, then subtract one-third of the remaining books, and finally add 3:  
   \[
   \text{Final count} = 40 - \frac{1}{4}(40) - \frac{1}{3}(40 - \frac{1}{4}(40)) + 3
   \]

2. **Simplify step by step:**  
   - First, calculate \(\frac{1}{4}(40)\):  
     \[
     \frac{1}{4} \times 40 = 10
     \]  
     Subtract this from 40:  
     \[
     40 - 10 = 30
     \]  
   - Next, calculate \(\frac{1}{3}(30)\):  
     \[
     \frac{1}{3} \times 30 = 10
     \]  
     Subtract this from 30:  
     \[
     30 - 10 = 20
     \]  
   - Finally, add 3:  
     \[
     20 + 3 = 23
     \]

3. **Result:**  
   The final count is **23 books**.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and intuitive, breaking the problem into smaller, sequential steps. It is easier to follow and less prone to errors.  
- **Method 2** is more algebraic and compact but requires careful handling of fractions and intermediate results, which could lead to mistakes if not done carefully.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its clarity and simplicity.  
- Using Method 1:  
  - Lois starts with 40 books.  
  - She gives 10 books to her nephew, leaving 30.  
  - She donates 10 books to the library, leaving 20.  
  - She purchases 3 new books, bringing her total to 23.  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recheck each step:  
  - One-fourth of 40 is 10, leaving 30.  
  - One-third of 30 is 10, leaving 20.  
  - Adding 3 gives 23.  
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **23**.

Q: Faith is getting paid $13.50 per hour. She normally works 8 hours a day, 5 days a week, plus 2 hours of overtime per day. How much will she earn by the end of the week?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Faith earns **$13.50 per hour**.
- She works **8 hours per day** for **5 days a week** (normal hours).
- She also works **2 hours of overtime per day** for **5 days a week**.
- Overtime pay is not explicitly mentioned, so we assume it is the same as her regular hourly rate ($13.50 per hour).
- We are calculating her total weekly earnings.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the total earnings for her regular hours: \( 8 \, \text{hours/day} \times 5 \, \text{days} \times 13.50 \, \text{\$/hour} \).  
- Calculate the total earnings for her overtime hours: \( 2 \, \text{hours/day} \times 5 \, \text{days} \times 13.50 \, \text{\$/hour} \).  
- Add the two results to find her total weekly earnings.

**Method 2:**  
- Combine her total daily hours (regular + overtime): \( 8 \, \text{hours} + 2 \, \text{hours} = 10 \, \text{hours/day} \).  
- Calculate her total weekly hours: \( 10 \, \text{hours/day} \times 5 \, \text{days} \).  
- Multiply her total weekly hours by her hourly rate: \( \text{total hours} \times 13.50 \, \text{\$/hour} \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Separate regular and overtime calculations**
1. **Regular hours earnings:**  
   - Faith works 8 hours per day for 5 days:  
     \( 8 \, \text{hours/day} \times 5 \, \text{days} = 40 \, \text{hours} \).  
   - Her hourly rate is $13.50:  
     \( 40 \, \text{hours} \times 13.50 \, \text{\$/hour} = 540.00 \, \text{\$} \).  

2. **Overtime hours earnings:**  
   - Faith works 2 overtime hours per day for 5 days:  
     \( 2 \, \text{hours/day} \times 5 \, \text{days} = 10 \, \text{hours} \).  
   - Her hourly rate is $13.50:  
     \( 10 \, \text{hours} \times 13.50 \, \text{\$/hour} = 135.00 \, \text{\$} \).  

3. **Total weekly earnings:**  
   - Add regular and overtime earnings:  
     \( 540.00 \, \text{\$} + 135.00 \, \text{\$} = 675.00 \, \text{\$} \).  

#### **Method 2: Combine total hours and calculate directly**
1. **Total daily hours:**  
   - Faith works 8 regular hours and 2 overtime hours per day:  
     \( 8 \, \text{hours} + 2 \, \text{hours} = 10 \, \text{hours/day} \).  

2. **Total weekly hours:**  
   - She works 10 hours per day for 5 days:  
     \( 10 \, \text{hours/day} \times 5 \, \text{days} = 50 \, \text{hours} \).  

3. **Total weekly earnings:**  
   - Multiply total weekly hours by her hourly rate:  
     \( 50 \, \text{hours} \times 13.50 \, \text{\$/hour} = 675.00 \, \text{\$} \).  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** breaks the problem into smaller, more detailed steps (regular and overtime hours separately). This is useful for clarity and understanding, especially if overtime pay differs from regular pay.  
- **Method 2** combines the hours upfront and calculates the total earnings in fewer steps. It is more efficient but assumes all hours are paid at the same rate.  
- Since overtime pay is the same as regular pay in this problem, **Method 2** is faster and equally reliable.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- Using **Method 2**:  
  1. Total daily hours: \( 8 + 2 = 10 \, \text{hours/day} \).  
  2. Total weekly hours: \( 10 \, \text{hours/day} \times 5 \, \text{days} = 50 \, \text{hours} \).  
  3. Total weekly earnings: \( 50 \, \text{hours} \times 13.50 \, \text{\$/hour} = 675.00 \, \text{\$} \).  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using **Method 1**:  
  - Regular earnings: \( 40 \, \text{hours} \times 13.50 = 540.00 \, \text{\$} \).  
  - Overtime earnings: \( 10 \, \text{hours} \times 13.50 = 135.00 \, \text{\$} \).  
  - Total earnings: \( 540.00 + 135.00 = 675.00 \, \text{\$} \).  
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the calculation is correct.  

### Final Answer:
The answer is **675.00**.
Extracted:
675.0
A:
