Q: Emma buys 2 containers of milk every school day for lunch. She does not go to school on the weekends. How many containers of milk does she buy in 3 weeks?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Emma buys **2 containers of milk** every school day.
- A school week consists of **5 days** (Monday through Friday).
- Emma does not go to school on weekends, so she does not buy milk on Saturdays or Sundays.
- We are calculating the total number of milk containers Emma buys in **3 weeks**.
- Assume there are no holidays or interruptions during the 3 weeks.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the total number of school days in 3 weeks: \(5 \, \text{days/week} \times 3 \, \text{weeks}\).
- Multiply the total number of school days by the number of milk containers Emma buys per day: \( \text{school days} \times 2 \, \text{containers/day}\).

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the number of milk containers Emma buys in one week: \(5 \, \text{days/week} \times 2 \, \text{containers/day}\).
- Multiply the weekly total by 3 weeks: \( \text{weekly containers} \times 3 \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the total number of school days in 3 weeks:  
   \[
   5 \, \text{days/week} \times 3 \, \text{weeks} = 15 \, \text{school days}.
   \]
2. Multiply the total school days by the number of milk containers per day:  
   \[
   15 \, \text{days} \times 2 \, \text{containers/day} = 30 \, \text{containers}.
   \]
3. Double-check:  
   - \(5 \times 3 = 15\) is correct for the total school days.  
   - \(15 \times 2 = 30\) is correct for the total containers.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate the number of milk containers Emma buys in one week:  
   \[
   5 \, \text{days/week} \times 2 \, \text{containers/day} = 10 \, \text{containers/week}.
   \]
2. Multiply the weekly total by 3 weeks:  
   \[
   10 \, \text{containers/week} \times 3 \, \text{weeks} = 30 \, \text{containers}.
   \]
3. Double-check:  
   - \(5 \times 2 = 10\) is correct for the weekly total.  
   - \(10 \times 3 = 30\) is correct for the total containers.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** directly calculates the total number of school days and then multiplies by the daily milk consumption. It is straightforward and efficient for this problem.  
- **Method 2** breaks the problem into smaller, more intuitive steps by first calculating the weekly total and then scaling it up for 3 weeks. This method is helpful for those who prefer smaller, incremental calculations.  
- Both methods are equally reliable and yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is slightly more efficient because it skips the intermediate step of calculating the weekly total.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.  
1. Total school days in 3 weeks:  
   \[
   5 \, \text{days/week} \times 3 \, \text{weeks} = 15 \, \text{school days}.
   \]
2. Total milk containers:  
   \[
   15 \, \text{days} \times 2 \, \text{containers/day} = 30 \, \text{containers}.
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using **Method 2**:  
   - Weekly total: \(5 \times 2 = 10\).  
   - Total for 3 weeks: \(10 \times 3 = 30\).  
- Both methods confirm the result.  
- The result is reasonable because Emma buys milk only on school days, and the calculations align with the given information.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **30**.

Q: Cole wants to fence his backyard on three sides. His backyard is 9 feet along the sides and 18 feet along the back. The neighbor behind him agreed to pay for half of their shared side of his fence, and the neighbor on his left agreed to pay for a third of their shared side. Fencing costs $3 per foot. How much does Cole have to pay for the fence?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Cole wants to fence three sides of his backyard: two sides of 9 feet each and one back side of 18 feet.
- The total length of fencing required is \( 9 + 9 + 18 = 36 \) feet.
- The neighbor behind Cole (shared back side of 18 feet) will pay for **half** of their shared side.
- The neighbor on Cole's left (shared side of 9 feet) will pay for **one-third** of their shared side.
- Fencing costs $3 per foot.
- Assumptions:
  - The neighbors will pay their agreed portions without dispute.
  - The cost of fencing is uniform at $3 per foot for all sides.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Calculate the total cost of fencing first, then subtract the contributions from the neighbors.**
- Step 1: Calculate the total cost of fencing all 36 feet.
- Step 2: Calculate the neighbor's contributions for the back side (half of 18 feet) and the left side (one-third of 9 feet).
- Step 3: Subtract the neighbors' contributions from the total cost to find Cole's share.

**Method 2: Calculate Cole's share of each side individually, then sum up his costs.**
- Step 1: Calculate Cole's share of the back side (half of 18 feet) and the left side (two-thirds of 9 feet).
- Step 2: Add the full cost of the right side (9 feet) since no neighbor contributes to it.
- Step 3: Multiply the total length of fencing Cole is responsible for by the cost per foot.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Total cost minus neighbor contributions**
1. **Calculate the total cost of fencing:**
   \[
   \text{Total length} = 36 \, \text{feet}, \quad \text{Cost per foot} = 3 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = 36 \times 3 = 108 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]

2. **Calculate the neighbor's contribution for the back side:**
   - The back side is 18 feet, and the neighbor pays for half:
     \[
     \text{Neighbor's contribution (back side)} = \frac{18}{2} \times 3 = 9 \times 3 = 27 \, \text{dollars}.
     \]

3. **Calculate the neighbor's contribution for the left side:**
   - The left side is 9 feet, and the neighbor pays for one-third:
     \[
     \text{Neighbor's contribution (left side)} = \frac{9}{3} \times 3 = 3 \times 3 = 9 \, \text{dollars}.
     \]

4. **Subtract the neighbors' contributions from the total cost:**
   \[
   \text{Cole's cost} = \text{Total cost} - (\text{Neighbor's contribution (back side)} + \text{Neighbor's contribution (left side)}).
   \]
   \[
   \text{Cole's cost} = 108 - (27 + 9) = 108 - 36 = 72 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]

---

#### **Method 2: Calculate Cole's share of each side individually**
1. **Calculate Cole's share of the back side:**
   - The back side is 18 feet, and Cole pays for half:
     \[
     \text{Cole's share (back side)} = \frac{18}{2} = 9 \, \text{feet}.
     \]

2. **Calculate Cole's share of the left side:**
   - The left side is 9 feet, and Cole pays for two-thirds:
     \[
     \text{Cole's share (left side)} = \frac{2}{3} \times 9 = 6 \, \text{feet}.
     \]

3. **Add the full length of the right side:**
   - The right side is 9 feet, and Cole pays for the entire length:
     \[
     \text{Cole's share (right side)} = 9 \, \text{feet}.
     \]

4. **Calculate the total length of fencing Cole is responsible for:**
   \[
   \text{Total length (Cole's share)} = 9 + 6 + 9 = 24 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

5. **Calculate the total cost for Cole:**
   \[
   \text{Cost per foot} = 3 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
   \[
   \text{Cole's cost} = 24 \times 3 = 72 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and focuses on the total cost first, then subtracts the neighbors' contributions. It is easy to follow and minimizes intermediate calculations.
- **Method 2** breaks the problem into smaller parts by calculating Cole's share of each side individually. While it is more detailed, it involves more intermediate steps and calculations, which increases the chance of error.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and easier to verify.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Total cost of fencing: \( 36 \times 3 = 108 \, \text{dollars} \).
2. Neighbor's contribution (back side): \( 27 \, \text{dollars} \).
3. Neighbor's contribution (left side): \( 9 \, \text{dollars} \).
4. Cole's cost: \( 108 - (27 + 9) = 108 - 36 = 72 \, \text{dollars} \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Total length of fencing: \( 36 \, \text{feet} \).
- Neighbors contribute for \( 9 + 3 = 12 \, \text{feet} \), leaving Cole responsible for \( 36 - 12 = 24 \, \text{feet} \).
- Cost for 24 feet: \( 24 \times 3 = 72 \, \text{dollars} \).
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

The answer is **72 dollars**.

72.0

Q: Movie tickets cost $5 each on a Monday, twice as much on a Wednesday, and five times as much as Monday on a Saturday. If Glenn goes to the movie theater on Wednesday and Saturday, how much does he spend?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- **Monday ticket price:** $5 each.  
- **Wednesday ticket price:** Twice as much as Monday, so $5 × 2 = $10 each.  
- **Saturday ticket price:** Five times as much as Monday, so $5 × 5 = $25 each.  
- Glenn goes to the movie theater on **Wednesday** and **Saturday**.  
- We assume Glenn buys one ticket on each day.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Calculate the ticket prices for Wednesday and Saturday individually, then add them together.  
**Method 2:** Use proportional reasoning based on the Monday ticket price to calculate the total cost directly: $5 × (2 + 5).  

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Calculate ticket prices individually and add them.**
1. **Wednesday ticket price:** $5 × 2 = $10.  
2. **Saturday ticket price:** $5 × 5 = $25.  
3. **Total cost:** Add the Wednesday and Saturday prices: $10 + $25 = $35.  

**Checks:**  
- Wednesday price is twice Monday's price: $5 × 2 = $10. This is correct.  
- Saturday price is five times Monday's price: $5 × 5 = $25. This is correct.  
- Adding $10 and $25 gives $35, which is reasonable.  

#### **Method 2: Use proportional reasoning based on Monday's price.**
1. Combine the Wednesday multiplier (2) and Saturday multiplier (5): 2 + 5 = 7.  
2. Multiply Monday's price by the combined multiplier: $5 × 7 = $35.  

**Checks:**  
- The combined multiplier (2 + 5 = 7) correctly accounts for both Wednesday and Saturday.  
- Multiplying $5 × 7 gives $35, which matches the result from Method 1.  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and intuitive, as it calculates each ticket price individually and adds them. This approach is less prone to errors and easier to follow.  
- **Method 2** is efficient and uses proportional reasoning, but it requires understanding the combined multiplier, which might be less intuitive for some.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is better due to its clarity and reliability.  
- Solution:  
  - Wednesday ticket price: $5 × 2 = $10.  
  - Saturday ticket price: $5 × 5 = $25.  
  - Total cost: $10 + $25 = $35.  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2: $5 × (2 + 5) = $5 × 7 = $35.  
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.  

### Final Answer:
The answer is **35**.

Q: Karlee has 100 grapes and 3/5 as many strawberries as grapes. Giana and Ansley, two of her friends, come visiting, and she gives each of them 1/5 of each fruit. How many fruits is Karlee left with in total?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Karlee has **100 grapes**.
- She has **3/5 as many strawberries as grapes**.  
  This means the number of strawberries is \( \frac{3}{5} \times 100 = 60 \).
- Karlee gives **1/5 of each fruit** to **Giana** and **1/5 of each fruit** to **Ansley**.  
  This means she gives away \( \frac{1}{5} \) of her grapes and strawberries to each friend, for a total of \( \frac{2}{5} \) of each fruit.
- We assume no fruits are lost, spoiled, or otherwise removed from the total.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Direct subtraction of the fruits given away.
- Calculate the total number of grapes and strawberries Karlee gives away (\( \frac{2}{5} \) of each fruit).
- Subtract the given-away fruits from the original totals to find how many fruits Karlee has left.

#### Method 2: Calculate the remaining fraction of fruits Karlee keeps.
- Karlee keeps \( 1 - \frac{2}{5} = \frac{3}{5} \) of each fruit.
- Multiply the original totals of grapes and strawberries by \( \frac{3}{5} \) to find how many fruits Karlee has left.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Direct subtraction.
1. **Calculate the number of grapes given away**:  
   Karlee gives \( \frac{2}{5} \times 100 = 40 \) grapes.  
   Remaining grapes: \( 100 - 40 = 60 \).

2. **Calculate the number of strawberries given away**:  
   Karlee gives \( \frac{2}{5} \times 60 = 24 \) strawberries.  
   Remaining strawberries: \( 60 - 24 = 36 \).

3. **Add the remaining fruits**:  
   Total fruits left: \( 60 + 36 = 96 \).

#### Method 2: Fraction of fruits kept.
1. **Calculate the fraction of grapes Karlee keeps**:  
   Karlee keeps \( \frac{3}{5} \times 100 = 60 \) grapes.

2. **Calculate the fraction of strawberries Karlee keeps**:  
   Karlee keeps \( \frac{3}{5} \times 60 = 36 \) strawberries.

3. **Add the remaining fruits**:  
   Total fruits left: \( 60 + 36 = 96 \).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** involves direct subtraction, which is straightforward and intuitive. It breaks the problem into smaller steps, making it easier to follow.
- **Method 2** uses fractions to calculate the remaining fruits directly, which is efficient but may be less intuitive for some people.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is slightly clearer for step-by-step reasoning.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and simplicity.  
1. Grapes given away: \( \frac{2}{5} \times 100 = 40 \). Remaining grapes: \( 100 - 40 = 60 \).  
2. Strawberries given away: \( \frac{2}{5} \times 60 = 24 \). Remaining strawberries: \( 60 - 24 = 36 \).  
3. Total fruits left: \( 60 + 36 = 96 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:  
  Grapes kept: \( \frac{3}{5} \times 100 = 60 \).  
  Strawberries kept: \( \frac{3}{5} \times 60 = 36 \).  
  Total fruits left: \( 60 + 36 = 96 \).  
- Both methods confirm the result. The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **96**.

Q: If 100 pieces, each 15 centimeters long, are cut from a 51-meter long ribbon, how much ribbon remains ?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The ribbon is initially 51 meters long.
- 100 pieces, each 15 centimeters long, are cut from the ribbon.
- We need to determine how much ribbon remains after cutting.
- Assumptions:
  - The ribbon is cut perfectly with no waste or leftover scraps.
  - The length of the ribbon is measured consistently in meters.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Convert all measurements to the same unit (meters), calculate the total length of the cut pieces, and subtract this from the original ribbon length.  
**Method 2:** Work directly in centimeters, calculate the total length of the ribbon in centimeters, subtract the total length of the cut pieces, and convert the result back to meters.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Convert to meters first**
1. Convert the length of each piece to meters:  
   \( 15 \, \text{cm} = 0.15 \, \text{m} \).  
2. Calculate the total length of the 100 pieces:  
   \( 100 \times 0.15 = 15 \, \text{m} \).  
3. Subtract the total length of the cut pieces from the original ribbon length:  
   \( 51 - 15 = 36 \, \text{m} \).  
4. Double-check the calculations:  
   - \( 100 \times 0.15 = 15 \, \text{m} \) is correct.  
   - \( 51 - 15 = 36 \, \text{m} \) is correct.  

#### **Method 2: Work in centimeters first**
1. Convert the total length of the ribbon to centimeters:  
   \( 51 \, \text{m} = 5100 \, \text{cm} \).  
2. Calculate the total length of the 100 pieces:  
   \( 100 \times 15 = 1500 \, \text{cm} \).  
3. Subtract the total length of the cut pieces from the original ribbon length:  
   \( 5100 - 1500 = 3600 \, \text{cm} \).  
4. Convert the remaining length back to meters:  
   \( 3600 \, \text{cm} = 36 \, \text{m} \).  
5. Double-check the calculations:  
   - \( 100 \times 15 = 1500 \, \text{cm} \) is correct.  
   - \( 5100 - 1500 = 3600 \, \text{cm} \) is correct.  
   - \( 3600 \, \text{cm} = 36 \, \text{m} \) is correct.  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward because it avoids unnecessary unit conversions and works directly in meters, which is the unit of the original ribbon length.  
- **Method 2** involves more steps and conversions, increasing the chance of errors, but it is still valid and reliable.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.  
1. Convert the length of each piece to meters:  
   \( 15 \, \text{cm} = 0.15 \, \text{m} \).  
2. Calculate the total length of the 100 pieces:  
   \( 100 \times 0.15 = 15 \, \text{m} \).  
3. Subtract the total length of the cut pieces from the original ribbon length:  
   \( 51 - 15 = 36 \, \text{m} \).  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recheck all calculations:  
   - \( 100 \times 0.15 = 15 \, \text{m} \) is correct.  
   - \( 51 - 15 = 36 \, \text{m} \) is correct.  
- The result is reasonable because the remaining ribbon length is less than the original length but still substantial.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **36 meters**.

Q: Susie and Britney each keep chickens, of two different breeds. Susie has 11 Rhode Island Reds and 6 Golden Comets. Britney has twice as many Rhode Island Reds as Susie, but only half as many Golden Comets. How many more chickens are in Britney's flock than in Susie's?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Susie has 11 Rhode Island Reds and 6 Golden Comets.  
- Britney has **twice as many Rhode Island Reds** as Susie, meaning Britney has \( 2 \times 11 = 22 \) Rhode Island Reds.  
- Britney has **half as many Golden Comets** as Susie, meaning Britney has \( \frac{6}{2} = 3 \) Golden Comets.  
- We are tasked with finding how many **more chickens** Britney has compared to Susie.  
- Assumptions: All chickens are accounted for, and no chickens are lost, sold, or added during the comparison.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total number of chickens for each person, then subtract Susie's total from Britney's total to find the difference.  

**Method 2:**  
Compare the number of Rhode Island Reds and Golden Comets separately, then sum the differences to find how many more chickens Britney has.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Total chickens approach**  
1. Calculate Susie's total number of chickens:  
   Susie has \( 11 + 6 = 17 \) chickens.  

2. Calculate Britney's total number of chickens:  
   Britney has \( 22 + 3 = 25 \) chickens.  

3. Subtract Susie's total from Britney's total:  
   \( 25 - 17 = 8 \).  

4. Double-check calculations:  
   - Susie's total: \( 11 + 6 = 17 \).  
   - Britney's total: \( 22 + 3 = 25 \).  
   - Difference: \( 25 - 17 = 8 \).  

Potential pitfalls: This method is straightforward, but errors could arise if the totals are miscalculated or if subtraction is performed incorrectly.

---

#### **Method 2: Separate breed comparison approach**  
1. Compare the number of Rhode Island Reds:  
   Britney has \( 22 - 11 = 11 \) more Rhode Island Reds than Susie.  

2. Compare the number of Golden Comets:  
   Britney has \( 3 - 6 = -3 \), meaning Britney has 3 fewer Golden Comets than Susie.  

3. Sum the differences:  
   \( 11 - 3 = 8 \).  

4. Double-check calculations:  
   - Rhode Island Reds difference: \( 22 - 11 = 11 \).  
   - Golden Comets difference: \( 3 - 6 = -3 \).  
   - Total difference: \( 11 - 3 = 8 \).  

Potential pitfalls: This method requires careful attention to signs (positive vs. negative differences) and summing correctly.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is more straightforward and intuitive, as it directly calculates the total number of chickens for each person and subtracts. It minimizes the risk of errors related to signs or summing differences.  
- **Method 2** is slightly more complex, as it involves comparing breeds separately and summing differences, which introduces more steps and potential for mistakes.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and reliability.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:  
1. Susie's total chickens: \( 11 + 6 = 17 \).  
2. Britney's total chickens: \( 22 + 3 = 25 \).  
3. Difference: \( 25 - 17 = 8 \).  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Susie's total: \( 11 + 6 = 17 \).  
- Britney's total: \( 22 + 3 = 25 \).  
- Difference: \( 25 - 17 = 8 \).  

The calculations are consistent and reasonable. Britney has 8 more chickens than Susie.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **8**.

Q: A mechanic charges $60 an hour to repair a car.  He works 8 hours a day for 14 days on one car.  The mechanic also used $2500 in parts.  How much did the car's owner have to pay?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The mechanic charges $60 per hour for labor.
- He works 8 hours per day for 14 days on the car.
- The mechanic also used $2500 in parts.
- We assume there are no additional fees, taxes, or discounts.
- The total cost is the sum of labor charges and parts cost.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total labor cost by multiplying the hourly rate ($60) by the total hours worked (8 hours/day × 14 days). Then, add the cost of parts ($2500) to the labor cost to find the total.

**Method 2:**  
First, calculate the daily labor cost by multiplying the hourly rate ($60) by the hours worked per day (8 hours). Then, multiply the daily labor cost by the number of days worked (14 days). Finally, add the cost of parts ($2500) to the labor cost to find the total.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total hours worked:  
   \( 8 \, \text{hours/day} \times 14 \, \text{days} = 112 \, \text{hours} \).  

2. Calculate the total labor cost:  
   \( 112 \, \text{hours} \times 60 \, \text{dollars/hour} = 6720 \, \text{dollars} \).  

3. Add the cost of parts:  
   \( 6720 \, \text{dollars} + 2500 \, \text{dollars} = 9220 \, \text{dollars} \).  

4. Double-check calculations:  
   - Total hours: \( 8 \times 14 = 112 \).  
   - Labor cost: \( 112 \times 60 = 6720 \).  
   - Total cost: \( 6720 + 2500 = 9220 \).  

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the daily labor cost:  
   \( 8 \, \text{hours/day} \times 60 \, \text{dollars/hour} = 480 \, \text{dollars/day} \).  

2. Calculate the total labor cost:  
   \( 480 \, \text{dollars/day} \times 14 \, \text{days} = 6720 \, \text{dollars} \).  

3. Add the cost of parts:  
   \( 6720 \, \text{dollars} + 2500 \, \text{dollars} = 9220 \, \text{dollars} \).  

4. Double-check calculations:  
   - Daily labor cost: \( 8 \times 60 = 480 \).  
   - Total labor cost: \( 480 \times 14 = 6720 \).  
   - Total cost: \( 6720 + 2500 = 9220 \).  

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is more direct because it calculates the total hours worked first, which simplifies the labor cost calculation. It avoids intermediate steps and is less prone to errors.  
- **Method 2** introduces an extra step (daily labor cost) that may be useful for breaking down the problem but is less efficient overall.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:  
1. Total hours worked:  
   \( 8 \times 14 = 112 \, \text{hours} \).  

2. Total labor cost:  
   \( 112 \times 60 = 6720 \, \text{dollars} \).  

3. Total cost:  
   \( 6720 + 2500 = 9220 \, \text{dollars} \).  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Labor cost: \( 112 \times 60 = 6720 \).  
- Total cost: \( 6720 + 2500 = 9220 \).  
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable given the problem's context.

---

The answer is **9220**.

Q: Melanie has twice as many cats as Annie, and Annie has three times fewer cats than Jacob. If Jacob has 90 cats, how many cats does Melanie have?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jacob has **90 cats**.
- Annie has **three times fewer cats than Jacob**, which means Annie has **1/3 as many cats as Jacob**.
- Melanie has **twice as many cats as Annie**.
- We assume all calculations are exact, and there are no fractional cats.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate how many cats Annie has by dividing Jacob's cats by 3.  
- Calculate how many cats Melanie has by multiplying Annie's cats by 2.

**Method 2:**  
- Use algebra to represent the relationships. Let:
  - \( J = 90 \) (Jacob's cats),
  - \( A = \frac{1}{3}J \) (Annie's cats),
  - \( M = 2A \) (Melanie's cats).  
- Substitute \( J = 90 \) into the equations and solve step by step.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Step-by-step calculations**
1. **Calculate Annie's cats:**  
   Annie has 1/3 as many cats as Jacob.  
   \[
   A = \frac{1}{3} \times 90 = 30
   \]  
   So, Annie has **30 cats**.

2. **Calculate Melanie's cats:**  
   Melanie has twice as many cats as Annie.  
   \[
   M = 2 \times 30 = 60
   \]  
   So, Melanie has **60 cats**.

3. **Check for errors or pitfalls:**  
   - The calculation for Annie's cats is straightforward: \( \frac{1}{3} \times 90 = 30 \).  
   - The calculation for Melanie's cats is also straightforward: \( 2 \times 30 = 60 \).  
   - No errors or unreasonable results are observed.

#### **Method 2: Step-by-step calculations using algebra**
1. **Set up equations:**  
   - Jacob's cats: \( J = 90 \).  
   - Annie's cats: \( A = \frac{1}{3}J \).  
   - Melanie's cats: \( M = 2A \).

2. **Substitute \( J = 90 \) into \( A = \frac{1}{3}J \):**  
   \[
   A = \frac{1}{3} \times 90 = 30
   \]  
   So, Annie has **30 cats**.

3. **Substitute \( A = 30 \) into \( M = 2A \):**  
   \[
   M = 2 \times 30 = 60
   \]  
   So, Melanie has **60 cats**.

4. **Check for errors or pitfalls:**  
   - The substitution process is clear and logical.  
   - The calculations match those in Method 1.  
   - No errors or unreasonable results are observed.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and intuitive, as it involves simple arithmetic without introducing variables. It is faster and less prone to errors for this specific problem.  
- **Method 2** is more systematic and generalizable, especially for more complex problems. However, it introduces unnecessary complexity for this straightforward problem.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice for this problem.  
- Using Method 1:  
  - Annie's cats: \( A = \frac{1}{3} \times 90 = 30 \).  
  - Melanie's cats: \( M = 2 \times 30 = 60 \).  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the relationships:  
  - Annie has 1/3 as many cats as Jacob: \( \frac{1}{3} \times 90 = 30 \).  
  - Melanie has twice as many cats as Annie: \( 2 \times 30 = 60 \).  
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.  

### Final Answer:
The answer is **60**.

Q: A taco truck buys 100 pounds of beef.  They use .25 pounds of beef per taco.  If they sell each taco for $2 and each taco takes $1.5 to make how much profit did they make if they used all the beef?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The taco truck buys **100 pounds of beef**.
- Each taco uses **0.25 pounds of beef**.
- Each taco is sold for **$2**.
- Each taco costs **$1.50** to make (this includes all costs: beef, other ingredients, labor, etc.).
- The truck uses **all 100 pounds of beef**.
- We assume no beef is wasted, and all tacos made are sold.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**
1. Calculate the total number of tacos made using the 100 pounds of beef.
2. Calculate the total revenue from selling all the tacos.
3. Calculate the total cost of making all the tacos.
4. Subtract the total cost from the total revenue to find the profit.

**Method 2:**
1. Calculate the profit per taco by subtracting the cost per taco from the selling price.
2. Calculate the total number of tacos made using the 100 pounds of beef.
3. Multiply the profit per taco by the total number of tacos to find the total profit.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. **Calculate the total number of tacos made:**
   - Each taco uses 0.25 pounds of beef.
   - Total tacos = Total beef ÷ Beef per taco = \( 100 \div 0.25 = 400 \) tacos.

2. **Calculate the total revenue:**
   - Each taco is sold for $2.
   - Total revenue = Number of tacos × Selling price per taco = \( 400 \times 2 = 800 \) dollars.

3. **Calculate the total cost:**
   - Each taco costs $1.50 to make.
   - Total cost = Number of tacos × Cost per taco = \( 400 \times 1.5 = 600 \) dollars.

4. **Calculate the profit:**
   - Profit = Total revenue - Total cost = \( 800 - 600 = 200 \) dollars.

#### **Method 2:**
1. **Calculate the profit per taco:**
   - Profit per taco = Selling price per taco - Cost per taco = \( 2 - 1.5 = 0.5 \) dollars.

2. **Calculate the total number of tacos made:**
   - Each taco uses 0.25 pounds of beef.
   - Total tacos = Total beef ÷ Beef per taco = \( 100 \div 0.25 = 400 \) tacos.

3. **Calculate the total profit:**
   - Total profit = Profit per taco × Number of tacos = \( 0.5 \times 400 = 200 \) dollars.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** involves calculating total revenue and total cost separately, then subtracting to find the profit. This method is straightforward and provides a clear breakdown of revenue and costs, which is useful for understanding the overall financial picture.
- **Method 2** focuses on calculating the profit per taco first, then scaling it up to the total number of tacos. This method is more efficient for problems where the profit per unit is already known or easy to calculate.

Both methods are valid and yield the same result. However, **Method 2** is slightly more efficient because it avoids calculating total revenue and total cost separately.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 2**:
1. Profit per taco = \( 2 - 1.5 = 0.5 \) dollars.
2. Total tacos = \( 100 \div 0.25 = 400 \) tacos.
3. Total profit = \( 0.5 \times 400 = 200 \) dollars.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recheck the number of tacos: \( 100 \div 0.25 = 400 \) tacos. Correct.
- Recheck the profit per taco: \( 2 - 1.5 = 0.5 \) dollars. Correct.
- Recheck the total profit: \( 0.5 \times 400 = 200 \) dollars. Correct.
- The result is reasonable: The truck makes a small profit of $0.50 per taco, and with 400 tacos sold, the total profit is $200.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **200**.

Q: Susan is taking a two week vacation. She works five days a week and has six days of paid vacation. The rest of her workdays will be unpaid vacation time. She gets paid $15 per hour and works 8 hours a day. How much pay will she miss on her vacation?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Susan is taking a two-week vacation. A workweek consists of 5 days, so two weeks equal **10 workdays**.
- Susan has **6 days of paid vacation**, meaning she will not lose pay for those days.
- The remaining **4 workdays** will be unpaid vacation time.
- Susan earns **$15 per hour** and works **8 hours per day**.
- We assume there are no additional bonuses, deductions, or overtime pay.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate Susan's daily pay: Multiply her hourly wage by the hours she works per day.  
- Multiply her daily pay by the number of unpaid vacation days (4 days).  

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate Susan's total pay for 10 workdays (if she worked all 10 days).  
- Subtract the pay for the 6 paid vacation days from the total pay to find the pay she misses for the 4 unpaid days.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate Susan's daily pay:  
   \[
   \text{Daily pay} = \text{Hourly wage} \times \text{Hours per day} = 15 \times 8 = 120 \, \text{dollars/day}.
   \]
2. Multiply the daily pay by the number of unpaid vacation days:  
   \[
   \text{Missed pay} = \text{Daily pay} \times \text{Unpaid days} = 120 \times 4 = 480 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate Susan's total pay for 10 workdays (if she worked all 10 days):  
   \[
   \text{Total pay for 10 days} = \text{Daily pay} \times 10 = 120 \times 10 = 1200 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
2. Calculate the pay for the 6 paid vacation days:  
   \[
   \text{Pay for 6 paid days} = \text{Daily pay} \times 6 = 120 \times 6 = 720 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
3. Subtract the pay for the 6 paid days from the total pay for 10 days to find the missed pay:  
   \[
   \text{Missed pay} = \text{Total pay for 10 days} - \text{Pay for 6 paid days} = 1200 - 720 = 480 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it focuses only on the unpaid days, avoiding unnecessary calculations. It is less prone to errors since it involves fewer steps.  
- **Method 2** is more comprehensive, as it calculates the total pay for all 10 days and then subtracts the paid portion. However, it introduces extra steps that are not strictly necessary for solving the problem.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and clarity.  
- Using Method 1:  
  \[
  \text{Missed pay} = \text{Daily pay} \times \text{Unpaid days} = 120 \times 4 = 480 \, \text{dollars}.
  \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:  
  \[
  \text{Total pay for 10 days} = 1200, \quad \text{Pay for 6 paid days} = 720, \quad \text{Missed pay} = 1200 - 720 = 480 \, \text{dollars}.
  \]
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **480 dollars**.

480.0

480.0

Q: Before James starts the strength phase training of his cycle he has a powerlifting total of 2200 pounds at a bodyweight of 245 pounds.  He manages to gain 15% on his total and 8 pounds of body weight.  What is the ratio of his lifting total to bodyweight?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- James starts with a powerlifting total of **2200 pounds** and a bodyweight of **245 pounds**.
- He gains **15%** on his powerlifting total during the strength phase.
- He gains **8 pounds** of bodyweight during the strength phase.
- We are tasked with finding the **ratio of his lifting total to his bodyweight** after these changes.
- Assumptions:
  - The percentage increase in his lifting total is applied to the original total.
  - The ratio is expressed as a simple division of total weight lifted by bodyweight.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct calculation using percentage increase and addition.**
- Calculate the new lifting total by applying the 15% increase to the original total.
- Calculate the new bodyweight by adding the 8-pound increase to the original bodyweight.
- Divide the new lifting total by the new bodyweight to find the ratio.

**Method 2: Use proportional scaling.**
- Represent the lifting total as 100% and calculate the new total as 115% of the original.
- Represent the bodyweight as 100% and calculate the new bodyweight as 108% of the original.
- Divide the scaled lifting total by the scaled bodyweight to find the ratio.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation using percentage increase and addition.**

1. **Calculate the new lifting total:**
   - Original total = 2200 pounds.
   - Increase = 15% of 2200 = \( 2200 \times 0.15 = 330 \).
   - New total = \( 2200 + 330 = 2530 \) pounds.

2. **Calculate the new bodyweight:**
   - Original bodyweight = 245 pounds.
   - Increase = 8 pounds.
   - New bodyweight = \( 245 + 8 = 253 \) pounds.

3. **Calculate the ratio:**
   - Ratio = \( \frac{\text{New lifting total}}{\text{New bodyweight}} = \frac{2530}{253} \).
   - Perform the division: \( 2530 \div 253 = 10 \).

4. **Double-check calculations:**
   - Lifting total increase: \( 2200 \times 0.15 = 330 \), correct.
   - Bodyweight increase: \( 245 + 8 = 253 \), correct.
   - Division: \( 2530 \div 253 = 10 \), correct.

---

#### **Method 2: Use proportional scaling.**

1. **Scale the lifting total:**
   - Original total = 100%.
   - New total = \( 100\% + 15\% = 115\% \).
   - New total = \( 2200 \times 1.15 = 2530 \) pounds.

2. **Scale the bodyweight:**
   - Original bodyweight = 100%.
   - New bodyweight = \( 100\% + 8\% = 108\% \).
   - New bodyweight = \( 245 \times 1.08 = 253 \) pounds.

3. **Calculate the ratio:**
   - Ratio = \( \frac{\text{New lifting total}}{\text{New bodyweight}} = \frac{2530}{253} \).
   - Perform the division: \( 2530 \div 253 = 10 \).

4. **Double-check calculations:**
   - Scaling for lifting total: \( 2200 \times 1.15 = 2530 \), correct.
   - Scaling for bodyweight: \( 245 \times 1.08 = 253 \), correct.
   - Division: \( 2530 \div 253 = 10 \), correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is straightforward and uses direct addition and percentage calculations. It is easy to follow and minimizes intermediate steps.
- **Method 2** uses proportional scaling, which is conceptually elegant but introduces unnecessary complexity for this problem.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and intuitive for this context.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

Using **Method 1**:
1. New lifting total = \( 2200 + 330 = 2530 \) pounds.
2. New bodyweight = \( 245 + 8 = 253 \) pounds.
3. Ratio = \( \frac{2530}{253} = 10 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- The lifting total increased by 15%, which is reasonable given the original total.
- The bodyweight increased by 8 pounds, which is consistent with the problem statement.
- The ratio of 10 (lifting total to bodyweight) is plausible for a powerlifter.

---

The answer is **10**.

Q: John cuts his grass to 2 inches.  It grows .5 inches per month.  When it gets to 4 inches he cuts it back down to 2 inches.  It cost $100 to get his grass cut.  How much does he pay per year?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- John cuts his grass to 2 inches.
- The grass grows 0.5 inches per month.
- When the grass reaches 4 inches, he cuts it back down to 2 inches.
- It costs $100 to cut the grass each time.
- We are tasked with finding how much John pays per year (12 months).
- Assumptions:
  - Grass grows at a constant rate of 0.5 inches per month.
  - John cuts the grass immediately when it reaches 4 inches.
  - There are no additional costs or irregularities in the grass-cutting schedule.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Calculate the time it takes for the grass to grow from 2 inches to 4 inches.**
- Determine how many months it takes for the grass to grow 2 inches (from 2 inches to 4 inches).
- Use this time interval to calculate how many times the grass is cut in a year.
- Multiply the number of cuts by $100 to find the total yearly cost.

**Method 2: Calculate the number of cuts directly based on the yearly growth cycle.**
- Determine how many 2-inch growth cycles (from 2 inches to 4 inches) occur in 12 months.
- Multiply the number of cycles by $100 to find the total yearly cost.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Calculate the time it takes for the grass to grow from 2 inches to 4 inches.**
1. The grass grows 0.5 inches per month.
2. To grow from 2 inches to 4 inches, the grass must grow 2 inches.
   - Time required = Growth needed ÷ Growth rate = \( 2 \div 0.5 = 4 \) months.
3. Therefore, the grass is cut once every 4 months.
4. In a year (12 months), the number of cuts = \( 12 \div 4 = 3 \).
5. Total yearly cost = Number of cuts × Cost per cut = \( 3 \times 100 = 300 \).

**Check for pitfalls:**
- The calculation assumes the grass grows at a constant rate and is cut exactly when it reaches 4 inches. This is consistent with the problem's assumptions.

---

#### **Method 2: Calculate the number of cuts directly based on the yearly growth cycle.**
1. Each growth cycle (from 2 inches to 4 inches) takes 4 months, as calculated in Method 1.
2. In 12 months, the number of cycles = \( 12 \div 4 = 3 \).
3. Total yearly cost = Number of cycles × Cost per cut = \( 3 \times 100 = 300 \).

**Check for pitfalls:**
- This method directly uses the growth cycle duration, which was already calculated in Method 1. It avoids redundant steps and is straightforward.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** involves calculating the time for each growth cycle and then determining the number of cuts in a year. It is slightly more detailed but requires an extra step.
- **Method 2** skips the intermediate step of calculating the time for each growth cycle and directly determines the number of cycles in a year. It is more efficient and avoids redundancy.
- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but **Method 2** is simpler and more efficient.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 2** is the better method.
1. Each growth cycle (from 2 inches to 4 inches) takes 4 months.
2. In 12 months, the number of cycles = \( 12 \div 4 = 3 \).
3. Total yearly cost = Number of cycles × Cost per cut = \( 3 \times 100 = 300 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recheck the growth cycle duration: \( 2 \div 0.5 = 4 \) months.
- Recheck the number of cycles in a year: \( 12 \div 4 = 3 \).
- Recheck the total cost: \( 3 \times 100 = 300 \).
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

The answer is **300**.

Q: Michael’s largest watermelon weighs 8 pounds.  His neighbor, Clay, grew a watermelon three times that size.  And their mutual friend, John, grew a watermelon that was half the size of Clay’s.  How big was John’s watermelon?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Michael’s largest watermelon weighs **8 pounds**.
- Clay’s watermelon is **three times the size** of Michael’s watermelon.
- John’s watermelon is **half the size** of Clay’s watermelon.
- Assumptions:
  - All weights are in pounds.
  - The relationships between the watermelons (e.g., "three times" and "half the size") are exact and involve no rounding.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Sequential calculation using multiplication and division.**
- First, calculate the weight of Clay’s watermelon by multiplying Michael’s watermelon weight by 3.
- Then, calculate John’s watermelon weight by dividing Clay’s watermelon weight by 2.

**Method 2: Express John’s watermelon weight as a direct fraction of Michael’s.**
- Since Clay’s watermelon is 3 times Michael’s, and John’s is half of Clay’s, John’s watermelon is \( \frac{1}{2} \times 3 = \frac{3}{2} \) times Michael’s watermelon.
- Multiply Michael’s watermelon weight by \( \frac{3}{2} \) to find John’s watermelon weight directly.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation**
1. **Calculate Clay’s watermelon weight:**
   - Clay’s watermelon is 3 times Michael’s watermelon.
   - \( 8 \times 3 = 24 \) pounds.
   - Clay’s watermelon weighs **24 pounds**.

2. **Calculate John’s watermelon weight:**
   - John’s watermelon is half the size of Clay’s watermelon.
   - \( 24 \div 2 = 12 \) pounds.
   - John’s watermelon weighs **12 pounds**.

3. **Check for errors:**
   - Multiplying Michael’s weight by 3 gives Clay’s weight: \( 8 \times 3 = 24 \).
   - Dividing Clay’s weight by 2 gives John’s weight: \( 24 \div 2 = 12 \).
   - All calculations are consistent and logical.

#### **Method 2: Direct fraction calculation**
1. **Express John’s watermelon weight as a fraction of Michael’s:**
   - Clay’s watermelon is 3 times Michael’s, and John’s is half of Clay’s.
   - John’s watermelon is \( \frac{1}{2} \times 3 = \frac{3}{2} \) times Michael’s watermelon.

2. **Calculate John’s watermelon weight:**
   - Multiply Michael’s watermelon weight by \( \frac{3}{2} \):
   - \( 8 \times \frac{3}{2} = \frac{8 \times 3}{2} = \frac{24}{2} = 12 \) pounds.
   - John’s watermelon weighs **12 pounds**.

3. **Check for errors:**
   - The fraction \( \frac{3}{2} \) is correctly derived from the problem’s relationships.
   - Multiplying \( 8 \) by \( \frac{3}{2} \) gives \( 12 \), consistent with Method 1.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more intuitive and straightforward, as it breaks the problem into smaller, sequential steps (first finding Clay’s weight, then John’s).
- **Method 2** is more efficient, as it combines the relationships into a single calculation. However, it requires a stronger understanding of fractions and proportional reasoning, which might introduce errors for some.
- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is easier to follow for most people.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and simplicity.
1. Calculate Clay’s watermelon weight: \( 8 \times 3 = 24 \) pounds.
2. Calculate John’s watermelon weight: \( 24 \div 2 = 12 \) pounds.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Clay’s watermelon is 3 times Michael’s: \( 8 \times 3 = 24 \), correct.
- John’s watermelon is half of Clay’s: \( 24 \div 2 = 12 \), correct.
- The relationships and calculations are consistent with the problem’s description.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **12 pounds**.

Q: A warehouse store sells cartons of gum. Each carton contains 5 packs of gum, and there are 3 sticks of gum in each pack. Each brown box contains 4 cartons of gum. How many sticks of gum are there in 8 brown boxes?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Each carton contains **5 packs of gum**.
- Each pack contains **3 sticks of gum**.
- Each brown box contains **4 cartons of gum**.
- There are **8 brown boxes**.
- We assume all cartons, packs, and sticks of gum are complete and undamaged.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the number of sticks of gum in one carton: \( 5 \text{ packs} \times 3 \text{ sticks per pack} \).  
- Calculate the number of sticks of gum in one brown box: \( 4 \text{ cartons} \times \text{ (sticks per carton)} \).  
- Multiply the result by the number of brown boxes: \( 8 \text{ brown boxes} \times \text{ (sticks per brown box)} \).

**Method 2:**  
- Start with the total number of brown boxes (8).  
- Calculate the total number of cartons in 8 brown boxes: \( 8 \text{ brown boxes} \times 4 \text{ cartons per box} \).  
- Calculate the total number of packs in all cartons: \( \text{ (total cartons)} \times 5 \text{ packs per carton} \).  
- Calculate the total number of sticks of gum: \( \text{ (total packs)} \times 3 \text{ sticks per pack} \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the number of sticks of gum in one carton:  
   \( 5 \text{ packs} \times 3 \text{ sticks per pack} = 15 \text{ sticks per carton} \).  
2. Calculate the number of sticks of gum in one brown box:  
   \( 4 \text{ cartons per box} \times 15 \text{ sticks per carton} = 60 \text{ sticks per brown box} \).  
3. Calculate the total number of sticks of gum in 8 brown boxes:  
   \( 8 \text{ brown boxes} \times 60 \text{ sticks per brown box} = 480 \text{ sticks of gum} \).

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate the total number of cartons in 8 brown boxes:  
   \( 8 \text{ brown boxes} \times 4 \text{ cartons per box} = 32 \text{ cartons} \).  
2. Calculate the total number of packs in 32 cartons:  
   \( 32 \text{ cartons} \times 5 \text{ packs per carton} = 160 \text{ packs} \).  
3. Calculate the total number of sticks of gum in 160 packs:  
   \( 160 \text{ packs} \times 3 \text{ sticks per pack} = 480 \text{ sticks of gum} \).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct because it calculates the number of sticks of gum per carton and per brown box before scaling up to 8 brown boxes. This reduces the number of intermediate steps.  
- **Method 2** breaks the problem into smaller, more granular steps, which may be helpful for someone who prefers to work incrementally. However, it involves more calculations and is less efficient.  
- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and easier to follow.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Sticks of gum per carton: \( 5 \times 3 = 15 \).  
2. Sticks of gum per brown box: \( 4 \times 15 = 60 \).  
3. Total sticks of gum in 8 brown boxes: \( 8 \times 60 = 480 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using **Method 2**:  
  1. Total cartons: \( 8 \times 4 = 32 \).  
  2. Total packs: \( 32 \times 5 = 160 \).  
  3. Total sticks: \( 160 \times 3 = 480 \).  
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the calculations are correct.  
- The result is reasonable given the problem's context.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **480**.

Q: Sofia went to the department store to buy a pair of shoes and 2 shirts. A shirt costs $7 while a pair of shoes is $3 more than the shirt. If she decides to buy a bag which costs half the total price of the 2 shirts and a pair of shoes, how much will she pay for all those items?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Sofia is buying:
  - 2 shirts, each costing $7.
  - A pair of shoes, which costs $3 more than a shirt. Therefore, the price of the shoes is \( 7 + 3 = 10 \).
  - A bag, which costs half the total price of the 2 shirts and the pair of shoes combined.
- We assume all prices are in dollars, there are no taxes or discounts, and all calculations are exact.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct calculation of the total cost.**
1. Calculate the cost of the 2 shirts.
2. Add the cost of the shoes to the total.
3. Calculate the cost of the bag as half the total price of the shirts and shoes.
4. Add the cost of the bag to the total.

**Method 2: Use algebra to represent the total cost.**
1. Let the cost of a shirt be \( S = 7 \), and the cost of the shoes be \( S + 3 = 10 \).
2. Write an expression for the total cost of the shirts and shoes.
3. Use the expression to calculate the cost of the bag as half the total price of the shirts and shoes.
4. Write a final expression for the total cost and solve.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of the total cost**
1. **Cost of 2 shirts:**
   \[
   2 \times 7 = 14
   \]
2. **Cost of the shoes:**
   \[
   7 + 3 = 10
   \]
3. **Total cost of the shirts and shoes:**
   \[
   14 + 10 = 24
   \]
4. **Cost of the bag:**
   \[
   \frac{1}{2} \times 24 = 12
   \]
5. **Total cost of all items:**
   \[
   24 + 12 = 36
   \]

#### **Method 2: Use algebra to represent the total cost**
1. Let the cost of a shirt be \( S = 7 \), and the cost of the shoes be \( S + 3 = 10 \).
2. Write an expression for the total cost of the shirts and shoes:
   \[
   2S + (S + 3) = 2(7) + (7 + 3) = 14 + 10 = 24
   \]
3. Write an expression for the cost of the bag:
   \[
   \text{Cost of bag} = \frac{1}{2} \times (2S + (S + 3)) = \frac{1}{2} \times 24 = 12
   \]
4. Write an expression for the total cost of all items:
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = (2S + (S + 3)) + \frac{1}{2} \times (2S + (S + 3)) = 24 + 12 = 36
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and involves direct calculations, making it easier to follow for most people. It is efficient and avoids unnecessary abstraction.
- **Method 2** uses algebra, which is more generalizable and can handle more complex variations of the problem. However, it introduces additional steps and may be less intuitive for simpler problems like this one.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is better for this problem due to its simplicity and clarity.
- Using Method 1:
  1. Cost of 2 shirts: \( 2 \times 7 = 14 \).
  2. Cost of the shoes: \( 7 + 3 = 10 \).
  3. Total cost of shirts and shoes: \( 14 + 10 = 24 \).
  4. Cost of the bag: \( \frac{1}{2} \times 24 = 12 \).
  5. Total cost of all items: \( 24 + 12 = 36 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate each step:
  - Shirts: \( 2 \times 7 = 14 \).
  - Shoes: \( 7 + 3 = 10 \).
  - Shirts + Shoes: \( 14 + 10 = 24 \).
  - Bag: \( \frac{1}{2} \times 24 = 12 \).
  - Total: \( 24 + 12 = 36 \).
- All calculations are correct, and the result is reasonable.

The answer is **36**.

Q: When all her clothes are washed and in her closet, Brie has 12 blouses, 6 skirts, and 8 slacks.  Today in her hamper, she has 75% of her blouses, 50% of her skirts, and 25% of her slacks.  How many pieces of clothing will she need to put in the washer?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Brie owns 12 blouses, 6 skirts, and 8 slacks when all her clothes are clean and in her closet.
- Today, in her hamper:
  - 75% of her blouses are dirty.
  - 50% of her skirts are dirty.
  - 25% of her slacks are dirty.
- We assume that the percentages given are accurate and apply to the total number of each type of clothing.
- We are tasked with finding the total number of clothing items in her hamper, which will need to be washed.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Calculate the number of dirty items for each type of clothing directly using percentages.**
- Multiply the total number of each type of clothing by the percentage that is dirty.
- Add the results for blouses, skirts, and slacks to find the total number of dirty items.

**Method 2: Calculate the number of clean items first, then subtract from the total for each type of clothing.**
- Multiply the total number of each type of clothing by the percentage that is clean (100% - dirty percentage).
- Subtract the clean items from the total to find the dirty items for each type.
- Add the results for all types of clothing to find the total number of dirty items.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of dirty items**
1. **Blouses**: Brie has 12 blouses, and 75% are dirty.
   - \( 12 \times 0.75 = 9 \) dirty blouses.
2. **Skirts**: Brie has 6 skirts, and 50% are dirty.
   - \( 6 \times 0.50 = 3 \) dirty skirts.
3. **Slacks**: Brie has 8 slacks, and 25% are dirty.
   - \( 8 \times 0.25 = 2 \) dirty slacks.
4. Add the dirty items together:
   - \( 9 + 3 + 2 = 14 \).

#### **Method 2: Calculate clean items first, then subtract**
1. **Blouses**: Brie has 12 blouses, and 25% are clean (100% - 75% dirty).
   - \( 12 \times 0.25 = 3 \) clean blouses.
   - Dirty blouses: \( 12 - 3 = 9 \).
2. **Skirts**: Brie has 6 skirts, and 50% are clean (100% - 50% dirty).
   - \( 6 \times 0.50 = 3 \) clean skirts.
   - Dirty skirts: \( 6 - 3 = 3 \).
3. **Slacks**: Brie has 8 slacks, and 75% are clean (100% - 25% dirty).
   - \( 8 \times 0.75 = 6 \) clean slacks.
   - Dirty slacks: \( 8 - 6 = 2 \).
4. Add the dirty items together:
   - \( 9 + 3 + 2 = 14 \).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct because it calculates the dirty items immediately using the given percentages. It involves fewer steps and is less prone to error.
- **Method 2** introduces an extra step of calculating clean items first, which is unnecessary for this problem. While it still works, it is less efficient and introduces more opportunities for mistakes.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and efficiency.
1. Calculate dirty blouses: \( 12 \times 0.75 = 9 \).
2. Calculate dirty skirts: \( 6 \times 0.50 = 3 \).
3. Calculate dirty slacks: \( 8 \times 0.25 = 2 \).
4. Add the dirty items together: \( 9 + 3 + 2 = 14 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:
  - Dirty blouses: \( 12 - (12 \times 0.25) = 12 - 3 = 9 \).
  - Dirty skirts: \( 6 - (6 \times 0.50) = 6 - 3 = 3 \).
  - Dirty slacks: \( 8 - (8 \times 0.75) = 8 - 6 = 2 \).
  - Total: \( 9 + 3 + 2 = 14 \).
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

---

The answer is **14**.

Q: Jake can wash his car with 1 bottle of car wash soap 4 times.  If each bottle costs $4.00, and he washes his car once a week for 20 weeks, how much does he spend on car soap?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jake washes his car once a week for 20 weeks.
- Each bottle of car wash soap costs $4.00.
- One bottle of soap can be used to wash the car 4 times.
- We assume Jake uses the soap efficiently and does not waste it.
- We need to calculate the total cost of soap for 20 weeks of car washing.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
Calculate how many washes Jake performs in 20 weeks, determine how many bottles of soap are needed, and multiply the number of bottles by the cost per bottle.

**Method 2:**  
Calculate the cost per wash by dividing the cost of one bottle by the number of washes it provides, then multiply the cost per wash by the total number of washes Jake performs in 20 weeks.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Jake washes his car once a week for 20 weeks, so he performs **20 washes**.
2. Each bottle of soap can wash the car **4 times**, so the number of bottles Jake needs is:  
   \[
   \text{Number of bottles} = \frac{\text{Total washes}}{\text{Washes per bottle}} = \frac{20}{4} = 5 \text{ bottles}.
   \]
3. Each bottle costs $4.00, so the total cost is:  
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = \text{Number of bottles} \times \text{Cost per bottle} = 5 \times 4 = 20 \text{ dollars}.
   \]

**Check for pitfalls:**  
- Ensure the division of washes by washes per bottle is correct.  
- Confirm the multiplication of bottles by cost per bottle is accurate.  

#### **Method 2:**
1. The cost per wash is calculated by dividing the cost of one bottle by the number of washes it provides:  
   \[
   \text{Cost per wash} = \frac{\text{Cost per bottle}}{\text{Washes per bottle}} = \frac{4}{4} = 1 \text{ dollar per wash}.
   \]
2. Jake performs **20 washes**, so the total cost is:  
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = \text{Cost per wash} \times \text{Total washes} = 1 \times 20 = 20 \text{ dollars}.
   \]

**Check for pitfalls:**  
- Ensure the division of cost per bottle by washes per bottle is correct.  
- Confirm the multiplication of cost per wash by total washes is accurate.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is straightforward and focuses on calculating the number of bottles needed, which is intuitive for problems involving discrete items like bottles.  
- **Method 2** is slightly more abstract, as it calculates the cost per wash first, but it is equally reliable and efficient.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is slightly clearer for this context because it directly relates to the number of bottles Jake needs.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

**Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and directness.  
1. Jake performs **20 washes**.  
2. Each bottle can wash the car **4 times**, so Jake needs:  
   \[
   \frac{20}{4} = 5 \text{ bottles}.
   \]
3. Each bottle costs $4.00, so the total cost is:  
   \[
   5 \times 4 = 20 \text{ dollars}.
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Verify the number of washes (20 weeks × 1 wash per week = 20 washes).  
- Confirm the number of bottles needed (20 washes ÷ 4 washes per bottle = 5 bottles).  
- Confirm the total cost (5 bottles × $4.00 per bottle = $20.00).  

The calculations are correct, and the result is reasonable.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **20 dollars**.

Q: Itzayana is 4 inches taller than Zora, who is 8 inches shorter than Brixton. If Zara is 64 inches tall and has the same height as Brixton, calculate the average height of the four people.
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Zora is 8 inches shorter than Brixton.
- Brixton is 64 inches tall (same height as Zara).
- Itzayana is 4 inches taller than Zora.
- We are tasked with calculating the average height of the four people: Brixton, Zara, Zora, and Itzayana.
- Assumptions:
  - Heights are measured in inches.
  - No additional information about the individuals is relevant to the calculation.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
- Calculate Zora's height by subtracting 8 inches from Brixton's height.  
- Calculate Itzayana's height by adding 4 inches to Zora's height.  
- Add the heights of all four individuals and divide by 4 to find the average.

**Method 2:**  
- Represent the heights algebraically:  
  - Brixton = Zara = 64 inches.  
  - Zora = Brixton - 8.  
  - Itzayana = Zora + 4.  
- Substitute values and calculate the average directly using the formula for the mean.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Direct Calculation**
1. **Calculate Zora's height:**  
   Brixton = 64 inches.  
   Zora = Brixton - 8 = 64 - 8 = **56 inches**.

2. **Calculate Itzayana's height:**  
   Itzayana = Zora + 4 = 56 + 4 = **60 inches**.

3. **Add all heights:**  
   Brixton = 64 inches, Zara = 64 inches, Zora = 56 inches, Itzayana = 60 inches.  
   Total height = 64 + 64 + 56 + 60 = **244 inches**.

4. **Calculate the average height:**  
   Average = Total height ÷ Number of people = 244 ÷ 4 = **61 inches**.

---

#### **Method 2: Algebraic Representation**
1. **Represent heights algebraically:**  
   Brixton = Zara = 64 inches.  
   Zora = Brixton - 8 = 64 - 8 = **56 inches**.  
   Itzayana = Zora + 4 = 56 + 4 = **60 inches**.

2. **Substitute values into the average formula:**  
   Average = (Brixton + Zara + Zora + Itzayana) ÷ 4.  
   Average = (64 + 64 + 56 + 60) ÷ 4 = **244 ÷ 4 = 61 inches**.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is straightforward and intuitive, breaking the problem into smaller steps. It is easy to follow and minimizes the risk of errors.  
- **Method 2** uses algebraic representation, which is efficient for larger or more complex problems but may be less intuitive for simple arithmetic tasks.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better suited for this problem due to its clarity and step-by-step approach.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

**Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and reliability.  
1. Calculate Zora's height: 64 - 8 = 56 inches.  
2. Calculate Itzayana's height: 56 + 4 = 60 inches.  
3. Add all heights: 64 + 64 + 56 + 60 = 244 inches.  
4. Calculate the average: 244 ÷ 4 = 61 inches.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Verify each calculation:  
  - Zora = 64 - 8 = 56 inches.  
  - Itzayana = 56 + 4 = 60 inches.  
  - Total height = 64 + 64 + 56 + 60 = 244 inches.  
  - Average = 244 ÷ 4 = 61 inches.  

- The result is reasonable given the heights provided.

---

The answer is **61 inches**.

3. Double-check:

- This method is less prone to errors because it separates the steps, making it easier to verify each calculation.

- **Method 2** is more methodical and easier to verify, as it explicitly separates the multiplication and subtraction steps. This makes it more reliable for complex problems or when double-checking is important.

- **Method 2** is chosen for its clarity and reliability.

Q: John assembles computers and sells prebuilt computers as a business. The parts for the computer cost $800.  He sells the computers for 1.4 times the value of the components.  He manages to build 60 computers a month.  He has to pay $5000 a month in rent and another $3000 in non-rent extra expenses a month.  How much profit does he make a month?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

- **Cost of computer parts:** $800 per computer.  
- **Selling price:** 1.4 times the cost of the components, i.e., \( 1.4 \times 800 \).  
- **Number of computers built and sold per month:** 60.  
- **Monthly rent:** $5000.  
- **Monthly non-rent expenses:** $3000.  
- **Profit formula:** Profit = Total revenue - Total costs (including component costs, rent, and other expenses).  
- **Assumptions:** All computers are sold, and there are no additional costs or discounts.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.

**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total revenue and total costs separately, then subtract costs from revenue to find profit.  
1. Compute the selling price per computer.  
2. Multiply the selling price by the number of computers sold to find total revenue.  
3. Compute total costs (component costs + rent + non-rent expenses).  
4. Subtract total costs from total revenue to find profit.

**Method 2:**  
Break the problem into smaller steps by calculating the profit per computer first, then scale it up for 60 computers, and finally subtract fixed monthly expenses.  
1. Compute the profit per computer (selling price - component cost).  
2. Multiply the profit per computer by the number of computers sold to find total profit from sales.  
3. Subtract fixed monthly expenses (rent + non-rent expenses) from the total profit.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Total revenue minus total costs**

1. **Selling price per computer:**  
   \[
   \text{Selling price} = 1.4 \times 800 = 1120
   \]

2. **Total revenue:**  
   \[
   \text{Total revenue} = \text{Selling price} \times \text{Number of computers} = 1120 \times 60 = 67200
   \]

3. **Total component costs:**  
   \[
   \text{Component costs} = \text{Cost per computer} \times \text{Number of computers} = 800 \times 60 = 48000
   \]

4. **Total fixed expenses:**  
   \[
   \text{Fixed expenses} = \text{Rent} + \text{Non-rent expenses} = 5000 + 3000 = 8000
   \]

5. **Total costs:**  
   \[
   \text{Total costs} = \text{Component costs} + \text{Fixed expenses} = 48000 + 8000 = 56000
   \]

6. **Profit:**  
   \[
   \text{Profit} = \text{Total revenue} - \text{Total costs} = 67200 - 56000 = 11200
   \]

---

#### **Method 2: Profit per computer scaled up**

1. **Profit per computer:**  
   \[
   \text{Profit per computer} = \text{Selling price} - \text{Component cost} = 1120 - 800 = 320
   \]

2. **Total profit from sales:**  
   \[
   \text{Total profit from sales} = \text{Profit per computer} \times \text{Number of computers} = 320 \times 60 = 19200
   \]

3. **Subtract fixed monthly expenses:**  
   \[
   \text{Profit} = \text{Total profit from sales} - \text{Fixed expenses} = 19200 - 8000 = 11200
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1:**  
  This method is more comprehensive, as it explicitly calculates total revenue and total costs separately. It is useful for understanding the breakdown of expenses and revenue but involves more intermediate steps.

- **Method 2:**  
  This method is more efficient, as it focuses on the profit per computer and scales it up. It is simpler and faster but assumes the reader understands the concept of profit per unit.

**Conclusion:** Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 2** is more efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

Using **Method 2**:  
1. Selling price per computer = \( 1.4 \times 800 = 1120 \).  
2. Profit per computer = \( 1120 - 800 = 320 \).  
3. Total profit from sales = \( 320 \times 60 = 19200 \).  
4. Subtract fixed expenses = \( 19200 - 8000 = 11200 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.

- **Recheck calculations:**  
  Selling price = \( 1120 \), profit per computer = \( 320 \), total profit from sales = \( 19200 \), fixed expenses = \( 8000 \).  
  Final profit = \( 19200 - 8000 = 11200 \).  

- **Verify reasonableness:**  
  The profit is positive, and the calculations align with the given data.

---

The answer is **11200**.

Q: Lizzy had $30. She loaned out $15 to her friend. How much will Lizzy have if her friend returned the money with an interest of 20%?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Lizzy initially had $30.
- She loaned $15 to her friend.
- Her friend returned the $15 with an interest of 20%.
- We assume the interest is calculated as 20% of the loaned amount ($15).
- We are tasked with finding how much Lizzy will have after her friend repays the loan with interest.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct calculation of interest and total repayment.**
- Calculate the interest as 20% of $15.
- Add the interest to the original loan amount to find the total repayment.
- Add the repayment to Lizzy's remaining money after the loan to find her total.

**Method 2: Use proportional reasoning.**
- Recognize that the total repayment is 120% of the loaned amount (100% principal + 20% interest).
- Calculate 120% of $15 directly.
- Add the repayment to Lizzy's remaining money after the loan to find her total.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of interest and total repayment**
1. **Calculate the interest:**  
   Interest = 20% of $15 = \( 15 \times 0.20 = 3 \).  
   So, the interest is $3.

2. **Calculate the total repayment:**  
   Total repayment = Loan amount + Interest = \( 15 + 3 = 18 \).  
   So, her friend repays $18.

3. **Add the repayment to Lizzy's remaining money:**  
   Lizzy had $30 initially but loaned $15, leaving her with \( 30 - 15 = 15 \).  
   After receiving the repayment, Lizzy's total is \( 15 + 18 = 33 \).

4. **Check for errors or unreasonable results:**  
   The calculations are straightforward, and the result is reasonable. Lizzy ends up with more money than she started with due to the interest.

#### **Method 2: Use proportional reasoning**
1. **Calculate 120% of the loaned amount:**  
   Total repayment = 120% of $15 = \( 15 \times 1.20 = 18 \).  
   So, her friend repays $18.

2. **Add the repayment to Lizzy's remaining money:**  
   Lizzy had $30 initially but loaned $15, leaving her with \( 30 - 15 = 15 \).  
   After receiving the repayment, Lizzy's total is \( 15 + 18 = 33 \).

3. **Check for errors or unreasonable results:**  
   The calculations are consistent with Method 1, and the result is reasonable.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Clarity:** Both methods are clear, but Method 2 is slightly more concise because it combines the interest and principal into a single step using proportional reasoning.
- **Reliability:** Both methods are reliable, as they use standard arithmetic operations.
- **Efficiency:** Method 2 is more efficient because it skips the intermediate step of separately calculating the interest.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 2** is better due to its efficiency and simplicity.  
1. Calculate 120% of $15:  
   \( 15 \times 1.20 = 18 \).  
   So, her friend repays $18.

2. Add the repayment to Lizzy's remaining money:  
   Lizzy had \( 30 - 15 = 15 \) after loaning the money.  
   After receiving the repayment, her total is \( 15 + 18 = 33 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Using Method 1, we found the same result: \( 15 + 18 = 33 \).  
- The result is reasonable because Lizzy ends up with more money than she started with due to the interest.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **33**.

Q: A box of chocolate bars was shared equally between Mike, Rita and Anita. If the box contains 12 bars, how many bars did Mike and Rita get (combined)?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The box contains **12 chocolate bars**.
- The bars are shared **equally** between **Mike, Rita, and Anita**.
- We are tasked with finding the **combined number of bars Mike and Rita received**.
- Assumptions:
  - The division is exact, with no leftover bars.
  - Each person receives the same number of bars.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Divide the total number of bars (12) by the number of people (3) to find how many bars each person gets.  
- Add the number of bars Mike and Rita received to find their combined total.

**Method 2:**  
- First, calculate the fraction of the total bars that Mike and Rita together receive. Since they are two out of three people, they receive \( \frac{2}{3} \) of the total bars.  
- Multiply \( \frac{2}{3} \) by the total number of bars (12) to find their combined total.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Divide the total number of bars by the number of people:  
   \[
   \text{Bars per person} = \frac{12}{3} = 4
   \]
   - Each person (Mike, Rita, and Anita) gets 4 bars.
2. Add the number of bars Mike and Rita received:  
   \[
   \text{Combined bars for Mike and Rita} = 4 + 4 = 8
   \]
3. Double-check:  
   - If each person gets 4 bars, the total is \( 4 + 4 + 4 = 12 \), which matches the original total.  
   - The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate the fraction of the total bars that Mike and Rita together receive:  
   \[
   \text{Fraction of bars for Mike and Rita} = \frac{2}{3}
   \]
2. Multiply this fraction by the total number of bars:  
   \[
   \text{Combined bars for Mike and Rita} = \frac{2}{3} \times 12 = 8
   \]
3. Double-check:  
   - \( \frac{2}{3} \times 12 = \frac{24}{3} = 8 \), which is consistent.  
   - The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and intuitive, as it directly calculates the number of bars per person and then adds the results for Mike and Rita.  
- **Method 2** is slightly more abstract, involving fractions, but it is equally valid and efficient.  
- Both methods are reliable, but **Method 1** is easier to understand and less prone to errors for most people.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and clarity.  
1. Divide the total number of bars by the number of people:  
   \[
   \text{Bars per person} = \frac{12}{3} = 4
   \]
2. Add the number of bars Mike and Rita received:  
   \[
   \text{Combined bars for Mike and Rita} = 4 + 4 = 8
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- The result is reasonable and error-free.
