Q: Brad wanted to set up a lemonade stand to earn some money.  Every gallon of lemonade would yield 16 glasses.  He figured it would cost him $3.50 to make every gallon of lemonade.  He made 2 gallons to sell and decided he would sell each glass for $1.00  He drank 5 glasses while sitting in his stand.  He sold all but 6 glasses of lemonade.  How much net profit did Brad earn?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Each gallon of lemonade yields **16 glasses**.
- Brad made **2 gallons** of lemonade.
- The cost to make each gallon is **$3.50**.
- Brad drank **5 glasses** of lemonade.
- He sold all but **6 glasses** of lemonade.
- Each glass of lemonade is sold for **$1.00**.
- Assumptions:
  - The cost of making lemonade is the only expense.
  - Brad drank 5 glasses without selling them, so they do not contribute to revenue.
  - The remaining 6 glasses were not sold and do not contribute to revenue.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Calculate total costs, total revenue, and subtract costs from revenue.
1. Compute the total cost of making 2 gallons of lemonade.
2. Determine the total number of glasses made from 2 gallons.
3. Subtract the 5 glasses Brad drank and the 6 glasses left unsold to find the number of glasses sold.
4. Calculate the revenue from selling the glasses.
5. Subtract the total cost from the revenue to find the net profit.

#### Method 2: Break the problem into smaller parts and calculate profit per glass.
1. Compute the cost per glass by dividing the cost per gallon by the number of glasses per gallon.
2. Determine the number of glasses sold by subtracting the glasses Brad drank and the unsold glasses from the total glasses made.
3. Calculate the profit per glass (selling price minus cost per glass).
4. Multiply the profit per glass by the number of glasses sold to find the total net profit.

### Step 3: Work through both methods in detail.
#### Method 1: Total costs and revenue approach.
1. **Total cost of making lemonade**:
   - Cost per gallon = $3.50.
   - Total gallons = 2.
   - Total cost = $3.50 × 2 = **$7.00**.

2. **Total number of glasses made**:
   - Glasses per gallon = 16.
   - Total glasses = 16 × 2 = **32 glasses**.

3. **Number of glasses sold**:
   - Total glasses made = 32.
   - Brad drank 5 glasses, so remaining glasses = 32 - 5 = 27.
   - Unsold glasses = 6.
   - Glasses sold = 27 - 6 = **21 glasses**.

4. **Revenue from selling glasses**:
   - Selling price per glass = $1.00.
   - Revenue = 21 × $1.00 = **$21.00**.

5. **Net profit**:
   - Net profit = Revenue - Total cost.
   - Net profit = $21.00 - $7.00 = **$14.00**.

#### Method 2: Profit per glass approach.
1. **Cost per glass**:
   - Cost per gallon = $3.50.
   - Glasses per gallon = 16.
   - Cost per glass = $3.50 ÷ 16 = **$0.21875 per glass**.

2. **Number of glasses sold**:
   - Total glasses made = 32.
   - Brad drank 5 glasses, so remaining glasses = 32 - 5 = 27.
   - Unsold glasses = 6.
   - Glasses sold = 27 - 6 = **21 glasses**.

3. **Profit per glass**:
   - Selling price per glass = $1.00.
   - Profit per glass = Selling price - Cost per glass.
   - Profit per glass = $1.00 - $0.21875 = **$0.78125 per glass**.

4. **Total net profit**:
   - Total net profit = Profit per glass × Number of glasses sold.
   - Total net profit = $0.78125 × 21 = **$14.00**.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and intuitive, as it directly calculates total costs and revenue before subtracting to find net profit.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and precise, as it breaks the problem into smaller parts and calculates profit per glass. However, it involves more intermediate steps and decimal calculations, which may introduce rounding errors.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and easier to follow.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- Using **Method 1**:
  - Total cost = $7.00.
  - Total revenue = $21.00.
  - Net profit = $21.00 - $7.00 = **$14.00**.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.
- Recalculate using both methods:
  - Method 1: $21.00 - $7.00 = $14.00.
  - Method 2: $0.78125 × 21 = $14.00.
- Both methods confirm the result is correct.

The answer is **$14.00**.

Q: Sammy has 8 gifts to wrap. Each gift requires 1.5 meters of ribbon. Unfortunately, she has no available ribbon so Tom let her use his 15-meter long ribbon. How many meters of ribbon will be left from Tom's ribbon?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Sammy has 8 gifts to wrap.
- Each gift requires **1.5 meters of ribbon**.
- Sammy has no ribbon, so Tom lets her use his ribbon, which is **15 meters long**.
- We assume that all ribbon is used efficiently with no waste or leftover scraps.
- The goal is to determine how much ribbon will remain after Sammy wraps all 8 gifts.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Total ribbon required vs. available ribbon.**
- Calculate the total ribbon Sammy needs by multiplying the number of gifts by the ribbon required per gift: \( 8 \times 1.5 \).
- Subtract the total ribbon used from the 15 meters Tom provided: \( 15 - \text{(total ribbon used)} \).

**Method 2: Iterative subtraction.**
- Start with the total ribbon Tom has (15 meters).
- Subtract 1.5 meters for each gift, one at a time, until all 8 gifts are accounted for.
- The remaining ribbon after 8 subtractions will be the answer.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Total ribbon required vs. available ribbon**
1. Calculate the total ribbon needed:
   \[
   \text{Total ribbon needed} = 8 \times 1.5 = 12 \, \text{meters}.
   \]
2. Subtract the total ribbon used from the ribbon Tom provided:
   \[
   \text{Remaining ribbon} = 15 - 12 = 3 \, \text{meters}.
   \]
3. Double-check:
   - If Sammy uses 12 meters, and Tom started with 15 meters, the leftover ribbon should indeed be \( 15 - 12 = 3 \, \text{meters} \).

**Potential pitfalls:** Miscalculating the total ribbon needed (e.g., forgetting to multiply correctly) or subtracting incorrectly.

---

#### **Method 2: Iterative subtraction**
1. Start with 15 meters of ribbon.
2. Subtract 1.5 meters for each gift:
   - After 1 gift: \( 15 - 1.5 = 13.5 \, \text{meters} \),
   - After 2 gifts: \( 13.5 - 1.5 = 12 \, \text{meters} \),
   - After 3 gifts: \( 12 - 1.5 = 10.5 \, \text{meters} \),
   - After 4 gifts: \( 10.5 - 1.5 = 9 \, \text{meters} \),
   - After 5 gifts: \( 9 - 1.5 = 7.5 \, \text{meters} \),
   - After 6 gifts: \( 7.5 - 1.5 = 6 \, \text{meters} \),
   - After 7 gifts: \( 6 - 1.5 = 4.5 \, \text{meters} \),
   - After 8 gifts: \( 4.5 - 1.5 = 3 \, \text{meters} \).
3. The remaining ribbon is \( 3 \, \text{meters} \).

**Potential pitfalls:** This method is more prone to arithmetic errors due to repeated subtraction, especially if done mentally or without careful tracking.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more efficient and straightforward because it involves fewer steps: a single multiplication and subtraction. It is less prone to errors and easier to verify.
- **Method 2** is more tedious and time-consuming, as it requires multiple subtractions. While it can be useful for visualizing the process, it is less practical for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

**Method 1 is better.**
1. Calculate the total ribbon needed:
   \[
   8 \times 1.5 = 12 \, \text{meters}.
   \]
2. Subtract the total ribbon used from the ribbon Tom provided:
   \[
   15 - 12 = 3 \, \text{meters}.
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

  \[
  8 \times 1.5 = 12 \, \text{meters}.
  \]
- Verify the subtraction:
  \[
  15 - 12 = 3 \, \text{meters}.

Q: A father is buying sand to fill his son's new sandbox, but he is worried that the sand will be too heavy for his car.  The sandbox is square, with each side being 40 inches long.  If a 30-pound bag of sand is enough to fill 80 square inches of the sandbox to an adequate depth, how many pounds of sand are needed to fill the box completely?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The sandbox is square, with each side measuring 40 inches.
- The area of the sandbox is the square of its side length.
- A 30-pound bag of sand fills 80 square inches of the sandbox to an adequate depth.
- We assume the sandbox is filled uniformly to the same depth.
- We need to calculate the total weight of sand required to fill the sandbox completely.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total area of the sandbox by squaring the side length.  
2. Determine how many 80-square-inch portions are in the total area by dividing the total area by 80.  
3. Multiply the number of portions by 30 pounds (the weight of one bag of sand) to find the total weight of sand needed.

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the weight of sand required per square inch by dividing 30 pounds by 80 square inches.  
2. Multiply the weight per square inch by the total area of the sandbox to find the total weight of sand needed.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. **Calculate the total area of the sandbox:**  
   The sandbox is square, so the area is given by:  
   \[
   \text{Area} = \text{side}^2 = 40 \times 40 = 1600 \, \text{square inches}.
   \]

2. **Determine how many 80-square-inch portions are in the total area:**  
   Divide the total area by 80 to find the number of portions:  
   \[
   \text{Number of portions} = \frac{\text{Total area}}{\text{Area per bag}} = \frac{1600}{80} = 20.
   \]

3. **Multiply the number of portions by the weight of one bag:**  
   Each portion requires 30 pounds of sand, so the total weight is:  
   \[
   \text{Total weight} = \text{Number of portions} \times \text{Weight per bag} = 20 \times 30 = 600 \, \text{pounds}.
   \]

#### **Method 2:**
1. **Calculate the weight of sand required per square inch:**  
   Each 30-pound bag fills 80 square inches, so the weight per square inch is:  
   \[
   \text{Weight per square inch} = \frac{\text{Weight per bag}}{\text{Area per bag}} = \frac{30}{80} = 0.375 \, \text{pounds per square inch}.
   \]

2. **Multiply the weight per square inch by the total area of the sandbox:**  
   The total area of the sandbox is 1600 square inches, so the total weight is:  
   \[
   \text{Total weight} = \text{Weight per square inch} \times \text{Total area} = 0.375 \times 1600 = 600 \, \text{pounds}.
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and intuitive, as it directly calculates the number of bags needed and multiplies by the weight per bag. It avoids intermediate fractional calculations, reducing the chance of rounding errors.
- **Method 2** involves calculating the weight per square inch, which introduces a fractional value (0.375). While this method is mathematically valid, it is slightly less intuitive and more prone to rounding errors if not handled carefully.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its clarity and simplicity.  
- Using Method 1:  
  1. Total area = \( 40 \times 40 = 1600 \, \text{square inches} \).  
  2. Number of portions = \( \frac{1600}{80} = 20 \).  
  3. Total weight = \( 20 \times 30 = 600 \, \text{pounds} \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:  
  1. Weight per square inch = \( \frac{30}{80} = 0.375 \, \text{pounds per square inch} \).  
  2. Total weight = \( 0.375 \times 1600 = 600 \, \text{pounds} \).  
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **600 pounds**.
Extracted:
600.0
A:
600.0

Q: Dolly wants to ride the Ferris wheel twice, the roller coaster three times, and the log ride seven times. The Ferris wheel costs 2 tickets, the roller coaster costs 5 tickets and the log ride costs 1 ticket. Dolly has 20 tickets. How many more tickets should Dolly buy?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Dolly wants to ride the Ferris wheel twice, the roller coaster three times, and the log ride seven times.
- The Ferris wheel costs 2 tickets per ride, the roller coaster costs 5 tickets per ride, and the log ride costs 1 ticket per ride.
- Dolly has 20 tickets.
- We assume Dolly needs exactly enough tickets to complete her planned rides and cannot partially ride any attraction.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Calculate the total number of tickets required for all rides, subtract the tickets Dolly already has, and determine how many more tickets she needs.  
**Method 2:** Break down the ticket requirements for each ride type, sum them incrementally, and compare the total to the tickets Dolly has to find the shortfall.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of total tickets needed.**
1. Calculate the tickets required for each ride type:
   - Ferris wheel: 2 rides × 2 tickets per ride = 4 tickets.
   - Roller coaster: 3 rides × 5 tickets per ride = 15 tickets.
   - Log ride: 7 rides × 1 ticket per ride = 7 tickets.
2. Add the tickets required for all rides:
   - Total tickets needed = 4 + 15 + 7 = 26 tickets.
3. Subtract the tickets Dolly already has:
   - Tickets needed = Total tickets needed - Tickets Dolly has = 26 - 20 = 6 tickets.

#### **Method 2: Incremental summation of ticket requirements.**
1. Start with the tickets Dolly has: 20 tickets.
2. Calculate the tickets required for each ride type:
   - Ferris wheel: 4 tickets.
   - Roller coaster: 15 tickets.
   - Log ride: 7 tickets.
3. Add the tickets required incrementally:
   - After Ferris wheel: 20 - 4 = 16 tickets remaining.
   - After roller coaster: 16 - 15 = 1 ticket remaining.
   - After log ride: 1 - 7 = -6 tickets (shortfall of 6 tickets).
4. Determine the shortfall:
   - Dolly needs 6 more tickets to complete all rides.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it calculates the total tickets needed upfront and subtracts the tickets Dolly already has. It avoids intermediate steps and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2** involves incremental calculations, which can be useful for tracking progress but introduces more steps and potential for mistakes.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and clarity.
- Solution:
  1. Tickets for Ferris wheel = 4.
  2. Tickets for roller coaster = 15.
  3. Tickets for log ride = 7.
  4. Total tickets needed = 4 + 15 + 7 = 26.
  5. Tickets needed = 26 - 20 = 6.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate:
  - Ferris wheel: 2 × 2 = 4.
  - Roller coaster: 3 × 5 = 15.
  - Log ride: 7 × 1 = 7.
  - Total tickets needed = 4 + 15 + 7 = 26.
  - Tickets needed = 26 - 20 = 6.
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **6**.

Q: Lilith is trying to break the world record for largest cap collection. She collects 3 caps per month in the first year, and 5 caps per month after the first year. Each Christmas, she also receives 40 caps from friends and family. She estimates that each year, she loses 15 of the caps she has collected. If Lilith has been collecting for 5 years, how many caps has she collected so far?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

- Lilith collects caps for 5 years.
- In the first year, she collects **3 caps per month**, totaling \( 3 \times 12 = 36 \) caps.
- After the first year, she collects **5 caps per month**, totaling \( 5 \times 12 = 60 \) caps per year for years 2 through 5.
- Each Christmas, she receives **40 caps** from friends and family, adding 40 caps per year.
- Each year, she **loses 15 caps** from her collection.
- We assume all caps collected, received, and lost are accounted for accurately, and there are no other sources of caps or losses.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.

#### Method 1: Calculate year-by-year totals.
- Break the problem into yearly calculations, summing caps collected, caps received, and caps lost for each year.
- Add the totals for all 5 years.

#### Method 2: Use a formula-based approach.
- Calculate the total caps collected over 5 years, total caps received over 5 years, and total caps lost over 5 years separately.
- Combine these totals into a single equation:  
  \[
  \text{Total caps} = (\text{caps collected} + \text{caps received}) - \text{caps lost}.
  \]

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### Method 1: Year-by-year totals.

1. **Year 1**:
   - Caps collected: \( 3 \times 12 = 36 \).
   - Caps received: \( 40 \).
   - Caps lost: \( 15 \).
   - Net caps for Year 1: \( 36 + 40 - 15 = 61 \).

2. **Year 2**:
   - Caps collected: \( 5 \times 12 = 60 \).
   - Caps received: \( 40 \).
   - Caps lost: \( 15 \).
   - Net caps for Year 2: \( 60 + 40 - 15 = 85 \).

3. **Year 3**:
   - Caps collected: \( 5 \times 12 = 60 \).
   - Caps received: \( 40 \).
   - Caps lost: \( 15 \).
   - Net caps for Year 3: \( 60 + 40 - 15 = 85 \).

4. **Year 4**:
   - Caps collected: \( 5 \times 12 = 60 \).
   - Caps received: \( 40 \).
   - Caps lost: \( 15 \).
   - Net caps for Year 4: \( 60 + 40 - 15 = 85 \).

5. **Year 5**:
   - Caps collected: \( 5 \times 12 = 60 \).
   - Caps received: \( 40 \).
   - Caps lost: \( 15 \).
   - Net caps for Year 5: \( 60 + 40 - 15 = 85 \).

6. **Total caps over 5 years**:
   - Sum the yearly totals: \( 61 + 85 + 85 + 85 + 85 = 401 \).

#### Method 2: Formula-based approach.

1. **Caps collected**:
   - Year 1: \( 3 \times 12 = 36 \).
   - Years 2–5: \( 5 \times 12 \times 4 = 240 \).
   - Total caps collected: \( 36 + 240 = 276 \).

2. **Caps received**:
   - \( 40 \times 5 = 200 \).

3. **Caps lost**:
   - \( 15 \times 5 = 75 \).

4. **Total caps**:
   - Combine the totals:  
     \[
     \text{Total caps} = (\text{caps collected} + \text{caps received}) - \text{caps lost}.
     \]
     \[
     \text{Total caps} = (276 + 200) - 75 = 401.
     \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** (Year-by-year totals):
  - Provides a clear breakdown of each year's contributions.
  - Useful for understanding the progression of Lilith's collection.
  - Slightly more time-consuming due to repetitive calculations.

- **Method 2** (Formula-based approach):
  - More efficient and concise.
  - Avoids repetitive calculations by grouping totals.
  - Requires careful attention to ensure all components are included.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

- **Method 2** is better due to its efficiency and clarity.  
- Using Method 2, the total caps collected is \( 401 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recalculate using Method 1: \( 61 + 85 + 85 + 85 + 85 = 401 \).  
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming accuracy.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **401**.

Valerie needs stamps for the following items:  
1. **Thank you cards**: She has three cards (for her grandmother, uncle, and aunt). Each card requires **1 stamp**.

4. **Job applications**: She has **twice as many job applications as rebates**. Since there are 5 rebates, she will send **2 × 5 = 10 job applications**. Each application requires **1 stamp**.

2. Substitute values:  
   - Total stamps = 3 + 1 + 2 + 5 + 10.  

3. Simplify:  
   - Total stamps = **21 stamps**.  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 2** is more concise and efficient, but it requires careful attention to ensure all contributions are included correctly.

- Thank you cards: 3 stamps.  
- Bills: 3 stamps.  
- Mail-in rebates: 5 stamps.  
- Job applications: 10 stamps.

Q: Alissa used discarded paper to make notepads for her friends. She would fold 5 pieces of letter-size paper 3 times then cut along the edges to form smaller note papers. She would then stack these smaller note papers and staple them together. How long would 1 notepad last if someone wrote 10 notes per day?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

- Alissa uses **5 pieces of letter-size paper** to make one notepad.
- Each piece of paper is **folded 3 times** and then cut along the edges to form smaller note papers.
- Folding a piece of paper 3 times results in **8 smaller note papers per sheet** (since each fold doubles the number of sections: 1 → 2 → 4 → 8).
- Therefore, each sheet of paper produces **8 smaller note papers**, and with 5 sheets, the total number of smaller note papers in one notepad is \( 5 \times 8 = 40 \).
- Someone writes **10 notes per day** using the notepad.
- We assume that all smaller note papers are used for writing notes, and no paper is wasted.

The goal is to determine how long (in days) one notepad will last if someone writes 10 notes per day.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct division approach**
- Calculate the total number of smaller note papers in one notepad.
- Divide the total number of note papers by the number of notes written per day to find the number of days the notepad will last.

**Method 2: Incremental subtraction approach**
- Start with the total number of smaller note papers in the notepad.
- Subtract 10 notes per day iteratively until the notepad is empty.
- Count the number of days required to deplete the notepad.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct division approach**
1. Calculate the total number of smaller note papers in one notepad:
   \[
   \text{Total note papers} = 5 \times 8 = 40
   \]
2. Divide the total number of note papers by the number of notes written per day:
   \[
   \text{Days the notepad will last} = \frac{\text{Total note papers}}{\text{Notes per day}} = \frac{40}{10} = 4
   \]
3. Check for potential pitfalls:
   - Ensure the division is exact (no remainder). Since \( 40 \div 10 = 4 \) with no remainder, the calculation is valid.
   - Confirm that all assumptions (e.g., no wasted paper) are reasonable.

#### **Method 2: Incremental subtraction approach**
1. Start with the total number of smaller note papers in the notepad:
   \[
   \text{Total note papers} = 40
   \]
2. Subtract 10 notes per day iteratively:
   - Day 1: \( 40 - 10 = 30 \) papers left.
   - Day 2: \( 30 - 10 = 20 \) papers left.
   - Day 3: \( 20 - 10 = 10 \) papers left.
   - Day 4: \( 10 - 10 = 0 \) papers left.
3. Count the number of days required to deplete the notepad:
   - It takes exactly 4 days to reduce the total to 0.
4. Check for potential pitfalls:
   - Ensure no steps are skipped or miscalculated.
   - Verify that the subtraction process aligns with the problem's assumptions.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more efficient because it uses a single division operation to arrive at the answer. It is straightforward and avoids unnecessary repetition.
- **Method 2** is more labor-intensive, as it involves multiple subtraction steps. While it is conceptually simple, it is less efficient for larger numbers and more prone to errors in manual calculations.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better due to its clarity and efficiency.

---

Q: John starts at an elevation of 400 feet.  He travels downward at a rate of 10 feet down per minute for 5 minutes.  What is his elevation now?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- John starts at an elevation of **400 feet**.
- He travels downward at a rate of **10 feet per minute**.
- He continues traveling downward for **5 minutes**.
- Assumptions:
  - The rate of descent is constant.
  - There are no interruptions or changes in the rate of descent.
  - Elevation is measured relative to sea level, and downward movement decreases elevation.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct calculation using multiplication and subtraction.**
- Multiply the rate of descent (10 feet per minute) by the time (5 minutes) to find the total descent.
- Subtract the total descent from the starting elevation to find the final elevation.

**Method 2: Sequential subtraction for each minute.**
- Start at the initial elevation (400 feet).
- Subtract 10 feet for each minute of descent, repeating this process for 5 minutes.
- Track the elevation after each minute to ensure accuracy.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation**
1. Calculate the total descent:
   \[
   \text{Total descent} = \text{Rate of descent} \times \text{Time} = 10 \, \text{feet/min} \times 5 \, \text{min} = 50 \, \text{feet}.
   \]
2. Subtract the total descent from the starting elevation:
   \[
   \text{Final elevation} = \text{Starting elevation} - \text{Total descent} = 400 \, \text{feet} - 50 \, \text{feet} = 350 \, \text{feet}.
   \]
3. Check for potential pitfalls:
   - Ensure the rate and time are correctly multiplied.
   - Ensure subtraction is performed correctly.

#### **Method 2: Sequential subtraction**
1. Start at the initial elevation of 400 feet.
2. Subtract 10 feet for each minute:
   - After 1 minute: \( 400 - 10 = 390 \, \text{feet} \),
   - After 2 minutes: \( 390 - 10 = 380 \, \text{feet} \),
   - After 3 minutes: \( 380 - 10 = 370 \, \text{feet} \),
   - After 4 minutes: \( 370 - 10 = 360 \, \text{feet} \),
   - After 5 minutes: \( 360 - 10 = 350 \, \text{feet} \).
3. The final elevation after 5 minutes is 350 feet.
4. Check for potential pitfalls:
   - Ensure subtraction is performed correctly at each step.
   - Ensure the process is repeated exactly 5 times.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more efficient because it uses a single multiplication and subtraction, making it faster and less prone to error.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and allows for step-by-step tracking, which can help verify the process but is more time-consuming and repetitive.
- For this problem, **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and reliability.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Calculate the total descent:
   \[
   10 \, \text{feet/min} \times 5 \, \text{min} = 50 \, \text{feet}.
   \]
2. Subtract the total descent from the starting elevation:
   \[
   400 \, \text{feet} - 50 \, \text{feet} = 350 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the total descent: \( 10 \times 5 = 50 \, \text{feet} \), which is correct.
- Verify the subtraction: \( 400 - 50 = 350 \, \text{feet} \), which is correct.
- The result is reasonable because John is descending, so his elevation should decrease.

---

The answer is **350 feet**.

Q: John buys 5 notebooks that have 40 pages each.  He uses 4 pages per day.  How many days do the notebooks last?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- John buys 5 notebooks.
- Each notebook has 40 pages.
- Total pages = 5 notebooks × 40 pages per notebook.
- John uses 4 pages per day.
- We assume John uses the pages consistently every day and does not skip any days.
- We are tasked with finding how many days the notebooks will last.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Calculate the total number of pages, then divide by the number of pages used per day.**  
- First, find the total number of pages by multiplying the number of notebooks by the pages per notebook.  
- Then, divide the total pages by the daily usage to determine the number of days.

**Method 2: Calculate how many days each notebook lasts, then multiply by the number of notebooks.**  
- First, determine how many days one notebook lasts by dividing the pages in one notebook by the daily usage.  
- Then, multiply the result by the number of notebooks to find the total number of days.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Total pages divided by daily usage**
1. Calculate the total number of pages:  
   \[
   \text{Total pages} = 5 \times 40 = 200 \, \text{pages}.
   \]
2. Divide the total pages by the daily usage:  
   \[
   \text{Days} = \frac{\text{Total pages}}{\text{Pages per day}} = \frac{200}{4} = 50 \, \text{days}.
   \]
3. Check for potential pitfalls:  
   - Ensure the division is exact (no remainder). Since 200 is divisible by 4, the result is a whole number, so no pages are left unused.

#### **Method 2: Days per notebook multiplied by the number of notebooks**
1. Calculate how many days one notebook lasts:  
   \[
   \text{Days per notebook} = \frac{\text{Pages per notebook}}{\text{Pages per day}} = \frac{40}{4} = 10 \, \text{days}.
   \]
2. Multiply by the number of notebooks:  
   \[
   \text{Total days} = \text{Days per notebook} \times \text{Number of notebooks} = 10 \times 5 = 50 \, \text{days}.
   \]
3. Check for potential pitfalls:  
   - Ensure the calculation for one notebook is correct. Since 40 is divisible by 4, the result is a whole number, and multiplying by 5 notebooks is straightforward.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct because it calculates the total pages first and then divides by the daily usage. It involves fewer steps and is less prone to intermediate errors.  
- **Method 2** breaks the problem into smaller parts, which can be helpful for mental math or if the problem involves more complex numbers. However, it introduces an extra step, which could increase the chance of error.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and straightforward for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Calculate the total number of pages:  
   \[
   \text{Total pages} = 5 \times 40 = 200 \, \text{pages}.
   \]
2. Divide the total pages by the daily usage:  
   \[
   \text{Days} = \frac{200}{4} = 50 \, \text{days}.
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using **Method 2**:  
   \[
   \text{Days per notebook} = \frac{40}{4} = 10 \, \text{days}.
   \]  
   \[
   \text{Total days} = 10 \times 5 = 50 \, \text{days}.
   \]
- Both methods confirm the result. The calculations are consistent, and the result is reasonable given the problem's context.

---

The answer is **50 days**.

Q: Carl wants to buy a new coat that is quite expensive. He saved $25 each week for 6 weeks.  On the seventh week, he had to use a third of his saving to pay some bills. On the eighth week, his dad gave him some extra money for him to buy his dream coat. If the coat cost $170, how much money did his dad give him?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Carl saves $25 each week for 6 weeks.  
- On the seventh week, Carl uses **one-third** of his total savings to pay bills.  
- On the eighth week, Carl’s dad gives him extra money to buy the coat.  
- The coat costs $170.  
- We assume Carl spends all his remaining savings and the money from his dad to buy the coat.  
- We need to calculate how much money Carl’s dad gave him.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate Carl’s total savings after 6 weeks.  
2. Subtract one-third of his savings (used for bills) to find his remaining savings after the seventh week.  
3. Subtract his remaining savings from the coat’s cost to determine how much his dad gave him.  

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate Carl’s total savings after 6 weeks.  
2. Determine how much Carl spent on bills (one-third of his savings).  
3. Subtract the amount spent on bills from his total savings to find his remaining savings.  
4. Subtract his remaining savings from the coat’s cost to determine how much his dad gave him.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. **Calculate Carl’s total savings after 6 weeks:**  
   Carl saves $25 per week for 6 weeks:  
   \[
   25 \times 6 = 150
   \]  
   Total savings = $150.  

2. **Subtract one-third of his savings (used for bills):**  
   Carl uses one-third of $150 for bills:  
   \[
   \frac{150}{3} = 50
   \]  
   Remaining savings after paying bills:  
   \[
   150 - 50 = 100
   \]  

3. **Subtract his remaining savings from the coat’s cost:**  
   The coat costs $170, and Carl has $100 left after paying bills. The amount his dad gave him is:  
   \[
   170 - 100 = 70
   \]  

#### **Method 2:**
1. **Calculate Carl’s total savings after 6 weeks:**  
   Carl saves $25 per week for 6 weeks:  
   \[
   25 \times 6 = 150
   \]  
   Total savings = $150.  

2. **Determine how much Carl spent on bills:**  
   Carl uses one-third of $150 for bills:  
   \[
   \frac{150}{3} = 50
   \]  

3. **Subtract the amount spent on bills from his total savings:**  
   Remaining savings after paying bills:  
   \[
   150 - 50 = 100
   \]  

4. **Subtract his remaining savings from the coat’s cost:**  
   The coat costs $170, and Carl has $100 left after paying bills. The amount his dad gave him is:  
   \[
   170 - 100 = 70
   \]  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods involve the same calculations and steps, but Method 1 is slightly more direct because it focuses on subtracting the one-third of savings first and then directly calculating the dad’s contribution.  
- Method 2 breaks the problem into smaller steps, which may be helpful for clarity but introduces redundancy.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.  
- Solution:  
   - Total savings after 6 weeks = $150.  
   - Amount spent on bills = $50.  
   - Remaining savings = $100.  
   - Dad’s contribution = $170 - $100 = $70.

Q: Winston had 10 gallons of gas in his car’s gas tank. He drives to the store and uses 6 gallons of gas. Then he drives to the doctor’s office and uses 2 gallons of gas. If the tank can hold up to 12 gallons of gas, how many gallons of gas will Winston need to refill the entire tank?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Winston starts with **10 gallons of gas** in his car’s gas tank.
- He drives to the store and uses **6 gallons of gas**.
- He then drives to the doctor’s office and uses **2 gallons of gas**.
- The gas tank has a **maximum capacity of 12 gallons**.
- We assume no gas is added or lost during the trips, and the gas usage is accurate.

The goal is to determine how many gallons of gas Winston needs to refill the tank to its full capacity of 12 gallons.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Calculate the remaining gas in the tank, then subtract it from the tank's capacity.**
- Subtract the gas used (6 gallons + 2 gallons) from the initial amount (10 gallons) to find how much gas is left.
- Subtract the remaining gas from the tank's maximum capacity (12 gallons) to find how much gas is needed to refill.

**Method 2: Work backward from the tank's capacity.**
- Start with the tank's maximum capacity (12 gallons).
- Subtract the total gas used (6 gallons + 2 gallons) from the capacity to find how much gas is missing.
- Compare this result to the initial amount of gas to confirm the calculation.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Calculate remaining gas, then subtract from capacity.**
1. Start with the initial amount of gas: **10 gallons**.
2. Subtract the gas used for the first trip:  
   \( 10 - 6 = 4 \) gallons remain.
3. Subtract the gas used for the second trip:  
   \( 4 - 2 = 2 \) gallons remain in the tank.
4. Subtract the remaining gas from the tank's capacity to find how much is needed to refill:  
   \( 12 - 2 = 10 \) gallons.
5. Double-check: If the tank holds 12 gallons and currently has 2 gallons, adding 10 gallons will fill it completely.

**Result from Method 1: 10 gallons are needed.**

---

#### **Method 2: Work backward from the tank's capacity.**
1. Start with the tank's maximum capacity: **12 gallons**.
2. Subtract the total gas used during the trips:  
   \( 6 + 2 = 8 \) gallons used in total.
3. Subtract the total gas used from the tank's capacity to find how much gas is missing:  
   \( 12 - 8 = 4 \) gallons missing.
4. Compare this to the initial amount of gas (10 gallons):  
   If 8 gallons were used from 10 gallons, then \( 10 - 8 = 2 \) gallons remain in the tank.  
   To refill the tank to 12 gallons, \( 12 - 2 = 10 \) gallons are needed.

**Result from Method 2: 10 gallons are needed.**

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward because it directly calculates the remaining gas and subtracts it from the tank's capacity. It involves fewer steps and is less prone to error.
- **Method 2** is slightly more complex because it works backward and requires additional checks to confirm the intermediate results. While it is a valid approach, it is less efficient for this problem.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and clarity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Start with the initial amount of gas: **10 gallons**.
2. Subtract the gas used for the first trip:  
   \( 10 - 6 = 4 \) gallons remain.
3. Subtract the gas used for the second trip:  
   \( 4 - 2 = 2 \) gallons remain in the tank.
4. Subtract the remaining gas from the tank's capacity:  
   \( 12 - 2 = 10 \) gallons needed to refill.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- If Winston starts with 10 gallons and uses 6 gallons + 2 gallons = 8 gallons, he has \( 10 - 8 = 2 \) gallons left.
- To refill the tank to its full capacity of 12 gallons, he needs \( 12 - 2 = 10 \) gallons.
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

**The answer is 10 gallons.**

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Sam has **7 fish**.
- Joe has **8 times as many fish as Sam**.
- Harry has **4 times as many fish as Joe**.
- We assume all fish counts are whole numbers and there are no errors in the given relationships.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate Joe's fish count by multiplying Sam's fish count by 8.  
- Calculate Harry's fish count by multiplying Joe's fish count by 4.  

**Method 2:**  
- Represent the relationships algebraically:  
  - Let Sam's fish count = \( S = 7 \).  
  - Joe's fish count = \( J = 8S \).  
  - Harry's fish count = \( H = 4J \).  
- Substitute \( S = 7 \) into the equations and solve step by step.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

1. Start with Sam's fish count: \( S = 7 \).  
2. Calculate Joe's fish count:  
   \[
   J = 8 \times S = 8 \times 7 = 56
   \]
3. Calculate Harry's fish count:  
   \[
   H = 4 \times J = 4 \times 56 = 224
   \]
4. Double-check calculations:  
   - \( J = 8 \times 7 = 56 \) is correct.  
   - \( H = 4 \times 56 = 224 \) is correct.

#### **Method 2: Algebraic representation**
1. Represent the relationships:  
   - \( S = 7 \) (Sam's fish count).  
   - \( J = 8S \) (Joe's fish count).  
   - \( H = 4J \) (Harry's fish count).  
2. Substitute \( S = 7 \) into \( J = 8S \):  
   \[
   J = 8 \times 7 = 56
   \]
3. Substitute \( J = 56 \) into \( H = 4J \):  
   \[
   H = 4 \times 56 = 224
   \]
4. Double-check calculations:  
   - \( J = 8 \times 7 = 56 \) is correct.  
   - \( H = 4 \times 56 = 224 \) is correct.

- **Method 2** uses algebraic representation, which is helpful for generalizing relationships but introduces extra steps that may not be necessary for this specific problem.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this problem due to its simplicity and clarity.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Start with Sam's fish count: \( S = 7 \).  
2. Calculate Joe's fish count:  
   \[
   J = 8 \times S = 8 \times 7 = 56
   \]
3. Calculate Harry's fish count:  
   \[
   H = 4 \times J = 4 \times 56 = 224
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

Q: Every week of last month Paul tried out a new cake recipe.  The first week, he followed a cake recipe that called for 24 cups of sugar. Each week after that, he reduced the amount of sugar by half. How much sugar did he use for the cake on the fourth week?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Paul followed a cake recipe every week for four weeks.
- The first week, the recipe called for **24 cups of sugar**.
- Each subsequent week, the amount of sugar was **reduced by half** compared to the previous week.
- We assume "reduced by half" means dividing the previous week's sugar amount by 2.
- We are tasked with finding the amount of sugar used **on the fourth week**.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Sequential halving approach**  
- Start with the first week's sugar amount (24 cups).
- Halve the sugar amount for each subsequent week until reaching the fourth week.
- Use division by 2 repeatedly to calculate the sugar for each week.

**Method 2: Formula-based approach using powers of 2**  
- Recognize that the sugar amount decreases geometrically, following the pattern:  
  \( \text{Sugar on week } n = \frac{24}{2^{n-1}} \), where \( n \) is the week number.  
- Plug \( n = 4 \) into the formula to directly calculate the sugar for the fourth week.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential halving approach**
1. **Week 1:** Start with 24 cups of sugar.  
   \( \text{Sugar on week 1} = 24 \).

2. **Week 2:** Halve the sugar from week 1.  
   \( \text{Sugar on week 2} = \frac{24}{2} = 12 \).

3. **Week 3:** Halve the sugar from week 2.  
   \( \text{Sugar on week 3} = \frac{12}{2} = 6 \).

4. **Week 4:** Halve the sugar from week 3.  
   \( \text{Sugar on week 4} = \frac{6}{2} = 3 \).

5. **Result:** The sugar used on the fourth week is **3 cups**.

**Checks and pitfalls:**  
- Each step involves simple division by 2, which is straightforward and reliable.  
- The sequential approach ensures no steps are skipped, but it can be tedious for larger numbers or more weeks.

---

#### **Method 2: Formula-based approach using powers of 2**
1. Recognize the geometric pattern:  
   \( \text{Sugar on week } n = \frac{24}{2^{n-1}} \).

2. Plug \( n = 4 \) into the formula:  
   \( \text{Sugar on week 4} = \frac{24}{2^{4-1}} = \frac{24}{2^3} = \frac{24}{8} = 3 \).

3. **Result:** The sugar used on the fourth week is **3 cups**.

**Checks and pitfalls:**  
- This method is efficient and avoids repetitive calculations.  
- It relies on correctly identifying the geometric pattern and applying the formula accurately.  
- For larger values of \( n \), this method is faster than sequential halving.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is straightforward and intuitive, as it mirrors the process described in the problem. However, it can be tedious for larger numbers of weeks or more complex patterns.  
- **Method 2** is more efficient and elegant, especially for larger values of \( n \), as it directly calculates the sugar amount using a formula. It requires recognizing the geometric progression, which may not be immediately obvious to all readers.  

**Conclusion:** Method 2 is better for this problem due to its efficiency and simplicity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 2**:  
1. The formula is \( \text{Sugar on week } n = \frac{24}{2^{n-1}} \).  
2. Plug \( n = 4 \) into the formula:  
   \( \text{Sugar on week 4} = \frac{24}{2^{4-1}} = \frac{24}{2^3} = \frac{24}{8} = 3 \).  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Using **Method 1**, sequential halving also gives \( 3 \) cups for the fourth week.  
- The result is reasonable, as halving the sugar each week leads to progressively smaller amounts.  
- Both methods agree, confirming the solution.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **3 cups**.
Extracted:
3.0
A:
3.0

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Luther made **12 pancakes** for breakfast.
- His family consists of **8 people**.
- Each person needs **2 pancakes** to have a second pancake.
- We assume no pancakes are wasted, and Luther needs to make enough pancakes so that everyone has exactly 2 pancakes.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total number of pancakes needed for 8 people to have 2 pancakes each, then subtract the number of pancakes Luther has already made to find how many more pancakes he needs to make.

**Method 2:**  
Determine how many pancakes Luther is short by calculating the difference between the pancakes required for a second round (8 pancakes) and the number of pancakes remaining after the first round.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Total pancakes needed minus pancakes already made**
1. Each person needs **2 pancakes**, and there are **8 people**.  
   Total pancakes needed = \( 8 \times 2 = 16 \).
2. Luther has already made **12 pancakes**.  
   Pancakes still needed = \( 16 - 12 = 4 \).

#### **Method 2: Pancakes needed for the second round**
1. Luther has already made **12 pancakes**, which is enough for **12 ÷ 8 = 1.5 pancakes per person**.  
   This means everyone has had **1 pancake**, and there are **4 pancakes left** (since \( 12 - 8 = 4 \)).
2. To give everyone a second pancake, Luther needs **8 pancakes** for the second round.  
   Pancakes still needed = \( 8 - 4 = 4 \).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and focuses on the total number of pancakes needed, making it easier to follow for most people. It avoids intermediate steps and directly calculates the shortfall.
- **Method 2** involves breaking the problem into smaller parts (first round and second round), which can be helpful for understanding but introduces more steps and potential for error.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better because it is simpler, more direct, and less prone to mistakes.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Total pancakes needed = \( 8 \times 2 = 16 \).
2. Pancakes still needed = \( 16 - 12 = 4 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- If Luther makes 4 more pancakes, the total number of pancakes will be \( 12 + 4 = 16 \).  
- Dividing 16 pancakes among 8 people gives \( 16 ÷ 8 = 2 pancakes per person \), which satisfies the requirement for everyone to have a second pancake.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **4**.

Q: Sarah starts saving $5.00 a week for 4 weeks.  Then she saves $10.00 a week for the next 4 weeks.  Then she saves $20.00 a week for the next 4 weeks.  How much money has she saved over 12 weeks?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Sarah saves money in three phases:
  - Phase 1: $5.00 per week for 4 weeks.
  - Phase 2: $10.00 per week for the next 4 weeks.
  - Phase 3: $20.00 per week for the final 4 weeks.
- Total duration: 12 weeks (4 weeks per phase).
- We assume Sarah saves consistently each week and there are no interruptions or changes in her saving pattern.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Calculate the total savings for each phase separately, then sum them up. This method involves breaking the problem into smaller parts and performing direct multiplication and addition.

**Method 2:** Use a single formula to calculate the total savings by factoring in the weekly savings rate and the number of weeks for each phase. This method combines all calculations into one streamlined equation.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Calculate savings for each phase separately.**
1. **Phase 1:** Sarah saves $5.00 per week for 4 weeks.
   - Calculation: $5.00 × 4 = $20.00.
   - Double-check: Multiplying $5.00 by 4 gives $20.00.

2. **Phase 2:** Sarah saves $10.00 per week for 4 weeks.
   - Calculation: $10.00 × 4 = $40.00.
   - Double-check: Multiplying $10.00 by 4 gives $40.00.

3. **Phase 3:** Sarah saves $20.00 per week for 4 weeks.
   - Calculation: $20.00 × 4 = $80.00.
   - Double-check: Multiplying $20.00 by 4 gives $80.00.

4. **Total savings:** Add the savings from all three phases.
   - Calculation: $20.00 + $40.00 + $80.00 = $140.00.
   - Double-check: Adding $20.00, $40.00, and $80.00 gives $140.00.

#### **Method 2: Use a single formula to calculate total savings.**
1. Combine the weekly savings rates and durations into one equation:
   - Total savings = ($5.00 × 4) + ($10.00 × 4) + ($20.00 × 4).

2. Perform the calculations:
   - $5.00 × 4 = $20.00.
   - $10.00 × 4 = $40.00.
   - $20.00 × 4 = $80.00.

3. Add the results:
   - $20.00 + $40.00 + $80.00 = $140.00.

4. Double-check:
   - Recalculate each term: $5.00 × 4 = $20.00, $10.00 × 4 = $40.00, $20.00 × 4 = $80.00.
   - Adding $20.00, $40.00, and $80.00 gives $140.00.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and breaks the problem into smaller, manageable parts. It is easy to follow and minimizes the risk of errors.
- **Method 2** is more efficient for advanced problem-solving, as it combines all calculations into one formula. However, it may be less intuitive for some learners.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and step-by-step approach.
- Solution:
  - Phase 1: $5.00 × 4 = $20.00.
  - Phase 2: $10.00 × 4 = $40.00.
  - Phase 3: $20.00 × 4 = $80.00.
  - Total savings: $20.00 + $40.00 + $80.00 = $140.00.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate each phase:
  - $5.00 × 4 = $20.00.
  - $10.00 × 4 = $40.00.
  - $20.00 × 4 = $80.00.
- Add the results: $20.00 + $40.00 + $80.00 = $140.00.
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

The answer is **140.00**.

Q: Xavier is twice as old as Yasmin is. Xavier will 30 years old in six years. What is the total of their ages now?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Xavier is twice as old as Yasmin is.
- Xavier will be 30 years old in six years.
- We assume ages are whole numbers and the relationship between their ages remains consistent.
- We need to find the total of their ages now.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Use the information about Xavier's future age to calculate his current age, then use the "twice as old" relationship to find Yasmin's age. Add their ages together.

**Method 2:**  
Set up an algebraic equation system based on the given relationships. Solve for Xavier's and Yasmin's ages, then add them together.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation using Xavier's future age**
1. Xavier will be 30 years old in six years. Subtract 6 from 30 to find Xavier's current age:  
   \( 30 - 6 = 24 \).  
   Xavier is currently 24 years old.

2. Xavier is twice as old as Yasmin. Divide Xavier's age by 2 to find Yasmin's age:  
   \( 24 \div 2 = 12 \).  
   Yasmin is currently 12 years old.

3. Add their ages together:  
   \( 24 + 12 = 36 \).  

4. Double-check:  
   - Xavier is 24 now, and in six years, he will be \( 24 + 6 = 30 \), which matches the problem statement.  
   - Yasmin is 12, and \( 24 \div 2 = 12 \), confirming the "twice as old" relationship.  

#### **Method 2: Algebraic approach**
1. Let Xavier's current age be \( X \) and Yasmin's current age be \( Y \).  
   From the problem, we know:  
   - \( X = 2Y \) (Xavier is twice as old as Yasmin).  
   - \( X + 6 = 30 \) (Xavier will be 30 years old in six years).

2. Solve for \( X \) using the second equation:  
   \( X + 6 = 30 \)  
   \( X = 30 - 6 \)  
   \( X = 24 \).  

3. Substitute \( X = 24 \) into \( X = 2Y \) to find \( Y \):  
   \( 24 = 2Y \)  
   \( Y = 24 \div 2 \)  
   \( Y = 12 \).  

4. Add their ages together:  
   \( X + Y = 24 + 12 = 36 \).  

5. Double-check:  
   - \( X = 24 \), and in six years, \( X + 6 = 30 \), which matches the problem statement.  
   - \( Y = 12 \), and \( X = 2Y \) confirms the "twice as old" relationship.  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and intuitive, relying on direct calculations. It is efficient and avoids unnecessary complexity.  
- **Method 2** uses algebra, which is more formal and systematic. It is useful for more complex problems but introduces extra steps that are not strictly necessary here.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is better for this problem due to its simplicity and clarity.  
- Solution:  
   - Xavier's current age: \( 30 - 6 = 24 \).  
   - Yasmin's current age: \( 24 \div 2 = 12 \).  
   - Total of their ages: \( 24 + 12 = 36 \).  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Xavier is 24 now, and in six years, he will be \( 24 + 6 = 30 \), which matches the problem statement.  
- Yasmin is 12, and \( 24 \div 2 = 12 \), confirming the "twice as old" relationship.  
- The total of their ages is \( 24 + 12 = 36 \), which is reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **36**.

Q: Charley has a bag that contains 51 white beads and 90 black beads. He reaches in the bag and pulls out 1/6 of the black beads and a third of the white beads. How many beads in total did Charley pull out?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Charley has a bag containing:
  - 51 white beads.
  - 90 black beads.
- He pulls out:
  - **1/6 of the black beads**.
  - **1/3 of the white beads**.
- We assume:
  - The fractions are applied to the total number of beads of each color.
  - The results of the fractions are exact (no rounding or partial beads).
  - The total number of beads pulled out is the sum of the beads pulled from each color.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct calculation using fractions.**
- Multiply the total number of black beads by 1/6 to find how many black beads Charley pulls out.
- Multiply the total number of white beads by 1/3 to find how many white beads Charley pulls out.
- Add the two results to find the total number of beads pulled out.

**Method 2: Convert fractions to decimals and calculate.**
- Convert 1/6 and 1/3 into their decimal equivalents (0.1667 and 0.3333, respectively).
- Multiply the total number of black beads by 0.1667 and the total number of white beads by 0.3333.
- Add the two results to find the total number of beads pulled out.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation using fractions**
1. **Calculate the number of black beads pulled out:**
   - Total black beads = 90.
   - Fraction pulled out = 1/6.
   - \( 90 \times \frac{1}{6} = 15 \).
   - Charley pulls out **15 black beads**.

2. **Calculate the number of white beads pulled out:**
   - Total white beads = 51.
   - Fraction pulled out = 1/3.
   - \( 51 \times \frac{1}{3} = 17 \).
   - Charley pulls out **17 white beads**.

3. **Add the results:**
   - Total beads pulled out = black beads + white beads.
   - \( 15 + 17 = 32 \).

4. **Check for potential pitfalls:**
   - Ensure the fractions are applied correctly to the total number of beads.
   - Ensure no rounding errors occur (fractions are exact).

#### **Method 2: Convert fractions to decimals and calculate**
1. **Convert fractions to decimals:**
   - \( 1/6 = 0.1667 \) (rounded to 4 decimal places).
   - \( 1/3 = 0.3333 \) (rounded to 4 decimal places).

2. **Calculate the number of black beads pulled out:**
   - Total black beads = 90.
   - Decimal equivalent of 1/6 = 0.1667.
   - \( 90 \times 0.1667 = 15.003 \) (round to 15).
   - Charley pulls out **15 black beads**.

3. **Calculate the number of white beads pulled out:**
   - Total white beads = 51.
   - Decimal equivalent of 1/3 = 0.3333.
   - \( 51 \times 0.3333 = 16.9983 \) (round to 17).
   - Charley pulls out **17 white beads**.

4. **Add the results:**
   - Total beads pulled out = black beads + white beads.
   - \( 15 + 17 = 32 \).

5. **Check for potential pitfalls:**
   - Decimal approximations can introduce rounding errors.
   - Ensure rounding does not affect the final result.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (fractions):**
  - More precise because fractions are exact and do not involve rounding.
  - Fewer steps and less prone to error.
  - Easier to verify correctness.
- **Method 2 (decimals):**
  - Requires converting fractions to decimals, which introduces rounding errors.
  - Slightly more complex due to the need for rounding intermediate results.
  - Less reliable for exact calculations.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is better due to its precision and simplicity.
- Using Method 1:
  - Black beads pulled out = \( 90 \times \frac{1}{6} = 15 \).
  - White beads pulled out = \( 51 \times \frac{1}{3} = 17 \).
  - Total beads pulled out = \( 15 + 17 = 32 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 1:
  - \( 90 \times \frac{1}{6} = 15 \).
  - \( 51 \times \frac{1}{3} = 17 \).
  - \( 15 + 17 = 32 \).
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

The answer is **32**.

A_model:

- James buys 6 t-shirts.
- Each t-shirt costs $20 at full price.
- The t-shirts are on sale for **50% off**.
- We assume the discount applies to each t-shirt individually.
- We assume there are no additional taxes, fees, or discounts.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the discounted price of one t-shirt by applying the 50% discount to $20.  
- Multiply the discounted price by the number of t-shirts (6) to find the total cost.

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the total cost of all 6 t-shirts at full price ($20 × 6).  
- Apply the 50% discount to the total cost to find the final amount James paid.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Calculate the discounted price per t-shirt first.**
1. Full price of one t-shirt = $20.  
2. Discount = 50% of $20 = \( 20 \times 0.5 = 10 \).  
3. Discounted price of one t-shirt = \( 20 - 10 = 10 \).  
4. Total cost for 6 t-shirts = \( 10 \times 6 = 60 \).  

**Check:**  
- If each t-shirt costs $10 after the discount, buying 6 t-shirts should cost \( 6 \times 10 = 60 \).  
- This calculation is consistent and logical.

#### **Method 2: Calculate the total cost at full price, then apply the discount.**
1. Full price of 6 t-shirts = \( 20 \times 6 = 120 \).  
2. Discount = 50% of $120 = \( 120 \times 0.5 = 60 \).  
3. Total cost after discount = \( 120 - 60 = 60 \).  

**Check:**  
- If the total discount is $60, subtracting it from the full price of $120 gives \( 120 - 60 = 60 \).  
- This matches the result from Method 1.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more intuitive because it breaks the problem into smaller, simpler steps (calculate the price of one t-shirt, then multiply by the quantity). This method is less prone to errors when dealing with individual items.  
- **Method 2** is efficient for bulk calculations but requires careful attention to ensure the discount is applied correctly to the total. It may be less intuitive for some people.  
- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is slightly clearer for this problem.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Full price of one t-shirt = $20.  
2. Discount = \( 20 \times 0.5 = 10 \).  
3. Discounted price of one t-shirt = \( 20 - 10 = 10 \).  
4. Total cost for 6 t-shirts = \( 10 \times 6 = 60 \).  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- If each t-shirt costs $10 after the discount, buying 6 t-shirts should cost \( 6 \times 10 = 60 \).  
- Using Method 2, the total cost at full price is $120, and the discount is $60, so the final cost is \( 120 - 60 = 60 \).

Q: As soon as you enter the taxi, you pay a ride fee of $2. Michelle rode a taxi from her office to her home, which constitutes 4 miles. If the taxi charge per mile is $2.5, how much did pay Michelle pay in total for her ride?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Michelle pays a flat ride fee of $2 as soon as she enters the taxi.
- The taxi charges $2.5 per mile.
- Michelle's ride was 4 miles long.
- We assume there are no additional fees, taxes, or discounts.
- The total cost of the ride is the sum of the flat fee and the cost for the miles traveled.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the cost of the ride in two steps:
1. Multiply the cost per mile ($2.5) by the number of miles (4) to find the total mileage cost.
2. Add the flat ride fee ($2) to the mileage cost to get the total cost.

**Method 2:**  
Combine the flat fee and the mileage cost into a single equation:  
\[
\text{Total Cost} = \text{Flat Fee} + (\text{Cost per Mile} \times \text{Miles Traveled})
\]  
Substitute the values and solve directly.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Step-by-step calculation**
1. **Calculate the mileage cost:**  
   \[
   \text{Mileage Cost} = \text{Cost per Mile} \times \text{Miles Traveled} = 2.5 \times 4 = 10
   \]  
   - The cost for 4 miles is $10.

2. **Add the flat fee to the mileage cost:**  
   \[
   \text{Total Cost} = \text{Flat Fee} + \text{Mileage Cost} = 2 + 10 = 12
   \]  
   - The total cost of the ride is $12.

3. **Check for potential pitfalls:**  
   - Ensure the multiplication and addition are correct.
   - Confirm that all components of the cost (flat fee and mileage cost) are included.

#### **Method 2: Direct equation**
1. **Write the equation for total cost:**  
   \[
   \text{Total Cost} = \text{Flat Fee} + (\text{Cost per Mile} \times \text{Miles Traveled})
   \]  

2. **Substitute the values:**  
   \[
   \text{Total Cost} = 2 + (2.5 \times 4)
   \]  

3. **Simplify the multiplication first:**  
   \[
   2.5 \times 4 = 10
   \]  

4. **Add the flat fee to the mileage cost:**  
   \[
   \text{Total Cost} = 2 + 10 = 12
   \]  

5. **Check for potential pitfalls:**  
   - Ensure the order of operations (multiplication before addition) is followed.
   - Verify that all terms are included in the equation.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** breaks the problem into smaller, more intuitive steps, making it easier to follow and verify. It is ideal for those who prefer step-by-step calculations.
- **Method 2** is more concise and efficient, as it combines all steps into a single equation. However, it requires careful attention to the order of operations.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more beginner-friendly and less prone to errors in complex problems.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
**Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and step-by-step approach.  
1. Calculate the mileage cost:  
   \[
   \text{Mileage Cost} = 2.5 \times 4 = 10
   \]  

2. Add the flat fee to the mileage cost:  
   \[
   \text{Total Cost} = 2 + 10 = 12
   \]  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate the mileage cost: \( 2.5 \times 4 = 10 \). This is correct.
- Add the flat fee: \( 2 + 10 = 12 \). This is correct.
- The result is reasonable, as the total cost includes both the flat fee and the mileage cost.

The answer is **12**.

Q: There are 28 students in a classroom. Half of them have 5 notebooks each and the other half have 3 notebooks each. How many notebooks in total are in the classroom?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are 28 students in the classroom.
- Half of the students (14 students) have 5 notebooks each.
- The other half of the students (14 students) have 3 notebooks each.
- We assume all students fall into one of these two groups, and no student has a different number of notebooks.
- We are tasked with finding the total number of notebooks in the classroom.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Calculate the total number of notebooks for each group separately and then sum them.**
- Multiply the number of students in each group by the number of notebooks they have.
- Add the results to find the total number of notebooks.

**Method 2: Use the average number of notebooks per student.**
- Calculate the average number of notebooks per student by weighting the two groups.
- Multiply the average by the total number of students to find the total number of notebooks.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Group-by-group calculation**
1. **Calculate the total notebooks for the first group (students with 5 notebooks):**
   - Number of students in this group = 14.
   - Number of notebooks per student = 5.
   - Total notebooks for this group = \( 14 \times 5 = 70 \).

2. **Calculate the total notebooks for the second group (students with 3 notebooks):**
   - Number of students in this group = 14.
   - Number of notebooks per student = 3.
   - Total notebooks for this group = \( 14 \times 3 = 42 \).

3. **Add the totals from both groups:**
   - Total notebooks = \( 70 + 42 = 112 \).

4. **Check for errors:**
   - The calculations for each group are straightforward multiplications, and the addition is simple. No errors are apparent.

#### **Method 2: Average-based calculation**
1. **Calculate the average number of notebooks per student:**
   - Half the students (14) have 5 notebooks, and the other half (14) have 3 notebooks.
   - Average notebooks per student = \( \frac{(14 \times 5) + (14 \times 3)}{28} \).
   - Simplify the numerator: \( (14 \times 5) + (14 \times 3) = 70 + 42 = 112 \).
   - Average = \( \frac{112}{28} = 4 \).

2. **Multiply the average by the total number of students:**
   - Total notebooks = \( 4 \times 28 = 112 \).

3. **Check for errors:**
   - The average calculation is consistent with the group-by-group method, and the multiplication is straightforward. No errors are apparent.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1 (Group-by-group calculation):**
  - This method is direct and intuitive, especially when dealing with distinct groups.
  - It is easy to follow and verify since each group is handled separately.
  - However, it involves more steps compared to Method 2.

- **Method 2 (Average-based calculation):**
  - This method is more efficient because it consolidates the calculations into fewer steps.
  - It is particularly useful when the problem involves evenly split groups, as in this case.
  - However, it requires understanding the concept of weighted averages, which might be less intuitive for some.

**Conclusion:** Both methods are reliable and yield the same result. Method 1 is better for clarity and step-by-step reasoning, while Method 2 is more efficient. For this problem, we will proceed with **Method 1** for its clarity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Total notebooks for the first group = \( 14 \times 5 = 70 \).
2. Total notebooks for the second group = \( 14 \times 3 = 42 \).
3. Total notebooks in the classroom = \( 70 + 42 = 112 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recalculate using Method 2:
  - Average notebooks per student = \( \frac{(14 \times 5) + (14 \times 3)}{28} = \frac{112}{28} = 4 \).
  - Total notebooks = \( 4 \times 28 = 112 \).
- Both methods agree, confirming the result is correct.

---

The answer is **112**.

Q: A family bought 1 box of pizza that is cut into 16 slices. Only three-fourths of the pizza was eaten by the family. How many slices of pizza were left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The pizza box contains 16 slices in total.
- Three-fourths (¾) of the pizza was eaten by the family.
- We need to determine how many slices remain uneaten.
- Assumptions:
  - The pizza slices are evenly divided into 16 equal parts.
  - No slices were lost, dropped, or otherwise removed from the box except by eating.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct calculation using fractions.**
- Multiply the total number of slices (16) by the fraction eaten (¾) to find the number of slices eaten.
- Subtract the number of slices eaten from the total number of slices to find the remaining slices.

**Method 2: Complementary fraction approach.**
- Determine the fraction of the pizza that remains uneaten (1 - ¾ = ¼).
- Multiply the total number of slices (16) by the fraction uneaten (¼) to directly calculate the number of remaining slices.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation using fractions**
1. Multiply the total number of slices by the fraction eaten:
   \[
   16 \times \frac{3}{4} = \frac{16 \times 3}{4} = \frac{48}{4} = 12
   \]
   So, 12 slices were eaten.

2. Subtract the number of slices eaten from the total number of slices:
   \[
   16 - 12 = 4
   \]
   Thus, 4 slices remain uneaten.

**Checks:**
- Verify the fraction calculation: \( \frac{48}{4} = 12 \) is correct.
- Verify subtraction: \( 16 - 12 = 4 \) is correct.

**Potential pitfalls:**
- Misinterpreting the fraction or incorrectly multiplying could lead to errors.

---

#### **Method 2: Complementary fraction approach**
1. Determine the fraction of the pizza that remains uneaten:
   \[
   1 - \frac{3}{4} = \frac{1}{4}
   \]
   So, ¼ of the pizza remains uneaten.

2. Multiply the total number of slices by the fraction uneaten:
   \[
   16 \times \frac{1}{4} = \frac{16}{4} = 4
   \]
   Thus, 4 slices remain uneaten.

**Checks:**
- Verify the fraction calculation: \( 1 - \frac{3}{4} = \frac{1}{4} \) is correct.
- Verify multiplication: \( \frac{16}{4} = 4 \) is correct.

**Potential pitfalls:**
- Miscalculating the complementary fraction or incorrectly multiplying could lead to errors.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** involves two steps: calculating the number of slices eaten and subtracting from the total. It is straightforward and intuitive but requires an additional subtraction step.
- **Method 2** directly calculates the number of slices remaining by using the complementary fraction. It is slightly more efficient because it skips the subtraction step.

Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but **Method 2** is slightly more efficient and avoids intermediate subtraction.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

**Method 2 is chosen for its efficiency.**

1. Determine the fraction of the pizza that remains uneaten:
   \[
   1 - \frac{3}{4} = \frac{1}{4}
   \]

2. Multiply the total number of slices by the fraction uneaten:
   \[
   16 \times \frac{1}{4} = \frac{16}{4} = 4
   \]

Thus, 4 slices remain uneaten.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Verify using Method 1:
  - \( 16 \times \frac{3}{4} = 12 \) slices eaten.
  - \( 16 - 12 = 4 \) slices remaining.
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

---

The answer is **4**.

Q: Aunt May milks her cows twice a day. This morning she got 365 gallons of milk. This evening she got 380 gallons. She sold 612 gallons to the local ice cream factory.  She had 15 gallons left over from yesterday.  How many gallons of milk does she have left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Aunt May milks her cows twice a day:
  - Morning: 365 gallons.
  - Evening: 380 gallons.
- She sold 612 gallons to the local ice cream factory.
- She had 15 gallons left over from yesterday.
- We assume no milk is spoiled, spilled, or otherwise lost during the process.
- The goal is to determine how many gallons of milk Aunt May has left after selling to the factory.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total milk collected today by adding the morning and evening amounts.  
2. Add the leftover milk from yesterday to the total collected today.  
3. Subtract the amount sold to the factory from the total milk available.  

**Method 2:**  
1. Start with the milk sold to the factory (612 gallons) and work backward to determine how much milk was left after selling.  
2. Add the leftover milk from yesterday to the total milk collected today.  
3. Subtract the milk sold to find the remaining milk.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. **Calculate the total milk collected today:**  
   Morning milk = 365 gallons.  
   Evening milk = 380 gallons.  
   Total milk collected today = 365 + 380 = **745 gallons**.

2. **Add the leftover milk from yesterday:**  
   Leftover milk = 15 gallons.  
   Total milk available = 745 + 15 = **760 gallons**.

3. **Subtract the milk sold to the factory:**  
   Milk sold = 612 gallons.  
   Remaining milk = 760 - 612 = **148 gallons**.

4. **Double-check calculations:**  
   - Total milk collected today: 365 + 380 = 745.  
   - Total milk available: 745 + 15 = 760.  
   - Remaining milk: 760 - 612 = 148.  
   All calculations check out.

#### **Method 2:**
1. **Start with the milk sold to the factory and work backward:**  
   Milk sold = 612 gallons.  
   Total milk available before selling = Total milk collected today + Leftover milk from yesterday.  

2. **Add the leftover milk from yesterday to the total milk collected today:**  
   Morning milk = 365 gallons.  
   Evening milk = 380 gallons.  
   Total milk collected today = 365 + 380 = **745 gallons**.  
   Leftover milk = 15 gallons.  
   Total milk available = 745 + 15 = **760 gallons**.

3. **Subtract the milk sold to find the remaining milk:**  
   Remaining milk = 760 - 612 = **148 gallons**.

4. **Double-check calculations:**  
   - Total milk collected today: 365 + 380 = 745.  
   - Total milk available: 745 + 15 = 760.  
   - Remaining milk: 760 - 612 = 148.  
   All calculations check out.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and intuitive, as it follows the natural flow of the problem: calculate total milk, add leftovers, and subtract the sold amount.  
- **Method 2** involves working backward, which can be useful in some contexts but is less direct for this problem.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer and more efficient for this specific problem.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Total milk collected today = 365 + 380 = **745 gallons**.  
2. Add leftover milk from yesterday: 745 + 15 = **760 gallons**.  
3. Subtract the milk sold to the factory: 760 - 612 = **148 gallons**.  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Total milk collected today: 365 + 380 = 745 gallons.  
- Total milk available: 745 + 15 = 760 gallons.  
- Remaining milk: 760 - 612 = 148 gallons.  
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **148 gallons**.
