Q: Beatrice is shopping for a new TV. First she looks at 8 TVs at one store in person. Then she looks at three times as many TVs at an online store. She looks at more TVs on an auction site online. If Beatrice looked at 42 TVs in all, how many did look at on the auction site?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Beatrice looks at **8 TVs** in person at one store.
- She looks at **three times as many TVs** at an online store. This means she looks at \( 3 \times 8 = 24 \) TVs online at the online store.
- She also looks at some TVs on an auction site.
- The total number of TVs she looks at is **42**.
- We assume no overlap between the TVs she looks at in person, online, or on the auction site.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Subtraction Approach**  
- Add the number of TVs Beatrice looks at in person and at the online store.
- Subtract this sum from the total number of TVs to find how many she looked at on the auction site.

**Method 2: Equation Approach**  
- Let \( x \) represent the number of TVs Beatrice looks at on the auction site.
- Write an equation for the total number of TVs: \( 8 + 24 + x = 42 \).
- Solve for \( x \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### Method 1: Subtraction Approach
1. Calculate the total number of TVs Beatrice looks at in person and at the online store:
   \[
   8 + 24 = 32
   \]
2. Subtract this sum from the total number of TVs:
   \[
   42 - 32 = 10
   \]
3. Beatrice looks at **10 TVs** on the auction site.

**Check:**  
- Total TVs: \( 8 + 24 + 10 = 42 \). This matches the given total, so the calculation is correct.

#### Method 2: Equation Approach
1. Let \( x \) represent the number of TVs Beatrice looks at on the auction site.
2. Write the equation for the total number of TVs:
   \[
   8 + 24 + x = 42
   \]
3. Simplify the left-hand side:
   \[
   32 + x = 42
   \]
4. Solve for \( x \) by subtracting 32 from both sides:
   \[
   x = 42 - 32
   \]
   \[
   x = 10
   \]
5. Beatrice looks at **10 TVs** on the auction site.

**Check:**  
- Substitute \( x = 10 \) back into the equation: \( 8 + 24 + 10 = 42 \). This matches the given total, so the calculation is correct.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction Approach):** This method is straightforward and involves fewer steps. It directly calculates the missing value by subtracting the known quantities from the total.
- **Method 2 (Equation Approach):** This method is more formal and generalizable, especially for more complex problems. However, it involves more steps and may be less intuitive for simple problems like this one.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this specific problem because it is simpler and faster.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1 (Subtraction Approach):**
1. Add the TVs Beatrice looks at in person and at the online store:
   \[
   8 + 24 = 32
   \]
2. Subtract this sum from the total number of TVs:
   \[
   42 - 32 = 10
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Total TVs: \( 8 + 24 + 10 = 42 \). This matches the given total, confirming the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **10**.

Q: Wendy works at a chocolate factory packing chocolates.  She can package 2 dozen chocolates in 5 minutes.  How many individual chocolates can she package in 4 hours?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Wendy can package **2 dozen chocolates in 5 minutes**.
- **1 dozen = 12 chocolates**, so 2 dozen = \( 2 \times 12 = 24 \) chocolates.
- Wendy works for **4 hours**, and **1 hour = 60 minutes**, so 4 hours = \( 4 \times 60 = 240 \) minutes.
- We assume Wendy works continuously at the same rate without breaks or interruptions.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate how many chocolates Wendy can package in 1 minute by dividing the number of chocolates she packages in 5 minutes by 5.  
- Multiply the chocolates per minute by the total number of minutes (240 minutes) to find the total number of chocolates.

**Method 2:**  
- Determine how many 5-minute intervals are in 4 hours by dividing 240 minutes by 5.  
- Multiply the number of 5-minute intervals by the number of chocolates Wendy can package in 5 minutes (24 chocolates).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Chocolates per minute approach**
1. Wendy can package 24 chocolates in 5 minutes.  
   Chocolates per minute = \( \frac{24}{5} = 4.8 \) chocolates per minute.
2. Total time = 240 minutes.  
   Total chocolates = \( 4.8 \times 240 = 1152 \) chocolates.
3. Double-check:  
   - \( 4.8 \times 240 = (4.8 \times 200) + (4.8 \times 40) \).  
   - \( 4.8 \times 200 = 960 \), and \( 4.8 \times 40 = 192 \).  
   - \( 960 + 192 = 1152 \).  
   The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2: 5-minute interval approach**
1. Total time = 240 minutes.  
   Number of 5-minute intervals = \( \frac{240}{5} = 48 \) intervals.
2. Wendy can package 24 chocolates in each 5-minute interval.  
   Total chocolates = \( 48 \times 24 \).  
3. Perform the multiplication:  
   - \( 48 \times 24 = (48 \times 20) + (48 \times 4) \).  
   - \( 48 \times 20 = 960 \), and \( 48 \times 4 = 192 \).  
   - \( 960 + 192 = 1152 \).  
   The calculation checks out.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** involves calculating chocolates per minute and multiplying by the total time. It is straightforward but requires handling decimals (4.8 chocolates per minute), which could introduce rounding errors if not careful.
- **Method 2** avoids decimals by working with whole numbers (5-minute intervals and 24 chocolates per interval). It is more intuitive and less prone to error.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 2** is better because it avoids decimals and is easier to follow.  
1. Total time = 240 minutes.  
   Number of 5-minute intervals = \( \frac{240}{5} = 48 \).  
2. Total chocolates = \( 48 \times 24 = 1152 \).  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Using Method 1: \( 4.8 \times 240 = 1152 \).  
- Using Method 2: \( 48 \times 24 = 1152 \).  
- Both methods agree, and the result is reasonable given the problem's context.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **1152**.

Q: Ginger owns a flower shop, where she sells roses, lilacs, and gardenias. On Tuesday, she sold three times more roses than lilacs, and half as many gardenias as lilacs.  If she sold 10 lilacs, what is the total number of flowers sold on Tuesday?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Ginger sold three types of flowers: roses, lilacs, and gardenias.
- On Tuesday:
  - She sold **three times more roses than lilacs**.
  - She sold **half as many gardenias as lilacs**.
  - She sold **10 lilacs**.
- Assumptions:
  - The relationships between the quantities of flowers sold are exact (e.g., "three times more" means \(3 \times 10 = 30\)).
  - No other flowers were sold beyond roses, lilacs, and gardenias.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct calculation using proportional relationships.**
- Use the given relationships to calculate the number of roses and gardenias sold based on the number of lilacs.
- Add the quantities of all three types of flowers to find the total.

**Method 2: Represent the problem algebraically.**
- Define variables for the number of roses (\(R\)), lilacs (\(L\)), and gardenias (\(G\)).
- Use the relationships \(R = 3L\) and \(G = \frac{1}{2}L\), substituting \(L = 10\) to calculate \(R\) and \(G\).
- Add \(R\), \(L\), and \(G\) to find the total.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation using proportional relationships**
1. Start with the number of lilacs: \(L = 10\).
2. Calculate the number of roses:
   - Roses are three times the number of lilacs: \(R = 3 \times 10 = 30\).
3. Calculate the number of gardenias:
   - Gardenias are half the number of lilacs: \(G = \frac{1}{2} \times 10 = 5\).
4. Add the quantities of all three types of flowers:
   - Total = \(R + L + G = 30 + 10 + 5 = 45\).

#### **Method 2: Represent the problem algebraically**
1. Define variables:
   - Let \(R\) = number of roses, \(L\) = number of lilacs, \(G\) = number of gardenias.
2. Write equations based on the relationships:
   - \(R = 3L\) (three times more roses than lilacs),
   - \(G = \frac{1}{2}L\) (half as many gardenias as lilacs).
3. Substitute \(L = 10\) into the equations:
   - \(R = 3 \times 10 = 30\),
   - \(G = \frac{1}{2} \times 10 = 5\).
4. Add the quantities of all three types of flowers:
   - Total = \(R + L + G = 30 + 10 + 5 = 45\).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and relies on direct proportional reasoning, making it quick and intuitive for this problem.
- **Method 2** is more formal and generalizable, especially for more complex problems involving multiple variables or unknowns.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient for this specific problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.
- Solution:
  - \(L = 10\),
  - \(R = 3 \times 10 = 30\),
  - \(G = \frac{1}{2} \times 10 = 5\),
  - Total = \(R + L + G = 30 + 10 + 5 = 45\).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify calculations:
  - Roses: \(3 \times 10 = 30\) (correct),
  - Gardenias: \(\frac{1}{2} \times 10 = 5\) (correct),
  - Total: \(30 + 10 + 5 = 45\) (correct).
- The relationships and calculations are consistent with the problem statement.

---

The answer is **45**.

Q: James buys 10 shirts and half as many pants.  The shirts cost $6 each and the pants cost $8 each.  How much did everything cost?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- James buys **10 shirts**.
- He buys **half as many pants as shirts**, so he buys \( \frac{10}{2} = 5 \) pants.
- Each shirt costs **$6**.
- Each pant costs **$8**.
- We assume there are no discounts, taxes, or additional fees.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the total cost of the shirts by multiplying the number of shirts by the cost per shirt.  
- Calculate the total cost of the pants by multiplying the number of pants by the cost per pant.  
- Add the two totals to find the overall cost.

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the cost of one shirt and one pant together: \( 6 + 8 = 14 \).  
- Multiply this combined cost by the number of pants (since there are half as many pants as shirts).  
- Add the cost of the remaining shirts to the total.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the total cost of the shirts:  
   \[
   \text{Cost of shirts} = 10 \times 6 = 60
   \]
2. Calculate the total cost of the pants:  
   \[
   \text{Cost of pants} = 5 \times 8 = 40
   \]
3. Add the two totals to find the overall cost:  
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = 60 + 40 = 100
   \]

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate the cost of one shirt and one pant together:  
   \[
   \text{Cost of one shirt and one pant} = 6 + 8 = 14
   \]
2. Multiply this combined cost by the number of pants (since there are half as many pants as shirts):  
   \[
   \text{Cost of 5 shirts and 5 pants} = 5 \times 14 = 70
   \]
3. Add the cost of the remaining 5 shirts (since there are 10 shirts total, and 5 were already included in the previous step):  
   \[
   \text{Cost of remaining 5 shirts} = 5 \times 6 = 30
   \]
4. Add the two totals to find the overall cost:  
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = 70 + 30 = 100
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and involves fewer steps. It directly calculates the cost of shirts and pants separately and adds them together. This method is less prone to errors because it avoids intermediate grouping.
- **Method 2** is slightly more complex because it groups items and requires additional steps to account for the remaining shirts. While it works, it introduces more opportunities for mistakes in calculations or logic.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and clarity.  
1. Calculate the cost of the shirts:  
   \[
   10 \times 6 = 60
   \]
2. Calculate the cost of the pants:  
   \[
   5 \times 8 = 40
   \]
3. Add the two totals:  
   \[
   60 + 40 = 100
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:  
   - Cost of 5 shirts and 5 pants: \( 5 \times 14 = 70 \).  
   - Cost of remaining 5 shirts: \( 5 \times 6 = 30 \).  
   - Total cost: \( 70 + 30 = 100 \).  
- Both methods agree, confirming the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **100**.

Q: A garden is filled with 105 flowers of various colors. There are twice as many red flowers as orange. There are five fewer yellow flowers than red. If there are 10 orange flowers, how many pink and purple flowers are there if they have the same amount and there are no other colors?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The garden has a total of **105 flowers**.
- There are **twice as many red flowers as orange flowers**.
- There are **five fewer yellow flowers than red flowers**.
- There are **10 orange flowers**.
- The remaining flowers are **pink and purple**, and they are in **equal amounts**.
- We assume no other colors are present in the garden.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Sequential calculation and subtraction.**
- Calculate the number of red flowers based on the orange flowers.
- Calculate the number of yellow flowers based on the red flowers.
- Subtract the total number of red, orange, and yellow flowers from 105 to find the remaining flowers.
- Divide the remaining flowers equally between pink and purple.

**Method 2: Use algebra to represent the problem.**
- Let \( R \) represent the number of red flowers, \( Y \) the number of yellow flowers, and \( P \) the number of pink flowers (equal to purple flowers).
- Write equations based on the relationships:
  - \( R = 2 \times 10 \) (red is twice orange).
  - \( Y = R - 5 \) (yellow is five fewer than red).
  - \( 105 = 10 + R + Y + 2P \) (total flowers).
- Solve the equations step by step to find \( P \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation and subtraction**
1. **Calculate the number of red flowers:**
   - Red flowers are twice the number of orange flowers.
   - \( R = 2 \times 10 = 20 \).

2. **Calculate the number of yellow flowers:**
   - Yellow flowers are five fewer than red flowers.
   - \( Y = R - 5 = 20 - 5 = 15 \).

3. **Calculate the total number of red, orange, and yellow flowers:**
   - \( 10 + 20 + 15 = 45 \).

4. **Find the remaining flowers:**
   - Total flowers in the garden = 105.
   - Remaining flowers = \( 105 - 45 = 60 \).

5. **Divide the remaining flowers equally between pink and purple:**
   - Pink and purple flowers are equal in number.
   - \( P = \frac{60}{2} = 30 \).

6. **Check calculations:**
   - Total flowers = \( 10 + 20 + 15 + 30 + 30 = 105 \), which matches the given total.

#### **Method 2: Use algebra to represent the problem**
1. **Define variables:**
   - \( R = \) number of red flowers.
   - \( Y = \) number of yellow flowers.
   - \( P = \) number of pink flowers (equal to purple flowers).

2. **Write equations:**
   - \( R = 2 \times 10 = 20 \) (red is twice orange).
   - \( Y = R - 5 = 20 - 5 = 15 \) (yellow is five fewer than red).
   - Total flowers: \( 105 = 10 + R + Y + 2P \).

3. **Substitute known values into the total equation:**
   - \( 105 = 10 + 20 + 15 + 2P \).
   - \( 105 = 45 + 2P \).

4. **Solve for \( P \):**
   - \( 2P = 105 - 45 \).
   - \( 2P = 60 \).
   - \( P = \frac{60}{2} = 30 \).

5. **Check calculations:**
   - Total flowers = \( 10 + 20 + 15 + 30 + 30 = 105 \), which matches the given total.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and intuitive, as it involves sequential calculations based on the relationships provided. It is easy to follow and verify.
- **Method 2** uses algebra, which is more systematic and scalable for more complex problems. However, it may be less intuitive for simpler problems like this one.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and easier to apply in this case.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and clarity.
1. Red flowers: \( R = 2 \times 10 = 20 \).
2. Yellow flowers: \( Y = R - 5 = 20 - 5 = 15 \).
3. Total red, orange, and yellow flowers: \( 10 + 20 + 15 = 45 \).
4. Remaining flowers: \( 105 - 45 = 60 \).
5. Pink and purple flowers: \( P = \frac{60}{2} = 30 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Total flowers = \( 10 + 20 + 15 + 30 + 30 = 105 \), which matches the given total.
- All calculations are consistent with the problem's constraints.

### Final Answer:
The number of pink and purple flowers is **30 each**. The answer is **30**.

Q: Adam's father deposited $2000 in the bank. It receives 8% interest paid throughout the year, and he withdraws the interest as soon as it is deposited.  How much will Adam’s father have, including his deposit and the interest received after 2 and a half years?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Adam's father deposited $2000 in the bank.
- The bank pays 8% annual interest, which is withdrawn immediately after being deposited (i.e., the interest does not compound).
- The time period is 2.5 years.
- We assume the interest is calculated annually and paid at the end of each year.
- The principal ($2000) remains in the account and does not change.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the annual interest for the principal ($2000) and multiply it by the number of years (2.5). Add the total interest to the original deposit to find the total amount.

**Method 2:**  
Break the calculation into yearly intervals. Compute the interest for each year and for the half-year separately, then sum the interest and add it to the original deposit.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct multiplication of annual interest by time**
1. Calculate the annual interest:  
   \[
   \text{Annual interest} = 2000 \times \frac{8}{100} = 2000 \times 0.08 = 160
   \]
   So, Adam's father earns $160 in interest each year.

2. Multiply the annual interest by the total time (2.5 years):  
   \[
   \text{Total interest} = 160 \times 2.5 = 400
   \]

3. Add the total interest to the original deposit:  
   \[
   \text{Total amount} = 2000 + 400 = 2400
   \]

#### **Method 2: Break into yearly intervals**
1. Calculate the interest for the first 2 years:  
   \[
   \text{Interest for 2 years} = 160 \times 2 = 320
   \]

2. Calculate the interest for the half-year:  
   \[
   \text{Half-year interest} = 2000 \times \frac{8}{100} \times \frac{1}{2} = 2000 \times 0.08 \times 0.5 = 80
   \]

3. Add the interest for 2 years and the half-year:  
   \[
   \text{Total interest} = 320 + 80 = 400
   \]

4. Add the total interest to the original deposit:  
   \[
   \text{Total amount} = 2000 + 400 = 2400
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is simpler and more direct, as it uses a single formula to calculate the total interest over the entire time period. It avoids breaking the calculation into smaller intervals, reducing the chance of error.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and breaks the problem into smaller steps, which can be helpful for understanding the process but is less efficient for straightforward calculations.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.  
- Using Method 1:  
   \[
   \text{Annual interest} = 2000 \times 0.08 = 160
   \]
   \[
   \text{Total interest} = 160 \times 2.5 = 400
   \]
   \[
   \text{Total amount} = 2000 + 400 = 2400
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:  
   \[
   \text{Interest for 2 years} = 160 \times 2 = 320
   \]
   \[
   \text{Half-year interest} = 2000 \times 0.08 \times 0.5 = 80
   \]
   \[
   \text{Total interest} = 320 + 80 = 400
   \]
   \[
   \text{Total amount} = 2000 + 400 = 2400
   \]
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the accuracy.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **2400**.

2400.0

Q: Calvin signed up for a gym training service to lose some pounds. If he weighed 250 pounds to start with and lost 8 pounds every month during the training sessions, what's his weight after one year?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Calvin starts with a weight of **250 pounds**.
- He loses **8 pounds every month** during the training sessions.
- The training lasts for **1 year**, which is **12 months**.
- We assume the weight loss is consistent and there are no interruptions or variations in the monthly weight loss.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct multiplication and subtraction.**
- Multiply the monthly weight loss (8 pounds) by the number of months (12) to find the total weight lost.
- Subtract the total weight lost from the starting weight (250 pounds).

**Method 2: Iterative subtraction.**
- Start with the initial weight (250 pounds).
- Subtract 8 pounds repeatedly for each of the 12 months.
- Keep track of the weight after each subtraction to ensure accuracy.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct multiplication and subtraction**
1. Calculate the total weight lost:
   \[
   \text{Total weight lost} = 8 \times 12 = 96 \, \text{pounds}.
   \]
2. Subtract the total weight lost from the starting weight:
   \[
   \text{Final weight} = 250 - 96 = 154 \, \text{pounds}.
   \]
3. Double-check:
   - Recalculate \( 8 \times 12 = 96 \) to confirm the total weight lost.
   - Recalculate \( 250 - 96 = 154 \) to confirm the final weight.

**Potential pitfalls:**
- Miscalculating the multiplication or subtraction could lead to an incorrect result, but the steps are straightforward and easy to verify.

#### **Method 2: Iterative subtraction**
1. Start with the initial weight of 250 pounds.
2. Subtract 8 pounds for each month:
   - After 1 month: \( 250 - 8 = 242 \).
   - After 2 months: \( 242 - 8 = 234 \).
   - After 3 months: \( 234 - 8 = 226 \).
   - After 4 months: \( 226 - 8 = 218 \).
   - After 5 months: \( 218 - 8 = 210 \).
   - After 6 months: \( 210 - 8 = 202 \).
   - After 7 months: \( 202 - 8 = 194 \).
   - After 8 months: \( 194 - 8 = 186 \).
   - After 9 months: \( 186 - 8 = 178 \).
   - After 10 months: \( 178 - 8 = 170 \).
   - After 11 months: \( 170 - 8 = 162 \).
   - After 12 months: \( 162 - 8 = 154 \).
3. Double-check:
   - Verify that 12 subtractions of 8 pounds each result in a total weight loss of \( 8 \times 12 = 96 \) pounds.
   - Confirm that \( 250 - 96 = 154 \).

**Potential pitfalls:**
- This method is more prone to errors due to the repetitive nature of the calculations, especially if one subtraction is performed incorrectly. However, it provides a step-by-step breakdown of the weight loss process.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more efficient because it uses a single multiplication and subtraction, making it faster and less prone to errors. It is also easier to double-check.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and provides a month-by-month breakdown, which can be useful for understanding the process but is more time-consuming and error-prone due to repetitive calculations.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and efficiency.
1. Calculate the total weight lost:
   \[
   8 \times 12 = 96 \, \text{pounds}.
   \]
2. Subtract the total weight lost from the starting weight:
   \[
   250 - 96 = 154 \, \text{pounds}.
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate \( 8 \times 12 = 96 \) to confirm the total weight lost.
- Recalculate \( 250 - 96 = 154 \) to confirm the final weight.
- The result is reasonable: Calvin loses 96 pounds over 12 months, which aligns with the given rate of 8 pounds per month.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **154 pounds**.

Q: A florist offers for sale 75 narcissus flowers and 90 chrysanthemums. Using all the flowers, how many bouquets containing 5 flowers each can he make?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The florist has **75 narcissus flowers** and **90 chrysanthemums**.
- Each bouquet must contain **5 flowers**.
- We assume all flowers are used, and no flowers are wasted or left over.
- We assume the bouquets can contain any combination of narcissus and chrysanthemums.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total number of flowers (narcissus + chrysanthemums), then divide the total by 5 to determine the number of bouquets. This method directly uses division to find the result.

**Method 2:**  
Calculate how many bouquets can be made separately from the narcissus and chrysanthemums, then add the results. This method breaks the problem into smaller parts and combines the results.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Total flowers divided by 5**
1. Add the total number of flowers:  
   \( 75 + 90 = 165 \).  
   The florist has **165 flowers** in total.

2. Divide the total number of flowers by 5 to find the number of bouquets:  
   \( 165 \div 5 = 33 \).  
   The florist can make **33 bouquets**.

3. Check for leftover flowers:  
   Since \( 165 \div 5 = 33 \) with no remainder, all flowers are used, and there are no leftovers.

#### **Method 2: Separate calculations for narcissus and chrysanthemums**
1. Calculate how many bouquets can be made from the narcissus:  
   \( 75 \div 5 = 15 \).  
   The florist can make **15 bouquets** from the narcissus.

2. Calculate how many bouquets can be made from the chrysanthemums:  
   \( 90 \div 5 = 18 \).  
   The florist can make **18 bouquets** from the chrysanthemums.

3. Add the results:  
   \( 15 + 18 = 33 \).  
   The florist can make **33 bouquets** in total.

4. Check for leftover flowers:  
   Since \( 75 \div 5 = 15 \) and \( 90 \div 5 = 18 \) both have no remainder, all flowers are used, and there are no leftovers.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it combines all flowers first and uses a single division operation. It avoids breaking the problem into smaller parts, which reduces the chance of errors.
- **Method 2** is slightly more detailed and involves two separate calculations, which may be useful for verifying results or understanding the contribution of each type of flower. However, it is less efficient for this problem.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.  
1. Add the total number of flowers:  
   \( 75 + 90 = 165 \).  
2. Divide the total number of flowers by 5:  
   \( 165 \div 5 = 33 \).  
3. Verify there are no leftover flowers:  
   \( 165 \div 5 = 33 \) with no remainder.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using **Method 2**:  
   \( 75 \div 5 = 15 \), \( 90 \div 5 = 18 \), \( 15 + 18 = 33 \).  
   Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The florist can make **33 bouquets**.  
The answer is **33**.

Q: Philip is a painter. He makes 2 paintings per day. If he already has 20 paintings, how many paintings in total will he have after 30 days?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Philip is a painter who creates **2 paintings per day**.
- He already has **20 paintings** completed.
- He will paint for **30 days**.
- We assume Philip paints consistently every day without interruptions or changes in his daily output.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total number of paintings Philip will create in 30 days, then add this to the 20 paintings he already has.  
Logic: Multiply the daily output (2 paintings/day) by the number of days (30 days), then add the result to the initial 20 paintings.

**Method 2:**  
Use a step-by-step cumulative approach: Start with the initial 20 paintings and repeatedly add 2 paintings for each day over 30 days.  
Logic: This method simulates the process of painting day by day, summing the total incrementally.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation**
1. Calculate the number of paintings Philip will create in 30 days:  
   \( 2 \, \text{paintings/day} \times 30 \, \text{days} = 60 \, \text{paintings} \).
2. Add the 60 paintings to the initial 20 paintings:  
   \( 20 + 60 = 80 \).

**Checks:**  
- Multiplication: \( 2 \times 30 = 60 \) is straightforward and correct.  
- Addition: \( 20 + 60 = 80 \) is simple and accurate.  
- No pitfalls in this method as it uses direct arithmetic.

#### **Method 2: Step-by-step cumulative addition**
1. Start with the initial 20 paintings.  
2. Add 2 paintings for each day over 30 days:  
   \( 20 + 2 + 2 + 2 + \dots + 2 \, (\text{30 times}) \).  
3. Simplify the repeated addition using multiplication:  
   \( 2 \times 30 = 60 \).  
4. Add the result to the initial 20 paintings:  
   \( 20 + 60 = 80 \).

**Checks:**  
- Repeated addition is equivalent to multiplication, so \( 2 \times 30 = 60 \) is valid.  
- Adding \( 20 + 60 = 80 \) is correct.  
- This method is more tedious but leads to the same result.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more efficient and straightforward, as it uses direct multiplication and addition. It avoids unnecessary steps and is less prone to errors.  
- **Method 2** is conceptually clear but involves repetitive addition, which is time-consuming and prone to mistakes in manual calculations.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and reliability.  
- Solution:  
   \( 2 \times 30 = 60 \) paintings created in 30 days.  
   \( 20 + 60 = 80 \) total paintings.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify multiplication: \( 2 \times 30 = 60 \).  
- Verify addition: \( 20 + 60 = 80 \).  
- The result is reasonable: Philip paints consistently, and the calculations align with the given information.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **80**.

Q: Will's mom gave him $74 to go shopping. He bought a sweater for $9, a T-shirt for $11 and a pair of shoes for $30. He then returned his shoes for a 90% refund.  How much money does Will have left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Will's mom gave him $74 to go shopping.
- Will bought three items:
  - Sweater: $9
  - T-shirt: $11
  - Shoes: $30
- Will returned the shoes for a **90% refund**.
- We assume no additional fees, taxes, or discounts apply.
- The goal is to calculate how much money Will has left after all transactions.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total cost of the items Will initially bought: $9 + $11 + $30.  
2. Determine the refund amount for the shoes (90% of $30).  
3. Subtract the net spending (total cost minus refund) from the initial $74.

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract the cost of the sweater and T-shirt directly from the initial $74.  
2. Subtract the cost of the shoes, then add back the refund amount (90% of $30).  
3. Calculate the remaining money step by step.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. **Calculate the total cost of items Will initially bought:**  
   Sweater: $9  
   T-shirt: $11  
   Shoes: $30  
   Total cost = $9 + $11 + $30 = **$50**.

2. **Determine the refund amount for the shoes:**  
   Refund = 90% of $30 = \( 0.9 \times 30 = 27 \).  
   Refund amount = **$27**.

3. **Calculate the net spending:**  
   Net spending = Total cost - Refund = $50 - $27 = **$23**.

4. **Subtract the net spending from the initial $74:**  
   Remaining money = $74 - $23 = **$51**.

---

#### **Method 2:**
1. **Subtract the cost of the sweater and T-shirt directly from the initial $74:**  
   Sweater: $9  
   T-shirt: $11  
   Remaining after these purchases = $74 - $9 - $11 = $74 - $20 = **$54**.

2. **Subtract the cost of the shoes:**  
   Remaining after buying shoes = $54 - $30 = **$24**.

3. **Add back the refund amount for the shoes:**  
   Refund = 90% of $30 = \( 0.9 \times 30 = 27 \).  
   Remaining after refund = $24 + $27 = **$51**.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more straightforward because it calculates the total cost first and then adjusts for the refund. It avoids intermediate steps and is less prone to errors.  
- **Method 2** involves more intermediate steps, which increases the chance of mistakes but may be easier for mental math.  

Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and reliable for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

**Method 1 is better.**  
1. Total cost of items = $9 + $11 + $30 = **$50**.  
2. Refund for shoes = \( 0.9 \times 30 = 27 \).  
3. Net spending = $50 - $27 = **$23**.  
4. Remaining money = $74 - $23 = **$51**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Verify refund calculation: \( 0.9 \times 30 = 27 \), correct.  
- Verify total cost: $9 + $11 + $30 = $50, correct.  
- Verify net spending: $50 - $27 = $23, correct.  
- Verify remaining money: $74 - $23 = $51, correct.  

The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **51**.

Q: Roy has 2 blue pens. He has twice as many black and 2 less than twice as many red than black pens. How many pens does Roy have in all?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Roy has **2 blue pens**.
- Roy has **twice as many black pens as blue pens**.
- Roy has **2 less than twice as many red pens as black pens**.
- We assume all pens are accounted for, and there are no fractional pens.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Sequential calculation using direct relationships.**
- Start with the number of blue pens (2).
- Calculate the number of black pens as twice the number of blue pens.
- Calculate the number of red pens as 2 less than twice the number of black pens.
- Add all the pens together.

**Method 2: Use algebra to represent relationships.**
- Let the number of blue pens = \( b = 2 \).
- Let the number of black pens = \( 2b \).
- Let the number of red pens = \( 2(2b) - 2 \).
- Substitute \( b = 2 \) into the equations and solve for the total.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation using direct relationships**
1. Start with the number of blue pens: \( 2 \).
2. Calculate the number of black pens:
   - Black pens = \( 2 \times 2 = 4 \).
3. Calculate the number of red pens:
   - Red pens = \( 2 \times 4 - 2 = 8 - 2 = 6 \).
4. Add all the pens together:
   - Total pens = Blue pens + Black pens + Red pens = \( 2 + 4 + 6 = 12 \).

#### **Method 2: Use algebra to represent relationships**
1. Define variables:
   - Blue pens = \( b = 2 \).
   - Black pens = \( 2b \).
   - Red pens = \( 2(2b) - 2 \).
2. Substitute \( b = 2 \) into the equations:
   - Black pens = \( 2b = 2 \times 2 = 4 \).
   - Red pens = \( 2(2b) - 2 = 2(2 \times 2) - 2 = 8 - 2 = 6 \).
3. Add all the pens together:
   - Total pens = \( b + 2b + (2(2b) - 2) \).
   - Substitute \( b = 2 \): \( 2 + 4 + 6 = 12 \).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and intuitive, as it follows the relationships step by step. It is easy to follow and avoids unnecessary complexity.
- **Method 2** is more formal and uses algebra, which is useful for more complex problems. However, for this problem, it introduces extra steps without adding clarity or efficiency.
- **Conclusion**: Method 1 is better for this problem due to its simplicity and directness.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Blue pens = \( 2 \).
2. Black pens = \( 2 \times 2 = 4 \).
3. Red pens = \( 2 \times 4 - 2 = 6 \).
4. Total pens = \( 2 + 4 + 6 = 12 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the relationships:
  - Black pens = \( 2 \times 2 = 4 \) (correct).
  - Red pens = \( 2 \times 4 - 2 = 6 \) (correct).
- Verify the total:
  - Total pens = \( 2 + 4 + 6 = 12 \) (correct).
- The solution is consistent and reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **12**.

Q: Mark buys a Magic card for $100, which then triples in value.  How much profit would he make selling it?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Mark buys a Magic card for $100.
- The card triples in value, meaning its new value is 3 times the original purchase price.
- Profit is defined as the difference between the selling price and the purchase price.
- We assume no additional costs (e.g., taxes, fees) are involved in the transaction.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the new value of the card by multiplying the purchase price by 3.  
- Subtract the original purchase price from the new value to determine the profit.

**Method 2:**  
- Use a formula for profit:  
  \[
  \text{Profit} = (\text{Multiplier} - 1) \times \text{Purchase Price}
  \]  
  - Here, the multiplier is 3 (since the value triples).  
  - This method directly calculates the profit without explicitly finding the new value.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. **Calculate the new value of the card:**  
   \[
   \text{New Value} = 3 \times 100 = 300
   \]
2. **Calculate the profit:**  
   \[
   \text{Profit} = \text{New Value} - \text{Purchase Price} = 300 - 100 = 200
   \]
3. **Check for pitfalls:**  
   - Ensure the multiplication and subtraction are accurate.  
   - Confirm that the profit is calculated as the difference between the selling price and the purchase price.

#### **Method 2:**
1. **Use the profit formula:**  
   \[
   \text{Profit} = (\text{Multiplier} - 1) \times \text{Purchase Price}
   \]
   Substituting the values:  
   \[
   \text{Profit} = (3 - 1) \times 100 = 2 \times 100 = 200
   \]
2. **Check for pitfalls:**  
   - Ensure the multiplier is correctly reduced by 1 to account for the original purchase price.  
   - Verify the multiplication is accurate.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more intuitive and straightforward, as it explicitly calculates the new value before determining the profit. This makes it easier to follow for someone unfamiliar with profit formulas.  
- **Method 2** is more efficient because it skips the intermediate step of calculating the new value, directly focusing on the profit. However, it requires familiarity with the profit formula and may be less intuitive.  
- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result. The choice depends on the context: Method 1 is better for clarity, while Method 2 is better for efficiency.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- For clarity, **Method 1** is chosen.  
1. **Calculate the new value of the card:**  
   \[
   \text{New Value} = 3 \times 100 = 300
   \]
2. **Calculate the profit:**  
   \[
   \text{Profit} = \text{New Value} - \text{Purchase Price} = 300 - 100 = 200
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using **Method 2**:  
  \[
  \text{Profit} = (3 - 1) \times 100 = 2 \times 100 = 200
  \]
- Both methods yield the same result. The calculations are consistent and reasonable.  

### Final Answer:
The answer is **200**.

Q: In two years, Ophelia will be four times as old as Lennon. If Lennon is currently eight years old, how old is Ophelia?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Lennon is currently 8 years old.
- In two years, Ophelia will be four times as old as Lennon.
- We assume ages are measured in whole years and that no other factors (e.g., birthdays) affect the calculation.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Algebraic Approach**  
- Let Ophelia's current age be \( x \).  
- In two years, Lennon will be \( 8 + 2 = 10 \) years old, and Ophelia will be \( x + 2 \).  
- According to the problem, \( x + 2 = 4 \times 10 \). Solve for \( x \).

**Method 2: Logical Reasoning and Backtracking**  
- Start with Lennon's age in two years (\( 10 \)) and calculate what four times that age would be (\( 4 \times 10 = 40 \)).  
- Since Ophelia will be 40 in two years, subtract 2 to find her current age.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Algebraic Approach**
1. Define Ophelia's current age as \( x \).  
2. In two years, Ophelia's age will be \( x + 2 \), and Lennon's age will be \( 8 + 2 = 10 \).  
3. Set up the equation:  
   \[
   x + 2 = 4 \times 10
   \]
4. Simplify the equation:  
   \[
   x + 2 = 40
   \]
5. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   x = 40 - 2 = 38
   \]
6. Check the solution:  
   - In two years, Ophelia will be \( 38 + 2 = 40 \), and Lennon will be \( 8 + 2 = 10 \).  
   - \( 40 = 4 \times 10 \), so the solution is correct.

#### **Method 2: Logical Reasoning and Backtracking**
1. In two years, Lennon will be \( 8 + 2 = 10 \) years old.  
2. Ophelia will be four times Lennon's age in two years:  
   \[
   4 \times 10 = 40
   \]
3. Since Ophelia will be 40 in two years, subtract 2 to find her current age:  
   \[
   40 - 2 = 38
   \]
4. Check the solution:  
   - In two years, Ophelia will be \( 38 + 2 = 40 \), and Lennon will be \( 8 + 2 = 10 \).  
   - \( 40 = 4 \times 10 \), so the solution is correct.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Algebraic Approach)**: This method is systematic and works well for problems involving relationships between variables. It is reliable and ensures clarity in setting up and solving the equation.  
- **Method 2 (Logical Reasoning)**: This method is faster and more intuitive for simple problems like this one, as it avoids the need for formal algebra. However, it may be less reliable for more complex scenarios.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- For this problem, **Method 2** is more efficient and straightforward.  
- Solution:  
  1. In two years, Lennon will be \( 10 \).  
  2. Ophelia will be \( 4 \times 10 = 40 \) in two years.  
  3. Subtract 2 to find Ophelia's current age: \( 40 - 2 = 38 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- In two years, Ophelia will be \( 38 + 2 = 40 \), and Lennon will be \( 8 + 2 = 10 \).  
- \( 40 = 4 \times 10 \), confirming the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **38**.

Q: Rachel is an artist. She posts a speed painting video each week on her Instagram account to promote her work. To save time, she paints and records 4 videos at a time. It takes her about 1 hour to set up her painting supplies and her camera. Then she records herself painting for 1 hour per painting. She takes another 1 hour to clean up. Each video takes 1.5 hours to edit and post. How long does it take Rachel to produce a speed painting video?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Rachel produces 4 speed painting videos at a time.
- It takes her:
  - 1 hour to set up her painting supplies and camera.
  - 1 hour per painting to record herself painting (4 paintings = 4 hours total).
  - 1 hour to clean up after painting.
  - 1.5 hours per video to edit and post (4 videos = 4 × 1.5 = 6 hours total).
- We assume all tasks are completed sequentially, and there is no overlap between activities.

The goal is to calculate how much time it takes Rachel to produce **one** speed painting video.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Calculate the total time for producing 4 videos, then divide by 4.
- Add up all the time required for setup, painting, cleanup, and editing/posting for 4 videos.
- Divide the total time by 4 to find the time per video.

#### Method 2: Break down the time required for each task and allocate it proportionally to one video.
- Setup and cleanup are shared across all 4 videos, so divide their time equally among the 4 videos.
- Painting and editing/posting are done individually for each video, so their time is directly added to the per-video calculation.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Total time for 4 videos, then divide by 4.
1. **Setup time**: 1 hour.
2. **Painting time**: 1 hour per painting × 4 paintings = 4 hours.
3. **Cleanup time**: 1 hour.
4. **Editing/posting time**: 1.5 hours per video × 4 videos = 6 hours.
5. **Total time for 4 videos**: 1 + 4 + 1 + 6 = 12 hours.
6. **Time per video**: 12 hours ÷ 4 = 3 hours.

#### Method 2: Break down tasks and allocate proportionally.
1. **Setup time per video**: 1 hour ÷ 4 videos = 0.25 hours.
2. **Painting time per video**: 1 hour (since each painting is done individually).
3. **Cleanup time per video**: 1 hour ÷ 4 videos = 0.25 hours.
4. **Editing/posting time per video**: 1.5 hours (since editing/posting is done individually).
5. **Total time per video**: 0.25 + 1 + 0.25 + 1.5 = 3 hours.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and efficient, as it calculates the total time for 4 videos and divides by 4. It avoids unnecessary intermediate steps.
- **Method 2** is more detailed, breaking down each task and allocating time proportionally. While it provides clarity on how each task contributes to the total, it involves more steps and is less efficient for this problem.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Using Method 1:
  - Total time for 4 videos = 12 hours.
  - Time per video = 12 ÷ 4 = 3 hours.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:
  - Setup time per video = 0.25 hours.
  - Painting time per video = 1 hour.
  - Cleanup time per video = 0.25 hours.
  - Editing/posting time per video = 1.5 hours.
  - Total time per video = 0.25 + 1 + 0.25 + 1.5 = 3 hours.
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the calculation is correct.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **3 hours**.

Q: If it takes 10 people 10 days to shovel 10,000 pounds of coal, how many days will it take half of these ten people to shovel 40,000 pounds of coal?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- **Given Information:**
  - 10 people can shovel 10,000 pounds of coal in 10 days.
  - Half of these 10 people (i.e., 5 people) need to shovel 40,000 pounds of coal.
- **Assumptions:**
  - The rate of work is proportional to the number of people and the amount of coal.
  - Each person works at the same rate.
  - There are no external factors (e.g., fatigue, efficiency changes) affecting the rate of work.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### **Method 1: Use proportional reasoning based on the rate of work.**
- First, calculate the rate at which 10 people shovel coal (pounds per day).
- Then, adjust the rate for 5 people.
- Finally, calculate the time required for 5 people to shovel 40,000 pounds.

#### **Method 2: Scale the problem step by step.**
- First, determine how long it would take 10 people to shovel 40,000 pounds.
- Then, adjust the time for 5 people by considering their reduced workforce.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### **Method 1: Proportional reasoning based on the rate of work.**
1. **Calculate the rate of work for 10 people:**
   - 10 people shovel 10,000 pounds in 10 days.
   - Rate of work = \( \frac{10,000 \text{ pounds}}{10 \text{ days}} = 1,000 \text{ pounds/day} \) for 10 people.
   - Therefore, the rate per person = \( \frac{1,000 \text{ pounds/day}}{10 \text{ people}} = 100 \text{ pounds/day/person} \).

2. **Adjust the rate for 5 people:**
   - 5 people will shovel coal at \( 5 \times 100 = 500 \text{ pounds/day} \).

3. **Calculate the time required for 5 people to shovel 40,000 pounds:**
   - Time = \( \frac{\text{Total coal}}{\text{Rate of work}} = \frac{40,000 \text{ pounds}}{500 \text{ pounds/day}} = 80 \text{ days} \).

#### **Method 2: Scale the problem step by step.**
1. **Determine how long it would take 10 people to shovel 40,000 pounds:**
   - 10 people shovel 10,000 pounds in 10 days.
   - To shovel 40,000 pounds (4 times the original amount), it would take \( 10 \text{ days} \times 4 = 40 \text{ days} \).

2. **Adjust the time for 5 people:**
   - Since 5 people work at half the rate of 10 people, it will take twice as long for them to shovel the same amount of coal.
   - Time for 5 people = \( 40 \text{ days} \times 2 = 80 \text{ days} \).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and focuses on calculating the rate of work, which is efficient and avoids unnecessary intermediate steps.
- **Method 2** involves scaling the problem step by step, which is intuitive but slightly longer and less efficient.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer and more reliable for proportional problems.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and efficiency.
- Solution: Using the rate of work, 5 people will take \( \frac{40,000 \text{ pounds}}{500 \text{ pounds/day}} = 80 \text{ days} \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- **Check the rate of work:**
  - 10 people shovel 10,000 pounds in 10 days → 1,000 pounds/day → 100 pounds/day/person.
  - 5 people shovel at 500 pounds/day.
- **Check the time calculation:**
  - \( \frac{40,000 \text{ pounds}}{500 \text{ pounds/day}} = 80 \text{ days} \).
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **80 days**.

Q: Wario is a field goal kicker on the high school football team.  He attempts 60 field goals throughout the season.  He misses 1/4 of the field goals.  Of the field goals missed 20 percent were wide right.  How many missed field goals went wide right?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Wario attempts **60 field goals** throughout the season.  
- He misses **1/4** of the field goals.  
- Of the missed field goals, **20%** were wide right.  
- Assumptions:  
  - The fraction of missed field goals (1/4) is applied to the total number of field goals attempted.  
  - The percentage of wide-right misses (20%) is applied only to the missed field goals.  
  - All calculations are exact, and no rounding is required.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total number of missed field goals by multiplying the total attempts (60) by the fraction missed (1/4).  
2. Calculate the number of wide-right misses by multiplying the missed field goals by the percentage wide right (20%).  

**Method 2:**  
1. Represent the problem as a sequence of fractions and percentages applied step by step.  
2. First, calculate the fraction of field goals missed (1/4 of 60).  
3. Then, calculate the fraction of wide-right misses (20% of the missed field goals).  

Both methods involve similar calculations but differ in how the steps are organized.

---

### Step 3: Work through all intermediate steps in detail for each method.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation using multiplication.**
1. **Calculate missed field goals:**  
   Total field goals attempted = 60.  
   Fraction missed = 1/4.  
   Missed field goals = \( 60 \times \frac{1}{4} = 15 \).  

2. **Calculate wide-right misses:**  
   Percentage wide right = 20% = 0.2.  
   Wide-right misses = \( 15 \times 0.2 = 3 \).  

3. **Check calculations:**  
   - Missed field goals: \( 60 \div 4 = 15 \), correct.  
   - Wide-right misses: \( 15 \times 0.2 = 3 \), correct.  

---

#### **Method 2: Sequential fraction and percentage application.**
1. **Calculate missed field goals:**  
   Fraction missed = 1/4.  
   Missed field goals = \( \frac{1}{4} \times 60 = 15 \).  

2. **Calculate wide-right misses:**  
   Fraction wide right = 20% = \( \frac{20}{100} = \frac{1}{5} \).  
   Wide-right misses = \( \frac{1}{5} \times 15 = 3 \).  

3. **Check calculations:**  
   - Missed field goals: \( \frac{1}{4} \times 60 = 15 \), correct.  
   - Wide-right misses: \( \frac{1}{5} \times 15 = 3 \), correct.  

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more straightforward, as it uses direct multiplication and avoids converting percentages into fractions.  
- **Method 2** is slightly more abstract, requiring conversion of percentages into fractions, which may introduce errors if not done carefully.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer and more efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

**Method 1 is better.**  
1. Missed field goals = \( 60 \times \frac{1}{4} = 15 \).  
2. Wide-right misses = \( 15 \times 0.2 = 3 \).  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Total field goals attempted = 60.  
- Missed field goals = \( 60 \div 4 = 15 \), correct.  
- Wide-right misses = \( 15 \times 0.2 = 3 \), correct.  
- The result is reasonable: 3 wide-right misses out of 15 total missed field goals aligns with the given percentages.

---

The answer is **3**.

Q: There are 480 zombies in the shopping mall. If the number of zombies doubled every day, how many days ago were there less than 50 zombies in the mall?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are currently 480 zombies in the shopping mall.
- The number of zombies doubles every day.
- We need to determine how many days ago there were fewer than 50 zombies in the mall.
- Assumptions:
  - The doubling process is consistent and occurs exactly once per day.
  - The number of zombies is a whole number at all times.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Backward calculation using division.**  
- Start with the current number of zombies (480) and divide by 2 repeatedly to find the number of zombies on previous days. Stop when the number becomes less than 50. Count the number of divisions performed.

**Method 2: Use logarithms to solve the doubling equation.**  
- The doubling process can be modeled as \( N = 480 \times (1/2)^d \), where \( N \) is the number of zombies, and \( d \) is the number of days ago. Solve for \( d \) when \( N < 50 \) using logarithms.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Backward calculation using division**
1. Start with 480 zombies.
2. Divide by 2 repeatedly to find the number of zombies on previous days:
   - Day 0 (current): 480 zombies.
   - Day 1: \( 480 \div 2 = 240 \) zombies.
   - Day 2: \( 240 \div 2 = 120 \) zombies.
   - Day 3: \( 120 \div 2 = 60 \) zombies.
   - Day 4: \( 60 \div 2 = 30 \) zombies (less than 50).
3. Count the number of divisions: It took 4 divisions to reach fewer than 50 zombies.
4. Therefore, 4 days ago, there were fewer than 50 zombies.

---

#### **Method 2: Use logarithms to solve the doubling equation**
1. The doubling process can be modeled as \( N = 480 \times (1/2)^d \), where \( N \) is the number of zombies, and \( d \) is the number of days ago.
2. Solve for \( d \) when \( N < 50 \):
   - \( 50 = 480 \times (1/2)^d \).
   - Divide both sides by 480: \( \frac{50}{480} = (1/2)^d \).
   - Simplify: \( \frac{50}{480} = \frac{5}{48} \).
   - Take the logarithm of both sides: \( \log(\frac{5}{48}) = d \cdot \log(\frac{1}{2}) \).
   - Solve for \( d \): \( d = \frac{\log(\frac{5}{48})}{\log(\frac{1}{2})} \).
3. Calculate the values:
   - \( \log(\frac{5}{48}) \approx \log(0.1041667) \approx -0.9828 \).
   - \( \log(\frac{1}{2}) \approx -0.3010 \).
   - \( d = \frac{-0.9828}{-0.3010} \approx 3.26 \).
4. Since \( d \) must be a whole number (days), round up to the nearest integer: \( d = 4 \).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1 (division):**  
  - Simple and intuitive, especially for problems involving repeated halving.  
  - Requires manual calculations but avoids complex mathematical operations.  
  - Reliable and easy to double-check.

- **Method 2 (logarithms):**  
  - More mathematically sophisticated and efficient for large numbers or complex doubling problems.  
  - Requires familiarity with logarithms and a calculator.  
  - Slightly less intuitive and prone to rounding errors.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this problem due to its simplicity and clarity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

Using **Method 1**, we determined that 4 days ago, there were fewer than 50 zombies in the mall.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Verify by recalculating backward:
  - Day 0: 480 zombies.
  - Day 1: \( 480 \div 2 = 240 \).
  - Day 2: \( 240 \div 2 = 120 \).
  - Day 3: \( 120 \div 2 = 60 \).
  - Day 4: \( 60 \div 2 = 30 \) (less than 50).
- The calculations confirm the result.

---

The answer is **4 days ago**.

4.0

Q: William's class set a goal each week of the number of cans of food that is to be collected. On the first day, 20 cans were collected. Then the number of cans increased by 5 each day. If they collected 5 days a week to reach their goal, how many cans of food was their goal per week?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- William's class collects cans of food over 5 days each week.
- On the first day, they collect **20 cans**.
- Each subsequent day, the number of cans collected increases by **5** (i.e., an arithmetic progression).
- We are tasked with finding the **total number of cans collected in 5 days**, which represents their weekly goal.
- Assumptions:
  - The increase in cans collected each day is consistent (arithmetic progression).
  - No days are skipped, and the collection happens for exactly 5 days.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Use the formula for the sum of an arithmetic sequence.**
- The total number of cans collected over 5 days is the sum of the first 5 terms of an arithmetic sequence.
- The formula for the sum of the first \(n\) terms of an arithmetic sequence is:
  \[
  S_n = \frac{n}{2} \cdot (a + l)
  \]
  where:
  - \(n\) is the number of terms (5 days),
  - \(a\) is the first term (20 cans on the first day),
  - \(l\) is the last term (number of cans collected on the 5th day).

**Method 2: Calculate the number of cans collected each day and sum them directly.**
- Start with 20 cans on the first day.
- Add 5 more cans each subsequent day to find the number of cans collected on each day.
- Add the daily totals to find the weekly total.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Use the formula for the sum of an arithmetic sequence.**
1. Identify the values:
   - \(n = 5\) (5 days),
   - \(a = 20\) (first term),
   - \(l = a + 4d = 20 + 4(5) = 20 + 20 = 40\) (last term, where \(d = 5\) is the common difference).

2. Plug the values into the formula:
   \[
   S_n = \frac{n}{2} \cdot (a + l)
   \]
   \[
   S_5 = \frac{5}{2} \cdot (20 + 40)
   \]
   \[
   S_5 = \frac{5}{2} \cdot 60 = 5 \cdot 30 = 150
   \]

3. The total number of cans collected in 5 days is **150**.

---

#### **Method 2: Calculate the number of cans collected each day and sum them directly.**
1. Calculate the number of cans collected each day:
   - Day 1: \(20\),
   - Day 2: \(20 + 5 = 25\),
   - Day 3: \(25 + 5 = 30\),
   - Day 4: \(30 + 5 = 35\),
   - Day 5: \(35 + 5 = 40\).

2. Add the daily totals:
   \[
   20 + 25 + 30 + 35 + 40 = 150
   \]

3. The total number of cans collected in 5 days is **150**.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more efficient because it uses a formula to directly calculate the total without needing to compute each day's total individually. It is especially useful for larger numbers of days.
- **Method 2** is more intuitive and straightforward, as it involves explicitly calculating and summing the daily totals. However, it is more time-consuming and prone to error for larger sequences.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is better due to its efficiency and reliability.
- Using the formula:
  \[
  S_5 = \frac{5}{2} \cdot (20 + 40) = 5 \cdot 30 = 150
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify using **Method 2**:
  \[
  20 + 25 + 30 + 35 + 40 = 150
  \]
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

---

The answer is **150**.

Q: Fred had 212 sheets of paper. He received another 307 sheets of paper from Jane and gave Charles 156 sheets of paper. How many sheets of paper does Fred have left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Fred initially had **212 sheets of paper**.
- Jane gave Fred an additional **307 sheets of paper**.
- Fred then gave **156 sheets of paper** to Charles.
- We assume no other transactions occurred, and no sheets were lost or damaged.
- The goal is to determine how many sheets of paper Fred has left.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Sequential addition and subtraction**
- First, add the sheets Fred received from Jane to his initial total: \( 212 + 307 \).
- Then subtract the sheets Fred gave to Charles: \( (212 + 307) - 156 \).

**Method 2: Net change approach**
- Calculate the net change in Fred's paper supply by combining the sheets received and given away: \( 307 - 156 \).
- Add this net change to Fred's initial total: \( 212 + (307 - 156) \).

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential addition and subtraction**
1. Start with Fred's initial total: \( 212 \).
2. Add the sheets received from Jane:  
   \[
   212 + 307 = 519
   \]
3. Subtract the sheets given to Charles:  
   \[
   519 - 156 = 363
   \]
4. Double-check each step:
   - Adding \( 212 + 307 \): Break it into smaller parts for verification:  
     \( 212 + 300 = 512 \), then \( 512 + 7 = 519 \).  
   - Subtracting \( 519 - 156 \): Break it into smaller parts:  
     \( 519 - 100 = 419 \), then \( 419 - 50 = 369 \), and finally \( 369 - 6 = 363 \).

#### **Method 2: Net change approach**
1. Calculate the net change in Fred's paper supply:  
   \[
   307 - 156 = 151
   \]
2. Add this net change to Fred's initial total:  
   \[
   212 + 151 = 363
   \]
3. Double-check each step:
   - Subtracting \( 307 - 156 \): Break it into smaller parts:  
     \( 307 - 100 = 207 \), then \( 207 - 50 = 157 \), and finally \( 157 - 6 = 151 \).  
   - Adding \( 212 + 151 \): Break it into smaller parts:  
     \( 212 + 100 = 312 \), then \( 312 + 50 = 362 \), and finally \( 362 + 1 = 363 \).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is straightforward and follows the sequence of events in the problem. It is easy to understand and minimizes intermediate calculations.
- **Method 2** is slightly more abstract, as it combines the sheets received and given away into a single net change before adding it to the initial total. While efficient, it may be less intuitive for some people.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for clarity and reliability in this context, as it mirrors the problem's narrative and avoids combining steps prematurely.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Add the sheets Fred received from Jane to his initial total:  
   \[
   212 + 307 = 519
   \]
2. Subtract the sheets Fred gave to Charles:  
   \[
   519 - 156 = 363
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recheck addition: \( 212 + 307 = 519 \). Verified by breaking into smaller parts: \( 212 + 300 = 512 \), then \( 512 + 7 = 519 \).
- Recheck subtraction: \( 519 - 156 = 363 \). Verified by breaking into smaller parts: \( 519 - 100 = 419 \), \( 419 - 50 = 369 \), \( 369 - 6 = 363 \).
- The result is reasonable: Fred started with 212 sheets, received 307 more (totaling 519), and gave away 156, leaving him with 363 sheets.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **363**.

Q: Bugs are thriving in a garden of 30 plants. The bugs are very hungry today and completely ate 20 plants. The next day, they were still full and only ate half of the remaining plants. After that, they ate only 1 of the plants that were left. How many plants remain?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The garden initially has **30 plants**.
- On the first day, the bugs eat **20 plants**, leaving some remaining.
- On the second day, the bugs eat **half of the remaining plants**.
- On the third day, the bugs eat **1 plant** from the remaining plants.
- We assume no plants grow back, and no plants are eaten beyond what is described.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Sequential subtraction.**
- Start with 30 plants.
- Subtract 20 plants eaten on the first day.
- Calculate half of the remaining plants and subtract that amount for the second day.
- Subtract 1 plant eaten on the third day.
- The remaining plants after these steps will be the answer.

**Method 2: Backward reasoning.**
- Start with the final number of plants after the third day.
- Add back the 1 plant eaten on the third day to find the number of plants before the third day.
- Double the number of plants to account for the half eaten on the second day.
- Add back the 20 plants eaten on the first day to find the initial total.
- Verify that this matches the given starting number of 30 plants.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential subtraction**
1. Start with 30 plants.
2. On the first day, the bugs eat 20 plants:
   \[
   30 - 20 = 10 \quad \text{(10 plants remain after the first day).}
   \]
3. On the second day, the bugs eat half of the remaining plants:
   \[
   \text{Half of 10} = 5, \quad 10 - 5 = 5 \quad \text{(5 plants remain after the second day).}
   \]
4. On the third day, the bugs eat 1 plant:
   \[
   5 - 1 = 4 \quad \text{(4 plants remain after the third day).}
   \]
5. Final result: **4 plants remain**.

#### **Method 2: Backward reasoning**
1. Start with the final number of plants after the third day: **4 plants**.
2. Add back the 1 plant eaten on the third day:
   \[
   4 + 1 = 5 \quad \text{(5 plants before the third day).}
   \]
3. Double the number of plants to account for the half eaten on the second day:
   \[
   5 \times 2 = 10 \quad \text{(10 plants before the second day).}
   \]
4. Add back the 20 plants eaten on the first day:
   \[
   10 + 20 = 30 \quad \text{(30 plants before the first day).}
   \]
5. Verify: The initial total matches the given starting number of 30 plants. Final result: **4 plants remain**.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Sequential subtraction)** is straightforward and follows the problem's timeline step by step. It is easy to follow and minimizes the risk of errors.
- **Method 2 (Backward reasoning)** is useful for verifying the solution and works well when starting from the end. However, it requires more steps and is less intuitive for this problem.
- **Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for solving this problem due to its clarity and directness.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Start with 30 plants.
2. Subtract 20 plants eaten on the first day:
   \[
   30 - 20 = 10.
   \]
3. Subtract half of the remaining plants eaten on the second day:
   \[
   10 - 5 = 5.
   \]
4. Subtract 1 plant eaten on the third day:
   \[
   5 - 1 = 4.
   \]
5. Final result: **4 plants remain**.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify each step:
  - After the first day: \(30 - 20 = 10\) (correct).
  - After the second day: \(10 - 5 = 5\) (correct).
  - After the third day: \(5 - 1 = 4\) (correct).
- The calculations are consistent, and the result is reasonable given the problem's context.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **4**.

Q: Sally sews 4 shirts on Monday, 3 shirts on Tuesday, and 2 shirts on Wednesday.  Each shirt has 5 buttons.  How many buttons does Sally need to sew all the shirts?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Sally sews 4 shirts on Monday, 3 shirts on Tuesday, and 2 shirts on Wednesday.
- Each shirt requires 5 buttons.
- We assume all shirts are completed and require the same number of buttons (5 per shirt).
- The goal is to calculate the total number of buttons Sally needs for all the shirts.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- First, calculate the total number of shirts Sally sews by summing the shirts made on each day: 4 (Monday) + 3 (Tuesday) + 2 (Wednesday).  
- Then, multiply the total number of shirts by the number of buttons per shirt (5).  

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the number of buttons needed for each day separately by multiplying the number of shirts sewn each day by 5.  
- Then, sum the results for all three days to get the total number of buttons.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the total number of shirts:  
   \( 4 + 3 + 2 = 9 \) shirts.  
2. Multiply the total number of shirts by the number of buttons per shirt:  
   \( 9 \times 5 = 45 \) buttons.  
3. Double-check:  
   - Adding the shirts again: \( 4 + 3 + 2 = 9 \).  
   - Multiplying again: \( 9 \times 5 = 45 \).  

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- Forgetting to sum all the shirts correctly or miscalculating the multiplication.

---

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate the number of buttons needed for each day:  
   - Monday: \( 4 \times 5 = 20 \) buttons.  
   - Tuesday: \( 3 \times 5 = 15 \) buttons.  
   - Wednesday: \( 2 \times 5 = 10 \) buttons.  
2. Add the results for all three days:  
   \( 20 + 15 + 10 = 45 \) buttons.  
3. Double-check:  
   - Recalculate each day:  
     - Monday: \( 4 \times 5 = 20 \).  
     - Tuesday: \( 3 \times 5 = 15 \).  
     - Wednesday: \( 2 \times 5 = 10 \).  
   - Re-add: \( 20 + 15 + 10 = 45 \).  

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- Miscalculating the buttons for a specific day or forgetting to add all three results.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more efficient because it involves fewer steps: summing the shirts first and then multiplying by 5. It is straightforward and minimizes the chance of error.  
- **Method 2** is more detailed and breaks the problem into smaller parts, which can be helpful for clarity but introduces more opportunities for small errors (e.g., forgetting to add all the results).  

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and efficiency.  
1. Total shirts: \( 4 + 3 + 2 = 9 \).  
2. Total buttons: \( 9 \times 5 = 45 \).

Q: Tim drops a ball off the roof of a 96-foot tall building. The ball bounces to half the height from which it fell on each bounce. How high will it bounce on the fifth bounce?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The ball is dropped from a height of 96 feet.
- On each bounce, the ball reaches half the height of the previous bounce.
- We are tasked with finding the height of the ball on the **fifth bounce**.
- Assumptions:
  - The ball's bounces follow a perfect geometric progression with a ratio of \( \frac{1}{2} \).
  - Air resistance, wind, or other external factors are negligible.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Iterative Calculation**
- Start with the initial height (96 feet) and calculate the height of each bounce step by step, halving the height at each bounce until the fifth bounce is reached.

**Method 2: Geometric Sequence Formula**
- Recognize that the heights of the bounces form a geometric sequence:
  - First term (\(a\)) = 96 feet.
  - Common ratio (\(r\)) = \( \frac{1}{2} \).
- Use the formula for the \(n\)-th term of a geometric sequence:
  \[
  h_n = a \cdot r^{n-1}
  \]
  where \(h_n\) is the height of the \(n\)-th bounce. Plug in \(n = 5\) to find the height of the fifth bounce.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Iterative Calculation**
1. Start with the initial height: \( h_0 = 96 \) feet.
2. First bounce: \( h_1 = \frac{96}{2} = 48 \) feet.
3. Second bounce: \( h_2 = \frac{48}{2} = 24 \) feet.
4. Third bounce: \( h_3 = \frac{24}{2} = 12 \) feet.
5. Fourth bounce: \( h_4 = \frac{12}{2} = 6 \) feet.
6. Fifth bounce: \( h_5 = \frac{6}{2} = 3 \) feet.

**Result:** The height of the ball on the fifth bounce is **3 feet**.

**Check for pitfalls:**
- This method is straightforward but requires careful tracking of each step to avoid errors.
- It is reliable for small numbers of bounces but becomes tedious for larger numbers.

---

#### **Method 2: Geometric Sequence Formula**
1. Use the formula for the \(n\)-th term of a geometric sequence:
   \[
   h_n = a \cdot r^{n-1}
   \]
   where:
   - \(a = 96\) (initial height),
   - \(r = \frac{1}{2}\) (common ratio),
   - \(n = 5\) (fifth bounce).
2. Plug in the values:
   \[
   h_5 = 96 \cdot \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{5-1}
   \]
   \[
   h_5 = 96 \cdot \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^4
   \]
3. Simplify the exponent:
   \[
   \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^4 = \frac{1}{16}
   \]
4. Multiply:
   \[
   h_5 = 96 \cdot \frac{1}{16} = \frac{96}{16} = 3
   \]

**Result:** The height of the ball on the fifth bounce is **3 feet**.

**Check for pitfalls:**
- This method relies on correctly applying the formula and simplifying exponents.
- It is efficient and scalable for larger numbers of bounces.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1 (Iterative Calculation):**
  - Strengths: Simple and intuitive for small numbers of bounces.
  - Weaknesses: Becomes tedious and error-prone for larger numbers of bounces.

- **Method 2 (Geometric Sequence Formula):**
  - Strengths: Efficient and reliable for any number of bounces.
  - Weaknesses: Requires familiarity with the geometric sequence formula and exponentiation.

**Conclusion:** Method 2 is better because it is more efficient and scalable, especially for larger numbers of bounces.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 2 (Geometric Sequence Formula):**
1. Formula: \( h_n = a \cdot r^{n-1} \).
2. Plug in values: \( h_5 = 96 \cdot \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^4 \).
3. Simplify: \( \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^4 = \frac{1}{16} \).
4. Multiply: \( h_5 = 96 \cdot \frac{1}{16} = \frac{96}{16} = 3 \).

**Result:** The height of the ball on the fifth bounce is **3 feet**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Using Method 1 (Iterative Calculation), we also found the height of the fifth bounce to be **3 feet**.
- The result is reasonable: the ball's height decreases by half with each bounce, and the progression aligns with expectations.

---

**The answer is 3 feet.**
