Q: During a recent three-day trip to Italy, Keziah and her mom went mushroom picking. They sold all the mushrooms they picked on the first day for a total of $58. The day after, they picked 12 mushrooms. On the last day, they picked double the mushrooms they had picked the previous day. If the price per mushroom was $2, what is the total number of mushrooms they picked?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Keziah and her mom went mushroom picking for three days.
- On the **first day**, they sold all the mushrooms they picked for $58.
- The price per mushroom is $2, so the number of mushrooms picked on the first day is \( \frac{58}{2} = 29 \).
- On the **second day**, they picked 12 mushrooms.
- On the **third day**, they picked **double** the number of mushrooms they picked on the second day, which is \( 2 \times 12 = 24 \).
- We assume no mushrooms were lost, spoiled, or discarded, and all mushrooms picked are accounted for.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct addition of mushrooms picked each day.**
- Calculate the number of mushrooms picked on each day (using the given information).
- Add the totals for all three days to find the total number of mushrooms picked.

**Method 2: Use algebra to represent the total number of mushrooms.**
- Let \( x_1 \), \( x_2 \), and \( x_3 \) represent the number of mushrooms picked on the first, second, and third days, respectively.
- Use the given relationships to express \( x_1 \), \( x_2 \), and \( x_3 \) in terms of known values, then sum them.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition**
1. **First day:** The mushrooms picked were sold for $58 at $2 per mushroom.  
   \[
   \text{Number of mushrooms on Day 1} = \frac{58}{2} = 29
   \]
2. **Second day:** They picked 12 mushrooms.  
   \[
   \text{Number of mushrooms on Day 2} = 12
   \]
3. **Third day:** They picked double the number of mushrooms from the second day.  
   \[
   \text{Number of mushrooms on Day 3} = 2 \times 12 = 24
   \]
4. **Total mushrooms:** Add the mushrooms from all three days.  
   \[
   \text{Total mushrooms} = 29 + 12 + 24 = 65
   \]

#### **Method 2: Algebraic approach**
1. Let \( x_1 \), \( x_2 \), and \( x_3 \) represent the number of mushrooms picked on the first, second, and third days, respectively.
2. From the problem:
   - \( x_1 = \frac{58}{2} = 29 \)
   - \( x_2 = 12 \)
   - \( x_3 = 2 \times x_2 = 2 \times 12 = 24 \)
3. Total mushrooms:
   \[
   \text{Total mushrooms} = x_1 + x_2 + x_3 = 29 + 12 + 24 = 65
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and involves directly calculating the number of mushrooms picked each day and summing them. It is clear and efficient for this problem.
- **Method 2** uses algebra, which is more general and can handle more complex relationships. However, for this specific problem, it introduces unnecessary complexity.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and clarity.
- Using Method 1:
  - Day 1: 29 mushrooms
  - Day 2: 12 mushrooms
  - Day 3: 24 mushrooms
  - Total mushrooms: \( 29 + 12 + 24 = 65 \)

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate each day:
  - Day 1: \( \frac{58}{2} = 29 \) (correct)
  - Day 2: \( 12 \) (given, correct)
  - Day 3: \( 2 \times 12 = 24 \) (correct)
- Re-add the totals: \( 29 + 12 + 24 = 65 \) (correct).

The answer is **65**.

Q: Abigail is trying a new recipe for a cold drink. It uses 1/4 of a cup of iced tea and 1 and 1/4 of a cup of lemonade to make one drink. If she fills a pitcher with 18 total cups of this drink, how many cups of lemonade are in the pitcher?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Abigail is making a drink that uses:
  - **1/4 cup of iced tea** per drink.
  - **1 and 1/4 cups of lemonade** per drink (equivalent to \(1 + \frac{1}{4} = \frac{5}{4}\) cups).
- The total volume of the pitcher is **18 cups**.
- The drink is made by combining iced tea and lemonade in the given proportions.
- We assume the recipe is followed exactly, and the entire pitcher is filled with the drink.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use proportions to calculate the fraction of the total drink that is lemonade.
- The total volume of one drink is \( \frac{1}{4} + \frac{5}{4} = \frac{6}{4} = \frac{3}{2} \) cups.
- The fraction of lemonade in one drink is \( \frac{\frac{5}{4}}{\frac{3}{2}} \).
- Multiply this fraction by the total volume of the pitcher (18 cups) to find the amount of lemonade.

#### Method 2: Calculate the number of drinks that fit into the pitcher, then multiply by the amount of lemonade per drink.
- Determine how many drinks can be made with 18 cups by dividing the total volume of the pitcher by the volume of one drink (\(\frac{3}{2}\) cups).
- Multiply the number of drinks by the amount of lemonade per drink (\(\frac{5}{4}\) cups).

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Use proportions.
1. Calculate the total volume of one drink:  
   \[
   \text{Volume of one drink} = \frac{1}{4} + \frac{5}{4} = \frac{6}{4} = \frac{3}{2} \text{ cups.}
   \]
2. Find the fraction of lemonade in one drink:  
   \[
   \text{Fraction of lemonade} = \frac{\frac{5}{4}}{\frac{3}{2}} = \frac{5}{4} \times \frac{2}{3} = \frac{10}{12} = \frac{5}{6}.
   \]
3. Multiply this fraction by the total volume of the pitcher:  
   \[
   \text{Amount of lemonade} = \frac{5}{6} \times 18 = \frac{90}{6} = 15 \text{ cups.}
   \]

#### Method 2: Calculate the number of drinks.
1. Calculate the total volume of one drink:  
   \[
   \text{Volume of one drink} = \frac{1}{4} + \frac{5}{4} = \frac{6}{4} = \frac{3}{2} \text{ cups.}
   \]
2. Determine how many drinks fit into the pitcher:  
   \[
   \text{Number of drinks} = \frac{\text{Total volume of pitcher}}{\text{Volume of one drink}} = \frac{18}{\frac{3}{2}} = 18 \times \frac{2}{3} = 12 \text{ drinks.}
   \]
3. Multiply the number of drinks by the amount of lemonade per drink:  
   \[
   \text{Amount of lemonade} = 12 \times \frac{5}{4} = \frac{60}{4} = 15 \text{ cups.}
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** uses proportions and is efficient for directly calculating the fraction of lemonade in the total drink. However, it requires careful handling of fractions and may be less intuitive for some.
- **Method 2** breaks the problem into smaller, more intuitive steps: calculating the number of drinks and then scaling up the amount of lemonade. This method is easier to follow and verify.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- **Method 2** is chosen for its clarity and step-by-step approach.
- Solution:  
  \[
  \text{Number of drinks} = 12, \quad \text{Amount of lemonade} = 12 \times \frac{5}{4} = 15 \text{ cups.}
  \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.
- Verify using Method 1:  
  \[
  \text{Fraction of lemonade} = \frac{5}{6}, \quad \text{Amount of lemonade} = \frac{5}{6} \times 18 = 15 \text{ cups.}
  \]
- Both methods agree, confirming the result.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **15 cups**.

Q: Two vampires have come to the sleepy village of Willowton, population 300. Every night, each vampire turns 5 more people into vampires. How many vampires are there after two nights?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The initial population of Willowton is 300 people.
- There are 2 vampires at the start.
- Each vampire turns 5 people into vampires every night.
- The process repeats every night, and the number of vampires grows exponentially as newly turned vampires also begin turning others.
- We assume no one leaves the village, no one resists being turned, and no vampires are destroyed.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Iterative Calculation by Night**
- Start with the initial number of vampires (2).
- For each night, calculate how many new vampires are created by multiplying the current number of vampires by 5.
- Add the new vampires to the existing total to get the updated number of vampires.
- Repeat for two nights.

**Method 2: Formula-Based Approach**
- Use a formula to calculate the total number of vampires after \( n \) nights:
  \[
  V_n = V_0 \times (1 + 5)^n
  \]
  where \( V_0 \) is the initial number of vampires, and \( n \) is the number of nights. This formula accounts for exponential growth, as each vampire creates 5 new vampires per night.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Iterative Calculation by Night**
1. **Night 0 (Initial State):**
   - Start with 2 vampires.

2. **Night 1:**
   - Each of the 2 vampires turns 5 people into vampires.
   - New vampires created: \( 2 \times 5 = 10 \).
   - Total vampires after Night 1: \( 2 + 10 = 12 \).

3. **Night 2:**
   - Each of the 12 vampires turns 5 people into vampires.
   - New vampires created: \( 12 \times 5 = 60 \).
   - Total vampires after Night 2: \( 12 + 60 = 72 \).

4. **Check for pitfalls:**
   - Ensure the calculations are consistent and account for all vampires, including those newly turned.
   - Verify that the exponential growth pattern is followed.

#### **Method 2: Formula-Based Approach**
1. Use the formula:
   \[
   V_n = V_0 \times (1 + 5)^n
   \]
   where \( V_0 = 2 \), \( n = 2 \), and \( 1 + 5 = 6 \).

2. Plug in the values:
   \[
   V_2 = 2 \times 6^2
   \]

3. Calculate \( 6^2 \):
   \[
   6^2 = 36
   \]

4. Multiply by the initial number of vampires:
   \[
   V_2 = 2 \times 36 = 72
   \]

5. **Check for pitfalls:**
   - Ensure the formula is applied correctly and matches the iterative method.
   - Confirm that the exponential growth factor \( (1 + 5)^n \) is accurate.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Iterative Calculation):**
  - Strengths: Easy to follow step-by-step, especially for small numbers of nights.
  - Weaknesses: Becomes cumbersome for a large number of nights due to repeated calculations.

- **Method 2 (Formula-Based Approach):**
  - Strengths: Efficient and scalable for any number of nights.
  - Weaknesses: Requires understanding and correctly applying the formula, which may be error-prone for beginners.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 2** is better for this problem because it is more efficient and scalable. Using the formula:
  \[
  V_2 = 2 \times 6^2 = 2 \times 36 = 72
  \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Using **Method 1**, we calculated the total vampires after two nights as 72.
- Using **Method 2**, we also calculated the total vampires after two nights as 72.
- Both methods agree, confirming the result is accurate and reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **72**.

Q: A shop is offering a discount on pens. If you buy 10 pens at the regular price, you can get the next 10 for half off. A customer came in and bought 20 pens for $30. What's the regular price of one pen in dollars?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- A customer bought 20 pens for $30.
- The shop offers a discount: if you buy 10 pens at the regular price, the next 10 pens are half off.
- We are tasked with finding the regular price of one pen.
- Assumptions:
  - The discount applies exactly as described (no additional discounts or taxes).
  - The regular price of one pen is consistent for all pens before the discount is applied.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
**Method 1: Use algebra to represent the regular price of one pen.**
- Let the regular price of one pen be \( x \) dollars.
- The first 10 pens are sold at the regular price, so their cost is \( 10x \).
- The next 10 pens are sold at half the regular price, so their cost is \( 10 \cdot \frac{x}{2} = 5x \).
- The total cost for 20 pens is \( 10x + 5x = 15x \), which equals $30.
- Solve for \( x \) to find the regular price of one pen.

**Method 2: Use proportional reasoning.**
- The total cost of 20 pens is $30, and the shop splits the cost into two groups: 10 pens at full price and 10 pens at half price.
- The second group of 10 pens costs half as much as the first group of 10 pens.
- Let the cost of the first group of 10 pens be \( y \). Then the second group of 10 pens costs \( \frac{y}{2} \).
- The total cost is \( y + \frac{y}{2} = 30 \). Solve for \( y \), then divide \( y \) by 10 to find the regular price of one pen.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Algebraic approach**
1. Let the regular price of one pen be \( x \) dollars.
2. The cost of the first 10 pens is \( 10x \).
3. The cost of the next 10 pens (at half price) is \( 5x \).
4. The total cost for 20 pens is \( 10x + 5x = 15x \).
5. Set up the equation:  
   \[
   15x = 30
   \]
6. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   x = \frac{30}{15} = 2
   \]
7. The regular price of one pen is \( x = 2 \) dollars.

#### **Method 2: Proportional reasoning**
1. Let the cost of the first group of 10 pens (at full price) be \( y \).
2. The cost of the second group of 10 pens (at half price) is \( \frac{y}{2} \).
3. The total cost for 20 pens is \( y + \frac{y}{2} = 30 \).
4. Combine terms:  
   \[
   y + \frac{y}{2} = \frac{2y}{2} + \frac{y}{2} = \frac{3y}{2}
   \]
   So,  
   \[
   \frac{3y}{2} = 30
   \]
5. Solve for \( y \):  
   \[
   y = \frac{30 \cdot 2}{3} = 20
   \]
6. The cost of the first group of 10 pens is \( y = 20 \). Divide by 10 to find the regular price of one pen:  
   \[
   \text{Regular price} = \frac{20}{10} = 2
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (algebraic approach)** is more direct and efficient, as it immediately sets up an equation for the total cost and solves for the regular price.
- **Method 2 (proportional reasoning)** is slightly longer but provides a clear breakdown of the relationship between the two groups of pens. It is useful for understanding the problem conceptually.
- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is simpler and faster.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- Using **Method 1**, we found that the regular price of one pen is \( x = 2 \) dollars.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.
1. If the regular price of one pen is $2:
   - The first 10 pens cost \( 10 \cdot 2 = 20 \) dollars.
   - The next 10 pens (at half price) cost \( 10 \cdot 1 = 10 \) dollars.
   - Total cost: \( 20 + 10 = 30 \) dollars.
2. The calculations are consistent with the problem statement.

---

The answer is **2**.

Q: Kimberly went hiking and took a 4-liter bottle full of water with her. The first time she drank from it, she consumed a quarter of the water in the bottle. Later on, she drank 2/3rd of the remaining water. How much water is left in the bottle (in liters)?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Kimberly starts with a 4-liter bottle of water.
- The first time she drinks, she consumes **1/4 of the water** in the bottle.
- Later, she drinks **2/3 of the remaining water**.
- We are tasked with finding how much water is left in the bottle after these two events.
- Assumptions:
  - No water is spilled or lost.
  - All calculations are exact, and the fractions are applied sequentially.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Sequential Fraction Reduction**
- Step 1: Calculate how much water Kimberly drinks the first time (1/4 of 4 liters).
- Step 2: Subtract this amount from the total to find the remaining water.
- Step 3: Calculate how much water she drinks the second time (2/3 of the remaining water).
- Step 4: Subtract this second amount from the remaining water to find the final amount left.

**Method 2: Fraction Multiplication**
- Step 1: Represent the remaining water after each drinking session as a fraction of the original 4 liters.
- Step 2: Multiply the fractions sequentially to determine the fraction of water left in the bottle.
- Step 3: Multiply this final fraction by 4 liters to find the amount of water left.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential Fraction Reduction**
1. **First drinking session:**
   - Kimberly drinks **1/4 of 4 liters**:
     \[
     \frac{1}{4} \times 4 = 1 \, \text{liter}.
     \]
   - Remaining water:
     \[
     4 - 1 = 3 \, \text{liters}.
     \]

2. **Second drinking session:**
   - Kimberly drinks **2/3 of the remaining 3 liters**:
     \[
     \frac{2}{3} \times 3 = 2 \, \text{liters}.
     \]
   - Remaining water:
     \[
     3 - 2 = 1 \, \text{liter}.
     \]

3. **Final amount of water left:**
   - After both drinking sessions, **1 liter** of water remains.

#### **Method 2: Fraction Multiplication**
1. **First drinking session:**
   - After drinking **1/4 of the water**, Kimberly has **3/4 of the water left**:
     \[
     \frac{3}{4} \, \text{of the original 4 liters}.
     \]

2. **Second drinking session:**
   - Kimberly drinks **2/3 of the remaining water**, leaving her with **1/3 of the remaining water**:
     \[
     \frac{1}{3} \times \frac{3}{4} = \frac{1}{4}.
     \]

3. **Final amount of water left:**
   - The fraction of water left in the bottle is **1/4 of the original 4 liters**:
     \[
     \frac{1}{4} \times 4 = 1 \, \text{liter}.
     \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Sequential Fraction Reduction):**
  - This method is straightforward and intuitive, as it breaks the problem into smaller, manageable steps.
  - It is easy to follow and verify each step, making it less prone to errors.
  - However, it involves more intermediate calculations.

- **Method 2 (Fraction Multiplication):**
  - This method is more efficient, as it directly calculates the fraction of water left after both drinking sessions.
  - It requires a good understanding of fractions and may be less intuitive for some people.
  - It is more compact but slightly harder to verify step by step.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for clarity and reliability, while Method 2 is better for efficiency. For this problem, Method 1 is preferred due to its step-by-step nature.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Kimberly drinks **1/4 of 4 liters**:
   \[
   \frac{1}{4} \times 4 = 1 \, \text{liter}.
   \]
   Remaining water:
   \[
   4 - 1 = 3 \, \text{liters}.
   \]

2. Kimberly drinks **2/3 of the remaining 3 liters**:
   \[
   \frac{2}{3} \times 3 = 2 \, \text{liters}.
   \]
   Remaining water:
   \[
   3 - 2 = 1 \, \text{liter}.
   \]

Final amount of water left:
\[
1 \, \text{liter}.
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- After the first session, Kimberly has 3 liters left, which is reasonable.
- After the second session, she drinks 2 liters, leaving 1 liter, which is consistent with the calculations.
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution.

---

The answer is **1 liter**.

Q: A zoo has 16 pandas, paired into mates (in the same zoo). Only 25% of the panda couples get pregnant after mating. If they each have one baby, how many panda babies are born?
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- The zoo has 16 pandas.  
- Pandas are paired into mates, meaning there are \( \frac{16}{2} = 8 \) panda couples.  
- Only 25% of the panda couples get pregnant after mating.  
- Each pregnant couple has one baby.  
- We assume no complications, and all pregnancies result in one baby.

**Method 1:** Calculate the number of pregnant couples by multiplying the total number of couples by 25%, then determine the number of babies (equal to the number of pregnant couples).  
**Method 2:** Use fractions to find the number of pregnant couples directly (25% is equivalent to \( \frac{1}{4} \)), then calculate the number of babies.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total number of panda couples: \( \frac{16}{2} = 8 \).  
2. Determine the number of pregnant couples: \( 8 \times 0.25 = 2 \).  
3. Since each pregnant couple has one baby, the total number of babies is equal to the number of pregnant couples: \( 2 \).  

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the total number of panda couples: \( \frac{16}{2} = 8 \).  
2. Use the fraction \( \frac{1}{4} \) to find the number of pregnant couples: \( 8 \times \frac{1}{4} = 2 \).  
3. Since each pregnant couple has one baby, the total number of babies is equal to the number of pregnant couples: \( 2 \).

- Both methods are clear and reliable, as they involve straightforward calculations.  
- Method 1 uses percentages, which may be more intuitive for some people.  
- Method 2 uses fractions, which can be simpler for mental math.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 may be slightly more accessible for those familiar with percentages.  

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

1. Calculate the total number of panda couples: \( \frac{16}{2} = 8 \).  
2. Determine the number of pregnant couples: \( 8 \times 0.25 = 2 \).  
3. Since each pregnant couple has one baby, the total number of babies is \( 2 \).

- We are tasked with finding the **total number of steaks Harvey sold**.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**

- Then, add the 4 steaks sold afterward to the initial number of steaks sold.  

**Method 2:**  
- Start with the total number of steaks (25).  
- Subtract the steaks Harvey had left after the first sale (12).

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Convert "two and one-half dozens" into a decimal (2.5 dozens) and multiply by 12 (the number of items in a dozen). This method directly calculates the total number of pens.

**Method 2:**  
Break "two and one-half dozens" into two parts:  
- Calculate the number of pens in 2 dozens (2 × 12).  
- Calculate the number of pens in 0.5 dozen (0.5 × 12).  
- Add the results together to find the total.

#### **Method 1: Direct multiplication**
1. Convert "two and one-half dozens" into a decimal:  
   \( 2.5 \) dozens.
2. Multiply by the number of pens in a dozen:  
   \( 2.5 \times 12 = 30 \).

     \( 0.5 \times 12 = 6 \) (for half a dozen),  
     \( 24 + 6 = 30 \).

1. Calculate the number of pens in 2 dozens:  
   \( 2 \times 12 = 24 \).
2. Calculate the number of pens in 0.5 dozen:  
   \( 0.5 \times 12 = 6 \).
3. Add the results together:  
   \( 24 + 6 = 30 \).
4. Double-check the addition:

- **Method 2** is more detailed and may be easier to understand for someone who prefers step-by-step reasoning. However, it involves more intermediate steps, which could introduce errors if not carefully checked.

  \( 2 \times 12 = 24 \),

Q: Tamika drove for 8 hours at an average speed of 45 miles per hour. Logan drove for 5 hours at 55 miles an hour. How many miles farther did Tamika drive?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Tamika drove for **8 hours** at an average speed of **45 miles per hour**.
- Logan drove for **5 hours** at an average speed of **55 miles per hour**.
- We are tasked with finding how many miles farther Tamika drove than Logan.
- Assumptions:
  - Both drivers maintained their average speeds throughout their respective trips.
  - No external factors (e.g., stops, detours) affected their driving distances.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct calculation of distances traveled by each driver.**
- Use the formula for distance: \( \text{Distance} = \text{Speed} \times \text{Time} \).
- Calculate Tamika's distance and Logan's distance separately, then subtract Logan's distance from Tamika's distance.

**Method 2: Compare the distances incrementally.**
- Break the problem into smaller steps:
  - First, calculate the difference in speeds: \( 45 - 55 = -10 \) miles per hour.
  - Then, calculate the difference in driving times: \( 8 - 5 = 3 \) hours.
  - Use these differences to determine how much farther Tamika drove.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of distances traveled.**
1. **Calculate Tamika's distance:**
   - \( \text{Distance} = \text{Speed} \times \text{Time} \)
   - \( \text{Tamika's Distance} = 45 \times 8 = 360 \) miles.

2. **Calculate Logan's distance:**
   - \( \text{Distance} = \text{Speed} \times \text{Time} \)
   - \( \text{Logan's Distance} = 55 \times 5 = 275 \) miles.

3. **Find the difference in distances:**
   - \( \text{Difference} = \text{Tamika's Distance} - \text{Logan's Distance} \)
   - \( \text{Difference} = 360 - 275 = 85 \) miles.

4. **Check for errors or unreasonable results:**
   - Both distances are positive and reasonable given the speeds and times.
   - The subtraction is straightforward and checks out.

---

#### **Method 2: Incremental comparison of distances.**
1. **Calculate the difference in speeds:**
   - \( \text{Speed Difference} = 45 - 55 = -10 \) miles per hour.
   - This indicates Logan drove faster than Tamika.

2. **Calculate the difference in driving times:**
   - \( \text{Time Difference} = 8 - 5 = 3 \) hours.
   - This indicates Tamika drove for 3 hours longer than Logan.

3. **Account for the extra driving time:**
   - Tamika drove for 3 additional hours at her speed of 45 miles per hour.
   - \( \text{Extra Distance} = 45 \times 3 = 135 \) miles.

4. **Account for Logan's faster speed during his 5 hours:**
   - Logan drove 10 miles per hour faster than Tamika for 5 hours.
   - \( \text{Extra Distance Logan Covered} = 10 \times 5 = 50 \) miles.

5. **Find the net difference in distances:**
   - \( \text{Net Difference} = \text{Extra Distance Tamika Covered} - \text{Extra Distance Logan Covered} \)
   - \( \text{Net Difference} = 135 - 50 = 85 \) miles.

6. **Check for errors or unreasonable results:**
   - The calculations align with the given speeds and times.
   - The result matches the direct calculation from Method 1.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more straightforward and efficient, as it directly calculates the distances traveled by each driver and subtracts them. It avoids unnecessary intermediate steps and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2** involves breaking the problem into smaller steps, which can be helpful for understanding the incremental differences but introduces more complexity and potential for mistakes.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

**Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and reliability.**

1. **Calculate Tamika's distance:**
   - \( \text{Tamika's Distance} = 45 \times 8 = 360 \) miles.

2. **Calculate Logan's distance:**
   - \( \text{Logan's Distance} = 55 \times 5 = 275 \) miles.

3. **Find the difference in distances:**
   - \( \text{Difference} = 360 - 275 = 85 \) miles.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recalculate Tamika's distance: \( 45 \times 8 = 360 \) miles.
- Recalculate Logan's distance: \( 55 \times 5 = 275 \) miles.
- Subtract: \( 360 - 275 = 85 \) miles.
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable given the problem's context.

---

The answer is **85 miles**.

Q: Mike gets paid 100 dollars a week.  He decides to spend half of that at an arcade.  He spends 10 dollars at the arcade on food and uses the rest on arcade tokens. He can play for 1 hour for $8. How many minutes can he play?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Mike earns $100 per week.
- He spends **half** of his weekly earnings at the arcade: \( \frac{100}{2} = 50 \) dollars.
- At the arcade, he spends **$10 on food** and uses the remaining money on arcade tokens.
- Each hour of play costs **$8**.
- We need to calculate how many **minutes** Mike can play based on the money he spends on tokens.

**Assumptions:**
- All money spent on tokens is used for playing arcade games.
- There are no additional fees or discounts.
- Time is calculated in minutes, and 1 hour equals 60 minutes.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate how much money Mike spends on tokens by subtracting the food cost from the arcade budget.  
2. Divide the token money by the hourly cost ($8/hour) to find the total hours of play.  
3. Convert hours into minutes by multiplying by 60.

**Method 2:**  
1. Start by calculating the cost per minute of play: \( \frac{8}{60} \) dollars per minute.  
2. Determine how much money Mike spends on tokens.  
3. Divide the token money by the cost per minute to directly calculate the total minutes of play.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. **Calculate money spent on tokens:**  
   Mike spends $50 at the arcade, but $10 is used for food.  
   \[
   \text{Money for tokens} = 50 - 10 = 40 \text{ dollars}.
   \]

2. **Calculate total hours of play:**  
   Each hour costs $8, so divide the token money by the hourly cost:  
   \[
   \text{Hours of play} = \frac{40}{8} = 5 \text{ hours}.
   \]

3. **Convert hours into minutes:**  
   Since 1 hour equals 60 minutes, multiply the total hours by 60:  
   \[
   \text{Minutes of play} = 5 \times 60 = 300 \text{ minutes}.
   \]

#### **Method 2:**
1. **Calculate cost per minute of play:**  
   Each hour costs $8, and there are 60 minutes in an hour. The cost per minute is:  
   \[
   \text{Cost per minute} = \frac{8}{60} = 0.1333 \text{ dollars per minute}.
   \]

2. **Determine money spent on tokens:**  
   Mike spends $50 at the arcade, but $10 is used for food.  
   \[
   \text{Money for tokens} = 50 - 10 = 40 \text{ dollars}.
   \]

3. **Calculate total minutes of play:**  
   Divide the token money by the cost per minute:  
   \[
   \text{Minutes of play} = \frac{40}{0.1333} \approx 300 \text{ minutes}.
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is more straightforward and intuitive, as it works with hours first and then converts to minutes. It avoids dealing with decimals, which reduces the risk of rounding errors.  
- **Method 2** is slightly more complex because it requires calculating the cost per minute and dividing by a decimal, which introduces potential rounding issues.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and reliability.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:  
1. Money for tokens: \( 50 - 10 = 40 \) dollars.  
2. Hours of play: \( \frac{40}{8} = 5 \) hours.  
3. Minutes of play: \( 5 \times 60 = 300 \) minutes.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recalculate using Method 2:  
  Cost per minute = \( \frac{8}{60} = 0.1333 \).  
  Minutes of play = \( \frac{40}{0.1333} \approx 300 \).  

Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

---

The answer is **300 minutes**.

Q: Kevin has a tree growing in his garden that is currently 180 inches tall. That is 50% taller than it was when he planted it there. How tall was the tree, in feet, then?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The tree is currently **180 inches tall**.
- The current height is **50% taller** than the height when it was planted.
- We are tasked with finding the original height of the tree **in feet**.
- Assumptions:
  - "50% taller" means the original height plus 50% of the original height equals the current height.
  - There are **12 inches in a foot**, so we will need to convert the final result from inches to feet.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Use algebra to calculate the original height.**
- Let the original height of the tree be \( x \) inches.
- The current height is 50% taller than the original, so \( 180 = x + 0.5x \), or \( 180 = 1.5x \).
- Solve for \( x \), then convert the result from inches to feet.

**Method 2: Use proportional reasoning.**
- If the current height is 50% taller, then the current height is 150% of the original height.
- Represent this as \( \text{current height} = 1.5 \times \text{original height} \), or \( 180 = 1.5 \times \text{original height} \).
- Solve for the original height, then convert the result from inches to feet.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Algebraic approach**
1. Let the original height of the tree be \( x \) inches.
2. The current height is 50% taller than the original, so:
   \[
   180 = x + 0.5x
   \]
3. Combine like terms:
   \[
   180 = 1.5x
   \]
4. Solve for \( x \) by dividing both sides by 1.5:
   \[
   x = \frac{180}{1.5} = 120 \, \text{inches}.
   \]
5. Convert the original height from inches to feet:
   \[
   \text{Height in feet} = \frac{120}{12} = 10 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

#### **Method 2: Proportional reasoning**
1. The current height is 150% of the original height. Represent this as:
   \[
   180 = 1.5 \times \text{original height}.
   \]
2. Solve for the original height by dividing both sides by 1.5:
   \[
   \text{Original height} = \frac{180}{1.5} = 120 \, \text{inches}.
   \]
3. Convert the original height from inches to feet:
   \[
   \text{Height in feet} = \frac{120}{12} = 10 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Algebraic approach):** This method is clear and systematic, breaking the problem into manageable steps. It explicitly shows the relationship between the original height and the current height, making it easy to follow.
- **Method 2 (Proportional reasoning):** This method is slightly more intuitive for those comfortable with percentages, as it directly uses the idea of 150% of the original height. It is efficient but assumes familiarity with proportional reasoning.
- **Comparison:** Both methods are equally reliable and yield the same result. However, Method 1 is more general and easier to adapt to similar problems, making it slightly more versatile.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- I will use **Method 1 (Algebraic approach)** for its clarity and general applicability.

1. Let the original height of the tree be \( x \) inches.
2. The current height is 50% taller than the original, so:
   \[
   180 = x + 0.5x
   \]
3. Combine like terms:
   \[
   180 = 1.5x
   \]
4. Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   x = \frac{180}{1.5} = 120 \, \text{inches}.
   \]
5. Convert the original height from inches to feet:
   \[
   \text{Height in feet} = \frac{120}{12} = 10 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the calculation:
  - \( 1.5 \times 120 = 180 \), confirming the current height is 50% taller than the original.
- Verify the conversion:
  - \( 120 \, \text{inches} \div 12 = 10 \, \text{feet} \), confirming the conversion is correct.
- The result is reasonable, as the original height is smaller than the current height, and the relationship matches the problem's description.

---

The answer is **10 feet**.

Q: The owner of a company needs to install a transformer to power his electrical equipment. He operates three units of machinery that require a running current of 40A each. These appliances require at least twice their running current for starting, and the transformer chosen must account for this. What is the minimum current load that his transformer of choice must be able to carry?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

- The company operates **three units of machinery**.
- Each unit requires a **running current of 40A**.
- Each unit requires **at least twice their running current for starting**, meaning the starting current for each unit is \( 2 \times 40A = 80A \).
- The transformer must be able to handle the **total starting current** of all three units simultaneously.
- We assume all three units may start at the same time, requiring the transformer to handle the combined starting current.
- We assume no additional loads or inefficiencies in the system.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.

**Method 1: Direct calculation of total starting current.**
- Multiply the starting current of one unit by the number of units to find the total starting current.
- This method directly calculates the required transformer capacity.

**Method 2: Break the problem into two steps:**
1. Calculate the running current for all three units combined.
2. Multiply the total running current by 2 (since starting current is twice the running current) to find the total starting current.
- This method emphasizes the relationship between running and starting currents.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of total starting current**
1. Each unit requires a starting current of \( 2 \times 40A = 80A \).
2. There are three units, so the total starting current is:
   \[
   80A \times 3 = 240A
   \]
3. The transformer must handle at least 240A to support the simultaneous starting of all three units.

#### **Method 2: Break the problem into two steps**
1. Calculate the total running current for all three units:
   \[
   40A \times 3 = 120A
   \]
2. Since the starting current is twice the running current, the total starting current is:
   \[
   2 \times 120A = 240A
   \]
3. The transformer must handle at least 240A to support the simultaneous starting of all three units.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it immediately calculates the total starting current without intermediate steps.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and emphasizes the relationship between running and starting currents, which may be helpful for understanding the problem but introduces unnecessary steps for this specific calculation.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer and less prone to error.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and directness.
- Using Method 1:
  \[
  80A \times 3 = 240A
  \]
- The transformer must handle a minimum current load of **240A**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.

- Recalculate using Method 2:
  1. Total running current: \( 40A \times 3 = 120A \).
  2. Total starting current: \( 2 \times 120A = 240A \).
- Both methods confirm the result.
- The result is reasonable, as the transformer must account for the simultaneous starting of all three units.

---

The answer is **240A**.

Q: There are 7 trucks that have 20 boxes. There are 5 trucks that have 12 boxes. Each box holds 8 containers of oil. If all of the oil is evenly redistributed onto 10 trucks, how many containers of oil will each truck have?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are **7 trucks** with **20 boxes each**.
- There are **5 trucks** with **12 boxes each**.
- Each box contains **8 containers of oil**.
- The total oil is to be **evenly redistributed** across **10 trucks**.
- Assumptions:
  - No oil is lost during redistribution.
  - All trucks can hold the redistributed oil.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Calculate the total number of containers of oil, then divide by the number of trucks.**
- Step 1: Compute the total number of boxes across all trucks.
- Step 2: Multiply the total number of boxes by 8 (containers per box) to find the total number of containers of oil.
- Step 3: Divide the total number of containers by 10 (the number of trucks after redistribution).

**Method 2: Break the problem into smaller parts by calculating the oil per group of trucks, then sum the results and redistribute.**
- Step 1: Calculate the total oil from the 7 trucks (20 boxes each).
- Step 2: Calculate the total oil from the 5 trucks (12 boxes each).
- Step 3: Add the two totals to find the total oil.
- Step 4: Divide the total oil by 10 trucks.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Total containers first, then redistribute**
1. **Calculate the total number of boxes:**
   - Trucks with 20 boxes: \( 7 \times 20 = 140 \) boxes.
   - Trucks with 12 boxes: \( 5 \times 12 = 60 \) boxes.
   - Total boxes: \( 140 + 60 = 200 \) boxes.

2. **Calculate the total number of containers of oil:**
   - Each box holds 8 containers: \( 200 \times 8 = 1600 \) containers.

3. **Redistribute the oil evenly across 10 trucks:**
   - Total containers: \( 1600 \div 10 = 160 \) containers per truck.

4. **Check for errors or unreasonable results:**
   - The total number of containers (1600) is divisible by 10, so the result is reasonable.

---

#### **Method 2: Calculate oil per group of trucks, then redistribute**
1. **Calculate the oil from the 7 trucks (20 boxes each):**
   - Boxes: \( 7 \times 20 = 140 \).
   - Containers: \( 140 \times 8 = 1120 \).

2. **Calculate the oil from the 5 trucks (12 boxes each):**
   - Boxes: \( 5 \times 12 = 60 \).
   - Containers: \( 60 \times 8 = 480 \).

3. **Add the total oil from both groups:**
   - Total containers: \( 1120 + 480 = 1600 \).

4. **Redistribute the oil evenly across 10 trucks:**
   - Total containers: \( 1600 \div 10 = 160 \) containers per truck.

5. **Check for errors or unreasonable results:**
   - The total number of containers (1600) matches the sum of the two groups, confirming the calculations.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Clarity:** Both methods are clear, but Method 1 is slightly more straightforward because it calculates the total boxes first, avoiding intermediate steps.
- **Reliability:** Both methods are reliable, as they involve simple arithmetic and yield the same result.
- **Efficiency:** Method 1 is more efficient because it combines the box totals early, reducing the number of calculations.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Solution:
  1. Total boxes: \( 140 + 60 = 200 \).

Q: A large puzzle costs $15. A small puzzle and a large puzzle together cost $23. How much would you pay for 1 large and 3 small puzzles?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The cost of a large puzzle is $15.
- The cost of a small puzzle and a large puzzle together is $23.
- We need to calculate the total cost of 1 large puzzle and 3 small puzzles.
- Assume all prices are in dollars, and there are no discounts, taxes, or additional fees.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
**Method 1:**  
- Use the given information to find the cost of a small puzzle by subtracting the cost of the large puzzle from the combined cost of a small and large puzzle.  
- Multiply the cost of a small puzzle by 3 and add the cost of 1 large puzzle to find the total cost.

**Method 2:**  
- Represent the cost of a small puzzle as a variable (e.g., \( x \)).  
- Use the equation \( x + 15 = 23 \) to solve for \( x \).  
- Substitute \( x \) into the expression \( 15 + 3x \) to calculate the total cost.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction and addition**
1. The cost of a small puzzle can be found by subtracting the cost of the large puzzle from the combined cost:  
   \[
   \text{Cost of small puzzle} = 23 - 15 = 8
   \]
2. Multiply the cost of a small puzzle by 3 to find the cost of 3 small puzzles:  
   \[
   3 \times 8 = 24
   \]
3. Add the cost of 1 large puzzle to the cost of 3 small puzzles:  
   \[
   15 + 24 = 39
   \]

#### **Method 2: Algebraic approach**
1. Let the cost of a small puzzle be \( x \).  
   From the given information, the cost of a small puzzle and a large puzzle together is $23:  
   \[
   x + 15 = 23
   \]
2. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   x = 23 - 15 = 8
   \]
3. Substitute \( x = 8 \) into the expression for the total cost of 1 large puzzle and 3 small puzzles:  
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = 15 + 3x = 15 + 3(8) = 15 + 24 = 39
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and relies on direct subtraction and addition, making it efficient and easy to follow.  
- **Method 2** uses algebra, which is slightly more abstract but equally reliable. It is useful for problems with more complex relationships or unknowns.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is simpler and faster for this specific problem.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.  
- Using Method 1:  
   \[
   \text{Cost of small puzzle} = 23 - 15 = 8  
   \text{Cost of 3 small puzzles} = 3 \times 8 = 24  
   \text{Total cost} = 15 + 24 = 39
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the cost of a small puzzle: \( 23 - 15 = 8 \).  
- Verify the cost of 3 small puzzles: \( 3 \times 8 = 24 \).  
- Verify the total cost: \( 15 + 24 = 39 \).  
- All calculations are correct and reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **39**.

Q: The Great Pyramid of Giza was the tallest man-made structure on earth for almost 4000 years.  It is 20 feet taller than 500 feet, and 234 feet wider than it is tall.  What is the sum of the height and width of the Great Pyramid of Giza in feet?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The Great Pyramid of Giza is 20 feet taller than 500 feet.  
- Its width is 234 feet wider than its height.  
- We are tasked with finding the sum of the height and width of the pyramid in feet.  
- Assume all measurements are accurate and consistent, and no additional information is needed.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the height by adding 20 feet to 500 feet.  
- Calculate the width by adding 234 feet to the height.  
- Add the height and width together to find the sum.  

**Method 2:**  
- Represent the height as \( h = 500 + 20 \).  
- Represent the width as \( w = h + 234 \).  
- Substitute \( h \) into \( w \), then calculate \( h + w \) directly.  

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of height and width.**
1. **Calculate the height:**  
   The pyramid is 20 feet taller than 500 feet:  
   \[
   \text{Height} = 500 + 20 = 520 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

2. **Calculate the width:**  
   The width is 234 feet wider than the height:  
   \[
   \text{Width} = 520 + 234 = 754 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

3. **Calculate the sum of height and width:**  
   Add the height and width together:  
   \[
   \text{Sum} = 520 + 754 = 1274 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

4. **Check for pitfalls:**  
   - Ensure addition is performed correctly.  
   - Confirm that the width is calculated relative to the height.  

#### **Method 2: Algebraic representation and substitution.**
1. **Represent the height:**  
   The height is 20 feet taller than 500 feet:  
   \[
   h = 500 + 20 = 520 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

2. **Represent the width:**  
   The width is 234 feet wider than the height:  
   \[
   w = h + 234 = 520 + 234 = 754 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

3. **Calculate the sum of height and width:**  
   Substitute \( h = 520 \) and \( w = 754 \) into the sum:  
   \[
   h + w = 520 + 754 = 1274 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

4. **Check for pitfalls:**  
   - Ensure substitution is performed correctly.  
   - Confirm that the width is calculated relative to the height.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and intuitive, as it calculates each value step by step and then adds them.  
- **Method 2** uses algebraic representation, which is useful for more complex problems but is unnecessary for this simple calculation.  
- **Method 1** is better for this problem due to its simplicity and clarity.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Calculate the height:  
   \[
   \text{Height} = 500 + 20 = 520 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

2. Calculate the width:  
   \[
   \text{Width} = 520 + 234 = 754 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

3. Calculate the sum of height and width:  
   \[
   \text{Sum} = 520 + 754 = 1274 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recheck calculations:  
  \[
  500 + 20 = 520, \quad 520 + 234 = 754, \quad 520 + 754 = 1274.
  \]
- The results are consistent and reasonable.  

### Final Answer:
The sum of the height and width of the Great Pyramid of Giza is **1274 feet**.  
The answer is **1274**.

Q: Caleb picked a handful of dandelion puffs. He gave 3 to his mom, another 3 to his sister, 5 to his grandmother, and 2 to his dog. Then, he divided the remaining dandelion puffs equally among his 3 friends. How many dandelion puffs did each friend receive if he originally picked 40 dandelion puffs?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Caleb originally picked **40 dandelion puffs**.
- He gave:
  - **3 to his mom**,
  - **3 to his sister**,
  - **5 to his grandmother**,
  - **2 to his dog**.
- After giving these away, he divided the remaining dandelion puffs **equally among his 3 friends**.
- We assume no dandelion puffs were lost, destroyed, or unaccounted for during the process.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Sequential subtraction and division**
1. Subtract the total number of dandelion puffs given away from the original total.
2. Divide the remaining dandelion puffs equally among the 3 friends.

**Method 2: Total given away first, then calculate per friend**
1. Add up all the dandelion puffs Caleb gave away.
2. Subtract this total from the original number of dandelion puffs to find the remaining amount.
3. Divide the remaining amount by 3 to determine how many each friend received.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential subtraction and division**
1. Start with the original total: **40 dandelion puffs**.
2. Subtract the number given to his mom:  
   \( 40 - 3 = 37 \).
3. Subtract the number given to his sister:  
   \( 37 - 3 = 34 \).
4. Subtract the number given to his grandmother:  
   \( 34 - 5 = 29 \).
5. Subtract the number given to his dog:  
   \( 29 - 2 = 27 \).
6. Divide the remaining dandelion puffs equally among 3 friends:  
   \( 27 \div 3 = 9 \).

**Check for pitfalls:** This method is straightforward but requires careful tracking of each subtraction step to avoid errors.

#### **Method 2: Total given away first, then calculate per friend**
1. Add up all the dandelion puffs Caleb gave away:  
   \( 3 + 3 + 5 + 2 = 13 \).
2. Subtract the total given away from the original total:  
   \( 40 - 13 = 27 \).
3. Divide the remaining dandelion puffs equally among 3 friends:  
   \( 27 \div 3 = 9 \).

**Check for pitfalls:** This method consolidates the subtraction into one step, which reduces the chance of error but requires accurate addition of the given-away amounts.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** involves step-by-step subtraction, which is clear and easy to follow but slightly longer.
- **Method 2** consolidates the subtraction into one step by first summing the given-away amounts, making it more efficient and less prone to error in multi-step problems.
- **Method 2** is better for this problem because it is faster and reduces the number of intermediate steps.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 2**:
1. Add up the dandelion puffs Caleb gave away:  
   \( 3 + 3 + 5 + 2 = 13 \).
2. Subtract the total given away from the original total:  
   \( 40 - 13 = 27 \).
3. Divide the remaining dandelion puffs equally among 3 friends:  
   \( 27 \div 3 = 9 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the total given away: \( 3 + 3 + 5 + 2 = 13 \). Correct.
- Verify the remaining amount: \( 40 - 13 = 27 \). Correct.
- Verify the division: \( 27 \div 3 = 9 \). Correct.
- The result is reasonable because 27 is evenly divisible by 3, and the calculations align with the problem's context.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **9**.

Q: Jim is baking loaves of bread. He has 200g of flour in the cupboard, 100g of flour on the kitchen counter, and 100g in the pantry. If one loaf of bread requires 200g of flour, how many loaves can Jim bake?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jim has the following amounts of flour:
  - 200g in the cupboard.
  - 100g on the kitchen counter.
  - 100g in the pantry.
- One loaf of bread requires **200g of flour**.
- Assumptions:
  - All the flour is usable and not spoiled.
  - No flour is wasted during the baking process.
  - The total amount of flour is the sum of the amounts in the cupboard, on the counter, and in the pantry.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct addition and division.**
- Add the amounts of flour from all three locations to find the total amount of flour.
- Divide the total amount of flour by the amount required for one loaf (200g) to determine how many loaves Jim can bake.

**Method 2: Incremental grouping.**
- Group the flour into 200g portions (since each loaf requires 200g).
- Count how many full 200g portions can be made from the total flour.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition and division**
1. Add the amounts of flour:
   - Flour in the cupboard: 200g.
   - Flour on the counter: 100g.
   - Flour in the pantry: 100g.
   - Total flour = 200g + 100g + 100g = **400g**.

2. Divide the total flour by the amount required for one loaf:
   - Total flour ÷ Flour per loaf = 400g ÷ 200g = **2 loaves**.

3. Check for any leftover flour:
   - Remainder = Total flour % Flour per loaf = 400g % 200g = 0g.
   - There is no leftover flour, so Jim can bake exactly 2 loaves.

#### **Method 2: Incremental grouping**
1. Start with the total flour and group it into 200g portions:
   - Flour in the cupboard: 200g → 1 loaf.
   - Flour on the counter: 100g → Not enough for a loaf yet.
   - Flour in the pantry: 100g → Combine with the 100g on the counter to make another 200g → 1 loaf.

2. Count the total number of 200g portions:
   - 1 loaf from the cupboard.
   - 1 loaf from combining the counter and pantry flour.
   - Total = **2 loaves**.

3. Check for leftover flour:
   - All 400g of flour is used up in two 200g portions, so there is no leftover flour.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and efficient because it involves simple addition and division, making it faster and less prone to error.
- **Method 2** is more intuitive for visualizing the problem but requires more steps and is less efficient for larger quantities of flour.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better for clarity and reliability in this case.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Add the amounts of flour: 200g + 100g + 100g = **400g**.
2. Divide the total flour by the amount required for one loaf: 400g ÷ 200g = **2 loaves**.
3. Check for leftover flour: 400g % 200g = 0g (no leftover flour).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate the total flour: 200g + 100g + 100g = 400g.
- Recalculate the number of loaves: 400g ÷ 200g = 2.
- Verify there is no leftover flour: 400g % 200g = 0g.
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **2 loaves**.

Q: Mary brought 50 stickers to class. She gave 4 stickers to each of her 5 friends. She gave everyone else in class two stickers each. If she has 8 stickers left over, how many students are in the class, including Mary?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Mary brought **50 stickers** to class.
- She gave **4 stickers** to each of her **5 friends**.
- She gave **2 stickers** to each of the remaining students in the class.
- Mary has **8 stickers left over** after distributing the stickers.
- We are tasked with finding the total number of students in the class, including Mary.
- Assumptions:
  - All stickers are accounted for (none are lost or damaged).
  - Each student in the class, except Mary, received stickers.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use algebra to represent the problem.
- Let the total number of students in the class (including Mary) be \( n \).
- Subtract Mary and her 5 friends to find the number of remaining students: \( n - 1 - 5 = n - 6 \).
- Calculate the total number of stickers distributed:
  - Mary gave \( 4 \times 5 = 20 \) stickers to her 5 friends.
  - She gave \( 2 \times (n - 6) \) stickers to the remaining students.
  - The total stickers distributed plus the 8 stickers left over must equal 50.
- Set up the equation: \( 20 + 2(n - 6) + 8 = 50 \), and solve for \( n \).

#### Method 2: Work backward from the total stickers.
- Start with the total number of stickers (50) and subtract the stickers Mary has left (8).
- Subtract the stickers given to her 5 friends (\( 4 \times 5 = 20 \)).
- The remaining stickers were given to the rest of the class at 2 stickers per student.
- Divide the remaining stickers by 2 to find the number of other students, then add Mary and her 5 friends to find the total number of students.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Algebraic approach
1. Start with the equation:  
   \[
   20 + 2(n - 6) + 8 = 50
   \]
2. Simplify the terms:  
   \[
   20 + 2n - 12 + 8 = 50
   \]
   \[
   2n + 16 = 50
   \]
3. Subtract 16 from both sides:  
   \[
   2n = 34
   \]
4. Divide by 2:  
   \[
   n = 17
   \]
5. Interpret the result: The total number of students in the class, including Mary, is **17**.

#### Method 2: Work backward
1. Start with the total stickers: 50.
2. Subtract the stickers Mary has left:  
   \[
   50 - 8 = 42
   \]
3. Subtract the stickers given to her 5 friends:  
   \[
   42 - 20 = 22
   \]
4. The remaining 22 stickers were given to the rest of the class at 2 stickers per student. Divide by 2 to find the number of other students:  
   \[
   22 \div 2 = 11
   \]
5. Add Mary and her 5 friends to the 11 other students:  
   \[
   11 + 5 + 1 = 17
   \]
6. Interpret the result: The total number of students in the class, including Mary, is **17**.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Algebraic approach)**: This method is systematic and generalizable. It clearly sets up the problem as an equation and solves for the unknown. However, it requires more algebraic manipulation, which might be less intuitive for some.
- **Method 2 (Work backward)**: This method is more intuitive and relies on breaking the problem into smaller, logical steps. It is easier to follow and verify but may be less generalizable for more complex problems.

**Conclusion**: Both methods are valid, but Method 2 is more straightforward and easier to follow for this specific problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
Using **Method 2**:
1. Start with 50 stickers.
2. Subtract the 8 stickers Mary has left: \( 50 - 8 = 42 \).
3. Subtract the 20 stickers given to her 5 friends: \( 42 - 20 = 22 \).
4. Divide the remaining 22 stickers by 2 to find the number of other students: \( 22 \div 2 = 11 \).
5. Add Mary and her 5 friends: \( 11 + 5 + 1 = 17 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.
- Total stickers distributed:  
  - 5 friends received \( 4 \times 5 = 20 \) stickers.  
  - 11 other students received \( 2 \times 11 = 22 \) stickers.  
  - Total distributed: \( 20 + 22 = 42 \).  
- Stickers left: \( 50 - 42 = 8 \), which matches the problem statement.
- Total students: \( 11 + 5 + 1 = 17 \).

The calculations are consistent and correct.

---

The answer is **17**.

Q: Loris needs three more books to have the same number as Lamont, who has twice the number of books Darryl has. If Darryl has 20 books, calculate the total number of books the three have.
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Darryl has **20 books**.  
- Lamont has **twice the number of books Darryl has**, so Lamont has \( 2 \times 20 = 40 \) books.  
- Loris needs **three more books** to have the same number as Lamont, meaning Loris currently has \( 40 - 3 = 37 \) books.  
- We are tasked with calculating the **total number of books** owned by Darryl, Lamont, and Loris.  
- Assumptions: All books are accounted for, and no books are shared or missing.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the number of books each person has individually:  
  - Darryl: 20 books.  
  - Lamont: \( 2 \times 20 = 40 \) books.  
  - Loris: \( 40 - 3 = 37 \) books.  
- Add the three totals together: \( 20 + 40 + 37 \).  

**Method 2:**  
- Start with Darryl's books (20).  
- Add Lamont's books as twice Darryl's total: \( 20 + 40 \).  
- Add Loris's books as Lamont's total minus 3: \( 20 + 40 + (40 - 3) \).  

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of individual totals**
1. Darryl has **20 books**.  
2. Lamont has **twice Darryl's books**, so \( 2 \times 20 = 40 \).  
3. Loris needs **three more books** to match Lamont, so Loris has \( 40 - 3 = 37 \).  
4. Add the totals:  
   \[
   20 + 40 + 37 = 97
   \]  
5. Double-check:  
   - Darryl: 20 books.  
   - Lamont: \( 2 \times 20 = 40 \).  
   - Loris: \( 40 - 3 = 37 \).  
   - Total: \( 20 + 40 + 37 = 97 \).  

#### **Method 2: Sequential addition**
1. Start with Darryl's books: **20**.  
2. Add Lamont's books as twice Darryl's total: \( 20 + 40 = 60 \).  
3. Add Loris's books as Lamont's total minus 3: \( 60 + (40 - 3) = 60 + 37 = 97 \).  
4. Double-check:  
   - Darryl: 20 books.  
   - Lamont: \( 2 \times 20 = 40 \).  
   - Loris: \( 40 - 3 = 37 \).  
   - Total: \( 20 + 40 + 37 = 97 \).  

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is straightforward and involves calculating each person's total individually before summing them. It is clear and avoids unnecessary intermediate steps.  
- **Method 2** uses sequential addition, which is slightly more compact but requires keeping track of intermediate sums. This could introduce errors if not carefully managed.  
- **Method 1** is better for clarity and reliability, especially when dealing with multiple variables.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

Using **Method 1**:  
1. Darryl has **20 books**.  
2. Lamont has \( 2 \times 20 = 40 \) books.  
3. Loris has \( 40 - 3 = 37 \) books.  
4. Total: \( 20 + 40 + 37 = 97 \).  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Verify each calculation:  
  - Darryl: 20 books.  
  - Lamont: \( 2 \times 20 = 40 \).  
  - Loris: \( 40 - 3 = 37 \).  
- Verify the total: \( 20 + 40 + 37 = 97 \).  
- The result is reasonable and consistent with the problem's conditions.

---

The total number of books the three have is **97**.

Q: Davida worked 35 hours on each of Weeks 1 and 2. She worked 48 hours each of Weeks 3 and 4. How many more hours did Davida work on Weeks 3 and 4 than on Weeks 1 and 2?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Davida worked **35 hours** each week for **Weeks 1 and 2**, totaling 2 weeks.
- Davida worked **48 hours** each week for **Weeks 3 and 4**, totaling 2 weeks.
- The goal is to determine how many **more hours** Davida worked during Weeks 3 and 4 compared to Weeks 1 and 2.
- Assume all hours worked are accurate, and there are no partial hours or additional weeks.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total hours worked for Weeks 1 and 2, then calculate the total hours worked for Weeks 3 and 4. Subtract the total hours for Weeks 1 and 2 from the total hours for Weeks 3 and 4.

**Method 2:**  
Find the difference in hours worked per week (48 - 35), then multiply this difference by the number of weeks (2 weeks for each period). This avoids calculating total hours for each period.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Total hours subtraction**
1. Calculate the total hours worked for Weeks 1 and 2:  
   - Davida worked **35 hours per week** for 2 weeks.  
   - Total hours for Weeks 1 and 2 = \( 35 \times 2 = 70 \).

2. Calculate the total hours worked for Weeks 3 and 4:  
   - Davida worked **48 hours per week** for 2 weeks.  
   - Total hours for Weeks 3 and 4 = \( 48 \times 2 = 96 \).

3. Subtract the total hours for Weeks 1 and 2 from the total hours for Weeks 3 and 4:  
   - Difference = \( 96 - 70 = 26 \).

4. Double-check calculations:  
   - \( 35 \times 2 = 70 \) and \( 48 \times 2 = 96 \) are correct.  
   - \( 96 - 70 = 26 \) checks out.

#### **Method 2: Weekly difference multiplied by weeks**
1. Find the difference in hours worked per week:  
   - Difference per week = \( 48 - 35 = 13 \).

2. Multiply the weekly difference by the number of weeks:  
   - Total difference = \( 13 \times 2 = 26 \).

3. Double-check calculations:  
   - \( 48 - 35 = 13 \) is correct.  
   - \( 13 \times 2 = 26 \) checks out.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** involves calculating total hours for each period and then subtracting, which is straightforward and ensures all intermediate steps are clear. However, it requires more steps and calculations.
- **Method 2** focuses directly on the difference in hours worked per week and multiplies by the number of weeks, making it more efficient and faster. It avoids unnecessary calculations of total hours.

**Conclusion:** Method 2 is better for this problem because it is simpler and avoids redundant calculations.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 2**:  
1. Find the difference in hours worked per week:  
   - \( 48 - 35 = 13 \).

2. Multiply the weekly difference by the number of weeks:  
   - \( 13 \times 2 = 26 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Using Method 1 as a check:  
   - Total hours for Weeks 1 and 2 = \( 35 \times 2 = 70 \).  
   - Total hours for Weeks 3 and 4 = \( 48 \times 2 = 96 \).  
   - Difference = \( 96 - 70 = 26 \).  

- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

---

The answer is **26**.

Q: A liter of chlorine costs $10 and is now sold at 20% off. A box of soap that costs $16 is now sold at 25% off. How much will you save if you buy 3 liters of chlorine and 5 boxes of soap?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The cost of 1 liter of chlorine is $10, but it is now sold at a 20% discount.
- The cost of 1 box of soap is $16, but it is now sold at a 25% discount.
- You plan to buy 3 liters of chlorine and 5 boxes of soap.
- We assume the discounts are applied to the original prices and there are no additional taxes or fees.
- The goal is to calculate the total savings from the discounts.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Calculate the savings per item, then multiply by the quantities.**
1. Compute the discount for 1 liter of chlorine and 1 box of soap.
2. Multiply the per-item savings by the quantities purchased (3 liters of chlorine and 5 boxes of soap).
3. Add the total savings for chlorine and soap.

**Method 2: Calculate the total cost before and after the discounts, then find the difference.**
1. Compute the total cost of 3 liters of chlorine and 5 boxes of soap at their original prices.
2. Compute the total cost of 3 liters of chlorine and 5 boxes of soap after applying the discounts.
3. Subtract the discounted total from the original total to find the savings.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Calculate savings per item, then multiply by quantities.**
1. **Savings per liter of chlorine:**
   - Original price = $10.
   - Discount = 20% of $10 = \( 10 \times 0.20 = 2 \).
   - Savings per liter = $2.

2. **Savings per box of soap:**
   - Original price = $16.
   - Discount = 25% of $16 = \( 16 \times 0.25 = 4 \).
   - Savings per box = $4.

3. **Total savings for chlorine:**
   - Savings per liter = $2.
   - Quantity = 3 liters.
   - Total savings for chlorine = \( 2 \times 3 = 6 \).

4. **Total savings for soap:**
   - Savings per box = $4.
   - Quantity = 5 boxes.
   - Total savings for soap = \( 4 \times 5 = 20 \).

5. **Total savings:**
   - Total savings = Savings for chlorine + Savings for soap.
   - Total savings = \( 6 + 20 = 26 \).

---

#### **Method 2: Calculate total cost before and after discounts, then find the difference.**
1. **Original total cost:**
   - Chlorine: \( 3 \times 10 = 30 \).
   - Soap: \( 5 \times 16 = 80 \).
   - Original total cost = \( 30 + 80 = 110 \).

2. **Discounted total cost:**
   - Chlorine: Discounted price per liter = \( 10 - 2 = 8 \).
     - Total for chlorine = \( 3 \times 8 = 24 \).
   - Soap: Discounted price per box = \( 16 - 4 = 12 \).
     - Total for soap = \( 5 \times 12 = 60 \).
   - Discounted total cost = \( 24 + 60 = 84 \).

3. **Total savings:**
   - Total savings = Original total cost - Discounted total cost.
   - Total savings = \( 110 - 84 = 26 \).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward because it focuses directly on the savings per item and scales them by quantity. It avoids the intermediate step of calculating total costs, making it slightly faster and less prone to error.
- **Method 2** is more comprehensive, as it calculates both the original and discounted totals, which can be useful for verifying the final savings. However, it involves more steps and intermediate calculations, which may introduce more opportunities for mistakes.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.

1. Savings per liter of chlorine = $2.
   - Total savings for chlorine = \( 2 \times 3 = 6 \).

2. Savings per box of soap = $4.
   - Total savings for soap = \( 4 \times 5 = 20 \).

3. Total savings = \( 6 + 20 = 26 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Using **Method 2**, the original total cost is $110, and the discounted total cost is $84. The difference is \( 110 - 84 = 26 \), confirming the result.
- Both methods yield the same result, and the calculations are consistent.

---

The answer is **26**.

Q: John adopts a dog from a shelter.  The dog ends up having health problems and this requires 3 vet appointments,  which cost $400 each.  After the first appointment, John paid $100 for pet insurance that covers 80% of the subsequent visits.  How much did he pay in total?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- John adopts a dog and incurs costs for three vet appointments, each costing $400.
- After the first appointment, John pays $100 for pet insurance.
- The insurance covers **80% of the costs** for the **second and third vet appointments**.
- We assume the insurance coverage applies only to the second and third appointments, not retroactively to the first.
- We are tasked with calculating the **total amount John paid**.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Sequential Calculation**
1. Calculate the cost of the first vet appointment (no insurance coverage).
2. Add the cost of the pet insurance.
3. Calculate the cost of the second and third appointments after applying the 80% insurance coverage.
4. Add all these costs together to find the total.

**Method 2: Grouped Calculation**
1. Calculate the total cost of all three vet appointments without insurance.
2. Subtract the 80% insurance coverage for the second and third appointments.
3. Add the cost of the pet insurance.
4. Combine all costs to find the total.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail.

#### **Method 1: Sequential Calculation**
1. **First vet appointment cost**:  
   The first appointment costs $400, and there is no insurance coverage.  
   - Cost = $400.

2. **Pet insurance cost**:  
   John pays $100 for pet insurance after the first appointment.  
   - Cost = $100.

3. **Second and third vet appointments with insurance**:  
   Each appointment costs $400, but insurance covers 80% of these costs.  
   - Insurance covers 80% of $400 = \( 0.8 \times 400 = 320 \).  
   - John pays the remaining 20% of $400 = \( 0.2 \times 400 = 80 \).  
   - For two appointments, John pays \( 80 \times 2 = 160 \).

4. **Total cost**:  
   Add the costs from all steps:  
   - Total = $400 (first appointment) + $100 (insurance) + $160 (second and third appointments).  
   - Total = $660.

---

#### **Method 2: Grouped Calculation**
1. **Total cost of all three appointments without insurance**:  
   Each appointment costs $400, so the total for three appointments is:  
   - \( 400 \times 3 = 1200 \).

2. **Insurance coverage for the second and third appointments**:  
   Insurance covers 80% of the costs for two appointments:  
   - Total insurance coverage = \( 0.8 \times 400 \times 2 = 640 \).

3. **John's share of the second and third appointments**:  
   Subtract the insurance coverage from the total cost of the second and third appointments:  
   - Total cost of two appointments = \( 400 \times 2 = 800 \).  
   - John's share = \( 800 - 640 = 160 \).

4. **Add the first appointment and pet insurance costs**:  
   - First appointment = $400.  
   - Pet insurance = $100.  
   - Total = $400 (first appointment) + $100 (insurance) + $160 (second and third appointments).  
   - Total = $660.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more intuitive because it calculates costs step by step in the order they occur, making it easier to follow.  
- **Method 2** groups costs and insurance coverage, which is efficient but slightly more abstract.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer and less prone to errors for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
Using **Method 1**:
1. First appointment cost = $400.  
2. Pet insurance cost = $100.  
3. Second and third appointments (after insurance): \( 80 \times 2 = 160 \).  
4. Total = $400 + $100 + $160 = $660.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Without insurance, the total cost of three appointments would be \( 400 \times 3 = 1200 \).  
- Insurance covers \( 0.8 \times 400 \times 2 = 640 \), leaving John to pay \( 1200 - 640 = 560 \) for the appointments. Adding the $100 insurance cost gives \( 560 + 100 = 660 \).  
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

The answer is **660**.
