Q: Bob buys 50 feet of rope.  He uses a 5th of it to make a small piece of art.  He takes the rest and gives half of it to the friend.  After that, he cuts 2-foot sections.  How many sections does he get?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Bob starts with **50 feet of rope**.
- He uses **1/5 of the rope** to make a small piece of art.
- The remaining rope is split into two equal halves, and **half of it is given to a friend**.
- Bob then cuts the remaining rope into **2-foot sections**.
- Assumptions:
  - No rope is wasted or lost during cutting or splitting.
  - All measurements are exact, and the rope is divisible without remainder.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Sequential calculation approach**  
- Calculate how much rope is used for the art (1/5 of 50 feet).  
- Subtract the rope used for the art to find the remaining rope.  
- Divide the remaining rope into two equal halves.  
- Determine how much rope Bob keeps after giving half to his friend.  
- Divide the rope Bob keeps into 2-foot sections and count the number of sections.

**Method 2: Fraction-based approach**  
- Represent the rope usage and division as fractions of the total length (50 feet).  
- Calculate the fraction of rope Bob keeps after each step.  
- Convert the final fraction into feet and divide by 2 to find the number of sections.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation approach**

1. **Calculate rope used for the art:**  
   Bob uses **1/5 of 50 feet** for the art:  
   \[
   \frac{1}{5} \times 50 = 10 \, \text{feet}.
   \]  
   Rope remaining:  
   \[
   50 - 10 = 40 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

2. **Divide the remaining rope into two halves:**  
   Bob splits the remaining 40 feet into two equal halves:  
   \[
   \frac{40}{2} = 20 \, \text{feet per half}.
   \]  
   He gives **20 feet** to his friend, leaving him with **20 feet**.

3. **Cut the remaining rope into 2-foot sections:**  
   Bob cuts the 20 feet of rope into 2-foot sections:  
   \[
   \frac{20}{2} = 10 \, \text{sections}.
   \]

---

#### **Method 2: Fraction-based approach**

1. **Fraction of rope remaining after the art:**  
   After using **1/5** of the rope for the art, Bob has **4/5** of the rope left:  
   \[
   \frac{4}{5} \times 50 = 40 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

2. **Fraction of rope Bob keeps after giving half to his friend:**  
   Bob keeps **1/2** of the remaining rope:  
   \[
   \frac{1}{2} \times 40 = 20 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

3. **Number of 2-foot sections:**  
   Bob cuts the 20 feet of rope into 2-foot sections:  
   \[
   \frac{20}{2} = 10 \, \text{sections}.
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more intuitive and follows a step-by-step approach, making it easier to track the calculations.  
- **Method 2** uses fractions, which can be more abstract but is efficient for problems involving proportional division.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better for clarity and reliability in this context.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

Using **Method 1**:  
1. Rope used for the art:  
   \[
   \frac{1}{5} \times 50 = 10 \, \text{feet}.
   \]  
   Rope remaining:  
   \[
   50 - 10 = 40 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

2. Rope Bob keeps after giving half to his friend:  
   \[
   \frac{40}{2} = 20 \, \text{feet}.
   \]

3. Number of 2-foot sections:  
   \[
   \frac{20}{2} = 10 \, \text{sections}.
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Total rope: 50 feet.  
- Rope used for art: 10 feet. Remaining: 40 feet.  
- Rope given to friend: 20 feet. Remaining: 20 feet.  
- Cutting into 2-foot sections:  
   \[
   \frac{20}{2} = 10 \, \text{sections}.
   \]  
All calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

The answer is **10**.

Q: Bonny just bought her 13th pair of shoes, which is 5 less than twice as many pairs of shoes as Becky owns.  Bobby has 3 times as many pairs of shoes as Becky has.  How many shoes does Bobby have?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

- Bonny owns 13 pairs of shoes.  
- Bonny's number of shoes is **5 less than twice as many pairs of shoes as Becky owns**.  
- Bobby owns **3 times as many pairs of shoes as Becky owns**.  
- We need to determine how many pairs of shoes Bobby owns.  
- Assume all numbers are whole numbers and there are no fractional pairs of shoes.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
- Use algebra to represent the relationships. Let Becky’s number of shoes be \( x \).  
- Bonny’s shoes are \( 2x - 5 \), and Bobby’s shoes are \( 3x \).  
- Solve for \( x \) using Bonny’s known number of shoes (13 pairs), then calculate Bobby’s shoes.

**Method 2:**  
- Use trial-and-error reasoning based on the relationships.  
- Start with Becky’s shoes as \( x \), calculate Bonny’s shoes as \( 2x - 5 \), and adjust \( x \) until Bonny’s shoes equal 13.  
- Once \( x \) is found, calculate Bobby’s shoes as \( 3x \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Algebraic Approach**  
1. Let Becky’s number of shoes be \( x \).  
2. Bonny’s shoes are \( 2x - 5 \).  
3. Set \( 2x - 5 = 13 \) (since Bonny owns 13 pairs).  
   \[
   2x - 5 = 13
   \]
4. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   2x = 13 + 5 = 18
   \]
   \[
   x = \frac{18}{2} = 9
   \]
5. Becky owns \( x = 9 \) pairs of shoes.  
6. Bobby’s shoes are \( 3x = 3 \times 9 = 27 \).  

#### **Method 2: Trial-and-Error Approach**  
1. Start with Becky’s shoes as \( x \).  
2. Calculate Bonny’s shoes as \( 2x - 5 \).  
3. Adjust \( x \) until \( 2x - 5 = 13 \).  
   - If \( x = 9 \):  
     \[
     2x - 5 = 2(9) - 5 = 18 - 5 = 13
     \]
     This satisfies the condition.  
4. Bobby’s shoes are \( 3x = 3 \times 9 = 27 \).  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1 (Algebraic Approach)** is more systematic and reliable. It uses clear equations to solve for \( x \) and avoids guesswork.  
- **Method 2 (Trial-and-Error)** can work for small numbers but becomes inefficient for larger or more complex problems. It relies on intuition and repeated testing, which is less precise.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

**Method 1 is better due to its clarity and efficiency.**  
1. Let Becky’s shoes be \( x \).  
2. Bonny’s shoes are \( 2x - 5 \).  
3. Solve \( 2x - 5 = 13 \):  
   \[
   2x = 18 \quad \text{(add 5 to both sides)}
   \]
   \[
   x = 9 \quad \text{(divide by 2)}
   \]
4. Bobby’s shoes are \( 3x = 3 \times 9 = 27 \).  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Becky owns \( x = 9 \) pairs of shoes.  
- Bonny’s shoes are \( 2x - 5 = 2(9) - 5 = 18 - 5 = 13 \), which matches the given information.  
- Bobby’s shoes are \( 3x = 3 \times 9 = 27 \).  
- All calculations check out, and the result is reasonable.

### Final Answer:  
The answer is **27**.

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The concert lasted **1 hour 20 minutes**, which is equivalent to **80 minutes**.
- There was a **10-minute intermission**, meaning the total time for songs was **80 - 10 = 70 minutes**.
- Each song lasted **5 minutes**, except for one song that lasted **10 minutes**.
- We assume all songs were sung consecutively, and no time was wasted between songs.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Divide the total time for songs (70 minutes) into the time taken by the 10-minute song and the remaining time for 5-minute songs. Then calculate how many 5-minute songs fit into the remaining time.

**Method 2:**  
Subtract the time for the 10-minute song from the total song time (70 minutes), then divide the remaining time by 5 to find the number of 5-minute songs. Add 1 to account for the 10-minute song.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total time for songs = 70 minutes.
2. Deduct the time for the 10-minute song:  
   \( 70 - 10 = 60 \) minutes remaining for 5-minute songs.
3. Divide the remaining time by 5 to find the number of 5-minute songs:  
   \( 60 \div 5 = 12 \) songs.
4. Add the 10-minute song to the count:  
   \( 12 + 1 = 13 \) songs.

**Checks:**  
- Total time for songs: \( 12 \times 5 + 10 = 60 + 10 = 70 \), which matches the given total song time.  
- No time is wasted, and all songs fit within the 70 minutes.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Total time for songs = 70 minutes.
2. Subtract the time for the 10-minute song:  
   \( 70 - 10 = 60 \) minutes remaining for 5-minute songs.
3. Divide the remaining time by 5 to find the number of 5-minute songs:  
   \( 60 \div 5 = 12 \) songs.
4. Add 1 to account for the 10-minute song:  
   \( 12 + 1 = 13 \) songs.

**Checks:**  
- Total time for songs: \( 12 \times 5 + 10 = 60 + 10 = 70 \), which matches the given total song time.  
- The logic aligns with the problem constraints.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is slightly more intuitive because it directly divides the remaining time after accounting for the 10-minute song. It avoids unnecessary steps and is straightforward.  
- **Method 2** is equally reliable but involves an extra step of adding 1 at the end, which might be less intuitive for some.  

Both methods are efficient and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is slightly clearer.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Total time for songs = 70 minutes.
2. Deduct the time for the 10-minute song:  
   \( 70 - 10 = 60 \) minutes remaining for 5-minute songs.
3. Divide the remaining time by 5 to find the number of 5-minute songs:  
   \( 60 \div 5 = 12 \) songs.
4. Add the 10-minute song to the count:  
   \( 12 + 1 = 13 \) songs.

Q: At a CD store, 40% of the CDs cost $10 each, and the rest cost $5 each. Prince bought half the CDs sold at $10 each, and all of the CDs sold at $5 each. If the total number of CDs was 200, how much money did Prince spend on buying the CDs?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and assumptions.
- Total number of CDs: 200.  
- 40% of the CDs cost $10 each.  
- The remaining 60% of the CDs cost $5 each.  
- Prince bought **half** of the CDs sold at $10 each and **all** of the CDs sold at $5 each.  
- We assume all CDs are sold at their respective prices, and there are no discounts or additional fees.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the number of CDs sold at $10 each (40% of 200).  
- Calculate the number of CDs sold at $5 each (60% of 200).  
- Determine how many CDs Prince bought at $10 each (half of the $10 CDs).  
- Multiply the number of CDs Prince bought by their respective prices and sum the total cost.  

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the total cost of all CDs sold at $10 and $5.  
- Determine the fraction of the total cost Prince paid (half of the $10 CDs and all of the $5 CDs).  

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of quantities and costs.**
1. **Calculate the number of CDs sold at $10 each:**  
   \( 40\% \times 200 = 0.4 \times 200 = 80 \) CDs.  

2. **Calculate the number of CDs sold at $5 each:**  
   \( 60\% \times 200 = 0.6 \times 200 = 120 \) CDs.  

3. **Determine how many $10 CDs Prince bought:**  
   Prince bought **half** of the $10 CDs:  
   \( \frac{80}{2} = 40 \) CDs.  

4. **Determine how many $5 CDs Prince bought:**  
   Prince bought **all** of the $5 CDs:  
   \( 120 \) CDs.  

5. **Calculate the total cost of the $10 CDs Prince bought:**  
   \( 40 \times 10 = 400 \) dollars.  

6. **Calculate the total cost of the $5 CDs Prince bought:**  
   \( 120 \times 5 = 600 \) dollars.  

7. **Add the costs to find the total amount Prince spent:**  
   \( 400 + 600 = 1000 \) dollars.  

#### **Method 2: Fractional cost approach.**
1. **Calculate the total cost of all $10 CDs:**  
   \( 80 \times 10 = 800 \) dollars.  

2. **Calculate the total cost of all $5 CDs:**  
   \( 120 \times 5 = 600 \) dollars.  

3. **Determine the fraction of the $10 CD cost Prince paid:**  
   Prince bought **half** of the $10 CDs, so he paid:  
   \( \frac{800}{2} = 400 \) dollars.  

4. **Determine the fraction of the $5 CD cost Prince paid:**  
   Prince bought **all** of the $5 CDs, so he paid:  
   \( 600 \) dollars.  

5. **Add the amounts Prince paid for $10 CDs and $5 CDs:**  
   \( 400 + 600 = 1000 \) dollars.  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more intuitive and straightforward, as it focuses on calculating quantities and costs step by step.  
- **Method 2** is slightly more abstract, relying on fractional cost calculations, but it is equally reliable.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is easier to follow for most people due to its direct approach.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and simplicity.  
- Using Method 1:  
  - Number of $10 CDs Prince bought: 40.  
  - Number of $5 CDs Prince bought: 120.  
  - Total cost: \( 40 \times 10 + 120 \times 5 = 400 + 600 = 1000 \).  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.
- Verify calculations:  
  - \( 40\% \times 200 = 80 \), \( 60\% \times 200 = 120 \).  
  - \( 40 \times 10 = 400 \), \( 120 \times 5 = 600 \).  
  - \( 400 + 600 = 1000 \).  
- The calculations are correct, and the result is reasonable given the problem context.  

### Final Answer:
The answer is **1000**.

Q: James binges on Cheezits and eats 3 bags that are 2 ounces each.  There are 150 calories in an ounce of Cheezits.  He then goes for a 40-minute run and burns 12 calories per minute.  How many excess calories did he eat?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- James eats 3 bags of Cheezits, each weighing 2 ounces.
- Each ounce of Cheezits contains 150 calories.
- James goes for a 40-minute run and burns 12 calories per minute.
- We assume all calorie values are accurate and consistent.
- We are tasked with finding the **excess calories** James consumed after accounting for the calories burned during his run.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct calculation of total calories consumed and burned.**
- Calculate the total calories James consumed from the Cheezits.
- Calculate the total calories James burned during his run.
- Subtract the calories burned from the calories consumed to find the excess calories.

**Method 2: Break the problem into smaller steps and use ratios.**
- First, calculate the calories consumed per bag and multiply by the number of bags.
- Then, calculate the calories burned per minute and multiply by the total minutes of the run.
- Subtract the calories burned from the calories consumed to find the excess calories.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation**
1. **Calculate total calories consumed:**
   - Each bag is 2 ounces, and James eats 3 bags.
   - Total ounces = \( 3 \times 2 = 6 \) ounces.
   - Calories per ounce = 150.
   - Total calories consumed = \( 6 \times 150 = 900 \) calories.

2. **Calculate total calories burned:**
   - James runs for 40 minutes and burns 12 calories per minute.
   - Total calories burned = \( 40 \times 12 = 480 \) calories.

3. **Find excess calories:**
   - Excess calories = Total calories consumed - Total calories burned.
   - Excess calories = \( 900 - 480 = 420 \).

4. **Double-check calculations:**
   - \( 6 \times 150 = 900 \) (calories consumed).
   - \( 40 \times 12 = 480 \) (calories burned).
   - \( 900 - 480 = 420 \) (excess calories).

---

#### **Method 2: Break into smaller steps**
1. **Calculate calories consumed per bag:**
   - Each bag is 2 ounces, and each ounce contains 150 calories.
   - Calories per bag = \( 2 \times 150 = 300 \) calories.

2. **Calculate total calories consumed:**
   - James eats 3 bags.
   - Total calories consumed = \( 3 \times 300 = 900 \) calories.

3. **Calculate calories burned per minute:**
   - James burns 12 calories per minute.
   - Total calories burned = \( 40 \times 12 = 480 \) calories.

4. **Find excess calories:**
   - Excess calories = Total calories consumed - Total calories burned.
   - Excess calories = \( 900 - 480 = 420 \).

5. **Double-check calculations:**
   - Calories per bag = \( 2 \times 150 = 300 \).
   - Total calories consumed = \( 3 \times 300 = 900 \).
   - Total calories burned = \( 40 \times 12 = 480 \).
   - Excess calories = \( 900 - 480 = 420 \).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it calculates total calories consumed and burned in fewer steps. It avoids breaking the problem into smaller sub-calculations, which reduces the risk of errors.
- **Method 2** is slightly more detailed and breaks the problem into smaller, manageable parts. While this can be helpful for clarity, it introduces more intermediate steps, which may increase the chance of mistakes.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this problem due to its simplicity and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Total calories consumed = \( 6 \times 150 = 900 \) calories.
2. Total calories burned = \( 40 \times 12 = 480 \) calories.
3. Excess calories = \( 900 - 480 = 420 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Calories consumed: \( 6 \times 150 = 900 \) (reasonable for 3 bags of Cheezits).
- Calories burned: \( 40 \times 12 = 480 \) (reasonable for a 40-minute run).
- Excess calories: \( 900 - 480 = 420 \) (consistent across both methods).

Q: A chef needs to make french fries.  He can get 25 fries out of 1 potato.  He has 15 potatoes and he needs 200 fries.  How many potatoes will he have leftover?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The chef can make **25 fries from 1 potato**.
- The chef has **15 potatoes** available.
- The chef needs to make **200 fries**.
- We assume all potatoes are of uniform size and yield exactly 25 fries each.
- We are tasked with determining how many potatoes will be **leftover** after the chef uses the required number to make 200 fries.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct calculation using division and subtraction.**
- Calculate how many potatoes are needed to make 200 fries by dividing 200 by 25 (fries per potato).
- Subtract the number of potatoes used from the total number of potatoes (15) to find the leftover potatoes.

**Method 2: Iterative approach using multiplication.**
- Start with the total number of fries needed (200) and iteratively subtract the fries produced by each potato (25 fries per potato) until the requirement is met.
- Count how many potatoes are used in the process, then subtract this count from the total number of potatoes (15) to find the leftovers.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation using division and subtraction**
1. Calculate how many potatoes are needed:
   \[
   \text{Potatoes needed} = \frac{\text{Fries needed}}{\text{Fries per potato}} = \frac{200}{25} = 8
   \]
   - The chef needs **8 potatoes** to make 200 fries.

2. Subtract the number of potatoes used from the total number of potatoes:
   \[
   \text{Leftover potatoes} = \text{Total potatoes} - \text{Potatoes used} = 15 - 8 = 7
   \]

3. Double-check:
   - Using 8 potatoes yields \( 8 \times 25 = 200 \) fries, which matches the requirement.
   - Subtracting 8 from 15 gives 7 leftover potatoes.

#### **Method 2: Iterative approach using multiplication**
1. Start with 200 fries needed and iteratively subtract the fries produced by each potato:
   - After 1 potato: \( 200 - 25 = 175 \) fries left.
   - After 2 potatoes: \( 175 - 25 = 150 \) fries left.
   - After 3 potatoes: \( 150 - 25 = 125 \) fries left.
   - After 4 potatoes: \( 125 - 25 = 100 \) fries left.
   - After 5 potatoes: \( 100 - 25 = 75 \) fries left.
   - After 6 potatoes: \( 75 - 25 = 50 \) fries left.
   - After 7 potatoes: \( 50 - 25 = 25 \) fries left.
   - After 8 potatoes: \( 25 - 25 = 0 \) fries left.

2. Count the number of potatoes used:
   - **8 potatoes** are used to make 200 fries.

3. Subtract the number of potatoes used from the total number of potatoes:
   \[
   \text{Leftover potatoes} = \text{Total potatoes} - \text{Potatoes used} = 15 - 8 = 7
   \]

4. Double-check:
   - Using 8 potatoes yields \( 8 \times 25 = 200 \) fries, which matches the requirement.
   - Subtracting 8 from 15 gives 7 leftover potatoes.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more efficient and straightforward because it uses direct division and subtraction, minimizing the number of steps and reducing the chance of error.
- **Method 2** is more labor-intensive and involves repetitive calculations, which increases the likelihood of mistakes. However, it can be useful for visualizing the process step by step.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and reliability.
- Solution:
  \[
  \text{Potatoes needed} = \frac{200}{25} = 8
  \]
  \[
  \text{Leftover potatoes} = 15 - 8 = 7
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Using 8 potatoes yields \( 8 \times 25 = 200 \) fries, which matches the requirement.
- Subtracting 8 from 15 gives 7 leftover potatoes, which is reasonable given the total number of potatoes available.

---

The answer is **7**.

Q: At peak hours, 11 am - 1 pm and 5 pm - 6 pm, the local fast-food restaurant serves 12 cars every 15 minutes.  Off-peak times, they serve 8 cars every 15 minutes.  From 4 pm - 6 pm, how many customers do they serve?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The fast-food restaurant serves **12 cars every 15 minutes** during peak hours (11 am - 1 pm and 5 pm - 6 pm).
- The restaurant serves **8 cars every 15 minutes** during off-peak hours.
- The time period in question is **4 pm - 6 pm**, which includes:
  - **4 pm - 5 pm**: Off-peak time.
  - **5 pm - 6 pm**: Peak time.
- We assume the service rate remains constant within each 15-minute interval.
- We are tasked with finding the total number of cars served from 4 pm to 6 pm.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Break the time into intervals and calculate cars served in each interval.**
- Divide the 2-hour period (4 pm - 6 pm) into four 15-minute intervals.
- Calculate the number of cars served during off-peak (4 pm - 5 pm) and peak (5 pm - 6 pm) times separately, then sum the results.

**Method 2: Use rates to calculate cars served per hour and multiply by the duration of each time period.**
- Determine the hourly rate for off-peak and peak times.
- Multiply the hourly rate by the duration of each time period (1 hour for off-peak, 1 hour for peak), then sum the results.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Break into intervals and calculate cars served.**
1. **4 pm - 5 pm (Off-peak):**
   - Off-peak rate: 8 cars every 15 minutes.
   - Number of 15-minute intervals in 1 hour: \( \frac{60}{15} = 4 \).
   - Cars served in 1 hour: \( 8 \times 4 = 32 \).

2. **5 pm - 6 pm (Peak):**
   - Peak rate: 12 cars every 15 minutes.
   - Number of 15-minute intervals in 1 hour: \( \frac{60}{15} = 4 \).
   - Cars served in 1 hour: \( 12 \times 4 = 48 \).

3. **Total cars served:**
   - Add the cars served during off-peak and peak times: \( 32 + 48 = 80 \).

#### **Method 2: Use rates to calculate cars served per hour.**
1. **Off-peak (4 pm - 5 pm):**
   - Off-peak hourly rate: \( 8 \times 4 = 32 \) cars per hour.
   - Duration: 1 hour.
   - Cars served: \( 32 \times 1 = 32 \).

2. **Peak (5 pm - 6 pm):**
   - Peak hourly rate: \( 12 \times 4 = 48 \) cars per hour.
   - Duration: 1 hour.
   - Cars served: \( 48 \times 1 = 48 \).

3. **Total cars served:**
   - Add the cars served during off-peak and peak times: \( 32 + 48 = 80 \).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more detailed, breaking the problem into smaller intervals, which makes it easier to follow for beginners or when dealing with irregular time periods.
- **Method 2** is more efficient, as it directly uses hourly rates and durations, reducing the number of intermediate steps.
- Both methods are equally reliable and yield the same result. However, **Method 2** is better for this problem due to its simplicity and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 2**:
1. Off-peak (4 pm - 5 pm): \( 8 \times 4 = 32 \) cars per hour.
   - Duration: 1 hour.
   - Cars served: \( 32 \times 1 = 32 \).

2. Peak (5 pm - 6 pm): \( 12 \times 4 = 48 \) cars per hour.
   - Duration: 1 hour.
   - Cars served: \( 48 \times 1 = 48 \).

3. Total cars served: \( 32 + 48 = 80 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 1:
  - Off-peak (4 pm - 5 pm): \( 8 \times 4 = 32 \).
  - Peak (5 pm - 6 pm): \( 12 \times 4 = 48 \).
  - Total: \( 32 + 48 = 80 \).
- Both methods confirm the result is correct and reasonable.

Q: There are 10 6-ounces of glasses that are only 4/5 full of water. How many ounces of water are needed to fill to the brim all those 10 glasses?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Each glass has a capacity of 6 ounces.
- There are 10 glasses.
- Each glass is currently **4/5 full** of water.
- We need to calculate how many ounces of water are required to fill all 10 glasses to the brim.
- Assumptions:
  - The glasses are uniform in size and shape.
  - No water is spilled or lost during the filling process.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Calculate the missing water for one glass, then scale up for 10 glasses.**
- First, determine how much water is missing from one glass by subtracting the current amount of water (4/5 of 6 ounces) from the full capacity (6 ounces).
- Multiply the missing water for one glass by 10 to find the total amount of water needed.

**Method 2: Calculate the total water currently in all 10 glasses, then subtract from the total capacity of all 10 glasses.**
- First, calculate the total capacity of all 10 glasses (10 × 6 ounces).
- Then, calculate the total amount of water currently in all 10 glasses (10 × 4/5 of 6 ounces).
- Subtract the total current water from the total capacity to find the total water needed.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Calculate missing water for one glass, then scale up.**
1. **Find the current amount of water in one glass:**
   - A glass is 4/5 full, so the current water in one glass is:
     \[
     \text{Current water in one glass} = \frac{4}{5} \times 6 = 4.8 \, \text{ounces}.
     \]

2. **Find the missing water in one glass:**
   - The full capacity of one glass is 6 ounces, so the missing water is:
     \[
     \text{Missing water in one glass} = 6 - 4.8 = 1.2 \, \text{ounces}.
     \]

3. **Scale up for 10 glasses:**
   - Multiply the missing water for one glass by 10:
     \[
     \text{Total missing water for 10 glasses} = 1.2 \times 10 = 12 \, \text{ounces}.
     \]

4. **Check for errors:**
   - Each glass is missing 1.2 ounces, and there are 10 glasses, so the total missing water logically adds up to 12 ounces.

---

#### **Method 2: Calculate total current water, then subtract from total capacity.**
1. **Find the total capacity of all 10 glasses:**
   - Each glass holds 6 ounces, so the total capacity is:
     \[
     \text{Total capacity} = 10 \times 6 = 60 \, \text{ounces}.
     \]

2. **Find the total current water in all 10 glasses:**
   - Each glass is 4/5 full, so the current water in one glass is:
     \[
     \text{Current water in one glass} = \frac{4}{5} \times 6 = 4.8 \, \text{ounces}.
     \]
   - Multiply by 10 to find the total current water:
     \[
     \text{Total current water} = 10 \times 4.8 = 48 \, \text{ounces}.
     \]

3. **Find the total missing water:**
   - Subtract the total current water from the total capacity:
     \[
     \text{Total missing water} = 60 - 48 = 12 \, \text{ounces}.
     \]

4. **Check for errors:**
   - The total capacity is 60 ounces, and the total current water is 48 ounces, so the missing water logically adds up to 12 ounces.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is straightforward and focuses on one glass at a time, making it easier to follow for smaller-scale problems. However, it requires an additional step to scale up for 10 glasses.
- **Method 2** works directly with the total quantities, which is more efficient for larger numbers of glasses. It also avoids intermediate scaling steps, reducing the chance of errors.

**Conclusion:** Method 2 is better for this problem because it is more efficient and works directly with total quantities.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 2**:
1. Total capacity of all 10 glasses:
   \[
   10 \times 6 = 60 \, \text{ounces}.
   \]

2. Total current water in all 10 glasses:
   \[
   10 \times \left(\frac{4}{5} \times 6\right) = 10 \times 4.8 = 48 \, \text{ounces}.
   \]

3. Total missing water:
   \[
   60 - 48 = 12 \, \text{ounces}.
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Each glass is missing 1.2 ounces, and there are 10 glasses:
  \[
  1.2 \times 10 = 12 \, \text{ounces}.
  \]
- The total capacity is 60 ounces, and the total current water is 48 ounces:
  \[
  60 - 48 = 12 \, \text{ounces}.
  \]

Both checks confirm the result.

---

The answer is **12 ounces**.

Q: Mark constructed a deck that was 30 feet by 40 feet.  It cost $3 per square foot.  He then paid an extra $1 per square foot for sealant.  How much did he pay?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Mark constructed a deck measuring **30 feet by 40 feet**.
- The cost of construction was **$3 per square foot**.
- He paid an additional **$1 per square foot** for sealant.
- We assume the deck is rectangular, and the area is calculated as length × width.
- We assume the costs are uniform across the entire deck and there are no additional fees or discounts.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total area of the deck first, then multiply the area by the cost per square foot for construction and sealant separately. Add the two costs to find the total.

**Method 2:**  
Combine the cost per square foot for construction and sealant into a single rate ($3 + $1 = $4 per square foot). Multiply the combined rate by the total area of the deck to find the total cost directly.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. **Calculate the area of the deck:**  
   Area = length × width = 30 × 40 = **1200 square feet**.

2. **Calculate the construction cost:**  
   Construction cost = area × cost per square foot = 1200 × $3 = **$3600**.

3. **Calculate the sealant cost:**  
   Sealant cost = area × cost per square foot = 1200 × $1 = **$1200**.

4. **Add the two costs:**  
   Total cost = construction cost + sealant cost = $3600 + $1200 = **$4800**.

#### **Method 2:**
1. **Combine the costs per square foot:**  
   Combined cost per square foot = $3 (construction) + $1 (sealant) = **$4 per square foot**.

2. **Calculate the area of the deck:**  
   Area = length × width = 30 × 40 = **1200 square feet**.

3. **Calculate the total cost directly:**  
   Total cost = area × combined cost per square foot = 1200 × $4 = **$4800**.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** breaks the problem into smaller steps, making it easier to follow and verify each calculation. It is particularly useful if the costs need to be tracked separately (e.g., for budgeting purposes). However, it involves more steps and intermediate calculations.
- **Method 2** is more efficient because it combines the costs upfront and calculates the total cost in fewer steps. It is ideal for situations where the combined cost is all that matters. However, it assumes the user is comfortable combining rates correctly.

**Conclusion:** Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but **Method 2** is more efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 2**:
1. Combine the costs per square foot: $3 + $1 = **$4 per square foot**.
2. Calculate the area of the deck: 30 × 40 = **1200 square feet**.
3. Calculate the total cost: 1200 × $4 = **$4800**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recalculate using **Method 1**:  
  Construction cost = 1200 × $3 = $3600.  
  Sealant cost = 1200 × $1 = $1200.  
  Total cost = $3600 + $1200 = **$4800**.  

- The calculations are consistent across both methods. The result is reasonable given the dimensions and costs provided.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **$4800**.

Q: Clinton has twice as many shoes in his wardrobe as belts.  He has 2 more belts than hats and currently has 5 hats.  How many shoes does Clinton have?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Clinton has twice as many shoes as belts.
- Clinton has 2 more belts than hats.
- Clinton currently has 5 hats.
- We assume all numbers are whole numbers and there are no fractional items.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Use algebra to represent the relationships between hats, belts, and shoes.  
- Let the number of hats be \( h \), belts be \( b \), and shoes be \( s \).  
- Use the given relationships: \( h = 5 \), \( b = h + 2 \), and \( s = 2b \).  
- Solve step by step.

**Method 2:**  
- Solve the problem sequentially using substitution and logical reasoning.  
- Start with the number of hats, calculate the number of belts, and then calculate the number of shoes.  
- Use direct arithmetic instead of algebra.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Algebraic Approach**
1. Start with the given relationships:
   - \( h = 5 \) (Clinton has 5 hats).
   - \( b = h + 2 \) (Clinton has 2 more belts than hats).
   - \( s = 2b \) (Clinton has twice as many shoes as belts).

2. Substitute \( h = 5 \) into \( b = h + 2 \):
   - \( b = 5 + 2 = 7 \).  
   - Clinton has 7 belts.

3. Substitute \( b = 7 \) into \( s = 2b \):
   - \( s = 2 \times 7 = 14 \).  
   - Clinton has 14 shoes.

4. Double-check:
   - Hats: \( h = 5 \).  
   - Belts: \( b = h + 2 = 5 + 2 = 7 \).  
   - Shoes: \( s = 2b = 2 \times 7 = 14 \).  
   - All calculations are consistent.

#### **Method 2: Sequential Substitution**
1. Start with the number of hats:
   - Clinton has 5 hats.

2. Calculate the number of belts:
   - Clinton has 2 more belts than hats: \( 5 + 2 = 7 \).  
   - Clinton has 7 belts.

3. Calculate the number of shoes:

   - Clinton has 14 shoes.

4. Double-check:
   - Hats: \( 5 \).  
   - Belts: \( 5 + 2 = 7 \).  
   - Shoes: \( 2 \times 7 = 14 \).  
   - All calculations are consistent.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Algebraic Approach):**  
  - This method is systematic and generalizable to more complex problems.  
  - It clearly defines relationships and uses substitution to solve step by step.  
  - However, it may be slightly more abstract for simpler problems like this one.

- **Method 2 (Sequential Substitution):**  
  - This method is straightforward and intuitive for problems with simple relationships.  
  - It avoids introducing variables and focuses on direct arithmetic.  
  - It is faster and easier to follow for this specific problem.

**Conclusion:**  
For this problem, **Method 2** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.

Using **Method 2**:
1. Clinton has 5 hats.

Q: In a car racing competition, Skye drove a 6-kilometer track. For the first 3 kilometers, his speed was 150 kilometers per hour. For the next 2 kilometers, his speed was 50 kilometers per hour more. For the remaining 1 kilometer, his speed was twice as fast as his speed on the first 3 kilometers. What is Skye's average speed for the entire race?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

- Skye drove a 6-kilometer track.
- For the first 3 kilometers, his speed was **150 kilometers per hour**.
- For the next 2 kilometers, his speed was **50 kilometers per hour more**, i.e., **200 kilometers per hour**.
- For the remaining 1 kilometer, his speed was **twice as fast as his speed on the first 3 kilometers**, i.e., **300 kilometers per hour**.
- We are tasked with finding Skye's **average speed** for the entire race.
- Average speed is calculated as **total distance divided by total time**.
- Assumptions:
  - Skye maintained constant speeds for each segment.
  - The track is flat, and external factors like acceleration or deceleration are ignored.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.

#### **Method 1: Calculate total time for each segment and use the formula for average speed.**
- Logic: Break the race into three segments, calculate the time taken for each segment using the formula \( \text{time} = \frac{\text{distance}}{\text{speed}} \), sum the times, and divide the total distance (6 km) by the total time.

#### **Method 2: Use weighted averages based on distances and speeds.**
- Logic: Since average speed is the weighted average of speeds over different distances, calculate the contribution of each segment to the total average speed directly using proportions.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Calculate total time and average speed.**

1. **Segment 1 (First 3 kilometers):**
   - Speed = 150 km/h.
   - Distance = 3 km.
   - Time = \( \frac{\text{distance}}{\text{speed}} = \frac{3}{150} = 0.02 \) hours.

2. **Segment 2 (Next 2 kilometers):**
   - Speed = 200 km/h.
   - Distance = 2 km.
   - Time = \( \frac{\text{distance}}{\text{speed}} = \frac{2}{200} = 0.01 \) hours.

3. **Segment 3 (Last 1 kilometer):**
   - Speed = 300 km/h.
   - Distance = 1 km.
   - Time = \( \frac{\text{distance}}{\text{speed}} = \frac{1}{300} = 0.00333 \) hours.

4. **Total time:**
   - Total time = \( 0.02 + 0.01 + 0.00333 = 0.03333 \) hours.

5. **Average speed:**
   - Average speed = \( \frac{\text{total distance}}{\text{total time}} = \frac{6}{0.03333} \approx 180 \) km/h.

---

#### **Method 2: Use weighted averages.**

1. **Segment contributions:**
   - Segment 1: \( \frac{3}{6} \times 150 = 75 \) km/h.
   - Segment 2: \( \frac{2}{6} \times 200 = 66.67 \) km/h.
   - Segment 3: \( \frac{1}{6} \times 300 = 50 \) km/h.

2. **Total average speed:**
   - Average speed = \( 75 + 66.67 + 50 = 191.67 \) km/h.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more precise because it directly calculates the total time and uses the formula for average speed. It avoids rounding errors and is mathematically rigorous.
- **Method 2** is faster but introduces rounding errors due to the weighted average approach. It is less reliable for problems requiring high precision.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

- **Method 1** is better due to its precision and reliability.
- Using Method 1:
  - Total time = \( 0.03333 \) hours.
  - Average speed = \( \frac{6}{0.03333} \approx 180 \) km/h.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recalculate total time:
  - Segment 1: \( \frac{3}{150} = 0.02 \) hours.
  - Segment 2: \( \frac{2}{200} = 0.01 \) hours.
  - Segment 3: \( \frac{1}{300} = 0.00333 \) hours.
  - Total time = \( 0.02 + 0.01 + 0.00333 = 0.03333 \) hours.
- Recalculate average speed:
  - \( \frac{6}{0.03333} = 180 \) km/h.
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

The answer is **180 km/h**.

Q: Ali had a stock of 800 books in his Room. He sold 60 on Monday, 10 on Tuesday, 20 on Wednesday, 44 on Thursday and 66 on Friday. How many books were not sold?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Ali started with **800 books** in his room.
- He sold books on the following days:
  - **60 on Monday**
  - **10 on Tuesday**
  - **20 on Wednesday**
  - **44 on Thursday**
  - **66 on Friday**
- We assume no books were added to the stock during the week.
- The goal is to determine how many books were **not sold** by the end of the week.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Total books sold, then subtract from initial stock.**
- Add up the number of books sold across all days.
- Subtract the total books sold from the initial stock of 800 to find the number of books not sold.

**Method 2: Subtract books sold day by day from the initial stock.**
- Start with the initial stock of 800 books.
- Subtract the number of books sold each day sequentially (Monday through Friday).
- The remaining number will be the books not sold.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Total books sold, then subtract from initial stock**
1. Add up the books sold:
   - Monday: 60
   - Tuesday: 10
   - Wednesday: 20
   - Thursday: 44
   - Friday: 66
   - Total books sold = \( 60 + 10 + 20 + 44 + 66 \)
   - \( 60 + 10 = 70 \)
   - \( 70 + 20 = 90 \)
   - \( 90 + 44 = 134 \)
   - \( 134 + 66 = 200 \)
   - Total books sold = **200**.

2. Subtract total books sold from the initial stock:
   - Initial stock = 800
   - Books not sold = \( 800 - 200 = 600 \).

3. Double-check:
   - Re-add the books sold: \( 60 + 10 + 20 + 44 + 66 = 200 \).
   - Subtract again: \( 800 - 200 = 600 \).

#### **Method 2: Subtract books sold day by day from the initial stock**
1. Start with the initial stock of 800 books.
2. Subtract books sold each day:
   - After Monday: \( 800 - 60 = 740 \)
   - After Tuesday: \( 740 - 10 = 730 \)
   - After Wednesday: \( 730 - 20 = 710 \)
   - After Thursday: \( 710 - 44 = 666 \)
   - After Friday: \( 666 - 66 = 600 \).

3. Double-check:
   - Sequential subtraction matches: \( 800 - 60 - 10 - 20 - 44 - 66 = 600 \).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more efficient because it involves fewer steps: summing the total books sold and subtracting from the initial stock. It is less prone to errors in intermediate steps.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and involves step-by-step subtraction, which can be useful for tracking changes day by day but is more time-consuming and prone to arithmetic mistakes.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Solution:
  - Total books sold = \( 60 + 10 + 20 + 44 + 66 = 200 \).
  - Books not sold = \( 800 - 200 = 600 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Re-add books sold: \( 60 + 10 + 20 + 44 + 66 = 200 \).
- Subtract again: \( 800 - 200 = 600 \).
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

---

The answer is **600**.

Q: Tobias is buying a new pair of shoes that costs $95. He has been saving up his money each month for the past three months. He gets a $5 allowance a month. He also mows lawns and shovels driveways. He charges $15 to mow a lawn and $7 to shovel. After buying the shoes, he has $15 in change. If he mows 4 lawns, how many driveways did he shovel?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Tobias is buying shoes that cost $95.
- After buying the shoes, he has $15 left in change.
- Tobias saved money over three months, receiving a $5 allowance each month. Total allowance: \( 3 \times 5 = 15 \).
- Tobias mows lawns for $15 each and shovels driveways for $7 each.
- Tobias mowed 4 lawns. Total earnings from mowing: \( 4 \times 15 = 60 \).
- We need to determine how many driveways Tobias shoveled to afford the shoes and still have $15 left.

**Assumptions:**
- Tobias spent all his savings on the shoes and has no other sources of income.
- All calculations are in dollars, and there are no additional expenses or taxes.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.

**Method 1: Use a direct equation to calculate the total money Tobias earned and solve for the number of driveways shoveled.**
- Total money earned = allowance + earnings from mowing + earnings from shoveling.
- Subtract the cost of the shoes and equate the remaining money to $15.
- Solve for the number of driveways shoveled.

**Method 2: Use trial-and-error reasoning based on the known earnings from mowing and allowance.**
- Start with the total money Tobias needs to have ($95 for shoes + $15 leftover = $110).
- Subtract the known earnings from mowing and allowance, then divide the remaining amount by $7 (the price per driveway shoveled).

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Direct equation approach**
1. Let \( x \) represent the number of driveways shoveled.
2. Total money earned = allowance + mowing earnings + shoveling earnings:
   \[
   \text{Total money earned} = 15 + 60 + (7 \times x)
   \]
3. After buying the shoes, Tobias has $15 left:
   \[
   15 + 60 + (7 \times x) - 95 = 15
   \]
4. Simplify the equation:
   \[
   75 + (7 \times x) - 95 = 15
   \]
   \[
   (7 \times x) - 20 = 15
   \]
   \[
   7 \times x = 35
   \]
5. Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   x = \frac{35}{7} = 5
   \]

#### **Method 2: Trial-and-error reasoning**
1. Tobias needs $110 total ($95 for shoes + $15 leftover).
2. Known earnings:
   - Allowance: $15.
   - Mowing: $60.
   - Total from allowance and mowing: \( 15 + 60 = 75 \).
3. Subtract known earnings from the total needed:
   \[
   110 - 75 = 35
   \]
4. Each driveway shoveled earns $7. Divide the remaining amount by $7:
   \[
   \frac{35}{7} = 5
   \]
5. Tobias shoveled 5 driveways.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** (Direct equation approach) is more systematic and algebraic, making it easier to follow for complex problems. It avoids guesswork and ensures accuracy.
- **Method 2** (Trial-and-error reasoning) is simpler and intuitive for smaller numbers but may become cumbersome for larger or more complex scenarios.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for clarity and reliability, especially for problems requiring precise calculations.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

Using **Method 1**, we found:
\[
x = 5
\]
Tobias shoveled 5 driveways.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.

1. Total earnings:
   - Allowance: $15.
   - Mowing: \( 4 \times 15 = 60 \).
   - Shoveling: \( 5 \times 7 = 35 \).
   - Total: \( 15 + 60 + 35 = 110 \).
2. Subtract the cost of the shoes:
   \[
   110 - 95 = 15
   \]
3. Tobias has $15 left, confirming the solution.

---

The answer is **5**.

5.0

Q: Each yogurt is topped with 8 banana slices.  One banana will yield 10 slices.  If Vivian needs to make 5 yogurts, how many bananas does she need to buy?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Each yogurt requires **8 banana slices**.
- One banana yields **10 slices**.
- Vivian needs to make **5 yogurts**.
- Assumptions:
  - All bananas yield exactly 10 slices (no waste or variation).
  - Vivian cannot buy a fraction of a banana; she must buy whole bananas.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the total number of banana slices needed for 5 yogurts: \( 5 \times 8 \).  
- Determine how many bananas are required by dividing the total slices by the slices per banana: \( \text{total slices} \div 10 \).  
- Round up to the nearest whole number since partial bananas cannot be purchased.

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the number of yogurts one banana can fully supply: \( 10 \div 8 \).  
- Determine how many bananas are needed to supply 5 yogurts by dividing 5 by the number of yogurts per banana.  
- Round up to the nearest whole number.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the total number of banana slices needed:  
   \( 5 \times 8 = 40 \) slices.
2. Determine how many bananas are required:  
   \( 40 \div 10 = 4 \) bananas.
3. Since \( 40 \div 10 \) is a whole number, no rounding is needed. Vivian needs exactly **4 bananas**.

**Check for pitfalls:**  
- This method assumes that the division \( 40 \div 10 \) is exact, which it is in this case.  
- If the total slices were not a multiple of 10, rounding up would be necessary.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate how many yogurts one banana can supply:  
   \( 10 \div 8 = 1.25 \) yogurts per banana.
2. Determine how many bananas are needed for 5 yogurts:  
   \( 5 \div 1.25 = 4 \) bananas.
3. Since \( 5 \div 1.25 \) is a whole number, no rounding is needed. Vivian needs exactly **4 bananas**.

**Check for pitfalls:**  
- This method involves dividing by a fraction, which can be error-prone if not calculated carefully.  
- The result \( 5 \div 1.25 = 4 \) is exact, so no rounding is required.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward because it directly calculates the total slices needed and divides by the slices per banana. It avoids dealing with fractions or decimals until the final step.  
- **Method 2** involves dividing by a fraction, which can be less intuitive and more prone to errors. However, it still works and gives the correct result.  
- **Method 1** is clearer, more reliable, and easier to follow.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Total slices needed: \( 5 \times 8 = 40 \).  
2. Bananas required: \( 40 \div 10 = 4 \).  
3. Since \( 40 \div 10 \) is exact, Vivian needs **4 bananas**.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the total slices: \( 5 \times 8 = 40 \).  
- Verify the slices per banana: \( 40 \div 10 = 4 \).  
- Verify that 4 bananas yield \( 4 \times 10 = 40 \) slices, which is exactly enough for 5 yogurts.  
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **4**.

Q: George went to a movie theater to see a movie. He paid $16 for the ticket and decided to buy some nachos. The nachos were half the price of the ticket. How much did George pay in total for the visit to the movie theater?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- George paid **$16** for the movie ticket.
- The nachos cost **half the price of the ticket**, which is \( \frac{16}{2} = 8 \) dollars.
- We assume there are no additional costs (e.g., taxes, tips, or other purchases).
- The total cost is the sum of the ticket price and the nachos price.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the cost of the nachos by dividing the ticket price by 2.
- Add the ticket price and the nachos price to find the total.

**Method 2:**  
- Represent the total cost as \( T = \text{ticket price} + \text{nachos price} \).
- Substitute the nachos price as \( \frac{\text{ticket price}}{2} \), so \( T = 16 + \frac{16}{2} \).
- Solve for \( T \) directly.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the cost of the nachos:
   \[
   \text{Nachos price} = \frac{\text{Ticket price}}{2} = \frac{16}{2} = 8 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
2. Add the ticket price and the nachos price:
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = \text{Ticket price} + \text{Nachos price} = 16 + 8 = 24 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
3. Double-check:
   - Nachos are half the ticket price: \( \frac{16}{2} = 8 \), correct.
   - Adding \( 16 + 8 = 24 \), correct.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Represent the total cost as:
   \[
   T = \text{Ticket price} + \text{Nachos price}.
   \]
2. Substitute the nachos price as \( \frac{\text{Ticket price}}{2} \):
   \[
   T = 16 + \frac{16}{2}.
   \]
3. Simplify:
   \[
   T = 16 + 8 = 24 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
4. Double-check:
   - Substituting \( \frac{16}{2} = 8 \) is correct.
   - Adding \( 16 + 8 = 24 \), correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and intuitive, as it breaks the problem into smaller, clear steps (calculate nachos price, then add).
- **Method 2** is more algebraic and compact, which may be faster for someone comfortable with substitution but could be less intuitive for others.
- Both methods are equally reliable and yield the same result.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and simplicity.
1. Calculate the nachos price:
   \[
   \text{Nachos price} = \frac{16}{2} = 8 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
2. Add the ticket price and the nachos price:
   \[
   \text{Total cost} = 16 + 8 = 24 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Nachos are half the ticket price: \( \frac{16}{2} = 8 \), correct.
- Adding \( 16 + 8 = 24 \), correct.
- The result is reasonable given the problem's context.

---

The answer is **24**.

Q: In the post-apocalyptic wasteland, 1 lizard is worth 8 bottle caps, 3 lizards are worth 5 gallons of water, and 1 horse is worth 80 gallons of water. Marla can scavenge for 20 bottle caps each day, but needs to pay 4 bottle caps per night for food and shelter. How many days does it take Marla to collect all the bottlecaps she needs to buy a horse?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

- 1 lizard = 8 bottle caps.
- 3 lizards = 5 gallons of water.
- 1 horse = 80 gallons of water.
- Marla can scavenge 20 bottle caps per day.
- Marla spends 4 bottle caps per night for food and shelter.
- We assume Marla starts with no bottle caps and no other resources.
- The goal is to determine how many days it will take Marla to collect enough bottle caps to buy a horse.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.

**Method 1: Direct conversion of the horse's value into bottle caps.**
1. Convert the horse's value (80 gallons of water) into lizards using the exchange rate of 3 lizards = 5 gallons of water.
2. Convert the lizards into bottle caps using the exchange rate of 1 lizard = 8 bottle caps.
3. Calculate how many bottle caps Marla needs to save per day after deducting her daily expenses (20 earned - 4 spent = 16 net bottle caps per day).
4. Divide the total bottle caps required by the net daily savings to find the number of days.

**Method 2: Incremental accumulation of bottle caps.**
1. Calculate how many bottle caps Marla can save each day after expenses (20 earned - 4 spent = 16 net bottle caps per day).
2. Incrementally calculate how many days it takes to accumulate enough bottle caps to buy the horse by working backward from the horse's value in bottle caps.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

---

#### **Method 1: Direct conversion of the horse's value into bottle caps.**

1. **Convert the horse's value (80 gallons of water) into lizards.**  
   - 3 lizards = 5 gallons of water.  
   - To find how many lizards are worth 80 gallons of water:  
     \[
     \text{Lizards} = \frac{80 \, \text{gallons}}{5 \, \text{gallons per 3 lizards}} \times 3 = 48 \, \text{lizards}.
     \]

2. **Convert the lizards into bottle caps.**  
   - 1 lizard = 8 bottle caps.  
   - For 48 lizards:  
     \[
     \text{Bottle caps} = 48 \, \text{lizards} \times 8 \, \text{bottle caps per lizard} = 384 \, \text{bottle caps}.
     \]

3. **Calculate Marla's net daily savings.**  
   - Marla earns 20 bottle caps per day but spends 4 bottle caps on food and shelter.  
   - Net daily savings:  
     \[
     \text{Net savings} = 20 - 4 = 16 \, \text{bottle caps per day}.
     \]

4. **Calculate the number of days required.**  
   - Total bottle caps needed = 384.  
   - Days required:  
     \[
     \text{Days} = \frac{\text{Total bottle caps}}{\text{Net daily savings}} = \frac{384}{16} = 24 \, \text{days}.
     \]

---

#### **Method 2: Incremental accumulation of bottle caps.**

1. **Calculate Marla's net daily savings.**  
   - Marla earns 20 bottle caps per day but spends 4 bottle caps on food and shelter.  
   - Net daily savings:  
     \[
     \text{Net savings} = 20 - 4 = 16 \, \text{bottle caps per day}.
     \]

2. **Incrementally calculate the total days required.**  
   - Total bottle caps needed = 384.  
   - Each day, Marla saves 16 bottle caps.  
   - To find the number of days:  
     \[
     \text{Days} = \frac{\text{Total bottle caps}}{\text{Net daily savings}} = \frac{384}{16} = 24 \, \text{days}.
     \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** involves converting the horse's value into bottle caps through intermediate steps (gallons → lizards → bottle caps). It is systematic and ensures all conversions are accounted for, but it requires more calculations.
- **Method 2** skips the intermediate conversions and directly calculates the total days based on the final bottle cap requirement. It is simpler and more efficient for this problem.

Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 2** is more straightforward and less prone to error.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.

Using **Method 2**:  
- Total bottle caps needed = 384.  
- Net daily savings = 16 bottle caps per day.  
- Days required:  
  \[
  \text{Days} = \frac{384}{16} = 24 \, \text{days}.
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.

1. Verify the conversions:  
   - 80 gallons → 48 lizards → 384 bottle caps.  
   - All conversions are correct.

2. Verify the daily savings:  
   - 20 earned - 4 spent = 16 net bottle caps per day.

3. Verify the total days:  
   - 384 bottle caps ÷ 16 bottle caps/day = 24 days.

All calculations check out.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **24 days**.

Q: Alfred likes to save $1,000.00 over 12 months for his Christmas shopping.  He has $100.00 left over from last year's holiday to put towards this year's goal.  How much money does Alfred now need to save each month in order to reach his goal in 12 months?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Alfred's total savings goal for Christmas shopping is **$1,000.00**.
- He already has **$100.00** left over from last year's holiday savings.
- He plans to save the remaining amount over **12 months**.
- We assume Alfred will save an equal amount each month and that there are no additional contributions or deductions.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Subtract the $100.00 Alfred already has from the $1,000.00 goal to find the remaining amount he needs to save.  
- Divide the remaining amount by 12 to calculate the monthly savings required.

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the total monthly savings required to reach $1,000.00 without considering the $100.00 initially.  
- Subtract the $100.00 from the total savings required over 12 months to adjust for the leftover amount.  
- Divide the adjusted amount by 12 to find the monthly savings.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtract first, then divide**
1. Subtract the $100.00 Alfred already has from the $1,000.00 goal:  
   \[
   1,000.00 - 100.00 = 900.00
   \]
   This means Alfred needs to save $900.00 more.

2. Divide the remaining $900.00 by 12 months to find the monthly savings:  
   \[
   900.00 \div 12 = 75.00
   \]
   Alfred needs to save $75.00 per month.

3. Double-check: Multiply the monthly savings by 12 and add the $100.00 he already has:  
   \[
   (75.00 \times 12) + 100.00 = 900.00 + 100.00 = 1,000.00
   \]
   The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2: Divide first, then adjust**
1. Calculate the total monthly savings required to reach $1,000.00 without considering the $100.00:  
   \[
   1,000.00 \div 12 = 83.33\ldots
   \]
   Alfred would need to save $83.33 per month if he had no leftover money.

2. Adjust for the $100.00 he already has by subtracting it from the total savings goal:  
   \[
   1,000.00 - 100.00 = 900.00
   \]
   This means Alfred only needs to save $900.00 more.

3. Divide the adjusted amount by 12 months to find the monthly savings:  
   \[
   900.00 \div 12 = 75.00
   \]
   Alfred needs to save $75.00 per month.

4. Double-check: Multiply the monthly savings by 12 and add the $100.00 he already has:  
   \[
   (75.00 \times 12) + 100.00 = 900.00 + 100.00 = 1,000.00
   \]
   The calculation checks out.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward because it directly subtracts the leftover amount before dividing, reducing the number of steps and potential for error.  
- **Method 2** involves an extra step of calculating the total monthly savings without considering the leftover amount, which is less efficient and introduces unnecessary complexity.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer and more reliable.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Subtract the $100.00 Alfred already has from the $1,000.00 goal:  
   \[
   1,000.00 - 100.00 = 900.00
   \]
2. Divide the remaining $900.00 by 12 months:  
   \[
   900.00 \div 12 = 75.00
   \]
   Alfred needs to save $75.00 per month.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Multiply the monthly savings by 12 and add the $100.00 he already has:  
  \[
  (75.00 \times 12) + 100.00 = 900.00 + 100.00 = 1,000.00
  \]
- The calculation checks out, and the result is reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **$75.00**.
Extracted:
75.0
A:
75.0
